## I

===Original===
“Be quiet, children,” said Mrs. Marble. “Can’t you see that father’s busy?”
So he was. He propped his aching forehead on his hand, and tugged at his reddish moustache in an unhappy attempt at concentration. It was difficult to keep thinking about these wretched figures all the time, and it would have been even if Winnie did not try to poke John with a ruler in the intervals of squirming and muttering over her geometry homework. Mr. Marble worried at his moustache while he peered at the column of figures on the scrap of paper before him. They seemed to be dancing in a faint mist under his eyes. He had been nerving himself for this effort for weeks now, and the instant that he began it he wanted to leave off. He was sure that looking at these figures would do no good. Nothing could do any good now.
The column of figures was headed briefly, “Debts.” Rent was three weeks overdue, and that was the smallest item entered. He owed over four pounds each to the butcher and the baker, and the milk bill came to over five. How on earth had Annie managed to run up a five-pound milk bill? He owed Evans, the grocer, more than six pounds. Mr. Marble felt that he hated Evans, and had hated him ever since the time, a dozen years ago now, when they had arrived in Malcolm Road as a young couple, and Evans, apron, basket and whiskers complete, had called to solicit for their custom. Annie had just told him that Evans had threatened to put the brokers in if he were not paid. The Bank would sack him for certain if anything like that happened. To Mr. Marble’s strained eyes the shape of Mr. Evans suddenly seemed to loom over the paper he was regarding, with a flash of teeth and a leer in his eye like the devil he was. Mr. Marble bit deep into the end of his pencil in a sudden flood of hatred.
===Chinese===
“安静点，孩子们，”马布尔太太（Mrs. Marble）说，“没看见爸爸正忙着吗？”
他也的确正忙着。他一只手撑着隐隐作痛的额头，另一只手揪着那两撇红棕色的胡子，郁郁地想打起精神来。要盯着这些可恶的数字不停地想下去，本来就不是件容易事，何况温妮（Winnie）一面在几何功课上扭来扭去、念念有词，一面还要见缝插针地拿尺子去戳约翰（John），那就更难了。马布尔先生（Mr. Marble）一面低头盯着面前纸片上那一栏数字，一面不住地捻着胡子。那些数字仿佛在他眼前的一层薄雾里跳舞。为了这桩苦差事，他已经给自己鼓了几个星期的劲，可刚一动笔，就想撂下不干。他明知道盯着这些数字看毫无用处。如今是什么办法都没有用处了。
那一栏数字顶上的标题简短得很：“债务。”房租已经拖了三个星期没交，而这还是单子上最小的一笔。他欠肉铺老板四英镑多，欠面包师傅的也是四英镑多，牛奶账算下来竟有五英镑出头。安妮（Annie）到底是怎么欠下五英镑的牛奶账的？他欠杂货商埃文斯（Evans）的超过六英镑。马布尔先生觉得自己恨埃文斯，从十二年前他们小两口搬进马尔科姆路（Malcolm Road）那会儿就恨上了——那时埃文斯系着围裙、挎着篮子、蓄着两撇连鬓胡子，登门招揽生意。安妮方才告诉他，埃文斯放了话，再不还钱就要叫法警上门。真闹到那一步，银行（the Bank）肯定得开除他。在马布尔先生熬得发花的眼睛里，埃文斯先生的身形忽然在他盯着的那张纸上浮现出来，龇着一口牙，眼里闪着邪光，十足是他那号人本来的魔鬼嘴脸。马布尔先生心头涌起一阵恨火，一口咬进铅笔头，咬得死深。

===Original===
There were some other items in the column too. The names of some of the men at the Bank appeared on the sheet of paper, and against them were set the amounts that Mr. Marble owed them. Some of these men had even smaller incomes than his, and yet they managed nevertheless to keep out of debt﻿—and even were sometimes able to lend money to poor devils like himself. But of course they weren’t married, or if they were they did not have extravagant wives like Annie. Not that Annie was extravagant, though. Not really. She was just careless. Rather like himself, thought Mr. Marble, with weary self-reproach, bending again to the figures. His debts amounted to no less than thirty pounds! On the asset side he had put nothing. He knew the amount of his assets too well to bother to do that. He was acutely aware of it. The balance in his account at the Bank was down to five shillings, and he had two florins in his pocket. There was no possibility of overdrawing. That would mean dismissal just the same.
It was his fault, he supposed weakly. He had seen this coming as long ago as last summer, and he had decided then that if they did without a holiday and spent nothing at Christmas they could get straight again. But they had had their holiday, and they had spent more than they should have done at Christmas. No, that had been Annie’s fault. She had said that people would think it funny if they did not go to Worthing as she had told them they were going to do. And she had said it so often that in the end they had gone. And of course she had really been the cause of all those figures that were set against the names of men in the Bank on Mr. Marble’s little list. A man had to have a drink occasionally, when he slipped out of the office at half-past eleven, and of course he had to stand his friends one too, if they were with him. He could have paid for them easily if Annie had not spent all his money for him. And he had to smoke too, and have a good lunch occasionally. Mr. Marble resolutely refused to think of how much he had spent on his hobby of photography. He knew that it was more than he should have done, and somewhere inside his conscience there was a nasty feeling that there was another bill, unreckoned in his list, due to the chemist at the end of the road for things he had bought for this purpose. The shelves in the bathroom upstairs were full of materials, and Mr. Marble did not like to think of this, for he had never used half of them, having lately found more amusement in contemplating his hobby and buying things for it than in actually doing anything.
It was all very annoying and exasperating. How his head ached, and how tired he felt! His mind was numb. The grim feeling of blank despair was swamped by complete lassitude of soul. He realized vaguely that his oft-repeated threat of sending the children to bed without any supper would soon be carried into effect despite himself. He would be sacked from the Bank, and he would never get another job. He knew that well enough. He supposed it would end like the cases one read about in the paper, with his children’s throats cut and himself and his wife dead of gas-poisoning. But at present he hardly cared. He wanted to relax. When those blessed kids had been packed off to bed he would drag the armchair up to the fire and put his feet up on the coalbox and read the paper and be comfortable for a bit. In the decanter in the sideboard there was a little drop of whisky left. Not much of course; three drinks perhaps, or maybe four. Mr. Marble hoped it was four. With a drink and a paper and the fire he could forget his troubles for a little, for he couldn’t do anything towards remedying them this evening. Mr. Marble hardly realized that he had said the same thing to himself every evening for months now. The prospect seemed ineffably alluring. He yearned towards the decanter in the sideboard. And the wind was shrieking outside, sending the rain spattering against the windows. That would make it seem even more comfortable when he was beside the fire.
===Chinese===
栏里还记着别的项目。纸上有几个银行同事的名字，名字后面记着马布尔先生欠他们的数目。这些人里，有的进项比他还少，居然也能不欠账——有时甚至还能借钱给他这样的可怜虫。不过话得说回来，他们当然没成家；就算成了家，也没有安妮那样大手大脚的老婆。可是话说回来，安妮也算不上大手大脚。真的算不上。她只是不上心罢了。倒有点像他自己，马布尔先生想到这里，又疲惫地自我埋怨着，重新低头去看数字。他的债务加起来竟有整整三十英镑！资产那一栏他什么也没填。自己的家底他清楚得很，懒得去填。这一点他心里清楚得发疼：他在银行的账户上只剩五先令，口袋里还有两枚弗罗林。透支是万万不能的。那同样意味着卷铺盖走人。
要说责任，大概也得怪他自己，他有气无力地这么想。早在去年夏天他就看出要出事，那时便打定了主意：全家不去度假，圣诞节一个子儿也不花，就能缓过劲来。可假照度了，圣诞节花的钱也过了头。不，那是安妮的错。安妮说，她逢人便讲他们要上沃辛（Worthing）去，到时候不去，人家要奇怪的。这话她说了一遍又一遍，到头来他们还是去了。自然，记在单子上、落在那些银行同事名下的一笔笔账，归根到底也都是她招来的。男人嘛，上午十一点半从办公室溜出来，总免不了要喝上一杯；朋友们要是在跟前，当然还得请他们一份。要不是安妮替他把钱花了个精光，这点账他轻轻松松就对付了。再说，烟总得抽，像样的午饭偶尔也得吃一顿。至于他在自己那门摄影癖好上到底花了多少钱，马布尔先生断然拒绝去想。他知道花得是多了些；而且在他良心深处的某个角落，有种说不出的别扭滋味——还有一笔账没记进单子里：欠街尾药店老板的，为这门癖好买的东西，月底就该结了。楼上浴室的架子上堆满了材料，马布尔先生不愿去想这些，因为其中一半他从没用过；近来他发现，空想这门癖好、为它买这买那，反倒比真动手干点什么更有乐趣。
这一切真叫人心烦意乱，叫人窝火。头好疼，人好乏！脑子已经木了。那股阴森森、一片空茫的绝望，早被灵魂彻头彻尾的倦怠淹了个透。他迷迷糊糊地意识到，他天天挂在嘴边的那句威胁——不许吃晚饭，直接上床——很快就要不由自主地兑现了。他会被银行开除，往后再也寻不到差事。这一点他清楚得很。他想，结局多半会同报上读到的那些案子一样：孩子们的喉咙被割断，他跟老婆煤气中毒死掉。不过眼下他几乎无所谓了。他只想松快松快。等这两个小祖宗打发上了床，他就把扶手椅拖到炉火跟前，两脚往煤箱上一架，看看报，舒舒服服待上一小会儿。餐柜的酒瓶里还剩一点点威士忌。当然不多了；大概够三杯，兴许四杯。马布尔先生但愿是四杯。有酒，有报，有炉火，他就能把烦恼忘掉一阵子——反正今天晚上，他对烦恼是无计可施的。马布尔先生几乎没意识到，同样这话，他每晚对自己念叨，已经念叨了好几个月。那前景美妙得难以言传。他一心惦记着餐柜里的那只酒瓶。外头风声凄厉，卷着雨点子噼噼啪啪直往窗玻璃上摔。这样一来，等他坐到炉火边，就更显出惬意来了。

===Original===
But the children must be disposed of first. For some obscure reason Mr. Marble had an objection to drinking whisky in front of his children. His wife did not matter so much, although he would have preferred to have her out of the way too. A glance at the clock disappointed him a little. It was only half-past seven, and the children would not be going to bed for another half-hour at the earliest. He felt suddenly irritable. He peered surreptitiously from under his eyebrows to see if he could catch them misbehaving so as to send them off at once. The whisky would taste all the better if he could come to it fresh from a parental triumph and an autocratic exertion of authority.
“Stop that noise, John,” he ordered, with queer, feeble savageness.
John looked round from his chair by the fire a little startled. Five seconds ago he had been mazed in the pages of *How England Saved Europe*, and had been leading the Fusilier Brigade over heaps of dead up the bloodstained hill of Albuera. He gazed vacantly at his father.
===Chinese===
不过，孩子们总得先打发走。不知怎么的，马布尔先生不愿当着孩子的面喝威士忌。老婆倒在其次，虽说他也巴不得她走开。他瞟了一眼时钟，有点失望。才七点半，孩子们最早也得再过半个钟头才上床。他顿时烦躁起来，从眉毛底下偷偷瞟过去，想抓他们个不规矩的现行，好立刻轰走。要能刚演完一场父亲的凯旋、刚专断地行使过一回权威，就走过去摸酒瓶，那威士忌的滋味还要更美。
“别出声，约翰。”他命令道，那股凶劲儿又古怪又虚弱。
约翰从炉边的椅子上回过头来，吓了一跳。五秒钟之前，他还神游在《英国如何拯救欧洲》（*How England Saved Europe*）的书页里，正带着燧发枪兵旅（Fusilier Brigade）踏过成堆的尸体，往阿尔武埃拉（Albuera）那座血染的山冈上冲。他茫茫然地望着父亲。

===Original===
“Don’t look at me like a fool,” spluttered Mr. Marble. “Do what you’re told and stop that noise.” The two orders were synonymous, but John did not realize that.
“What did you tell me?” he asked vaguely.
“No impertinence, now. I said stop that noise.”
“What noise, father?” asked John, more to gain time to collect his thoughts than for any other reason. But the question was fatal.
“Don’t try to deny it,” said Mr. Marble.
“Now, you were making a noise, you know, Johnny,” said Mrs. Marble.
===Chinese===
“别像个傻子似的瞪着我，”马布尔先生气急败坏地说，“叫你怎么着就怎么着，别出声。”这两道命令其实是同一个意思，可约翰没有听出来。
“您跟我说什么来着？”他迷迷糊糊地问。
“不许顶嘴。我说了，别出声。”
“什么声音啊，爸爸？”约翰问。这一问，多半是想争取点工夫好理清思绪，倒没别的意思。可这一问闯了大祸。
“还想抵赖。”马布尔先生说。
“好了，你方才就是在闹腾，你自己知道的，约翰尼。”马布尔太太说。

===Original===
“You were kicking with your feet,” chimed in Winnie.
“I didn’t deny it,” protested John.
“You did,” said Mrs. Marble.
“You did,” said Winnie.
“Be quiet, Winnie,” snapped Mr. Marble, rounding on his usual favourite in unusual fashion. “You’re as bad as he is, and you know it. Have you done your homework yet? I send you to a good school, and this is all the return I get for it.”
“Why, I got a scholarship,” replied Winnie, with a jerk of her head.
===Chinese===
“你还蹬脚了呢。”温妮跟着帮腔。
“我没有抵赖。”约翰抗议道。
“你就是抵赖了。”马布尔太太说。
“你就是抵赖了。”温妮说。
“你也安静，温妮。”马布尔先生厉声呵斥，一反常态地朝他素来偏爱的这个孩子发作起来。“你跟他一样不省心，你自己心里清楚。功课做了没有？我花那么多钱送你们上好学堂，换来的就是这个。”
“哟，我可是拿了奖学金的。”温妮答道，小脑袋一扬。

===Original===
“Are you being impertinent too?” demanded Mr. Marble. “I don’t know what you children are coming to. It’s time for bed when you start being rude to your parents.”
The fatal words were said, and the children looked at each other in dismay. Mrs. Marble made a typical fainthearted effort on their behalf.
“Oh, not just yet, father,” she said.
That was all the opposition that Mr. Marble needed to make him quite decided on the matter.
“At once,” he said. “John, go to bed﻿—and leave that book down here too. Winnie, pack your things neatly ready for the morning and go along too. Let this be a lesson to you.”
“But I haven’t done my homework,” wailed Winnie, “and there’ll be such a row if I haven’t done it tomorrow.”
===Chinese===
“你也想顶嘴是不是？”马布尔先生质问道。“真不知道你们这些孩子要变成什么样子。对爹妈无礼，那就该上床睡觉了。”
这句要命的话一出口，两个孩子慌慌张张地你看我、我看你。马布尔太太照例怯生生地替他们讨了个情。
“哎呀，还不到时候呢，他爸。”她说。
马布尔先生要的正是这么一点违抗，好让他在这件事上把主意彻底拿定。
“马上去。”他说。“约翰，上床去——那本书也留在楼下。温妮，把东西收拾齐整，预备好明早上学，也一块儿去。让这一回给你们长个记性。”
“可我功课还没做完呢，”温妮哭丧着脸说，“明天要是交不上，可有一场好吵的。”

===Original===
John did not answer. He was wondering how the Fusiliers would get on without him for the rest of their advance. Even Mrs. Marble was moved to further protest at this drastic action, but her halfhearted entreaties were ignored by both sides.
“Be quick, I am waiting,” said Mr. Marble.
It was inevitable. Sullenly Winnie began to stack her books together. John stood up and put *How England Saved Europe* on the table. It was then, at the eleventh hour, that diversion came. It came in the form of a loud knocking at the street door. For a second everyone looked at each other startled, for visitors were a rarity in Malcolm Road, especially at the extraordinary hour of half-past seven. Winnie recovered first.
“I’ll go,” she said, and slipped through into the hall.
The others heard her tugging at the latch, and then the gas suddenly flickered as a gust of wind came rushing in with the opening of the street door. A strange, loud, masculine voice made itself heard asking for Mr. Marble. He was about to go out too when Winnie reappeared.
“Somebody for you, father,” she said, and even as she spoke the owner of the strange voice came in behind her.
===Chinese===
约翰没有作声。他心里正犯愁：往后剩下的冲锋，没有他领着，燧发枪兵们可怎么打下去。就连马布尔太太也给这番狠辣的处置弄得想再争几句，可她那半心半意的恳求，两边谁都没有理会。
“快点，我等着呢。”马布尔先生说。
大势已去，无可挽回。温妮闷闷地开始把书本摞起来。约翰站起身，把《英国如何拯救欧洲》搁在桌上。就在这最后关头，忽然来了个岔子——临街的大门上响起咚咚的敲门声。一时间全家人惊得面面相觑：马尔科姆路这一带难得有客上门，何况是在七点半这种稀奇的钟点。温妮第一个回过神来。
“我去。”她说着，一溜身钻进过道里去了。
其余人听见她拽门闩的声音，跟着临街大门一开，一股风灌进来，煤气灯火苗忽地一暗。一个陌生的、响亮的男人嗓音嚷着要见马布尔先生。马布尔先生正要也出去，温妮回来了。
“爸爸，有人找你。”她说。话音未落，那个陌生嗓音的主人已经跟着她走进屋来。

===Original===
He was tall, and young, and seemed to be a study in browns, for he wore a brown trench coat and muffler, a brown tweed suit underneath, with brown shoes and socks. His face, too, was brown, although the shrieking wind outside had whipped it to a warm flush. He was young and debonair and handsome, and the sparkle of the raindrops on his muffler and the flash of his dark eyes and the gust of cold wind that entered the room at the same time as himself all combined to make his unexpected appearance as dramatic as even John, standing amazed by the fire, could wish for.
The stranger paused for a moment at the doorway.
“Good evening,” he said, a little diffidently.
“Good evening,” replied Mr. Marble, wondering who on earth he was.
“I suppose you are my Uncle William,” said the new arrival. “I didn’t expect you to know me.”
“I’m afraid I don’t.”
===Chinese===
来人高高的个子，年轻，通身上下像是一幅棕色的习作：棕色风衣，棕色围巾，里头一身棕色花呢西装，棕色的鞋，棕色的袜。连脸膛也是棕色的，虽然外头呼啸的风已经把他吹得红光满面。他年轻、洒脱、英俊；围巾上晶亮的水珠，乌黑眸子里闪动的光，还有跟他一道涌进屋来的那股冷风，凑在一起，把这场不期而至的登场烘托得戏剧味十足——连站在炉边看得出神的约翰，心里盼的热闹，也就不过如此了。
陌生人在门口略停了停。
“晚上好。”他说，带着几分怯意。
“晚上好。”马布尔先生应道，心里直纳闷这位到底是谁。
“您就是我的舅舅威廉（William）吧，”新来的客人说，“我本来也没指望您能认出我来。”
“怕是真不认得。”

===Original===
“My mother was Mrs. Medland, Mrs. Winnie Medland, your sister, sir, I think. I have just come from Melbourne.”
“Oh, of course. You’re Winnie’s boy? Come on in﻿—no, let’s get your coat off first. Annie, poke up the fire. Winnie, clear that stuff off that chair.”
Mr. Marble bustled out into the hall with his guest. His family heard him helping him off with his coat, and then﻿—
“And how is your mother now?”
There was no immediate answer to this question. The trench coat and the hat had been hung up on the hall-stand and the pair were about to reappear in the dining-room before the listeners there heard the hesitant, almost whispered, reply.
“She’s dead. She died﻿—six months ago.”
===Chinese===
“我母亲是梅德兰太太（Mrs. Medland），温妮·梅德兰太太，我想，是您的姐姐。我刚从墨尔本（Melbourne）来。”
“哦，当然了。你就是温妮的儿子？快请进——不，先把你外衣脱了。安妮，把火拨旺点儿。温妮，把椅子上的东西挪开。”
马布尔先生风风火火地陪着客人到过道里去了。家里人听见他张罗着帮客人脱大衣，接着——
“你母亲如今可好？”
这句话没有得到立刻的回答。风衣和帽子挂上衣帽架，两个人眼看就要重新回到饭厅了，屋里侧耳听着的人方才听见那句迟疑的、近乎耳语的回答。
“她去世了。她是——六个月前去世的。”

===Original===
Mr. Marble was still muttering the conventional condolences as they reentered the dining-room, but he changed to clumsy brightness at the earliest possible moment. Truth to tell, he was not particularly interested in his sister Winnie, of whom he probably had not thought for the thirteen and a half years that had elapsed since he had had his daughter christened after her. Also he was feeling a little annoyed with this young man for turning up and interfering with the comfort of his evening. But Mr. Marble was not the man to show it. Hostility of any kind﻿—even the instinctive hostility towards strangers﻿—was a feeling to be carefully concealed on every occasion. That was the lesson learned as a result of a lifetime spent in carrying out the orders of other people.
“Annie,” said Mr. Marble, “this is our young nephew, Jim. Do you remember him when he was a little boy, just going out to Australia with Winnie and Tom? I think I can. You wore a sailor suit, didn’t you, er﻿—Jim. Here, Winnie, this is a new cousin for you, one you never knew you had. Now sit down, sit down, man, and let’s hear all your news.”
“Take that chair, Mr.﻿—Jim, I mean,” said Mrs. Marble, stumbling in embarrassed fashion at having to address an opulently clothed and handsome stranger by his Christian name, “you must be frozen.”
The new arrival was nearly as shy as was his hostess, but he suffered himself to be thrust into the best chair in the house﻿—the one Mr. Marble had coveted all the evening﻿—while Mrs. Marble ransacked her brain for something to talk about, and while the children drew as close as they could while remaining in the background.
Mr. Marble plunged heavily into conversation.
“When did you arrive?” he asked.
===Chinese===
两人再进饭厅时，马布尔先生嘴里还在嗫嚅着那套照例的吊唁话，可一逮着机会，就赶紧换上一副笨拙的欢喜劲儿。说实在的，他对妹妹温妮算不得挂心，打从给女儿取了她的名字抱去受洗，这十三年半里，他多半压根没想起过她。同时，对这个年轻人他还有几分恼火：偏偏这时候冒出来，搅了他一晚上的清净。可马布尔先生不是那种把心思挂在脸上的人。任何一种敌意——哪怕是对生人本能的戒心——都是无论何时都得小心藏好的感情。那是一辈子听人差遣、照人吩咐行事换来的教训。
“安妮，”马布尔先生说，“这是咱们的小外甥吉姆（Jim）。他还是个小娃娃、跟着温妮和汤姆（Tom）去澳大利亚那阵子，你还记得他吗？我想我还记得。你那时候穿的是水手服吧，呃——吉姆。来，温妮，这是给你新添的一位表哥，你从来不知道自己还有这么一位。好了，坐，快坐下，小伙子，把你的新鲜事都讲给我们听听。”
“坐那把椅子，梅——吉姆先生，我是说，吉姆，”马布尔太太说。她窘得话都磕磕绊绊，因为不得不直呼一位衣着阔气、相貌堂堂的生人的教名。“您准是冻透了。”
新来的客人差不多同女主人一样腼腆，可他还是由着人家把他按进全屋最好的一把椅子里——马布尔先生惦记了它一整晚——马布尔太太则搜肠刮肚，想寻出句把话来讲，孩子们缩在后头，又想凑得越近越好。
马布尔先生闷头闷脑、一头扎进了谈话。
“你什么时候到的？”他问。

===Original===
“This morning only. I came on the *Malina*, arriving at Tilbury at twelve. In fact, I only reached London and found a hotel and had something to eat before coming here.”
“But how did you know we lived here?”
“Mother told me your address before she d‑died.” The stumble was excusable. After all, the boy was no more than twenty. “We’d often talked about this trip. She was coming with me, you see. She never liked Australia﻿—I don’t know why﻿—and after father died﻿—”
“Tom dead as well? That’s bad luck.”
“Yes. He died the beginning of last year. It was that really that made mother﻿—”
“Quite, quite,” Mrs. Marble clucked in sympathetic chorus. She hated to hear of anyone dying.
===Chinese===
“就今天早上。我坐的是玛丽娜号（*Malina*），十二点进的蒂尔伯里（Tilbury）。说实在的，我到了伦敦，寻了家旅馆，胡乱吃了点东西，就上您这儿来了。”
“那你怎么知道我们住这儿？”
“母亲去——去世前把地址告诉了我。”这口结巴情有可原。这孩子毕竟还不满二十岁。“我们常念叨这趟旅行。她本来要跟我一块儿来的。她一直不喜欢澳大利亚——我也不知为什么——后来父亲一去世，就——”
“汤姆也过世了？真不走运。”
“是啊。他是去年年头没的。其实正是那件事，让母亲——”
“可不是，可不是。”马布尔太太啧啧连声地跟着表示同情。她最听不得谁死了。

===Original===
Mr. Marble made haste to change the conversation to matters more interesting.
“And how was your father’s business getting on?” he demanded.
“Oh, pretty well. He made a lot of money during the war. He didn’t want to, you know, but it just came, he said. But mother sold out after he died. She said she couldn’t run that big shipping office by herself, and I was too young, and they offered a good price for it, so she took it.”
“So you’re a young man of leisure now, eh?”
“I suppose so. I’ve only just come out of college, you know. Melbourne University. I’m having a look round to start with. That’s what mother always planned for me.”
“Quite right too,” said Mr. Marble, with the instinctive deference towards the independently rich which was by now an inevitable trait in his character.
===Chinese===
马布尔先生赶紧把话头岔到更有意思的事情上去。
“你父亲那摊生意后来如何？”他问。
“哦，还不赖。打仗那几年他发了财。其实他并不想发，可他自己说，钱是自个儿送上门的。他一去世，母亲就把生意盘了出去。她说那么大一家航运行，她一个人张罗不动，我又太年轻，人家出的价钱也公道，她就卖了。”
“这么说，你如今是个有闲的阔少爷了，嗯？”
“就算是吧。您知道，我刚出大学门。墨尔本大学。眼下先四处走走看看。母亲从前给我盘算的就是这样。”
“那敢情好。”马布尔先生说，语气里带着对不靠薪水吃饭的有钱人本能的恭谨——这如今已成了他性格里免不了的一块。

===Original===
For a moment the conversation flagged, and the boy, still a little shy, had leisure to look about him. These were the only relatives he had on earth, and he would like to make the most of them, although, he confessed to himself, he was not greatly attracted at first sight. The room was frankly hideous. The flowered wallpaper was covered with photographs and with the worst kind of engravings. The spurious marble mantelpiece was littered with horrible vases. Of the two armchairs one was covered with plush, the other with a chintz that blended unhappily with the wallpaper. The other chairs were plain bentwood ones. On a table in the window were dusty aspidistras in vast green china pots. In the armchair opposite him sat his uncle, in a shabby blue suit flagrantly spotted here and there. He was a small man, with sparse reddish hair and a bristling moustache of the same colour. His weak grey eyes bore a worried expression﻿—more worried even than the expression he had already noticed in the eyes of the tired men who had sat opposite him in the bus which had brought him here. He had a silver watchchain across his rumpled waistcoat, and on his feet were shapeless carpet slippers, above which showed heather mixture socks sagging garterless round his ankles. Beside him, uncomplaining and uncomfortable on one of the bentwood chairs, sat his wife, frail, pale and shabby; the most noticeable thing about her was her lopsided steel rimmed spectacles. The children were only visible to him when he turned his head uncomfortably. They certainly were more attractive. The little girl, Winnie, bore in her sharp features undeniable promise of good looks, as she sat with her hands in her lap beside the table, and the boy﻿—John, wasn’t he?﻿—was quite a fair specimen of the fourteen-year-old. Nevertheless, young Medland did not feel at all at ease in his present situation. Six weeks on board a first-class liner, the only male passenger unmarried between the ages of fifteen and fifty is not the best introduction to the life of a poverty-stricken suburban home. Medland felt the sudden need to think about something else.
“May I smoke?” he asked.
“Why, yes, of course,” said Mr. Marble, rousing himself suddenly to his hospitable duties.
Mr. Marble plunged into his pocket in search of the battered yellow packet of cigarettes that lay there. It held three cigarettes, he knew, and he had been treasuring them to smoke himself later in the evening. He spent as long as he could before producing them, and he was successful in his tactics. Medland already had produced his case and was offering it to him.
===Chinese===
谈话冷了一会儿场，那年轻人还带着几分怯意，正好得空打量起四周来。这些人是他在这个世上仅有的亲人，他想同他们好好处一处，虽说他也暗自承认，乍一照面，他们并没怎么引他喜欢。这屋子实在丑得可以。碎花壁纸糊的墙上挂满了相片，还有最不入流的那种雕版画。假大理石的壁炉台上乱堆着难看的花瓶。两把扶手椅，一把蒙着长毛绒，另一把蒙的印花布，同壁纸配得别别扭扭。其余是几把普普通通的曲木椅。窗前的桌上，大绿瓷盆里的蜘蛛抱蛋落满了灰。他对面的扶手椅里坐着舅舅，一身寒酸的蓝西装，东一点西一块地净是油渍。舅舅是个小个子男人，头发稀疏、颜色发红，同样颜色的胡子硬邦邦地支棱着。一双灰色的眼睛没什么精神，里头的愁苦，比年轻人在载他来的那辆公共汽车上、从对面疲惫男人们的眼睛里瞧见的还要深。皱巴巴的背心上横着一条银表链，脚上趿着一双走了形的绒毯拖鞋，鞋口上露出的杂色花呢袜子没系袜带，松松垮垮地堆在脚踝上。舅舅身边那把曲木椅上，坐着不声不响、浑身不自在的舅母，瘦弱、苍白、寒酸；她身上最打眼的，是那副歪斜的钢框眼镜。孩子们得他不自在地转过头去才看得见。这两个孩子确实招人喜欢些。小姑娘温妮坐在桌子旁边，两手搁在膝上，清秀的眉眼间已分明看得出是个美人胚子；那个男孩——是叫约翰来着？——在十四岁的少年里也称得上相当齐整的一个。尽管如此，年轻的梅德兰在眼下这处境里还是浑身不得劲。在头等邮轮上待了六个星期，又是全船十五到五十岁之间唯一没成家的男客，这可不是叫人领教穷家小户生活的最好引子。梅德兰忽然觉得，非得想点别的什么不可。
“我可以抽支烟吗？”他问。
“哦，当然，当然。”马布尔先生说，猛然回过神来，记起自己待客的本分。
马布尔先生把手伸进口袋，去摸那盒压得皱皱巴巴的黄纸壳香烟。他知道里头还剩三支，一直存着打算夜里自己慢慢受用。他磨磨蹭蹭，迟迟不肯掏出来，这一套战术成功了：梅德兰已经先掏出自己的烟盒，正递过来敬他。

===Original===
It was a leather case, the parting gift of one of the middle-aged women on the boat. Women never realize that a leather case spoils cigarettes. But this was far more than a cigarette case. It was a substantial wallet, with pockets for stamps and visiting cards, and at the back, sagging open in consequence of the way in which Medland held it, was a compartment for money. And this was full. Marble noticed, as the case was tendered to him, a thick fold of Treasury notes, twenty pounds at least, maybe thirty, decided Marble, gauging it with a bank clerk’s eye. Beside it was another fold of banknotes﻿—five pound notes, most probably. The sight positively dazzled poor Mr. Marble’s vision. And it brought, too, a ray of hope into the grim cells of dumb despair in his soul. It was more than flesh and blood could bear not to remark on it.
“That’s a nice case,” said Mr. Marble, tendering a lighted match to his guest.
“Yes.” Medland drew on his cigarette to make sure it was well lighted. “It was a present,” he added modestly, and he held it out so that his uncle could see it more freely.
The banknotes flashed once more before Marble’s tortured eyes.
===Chinese===
那是只皮面烟盒，船上一位中年太太临别送的礼。女人们永远不明白，皮烟盒是会捂坏烟的。可这远不止是一只烟盒。那是只厚实的皮夹，带插邮票的格子、名片格子，背面还有个放钞票的夹层——因为梅德兰递过来的姿势，那夹层正敞着口。夹层塞得满满的。烟盒递到他跟前时，马布尔瞧见一沓厚厚的国库券（Treasury notes），至少二十英镑，兴许三十，马布尔用银行职员的眼睛一掂量，心里有了数。旁边还叠着又一沓钞票——多半是五英镑面额的。这光景简直把可怜的马布尔先生的双眼都晃花了。它还给他灵魂里那哑口无言的绝望黑牢，投进了一线希望。是凡人的血肉之躯，谁也憋不住不就此说上两句。
“好漂亮的烟盒。”马布尔先生说着，把划着的火柴递给客人。
“可不是。”梅德兰吸了口烟，确信点着了。“人家送的。”他谦虚地补了一句，把烟盒递过去，好让舅舅看个真切。
那沓钞票在马布尔受尽煎熬的眼睛跟前又闪了一下。

===Original===
“Well lined too,” said Marble, striving to keep the envy out of his tone.
“Yes, I got them at Port Said﻿—oh, you mean the notes?” Medland did his best not to show surprise at his uncle’s bad taste. To assist in this he went into even further explanation. “I had to cash one of my letters of credit as soon as I got to London. The voyage left me without a bean, pretty nearly, and what I had was Australian money, of course.”
It was an idle enough speech, but it sufficed to set Mr. Marble thinking rapidly and unsteadily. This boy had arrived just in time to save him. He surely would not deny his newfound uncle a loan? Those Treasury notes would save him, let alone the banknotes. And a loan from a nephew was not the same thing at all as a debt to that devil Evans, who would be putting the bailiffs in directly. It was not even in the same class as a debt to those men at the office, to repay whom only enough to keep them from complaining to higher officials had absorbed all his month’s salary. On the heels of these thoughts came appalling realization of the peril of his position. It was the third of the month only, and he had ten shillings in the world with which to stave off his creditors and to support his family until his next payday. Before this he had shut his eyes to the position with all the small resolution he possessed. But now that there was a chance of escape the danger in which he stood was forced home upon him, making him shudder involuntarily a little and setting his heart thumping heavily in his chest. Mechanically he glanced across at the sideboard in which was the decanter. But he checked himself. He was not going to have to waste one of his last three﻿—or was it four?﻿—drinks on this boy. He thrust the thought of the whisky fiercely on one side and turned to making cautious advances towards the nephew.
“Did you have much difficulty in finding your way here?” he asked﻿—the inevitable question always addressed to the newcomer to the suburbs.
===Chinese===
“里头也够厚实的。”马布尔说，竭力不让话音里带出眼红。
“是啊，在塞得港（Port Said）换的——哦，您是说这些票子？”梅德兰竭力不露出惊讶，免得显出舅舅失了体统。为了遮掩，他索性进一步解释。“我一到伦敦就不得不兑了一张信用证。这趟航程差不多把我坐得一文不名，手头剩的那点，又都是澳大利亚票子。”
这话本来不过随口一提，却足够叫马布尔先生的脑筋飞快地、颠三倒四地转起来。这孩子来得正好，来得正是时候，正好救他。他总不至于连新认下的舅舅张口借点钱，都回绝吧？光那些国库券就够救他一命了，更别说其余的钞票。再说，向外甥张口，同欠那个恶魔埃文斯的债完全是两码事——埃文斯可是一转眼就要放法警进门的。这甚至同欠办公室那几个人的债也不可同日而语：为了填住他们的嘴、好叫他们别往上司跟前告状，他这个月的薪水已经全数填了进去。这些念头刚过，紧跟着便涌来一阵骇人的清醒：他看清了自己处境的危险。今天才三号，而他全部家当只有十先令，要拿它挡住债主、养活一家老小，一直撑到下个发薪的日子。在这之前，他一直凭着自己那点可怜的决断闭眼不管。可如今既然有了一条脱身的活路，他所站的险境便不由分说地逼到眼前，逼得他不由自主地打了个寒噤，心口沉沉地擂起鼓来。他下意识地朝餐柜瞟了一眼，酒瓶就在里头。可他忍住了。他不能把这最后三杯——还是四杯来着？——里头的一杯，糟蹋在这孩子身上。他把威士忌的念头狠狠推到一边，转过头来，开始小心翼翼地向外甥套话。
“这儿难找不难找？”他问——郊区人家对头一回上门的客人，照例总要问上这么一句。

===Original===
“Oh, no,” replied Medland. “I had your address, of course, mother dug it up from your old letters before she died. So I knew it was Dulwich, and in Trafalgar Square I saw dozens of buses all going to Dulwich, and I got on one and came as far as the terminus. Then it was easy. The first person I asked told me the way to Malcolm Road.”
“Just so. And where did you say you were staying?”
Medland had not said he was staying anywhere, but he told him. It was a substantial Strand hotel. It was then that Medland, apropos of this, made the remark that was to alter everything.
“It’s funny to think of,” he said, striving to keep the conversation going, “but besides you there isn’t a soul in England who knows anything about me. I don’t think I was in the hotel more than an hour, and I only left my hand luggage there. The rest of my stuff is at Euston. What with going to the Bank and so on I simply didn’t have time to collect it, even if it had got there. I was thinking to myself as I came here that if I got lost and never found my way back no one would mind at all﻿—except you, of course.”
===Chinese===
“哦，不难，”梅德兰答道，“我有你们的地址嘛，母亲去世前从旧信里翻出来的。所以我知道是在达利奇（Dulwich）。到了特拉法加广场（Trafalgar Square），见着一长串公共汽车全是开往达利奇的，我就上了一辆，坐到底。往后就好办了，我开口问的头一个人，就指给我马尔科姆路怎么走。”
“原来如此。你方才说住在哪里来着？”
梅德兰其实没说过自己住哪儿，不过他还是告诉了他。那是斯特兰德（Strand）大街上一家颇有规模的旅馆。就在这时，梅德兰就着这个话头，说出了那句即将改变一切的话。
“想起来挺好笑的，”他说，一心想让谈话别冷场，“可在整个英格兰，除了你们一家，再没有一个人知道我的一丁点事。我在旅馆里待的工夫怕还不到一个钟头，只把手提行李寄存在那儿，其余的东西都在尤斯顿（Euston）。又要上银行办事，又要忙别的，实在腾不出工夫去取——就算行李已经运到了也罢。我上这儿来的路上还在想，我要是迷了路，再也寻不回去，压根儿不会有人在意——当然，你们一家除外。”

===Original===
“H’m!” said Mr. Marble, and another train of thought came to him on the instant, and he shuddered again.
Medland’s shyness was turning to boyish talkativeness. He looked round to the two children.
“Well,” he said, and smiled, “you two don’t seem to have much to say for yourselves.”
Winnie and John still remained silent. They had been keeping as quiet as mice so as not to draw attention to themselves and raise again the postponed question of bed. But beside this John was lost in admiration of this weather-tanned man who had come all the way from Australia, and who treated such an amazing trip through pirate-haunted seas with so little concern that he had said no word about it. And he spoke so casually about hotels too. John had noticed last year at Worthing that his father spoke of people who lived in hotels as opposed to those who live in rooms, and even in boardinghouses, with awe in his voice. And this man lived in a hotel and thought nothing of it!
===Chinese===
“嗯哼！”马布尔先生应了一声。他脑子里当即又掠过一串念头，他又打了个哆嗦。
梅德兰的羞怯正化成少年人的健谈。他转头朝两个孩子望过去。
“喂，”他微笑着说，“你们俩好像没什么话要跟我讲啊。”
温妮和约翰照旧不作声。他们一直安安静静像两只小老鼠，就怕惹人注意，把上床睡觉那桩搁下了的事重新勾起来。可除此之外，约翰心里正对这位风尘仆仆、一脸古铜色的汉子敬慕得五体投地：他从澳大利亚远道而来，横渡那些海盗出没的海面，这等了不得的旅程他却看得云淡风轻，提都不提。他讲起旅馆来又是那样随口。约翰去年在沃辛就留意到，父亲提起住旅馆的人——同那些只租房间住的、甚至住供膳宿舍的人相比——嗓音里总带着敬畏。而这个人自己就住在旅馆里，还全不当回事！

===Original===
As for Winnie, she was thinking that he was the most beautiful man she had ever seen. His warm brown face and his brown tweeds with their intoxicating scent were wonderful. Then when he looked straight at you and smiled, as he had just done, he was handsomer than anyone she could imagine, far handsomer than the fairy prince in the pantomime at Christmas.
“Speak up, children,” said their father. To Medland’s fastidious ear it sounded as if he might have added, “Tell the pretty gentleman his fortune.”
The children grinned shyly. Winnie could say nothing. But John made an effort, unused as he was to conversation owing to severe repression by his father during his queer moods of late.
“You have kangaroos in Australia, haven’t you?” he said, with a fourteen-year-old wriggle.
“You’re right,” said Medland. “I’ve hunted them too.”
===Chinese===
至于温妮，她心里想的是：这是她生平见过的最俊美的男人。他暖烘烘的棕色脸膛，他一身棕色花呢衣裳和衣裳上那股叫人微醺的呢子味儿，都妙极了。尤其是他像方才那样直直望着你微笑的时候，他比她能想见出的任何人都俊，比圣诞节童话剧里那位童话王子还要俊得多。
“说话呀，孩子们。”他们的父亲说。在梅德兰讲究的耳朵听来，他这话后面仿佛还缀着半句：“给这位漂亮的先生算上一卦。”
两个孩子羞答答地咧嘴笑了。温妮一句也说不出。约翰倒试了一试——近来他父亲犯着古怪脾气，动不动就压着他，他早已不惯同人说话了。
“澳大利亚有袋鼠，是吧？”他说着，十四岁的身子扭了一扭。
“没错，”梅德兰说，“我还猎过呢。”

===Original===
“Ooh,” gasped John ecstatically. “On horseback?”
“Yes, for miles and miles across the country, as fast as your horse could gallop. I’ll tell you about it some day.”
Both children writhed in delight.
“And bushrangers?” said John. “Did﻿—did you ever see Ned Kelly?”
To Medland’s credit he did not laugh.
“No such luck,” he said. “There weren’t many round where I lived. But I know a topping book about them.”
===Chinese===
“哦——”约翰如痴如狂，倒抽了一口气。“骑着马？”
“骑马。一跑就是几十英里旷野，马能撒开多快跑多快。哪天讲给你们听。”
两个孩子乐得直在椅子上扭。
“还有丛林大盗呢？”约翰说。“你——你见过内德·凯利（Ned Kelly）吗？”
亏得梅德兰没有笑出来。
“没那么好的运气，”他说，“我住的那一带没剩几个了。不过我知道一本讲他们的顶呱呱的书。”

===Original===
“*Robbery Under Arms*,” said both children at once.
“Oh, you’ve read it?”
“Read it? I should think they had.” This was Mr. Marble’s contribution to the conversation. “They’re terrors for reading, those two kids are. Never see ’em without a book.”
“That’s fine,” said Medland.
But the conversation wilted beyond recovery at this intrusion. And Marble, intent on getting Medland to himself, flashed a look at the children and jerked his head skywards. They understood, and climbed down dolefully from their chairs.
===Chinese===
“《武装抢劫》（*Robbery Under Arms*）。”两个孩子异口同声。
“哦，你们读过？”
“岂止读过。”这是马布尔先生对这场谈话的贡献。“这两个孩子读起书来不要命，什么时候手上都捧着书。”
“那真好。”梅德兰说。
可这一插话，谈话便蔫得再也抬不起头来。马布尔一心要把梅德兰独占下来，朝孩子们飞快使了个眼色，下巴朝天一扬。他们懂了，垂头丧气地从椅子上爬下来。

===Original===
“Bedtime, children?” said Mr. Marble in a tone of surprise that was unsuccessful in its purpose of deceiving Medland, since he had caught the tail end of Marble’s signal. “Good night, then. Why, aren’t you going to kiss me?”
They had not been intending to do so. The custom had died out months before, when Marble had begun turning to the decanter in the sideboard for distraction from his troubles, and with children a custom three months unused might as well never have existed. Besides, John was nearly too old for kissing now. Both John and Winnie kissed their father awkwardly, and their mother casually. Then John shook hands with his new cousin. It was the first time he had ever shaken hands as man to man, with eye meeting eye in man’s fashion, and he was very proud of it. Winnie, too, tried to shake hands in imitation of her brother, but there was something in Medland’s smile and in the gentle traction he exerted on her hand that made her lean forward and kiss the boyish mouth tendered to her. It felt funny, different from other kisses she had known. It was a very silent pair that went up to bed.
Marble turned away with evident relief as they closed the door.
“Now we can be comfortable,” he said. “Draw your chair up closer to the fire, er﻿—Jim. What a night,” he added, as the wind howled outside.
Medland nodded moodily. He was feeling awkward. He was not at all at home with these strange people. He didn’t like the way Marble behaved towards his children. The kids were all right of course, and the mother was a nonentity. But there was an atmosphere about the place that he hated. He pulled himself together and tried to shake off the brooding premonitory mood that was oppressing him. It was absurd, of course. Old Marble was only a very ordinary sort of chap. Seedy and down at heel, but quite all right. He was smiling oilily at present, but that didn’t mean anything necessarily. Hang it all, if he didn’t like the place he could clear out in a few minutes’ time and never come back to it. For the matter of that Medland’s thoughts swerved suddenly to the utterly absurd﻿—he could change his hotel next morning and then they could never find him again. The bare idea was sufficient to bring his mind back to reality. There was no reason why he should think about things like that at all. The kids were fine, and he’d see a lot of them while he was in England. He could take them to a lot of the places he felt he had to go to, the Tower of London and St. Paul’s, for instance. That would be topping.
===Chinese===
“到睡觉的钟点了，孩子们？”马布尔先生用装出来的诧异口气说，可惜没能骗过梅德兰，因为马布尔递眼色的后半截正巧叫他瞧见了。“那么，晚安吧。咦，不来亲亲我？”
他们本来没打算亲。这习惯几个月前就断了——打从马布尔开始靠餐柜里的酒瓶排遣愁闷起就断了；在孩子身上，三个月没用的习惯，就跟从来不曾有过的一样。再说约翰这个年纪，也差不多过了让人亲的时候了。兄妹俩都别别扭扭地亲了亲父亲，又随随便便地亲了亲母亲。然后约翰同他新认识的表哥握了握手。这是他平生头一回像大人对大人那样同人握手，眼睛望着眼睛，照大人的规矩来，他为这个得意极了。温妮也学着哥哥的样子把手伸过去，可梅德兰的笑意里有点什么，他握着她的手又轻轻一带，那点什么东西叫她不由得俯下身去，亲了亲递到她面前的那张少年人的嘴。那滋味怪怪的，同她从前领受过的亲吻都不一样。上楼去睡觉的这一对儿，一路没说一句话。
门一关上，马布尔便明显松了口气，转过身来。
“这下咱们可以舒舒服服的了，”他说。“把椅子往炉火跟前挪挪，呃——吉姆。好大的风。”外头风声怒号，他又添了一句。
梅德兰闷闷地点了点头。他觉得浑身别扭。同这些陌生人待在一处，他一点儿也不自在。他不喜欢马布尔对孩子们的那副做派。孩子们当然没得说，那位母亲又是个无足轻重的人。可这地方有股味儿，他打心眼里讨厌。他打起精神，想甩脱那股压在心头的、预感似的阴沉情绪。那念头当然荒唐。老马布尔不过是个再普通不过的家伙。寒酸，邋遢，可人挺不坏。这会儿他正笑得一脸油光，不过那未必就说明什么。见鬼，他要是嫌这地方不好，几分钟后抬腿就能走，从此不再回来。再往这上头想——梅德兰的念头猛地拐进了荒诞不经的岔道——他明早还可以换一家旅馆，叫他们永远也别想再找到他。单是这个念头，就足以把他的心思拽回现实。他根本没有任何理由去想这种事。孩子们都好得很，在英国的日子里他可以常同他们处一处。他可以带他们去那些他觉得自己非去不可的地方，比如伦敦塔（Tower of London），比如圣保罗大教堂（St. Paul's）。那才叫棒呢。

===Original===
Mr. Marble was speaking to his wife.
“What about some supper, Annie?” he was saying. “I expect our young friend here is hungry.”
“But﻿—” began Mrs. Marble hopelessly, and then checked herself hastily and clumsily as she caught sight of her husband frowning at her.
“Don’t worry about me, please,” put in Medland. “I dined just before I came out.”
“That’s all right, then,” replied Marble. “I dined just after I came in.”
===Chinese===
马布尔先生在跟老婆说话。
“安妮，弄点宵夜怎么样？”他说。“咱们这位年轻朋友准是饿了。”
“可是——”马布尔太太绝望地起了个头，一抬眼看见丈夫朝她皱眉，又慌里慌张、笨嘴笨舌地把话咽了回去。
“请别为我操心，”梅德兰插话道，“我出门前刚吃过晚饭。”
“那就好，”马布尔答道，“我也是刚进家门就吃过了。”

===Original===
And he laughed. The laughter was just the least bit strained.
Conversation began again, resuming its hopeless, desultory way while Medland wondered in a bored, young man’s fashion why on earth he did not get up and go at once. There were really several reasons. One was that the wind and the rain were continually making themselves heard outside; another was that the fire was most attractive﻿—it was the most attractive thing in the whole house﻿—but deep down there was a feeling of relief that he was not in a hotel with nothing special to do. Medland had laid plans for a very exciting time on his arrival in England, but at the moment he was feeling a little homesick and not in the mood for excitement of any kind. It might have been as well if he had felt otherwise.
Mrs. Marble came into the picture at times. She asked him homely questions as to whether he was seasick on the voyage, and whether he had enough to eat, and whether he was warmly enough clad to face an English winter. Medland answered politely enough, but Marble was positively rude to her on more than one occasion. Medland found himself regarding the little man curiously. His face was a little moist, and his eyes were brighter than they had been before, as though he was growing excited about something of which the others knew nothing. He cut his wife short repeatedly, and his questions grew more and more personal. Medland realized that to a person of Marble’s character the idea of conversation would consist of a series of questions, but even that was no excuse for this searching cross-examination as to his resources, his friends and his knowledge of affairs.
Poor Marble! And poor Medland! Marble was being affected more and more by the realization of his position, rendered more acute by envious contrast with Medland’s, while Medland’s every answer seemed calculated to urge Marble on to﻿—something. Marble was not quite sure what it was. It could not just be borrowing money; he had decided to attempt that hours ago. The thumping heart within him seemed to indicate something more unusual than that. Marble was nerving himself to definite action﻿—for the first time in his life, be it added.
With the cunning of the weak, he did his best to disguise the state he was in, while all the time, without conscious volition on his part, his furtive mind was twisting and turning, devising the course of action he was likely to take. No wonder Medland looked at him oddly at times.
===Chinese===
说罢他笑了。那笑声只是有一丝丝勉强。
谈话又续上了，还是那副不死不活、东拉西扯的样子，梅德兰则带着年轻人特有的腻烦纳闷：自己到底为什么不立刻起身走人。其实缘故有好几条。一条是外头的风雨吵个没完；一条是那炉火实在迷人——这是全家数第一迷人的东西——可在更深处，还有一层如释重负：总算不用待在旅馆里，闲得不知道做什么好。梅德兰原为抵达英国预备了一整套痛痛快快的玩乐计划，可这会儿他有点想家，什么寻热闹的心思都提不起来。倘若他当时是另一种心情，事情兴许反倒会好些。
马布尔太太时不时插进来搭几句话。她问的都是家常话：海上晕不晕船，吃得够不够，身上穿得暖不暖，顶不顶得住英国的冬天。梅德兰回得客客气气，马布尔却不止一回对她近乎粗鲁。梅德兰不由得好奇地端详起这个小个子男人来。他脸上微微沁着油汗，眼睛比先前亮，仿佛正为什么旁人一概不知的事情兴奋着。他一遍遍打断老婆，问的问题也越来越往人心里钻。梅德兰算是明白了：在马布尔这种人的观念里，谈话大概就等于一连串的盘问；可饶是这样，这般追问他的家底、他的朋友、他对世事的阅历，刨根究底，也实在说不过去。
可怜的马布尔！也可怜的梅德兰！马布尔对自身处境的自觉越来越沉，再同梅德兰的处境一比，越发沉得钻心；而梅德兰的每一句回答，都像是存心推着马布尔往前走——走向一件什么事。到底是什么事，马布尔自己也拿不准。总不会光是借钱；借钱的主意他几个钟头前就打定了。他胸腔里擂动的那颗心，预示的好像是比这非同寻常得多的什么东西。马布尔正在给自己鼓足劲，要去干一件确定无疑的事——顺带说一句，这是他平生头一遭。
他怀着弱者的狡黠，竭力遮掩自己眼下这副状态；可与此同时，又不由他做主，他那颗鬼鬼祟祟的脑子一直在七拐八绕，暗暗盘算着他多半要走的那一步棋。难怪梅德兰时不时古怪地看他一眼。

===Original===
Time seemed to be passing with extraordinary rapidity. It seemed to Marble that every time he looked at the clock on the mantelpiece another half-hour had fled. Twice had he detected in Medland’s manner an intention of leaving as soon as the conversation broke, and each time he had flung himself into the breach, talking nonsense, as he was painfully aware, in order to stave off the crisis that would arise when that time came.
His fevered mind roused itself to additional activity. He nerved himself to make the sacrifice which he realized was inevitable, and gathered himself together in his chair as casually as he could manage.
“What about a drink?” he asked. In such a matter-of-fact way did he bring the question out that Medland did not guess the wrench it cost him.
Medland hesitated before he replied; he was not yet a man of the world enough to regard an offer of a drink as an ordinary event; and during that little hesitation Marble had risen and walked across to the sideboard, beyond the table. For a moment he was lost to view as he dived down below the level of the table; when he rose again he had a siphon of soda-water﻿—half full﻿—under his arm, two tumblers in one hand, and in the other, held very carefully, a decanter of whisky, a quarter full. He set these things out on the table near his chair; he was standing very close to his wife as he did so. He took the opportunity of mumbling something to her. He spoke swiftly and obscurely, so obscurely that Medland, though he noticed the act, could not hear the words, and took them to be some hint as to domestic arrangements﻿—probably a comment on the shortage of whisky. What Marble had really said was: “Want talk business. Go to bed. Say headache.”
===Chinese===
时间似乎过得出奇地快。马布尔觉得，自己每回抬眼看壁炉台上的时钟，就又飞走了半个钟头。他有两回瞧出梅德兰的意思，是等谈话一断就要告辞；每一回他都赶紧抢上前去堵住缺口，说些不着边际的闲话——他自己也痛切地觉出那些话有多蠢——好把那个时刻一到就要来的危机，再往后拖一拖。
他那发烧似的脑子愈发加紧地活动起来。他横下心来，去作那件他明知免不了的牺牲，又尽量装出随随便便的做派，在椅子里把身子坐直了。
“喝上一杯怎么样？”他问。这话说得那样顺口，梅德兰哪里猜得到，他下这一回决心，心里割了多少肉。
梅德兰答话前踌躇了一下；他还不够老于世故，不把人家敬酒当成寻常事；就趁这一踌躇的工夫，马布尔已经起身，绕过桌子朝餐柜走去。一时间他弯腰隐到桌面底下，不见了；再直起身来时，胳膊底下夹着一支半满的苏打水虹吸瓶，一只手托着两只平底大酒杯，另一只手里小心翼翼拎着酒瓶——瓶里的威士忌只剩四分之一。他把这些物件摆到自己椅子近旁的桌上；摆的时候，他站得离老婆极近，趁势朝她咕哝了一句什么。他说得又快又含糊，含糊到梅德兰虽然看见了这个举动，却一个字也没听清，只当是交代家务上的事——多半是嘀咕威士忌不多了。马布尔真正说的是：“要谈事。你去睡。说头疼。”

===Original===
Annie Marble heard the words some time before she attached any meaning to them. That was usual with her. Even when she realized their significance, slight enough to her, she did not act at once upon them. It was always a long time before she could coordinate her faculties to change from one course of action to another.
Marble poured out a drink with great deliberation. It was not a very generous one, for he was confronted with the problem of offering his guest as much as possible while conserving for himself enough at least to keep up appearances; yet his whole soul was crying out for that whisky. His hand shook a little as he poured, so that the decanter chattered faintly on the rim of the tumbler, but he took a firm grasp of his nerves with a last despairing effort and completed the business, without having consulted his guest as to quantity even to the usual conventional extent. Then he sat back in his chair, half in anguish, half in satisfaction. He had managed the pouring out perfectly, he told himself. He had given Medland plenty while quite a respectable amount remained in the decanter. Easily enough for two more drinks, anyway, and the half-formed plot in Marble’s mind demanded that there should be enough for two drinks left in the decanter. But it was a frightful effort for him to sip casually from the cool tumbler in his hand. He wanted that drink so terribly badly, but all he could allow himself to do was to nod perfunctorily across to Medland, sip just a little, and then put the glass down indifferently beside him. But even that small amount was enough to calm his shrieking nerves, so that his shaking body became as calm and detached as his scheming, uncontrolled mind.
As he replaced the glass Annie Marble rose. She knew the role it was necessary for her to assume, and by some strange freak of mental poise she did it perfectly. Her dim mind had never fully realized the grim peril in which lay the fortunes of her husband and herself; nothing that Marble could say﻿—and he said little﻿—would force it home to her as long as she could go on obtaining credit at the shops; but she knew they were in some trouble, and that Marble was intending that this young nephew of his should help them out. It behoved her therefore to do her best, quite apart from the fact that her negative personality responded in small actions to every whim of her husband, just as he wished.
===Chinese===
安妮·马布尔听见这句话之后，过了好一阵子才咂摸出意思来。她一向如此。哪怕明白了其中的分量——在她看来那分量也轻得很——她也不会立刻照办。她总要拖上老半天，才能把浑身的劲头聚拢起来，从一件事换到另一件事上去。
马布尔斟酒，斟得又慢又稳。这一杯不算丰厚，因为他面前摆着道难题：既要让客人尽量多喝，又得给自己至少留下撑场面的一份；可他整个灵魂都在朝那瓶威士忌哭喊。斟酒时他的手微微发抖，酒瓶口在杯沿上磕出细细的轻响；他作了最后一次绝望的努力，狠狠攥牢自己的神经，把这件事办完了——连请客人示个分量这种例行的客套都省去了。然后他仰回椅背，一半是绞痛，一半是满足。方才这一杯斟得漂亮，他对自己说。给梅德兰的那杯给足了，瓶里也还稳稳留着体面的一截。无论如何够再斟两杯的了，而马布尔心里那桩酝酿到一半的计划，恰恰需要瓶里剩下两杯的量。可是，要装出漫不经心的样子，抿一抿手里那杯凉的，对他是天大的苦役。他馋那杯酒馋得要命，可他容许自己做的，只有朝梅德兰那头敷衍地点点头，浅浅呷上一口，然后若无其事地把杯子搁在身边。然而就这一点点下肚，也足够叫他那嘶喊的神经安定下来，叫他发抖的身子，变得同他那颗一面暗自谋划、一面不肯受管的心一样平静，一样超然了。
马布尔把杯子放回桌上时，安妮·马布尔站起身来。她知道自己该接的是什么角色，而凭着心智上某种奇异的定力，她把这个角色演得无可挑剔。她那颗昏沉的脑子里，从来不曾真正弄明白，她同丈夫的身家正悬在怎样的险境上；只要铺子里还赊得出账，马布尔说什么——他本来也说不了什么——都休想叫她明白；可她知道家里遇上了难处，也知道马布尔打的算盘，是要让这个年轻外甥拉他们一把。因此她理应尽力配合，何况她那毫无主见的天性，本就惯于用一个个细小的动作去应和丈夫的每一个心血来潮，正合他的心意。

===Original===
“I think I shall go to bed, Will,” she said rising a little wearily from the uncomfortable bentwood chair. “I’ve got a bit of a headache.”
Mr. Marble was greatly concerned.
“Really, dear?” he said, rising. “That’s bad luck. Have a nightcap before you go up?” And he nodded towards the decanter.
But even as he nodded, with his face away from Medland, there was a little scowl between his eyebrows that gave Mrs. Marble her cue.
“No, dear, thank you,” she said, “I’ll just go straight up and it will be better in the morning.”
“Just as you like,” said Mr. Marble.
===Chinese===
“我想我该去睡了，威尔（Will）。”她说着，从那把硌人的曲木椅里略显疲惫地站起身。“我有点头疼。”
马布尔先生大为关切。
“真的吗，亲爱的？”他站起来说。“那可真不巧。上楼前来杯睡前酒？”说着他朝酒瓶那边点了点头。
可就在他点头的时候——脸正背着梅德兰——他眉心拧起一个小小的疙瘩，马布尔太太见了，便得了暗号。
“不了，谢谢你，亲爱的，”她说，“我这就上去，睡一觉明早就好了。”
“随你的便。”马布尔先生说。

===Original===
Mrs. Marble moved across to Medland.
“Good night, er﻿—Jim,” she said, shaking hands.
“Good night. I hope it will really be better tomorrow.”
“Good night, dear,” said Mr. Marble, “I won’t disturb you when I come up if I can help it. I expect I shall be a bit late.”
He pecked her on her cold cheek﻿—a typical marital kiss. But Mr. Marble was not in the habit of kissing his wife good night at all, and he never worried in the least about disturbing her when he came to bed. However, it gave the scene that calm domestic atmosphere that Mr. Marble’s subconscious mind, which had him in full control, had decided was necessary to the occasion.
Mrs. Marble had gone, and they heard the dragging steps on the floor of the room above.
===Chinese===
马布尔太太挪到梅德兰跟前。
“晚安，呃——吉姆。”她一边握手一边说。
“晚安。但愿您明天真就见好。”
“晚安，亲爱的。”马布尔先生说，“我上楼的时候尽量不吵你。我估计要坐得晚一点。”
他在她冰凉的脸颊上啄了一口——一个地道的夫妻之吻。只是马布尔先生平日根本没有吻着老婆道晚安的习惯，上床时吵不吵醒她，他也从来不上心。不过这样一来，这场面便有了那种安安稳稳的家庭气氛，而这正是马布尔先生的潜意识——它已经把他整个人牢牢攥在手心——认定此情此景所必需的。
马布尔太太走了，他们听得见楼上房间的地板里，她拖拖沓沓的脚步声。

===Original===
“No need for hurry, I suppose, seeing that you’re a gay young bachelor,” said Mr. Marble.
“None at all,” replied Medland, and regretted saying it the instant it was said. He had really no desire to go on being bored for a further interminable period. But his answer had committed him to another half-hour at the very least, and he endeavoured to reconcile himself to it.
Just for a brief space Mr. Marble regained full control over himself, and he made a brief and unavailing struggle against the inevitable which a stronger power within him was forcing upon him. He began to talk again on the subject of Medland’s money﻿—the subject on which his lack of decent reticence had already annoyed his guest.
“So you’re quite a well-off young man now, it seems?” he said, with exasperating joviality.
“I suppose so,” was the curt reply.
===Chinese===
“我想，你这位快活的年轻单身汉，就用不着赶时候回去了吧。”马布尔先生说。
“一点也不用。”梅德兰答道，话一出口就后悔了。他实在不想再闷坐上一个漫无尽头的时段。可这一答，就等于把自己又拴上了至少半个钟头，他只好想法子让自己认了。
只有一小会儿工夫，马布尔先生重新把自己完全攥在了自己手里；他作了一场短暂而徒劳的挣扎，想抵挡他体内那股更强大的力量正硬塞给他的那个“必然”。他又谈起梅德兰的钱来——正是这个话题，他方才那副不知收敛的穷追，已经惹恼了客人。
“这么说，你如今是位相当阔气的少爷了，嗯？”他带着惹人冒火的乐呵呵劲儿说。
“算是吧。”回答又干又短。

===Original===
“A good bit to spare for investments, I suppose?”
It was a blundering way of putting it, and it failed. Even on the voyage over more than one man had come to Medland with get-rich-quick schemes, and he had contrived to see through them. And so many people had borrowed money from him that the process was both familiar and annoying to him. Medland determined to stop this attempt once and for all. It might be awkward for a bit, but it would save endless trouble in the future. He looked straight into Marble’s eyes.
“No,” he said, “I haven’t any to invest. I’m quite satisfied with the arrangement that my father made before he died. I’ve got just enough, and no more. And I put up with it.”
That settled the matter definitely enough for anyone, but, to Medland’s surprise, Marble showed no sign of discomfiture. Medland did not know it, but at a bound the lurking power within his uncle had regained possession of him, and had at once begun to smooth the way for the inevitable.
===Chinese===
“闲钱不少，可以拿出来投投资了吧？”
这话问得笨拙透顶，也就问砸了。就连来的路上那条船上，都不止一个人揣着一夜暴富的算盘凑到梅德兰跟前，全叫他一眼看穿。跟他借钱的人又是那么多，这套把戏他熟透了，也烦透了。梅德兰打定主意，这回要把这种企图一次了断。眼下也许会有点难堪，往后却能省下数不清的麻烦。他直直地望进马布尔的眼睛。
“不，”他说，“我没有闲钱可投。父亲去世前把事情都安排好了，我对那安排满意得很。我有的刚够花，多一分也没有。我也认了。”
对谁来说，这话都把事情堵得死死的了。可出乎梅德兰的意料，马布尔竟没有半点下不来台的意思。梅德兰不知道，就在这一瞬，蛰伏在他舅舅体内的那股力量一个箭步重新占有了他，并立刻动手，为那个“必然”铺平道路。

===Original===
“A good thing too,” said Marble, and his manner of saying it left Medland seriously in doubt as to whether his former question had really been a feeler for a loan. “The market’s in a rotten state at present. I shouldn’t like to buy at all just now, not gilt-edged. Sit tight and hang on to what you’ve got, that’s my motto all the time nowadays.”
He said it in all sincerity, and Medland actually felt himself warming towards him. At that time Medland was in serious danger of falling into the delusion that so often attacks men of wealth who have had their wealth from boyhood and have been “stung” too often by the unscrupulous﻿—he was in danger of imagining that everyone with whom he came into contact was seeking profit at his expense. The surest way to his heart was to convince him of the contrary, and that Mr. Marble, in those few instants, had nearly succeeded in doing.
The conversation swung easily into discussion of the investment market, and that without the personal note that Medland so much resented. Somewhere within him Marble possessed a clever turn for finance, which hitherto he had been unable, as well as too lazy, to exert. Medland, with a hard head for business inherited from his shipbroking father, recognized a surprisingly kindred spirit. For the first time that evening he really began to enjoy himself. He drained his glass almost without thinking of it﻿—enthusiasm succeeded in overcoming his juvenile distaste for whisky even though he had never been able to make himself like it.
Marble was watching him with fierce intentness through narrowed eyes. Medland hardly noticed it, and attached no importance to it if he did. Then Marble pulled himself out of his chair, glass in hand, and addressed himself to the decanter. That foolish heart of his was thumping again, thumping heavily, but it did not affect his actions. They were quite under control﻿—the control of that inward force which had taken charge of him and which recognized the inevitable.
===Chinese===
“不投才好呢。”马布尔说。他这话说出口的做派，倒叫梅德兰认真犯起了嘀咕：先前那一问，到底是不是在转弯抹角地探借钱的口风。“眼下市道烂得很。这个当口我什么都不敢买，金边证券也不敢。捂紧手里的，攥住了别撒手——近来我时时刻刻都是这个章程。”
这话他句句出自真心，梅德兰竟当真觉得心里对他热乎起来。那时的梅德兰，正处在一种实实在在的危险里：险些染上那种常常缠上有钱人的错觉——那种从小到大坐吃祖产、又被无耻之辈“宰”过太多回的有钱人——他险些要相信，凡同他打交道的，个个都在打他钱包的主意。通到他心里去的、最稳当的一条路，就是让他相信恰恰相反；而这恰恰是马布尔先生在那短短几秒钟里，差一点就要做到了的。
谈话顺势滑进了投资市场的讨论，而且没沾上梅德兰最反感的那种个人打算的味儿。马布尔骨子里原有一手漂亮的金融本领，只是一来一向没机会施展，二来人也太懒。梅德兰从他做船务经纪的父亲那里继承了精明的生意头脑，此刻竟辨认出一种出乎意料的同道中人。这是那天晚上他头一回真正觉得自在。他几乎不假思索地喝干了那杯酒——热情居然压过了他少年人对威士忌的天然厌恶，尽管他始终没能让自己爱上这东西。
马布尔眯起眼睛，一路凶狠而专注地盯视着他。梅德兰几乎没有察觉；就算察觉了，也没当回事。随后马布尔端着杯子，从椅子里拔身而起，径直去伺候那酒瓶。他那颗愚蠢的心又擂起来了，擂得沉甸甸的，可这半点不影响他的动作。他的动作完全受着控制——受着那股已经接管了他、并且认清了“必然”的内在力量的控制。

===Original===
Marble reached across and drew Medland’s glass from his hand. “There’s only one more drink apiece,” said Marble. “I’m sorry, but we weren’t expecting visitors tonight, you know.”
He said it in such a matter-of-fact way that Medland had no chance even of trying to refuse the second drink. Idly Medland watched Marble pour out the whisky from the decanter with the painful care that had characterized his action before. The liquid stood level in the two tumblers. Marble was apparently about to splash in the rest of the soda-water from the siphon when he paused as though listening.
“Just a minute,” he said, “I think one of the kids is calling out.”
Medland had heard nothing, but he was unused to the noises of the house and made no question. Mr. Marble had heard nothing, either. He had said what he did as an excuse to withdraw from the room and go upstairs. It was the most natural action in the world for him to creep out of the room to listen to hear if either of his children was frightened, and it was most natural too, that he should be carrying in his hand the tumbler that he had held at the moment his attention was distracted. Medland watched him go; everything was so natural that he did not give a second thought to it.
Hardly more than a minute later Marble came tiptoeing back down the stairs and into the room, the glass still in his hand.
===Chinese===
马布尔探过身，把梅德兰手里的杯子抽了出来。“咱们一人只剩最后一杯了，”马布尔说，“对不住，谁想到今儿晚上会有客来呢。”
他说得那样理所当然，梅德兰连试着推辞第二杯的机会都没有。梅德兰懒洋洋地看着马布尔从瓶里往外斟威士忌，还是先前那副小心翼翼到发苦的架势。两只平底杯里的酒一般齐。马布尔眼看就要把虹吸瓶里剩下的苏打水打进去，却忽然顿住，像在侧耳听着什么。
“稍等，”他说，“我好像听见哪个孩子在喊。”
梅德兰什么也没听见，可他不熟悉这房子里的种种响动，也就没有多问。马布尔先生其实也什么都没听见。他那么说，无非是找个由头退出屋去，上楼一趟。做父亲的蹑手蹑脚走出房门，去听听哪个孩子是不是吓着了——这是天底下再自然不过的事；而手里还端着注意力被引开那一刻拿着的那只平底杯，也同样再自然不过。梅德兰目送他出去；一切都那么自然，他连想都懒得再想第二下。
顶多一分钟工夫，马布尔便踮着脚尖下楼来了，走回屋里，手里仍端着那只杯子。

===Original===
“False alarm,” he said. “One gets used to these things when one is the father of a family.”
He turned again to the siphon, and it hissed into the tumblers. Then he passed Medland’s across to him. As he took it the wind outside howled again louder than ever; the windows rattled and they heard the rain pelting against the glass.
“What a night,” said Medland.
“Drink up,” replied Marble, very, very calmly.
===Chinese===
“虚惊一场，”他说，“当了一家之主，这种事就习惯了。”
他又转向虹吸瓶，嘶的一声，苏打水冲进两只平底杯。然后他把梅德兰那杯递了过去。就在梅德兰伸手去接的当口，外头的风比先前吼得更凶了；窗子震得咯咯直响，听得见雨点子密密地砸在玻璃上。
“好大的风。”梅德兰说。
“喝吧。”马布尔答道，非常、非常平静。

## II

===Original===
When Annie Marble woke in the early morning she was oppressed with a headache﻿—a real headache, this time. She had had a restless night, although, true to his word, astonishingly enough, her husband had not disturbed her when he came up. At this very moment he was sleeping heavily at her side. She turned in the bed and looked at him in the half light that was straying through the untidy blinds. He lay on his back, his sparse hair standing on end, his eyes closed and his mouth open, his bristling red moustaches reinforced now by a coarse but scanty growth of beard. His hands clutched the sheets, and his breath passed in and out of his mouth with a stertorous sound. To most people he would have been an unpleasing picture, but Annie Marble did not think so. She was used to it in any case, and his present helpless attitude and appearance always roused the mother spirit within her which was almost her sole ordinary characteristic now. She would have liked to have taken him into her arms and hugged him a little, but she would not do so for fear of disturbing him.
Instead, she began to wonder whether he had been successful in his management of his interview with the strange nephew last night. She hoped so. She knew he had been worried about money lately; he had told her so on occasions. And he had cut down the money he was accustomed to give her. That didn’t matter much, as Mr. Evans and the milkman and the others were all so obliging. But he had been bothered about it, she knew. So she hoped the nephew﻿—she was sure she would never learn to call such a splendid young man plain “Jim”﻿—had done something for them. He ought to have done, for he had stayed long enough. She had heard them talking long after she had settled herself to sleep. The remembrance brought a new rush of dim recollections to her mind. Will had come upstairs then, just when she was nearly asleep. She remembered wondering what he came for. He had gone into the bathroom; she had heard his keys rattle as he unlocked, she supposed, his photographic cupboard. It was probably to get something he wanted to show Jim. There, she had got it quite natural that time. Jim must be interested in photography too.
For a space her muddled thoughts followed no settled line. Then she came back to last night. If Jim was interested in photography he must have done something for Will﻿—to Annie’s mind everything was done for everybody by somebody else. And she must have been dreaming when she thought she heard that loud cry. She was awake just afterwards, she knew; she must have been dreaming that someone had called out loud, and had awakened still dreaming it. Yes, that must have been it, and she must have gone to sleep again and started dreaming again at once, for she had a hazy, muddled recollection of hearing a strange noise downstairs, as though something was being dragged along the linoleum of the passage downstairs, and one or two sharp taps as though some things were dropping sharply from one step to another on the little dark staircase just outside the kitchen door. What a silly thing to dream!
So Jim must have done something for Will. That was a good thing. She hoped Will would tell her all about it when he had the chance, because generally he did not tell her anything, and she was not much good at guessing. It was a little bit of a pity that Will said so little, for he could talk so nicely when he was in the mood. But, there, you can’t have everything. And Will was *such* a dear all the time. And just now he looked such a baby, such a nice little baby. She did wish she could take him in her arms just for a moment or two, the way she used to hold John, and Winnie too, when they were little. They weren’t such babies now, and tried to get on without their mother, and she felt a little bit lonely sometimes. When Will wasn’t worried by things she could still love him like that, though. It was a pity that he was worried such a lot nowadays. But now that Jim had done something for him it would be all right perhaps. She would buy some new nightdresses, like those in the windows in Rye Lane, very warm and nice, but looking quite nice, too, with nearly real lace on the edges of the sleeves. Then perhaps﻿—but here the alarm on the table at her side went off, and she had to stop thinking.
===Chinese===
安妮·马布尔（Annie Marble）清早一觉醒来，便觉得头疼难当——这一回，可是真的头疼。她一夜睡得不安稳，虽说丈夫上楼来的时候居然言而有信，并没有搅扰她。这会儿，他正在她身边沉沉睡着。她在床上转过身，就着从歪斜的窗帘缝里漏进来的微光端详他。他仰面躺着，稀疏的头发根根直立，眼睛闭着，嘴巴张着，两撇扎煞的红胡子底下，如今又添了一层又粗又稀的胡茬。他两手攥着床单，气息从嘴里一进一出，带着粗重的鼾声。换了大多数人，都会觉得这副样子不雅观，安妮·马布尔却不这么想。反正她早已看惯；何况他眼下这般无助的姿态神情，总能唤起她心头的母性——那母性，差不多是她如今仅剩的寻常品性了。她真想把他搂进怀里，稍稍疼他一疼，只是怕搅醒他，终于没有那么做。
她没有动手，转而琢磨起昨晚丈夫同那位陌生外甥的一席谈话，究竟张罗得顺不顺利。但愿顺利。她知道他近来为钱发愁；有几回他亲口跟她说过。他还把往常给她的那点钱削减了。这倒没什么要紧，埃文斯（Evans）先生、送奶的，还有别几家，都肯通融。可他心里为这事犯愁，这她知道。所以她指望那位外甥——她敢说，自己永远学不会把那么一位出色的年轻人径直唤作“吉姆（Jim）”——总归帮了他们什么忙。他理应帮忙的，谁叫他待了那么久呢。她安顿下睡觉之后好久，还听得见他们俩在说话。这一记起，又有一些模糊的旧忆涌上她的心头。后来威尔（Will）上楼来了，就在她快要睡着那会儿。她记得自己当时还纳闷，他上来做什么。他进了浴室；她听见钥匙在他手里哗啦一响——她猜是开了他放照相器材的柜子。多半是要拿件什么东西给吉姆看吧。瞧，这一回她不是把事情想得挺自然了嘛。吉姆想必也喜欢照相。
有一阵子，她那乱作一团的思绪漫无定向。随后她又回到昨晚上来。既然吉姆喜欢照相，那他准是替威尔做了什么事——在安妮的心目中，天下的事本来就是你替我做、我替你做，人人如此。那么，她以为自己听见一声大叫的时候，必定是在做梦了。她知道自己随后就醒了；想必是梦见有人大声喊叫，人醒了，那梦却还没醒。对，准是这么回事；随后她一定又睡着了，而且立刻接着做了起来，因为她依稀记得，听见楼下有什么古怪的动静——好像有什么东西顺着楼下过道的油地毡拖过去，还有一两声脆响，好像有什么东西在厨房门外那道黑黢黢的小楼梯上，一级一级地磕下去。这样的梦，做得可真傻！
这么说，吉姆准是替威尔做了点事。那是好事。她盼着威尔得着机会，能把这事原原本本讲给她听，因为平常他什么事都不对她讲，而她又不大会猜。威尔话这么少，未免有点可惜，因为他兴致来的时候，多会说话呀。可是唉，人不能什么都占全。何况威尔向来*多么*招人疼。这会儿他看上去多像个娃娃，一个多可爱的小娃娃啊。她真希望能把他搂进怀里，就那么一小会儿也行，像从前抱约翰（John）、也抱温妮（Winnie）那样——在他们还小的时候。如今他们不再是娃娃了，事事想撇开妈妈自己来，她有时便觉得有点冷清。不过，只要威尔不为事情烦心，她照样能那样疼他。只可惜他如今烦心的时候太多了。好在如今吉姆帮了他，兴许就会好起来了。她要添几件新睡衣，就像赖巷（Rye Lane）橱窗里摆的那样，又暖和又实惠，样子也不难看，袖口还镶着几可乱真的花边。到那时候，兴许——可就在这时，她手边小桌上的闹钟响了，她只好不再想下去。

===Original===
The sudden noise brought her husband to a sitting-up position in an instant. He was still grasping the sheets, and his erect hair made him look so like a frightened baby that Mrs. Marble laughed. He glared and blinked at her uncomprehendingly for a while.
“W﻿—what is it?” he demanded.
To Mrs. Marble, mentally constituted as she was, no strange mood came amiss, or seemed strange to her.
“It’s just the clock, dear,” she said, “half-past seven.”
===Chinese===
这突如其来的响声，霎时把她丈夫弹坐了起来。他手里还攥着床单，头发根根直立，活脱脱一个受了惊的娃娃，马布尔太太（Mrs. Marble）不由得笑出了声。他瞪着她直眨眼，好一阵子没回过神来。
“什……什么事？”他问道。
以马布尔太太这样的心性，无论多么古怪的脾气临到头上，她都不觉得碍事，甚至不觉得古怪。
“就是闹钟，亲爱的，”她说，“七点半了。”

===Original===
“The clock?” said Mr. Marble. “I thought﻿—I was dreaming. Just the clock?”
He still muttered to himself as he snuggled down into the bed again, with his face hidden by the pillows. Annie had never known him mutter to himself before, but he was still muttering and mumbling to himself as she began to dress. Then suddenly he stopped muttering, and sat upright again in the bed.
“By God!” he said. “I wasn’t dreaming.”
He threw off the bedclothes and climbed stiffly out of the bed. He looked like a pathetic little boy in his striped blue and white pyjamas as he hobbled across the room, to where his clothes were piled untidily on a chair. He tumbled some of these on the floor as he seized his coat, and he plunged his hand into the breast-pocket. Annie could not see what he found there, but apparently it confirmed his suspicions. He stared vacantly across the room for several seconds, the coat dangling from his hands.
“No,” he repeated, “I wasn’t dreaming.”
===Chinese===
“闹钟？”马布尔先生（Mr. Marble）说，“我当是——我当是做梦呢。就只是闹钟？”
他重新蜷进被窝，把脸埋进枕头，嘴里还在喃喃自语。安妮从没听他这样自言自语过；可等她动手穿衣，他仍旧嘟嘟囔囔个不停。忽然间他不嘟囔了，又在床上猛地坐直了。
“天哪！”他说，“我不是在做梦。”
他掀开被子，僵着身子爬下床来。他穿着那身蓝白条纹睡衣，看上去像个怪可怜的小男孩，一步一挪地蹭过房间，走向那把胡乱堆着衣服的椅子。他抓起上衣时，把几件衣服翻落到地板上，随即一只手直插进胸前内袋。安妮看不见他摸到了什么，但那东西显然坐实了他的疑心。他两手拎着上衣垂在身前，直勾勾地望着房间的另一头，好几秒钟没有动静。
“不，”他又说了一遍，“我不是在做梦。”

===Original===
He hobbled stiffly but feverishly back across the room and thrust his feet into his carpet slippers, and then hurried out of the room. Annie, amazed, heard him enter Winnie’s bedroom next door. Then she heard him pull up the blind there, while Winnie sleepily inquired what was the matter, unanswered. Annie simply did not understand it. It was the first time she could ever remember his getting out of bed before breakfast was ready. But she could not wait to reflect on the matter. She huddled on the rest of her clothes and hastened downstairs to look after breakfast.
There was no end to the surprises of that day. To begin with, Mr. Marble came down in his Sunday clothes of neat blue serge, instead of the dilapidated suit which he usually wore to business, and he only replied to Annie’s innocent and inevitable remark on this strange phenomenon with a scowl. He had not come straight down into the dining-room, as was his wont, either. Instead, he had gone into the tiny and seldom entered sitting-room at the back, and Annie, hastening in duty bound to see what he wanted, found him staring out of the window at the little patch of muddy backyard beyond. It was the same view as he must have seen when he went so surprisingly into Winnie’s bedroom. He could see it at any time when he was home, and he must have seen it several hundred, but for all that he was peering through the window at the muddy bed, flowerless as ever, with an intentness that even Annie noticed. It was extraordinary. It was true that by getting up as soon as she had done he was a quarter of an hour ahead of his usual time, yet even that was no reason for his wasting a good five minutes after his breakfast out in the yard wandering aimlessly up and down as though he was looking for something. Yet even Annie could see that he was relieved not to find anything.
During breakfast there was nothing unusual. Mr. Marble ate little, but that was his way, and he said less, but no one ever said anything at breakfast at 53 Malcolm Road. John was deep in homework he had to prepare for school, and Winnie sewed a button on her glove in the interval of eating porridge. But after breakfast, while Mrs. Marble was in the passage with her husband helping him on with his coat, he pulled from his pocket, loose, as though he had laid them there ready, a small roll of Treasury notes.
===Chinese===
他又僵着身子、却火燎火急地蹭回房间另一头，两脚往毡拖鞋里一插，便匆匆出了屋。安妮惊愕不已，听见他进了隔壁温妮的卧室。接着又听见他把那儿的窗帘哗啦拉起，温妮睡意朦胧地问出了什么事，却没人答理。安妮简直摸不着头脑。在她记忆里，早饭没备好他就先起床，这可是头一遭。可她来不及细想，赶忙胡乱套上剩下的衣裳，急急下楼照管早饭去了。
这一天的稀奇事没有个完。头一桩，马布尔先生下楼时穿的是那身齐整的藏青哔叽礼拜服，而不是平日上班那套寒酸旧西装；安妮天真地——也是免不了地——对这桩稀罕事发了句议论，只换回他一记怒视。他也没有像惯常那样径直进饭厅，反倒进了后头那间难得有人踏进的小客厅；安妮赶紧跟进去看他要什么，却见他正朝窗外呆望，望着外头那一小块泥泞的后院。这景致，与他方才莫名其妙闯进温妮卧室时看到的，想必是同一幅。他在家的日子里随时都看得见，少说也看过几百回了；饶是如此，他还是扒着窗子，盯住那片泥乎乎的花坛——照旧光秃秃不见一朵花——那份专注，连安妮都察觉出来了。真是蹊跷。不错，他起得跟她一样早，比平时早了一刻钟；可就算这样，也不该早饭之后又白白搭上足足五分钟，在院子里漫无目的地踱来踱去，像在找什么东西。然而连安妮都看得出来：什么也没找着，他倒松了一口气。
早饭桌上倒没有什么异样。马布尔先生吃得少，可他向来如此；话说得更少，不过马尔科姆路（Malcolm Road）53号的早饭桌上，本来谁也不说话。约翰埋头赶着学校要交的功课，温妮一边喝着麦片粥，一边抽空往手套上缝扣子。可是早饭后，马布尔太太正在过道里伺候丈夫穿外衣，他从口袋里掏出一小卷国库券来——掏得那样松泛，仿佛早就搁在那儿备好了似的。

===Original===
“Here,” he said, “take these, and for God’s sake go and pay Evans off this morning. And we aren’t going to deal with him any more. Get what you want from Richards’ in the future. There’s enough there for Evans’ bill and a bit over.”
Annie took the notes thankfully.
“Oh, I am so glad, dear,” she said. “So Jim did do something for you after all?”
“Eh?” said Mr. Marble suddenly, and she shrank back as she caught sight of the expression on his face. “What do you mean?” he said.
“Nothing, dear, except that. Why﻿—what﻿—?”
But Mr. Marble had snatched open the door and was striding off. He was muttering again.
===Chinese===
“拿着，”他说，“这些你收好，千万在今天上午就去把埃文斯的账结清。往后咱们不再跟他家打交道了。以后要什么，就上理查兹（Richards）家去买。这点钱付埃文斯的账够了，还能剩下一点。”
安妮感激不尽地接过了钞票。
“哎呀，我真是太高兴了，亲爱的，”她说，“这么说，吉姆到底还是帮了你的忙？”
“啊？”马布尔先生猛地喝了一声。她一瞧见他脸上的神情，立刻缩了回去。“你这是什么意思？”他说。
“没什么，亲爱的，就提了这么一句。怎么——怎么啦——？”
可马布尔先生已经一把拽开门，大步走了。他又嘟囔起来了。

===Original===
Truly, Annie had much to think about as she began her daily household duties, and even she felt what a pity it was that she could not think very clearly. There was Will’s stiffness, now. He was so stiff that he could hardly walk that morning, and she had never known that since before they were married and he used to play football. But Will couldn’t have been playing football last night, could he now? It made her anxious. Then upstairs a new surprise awaited her. Will’s other suit, his old everyday one, lay in a tumbled heap on the bedroom floor. She picked it up and put it away. It was sopping wet, and it was thick with mud. That must be why he did not wear it in the morning. How could he have got it so wet and muddy? The idea of football came to her mind again, but of course that was silly. Will did not play football now, and if he did it wouldn’t be late at night and in his ordinary clothes. With a sigh she left the problem alone and went on tidying the room. Then there were Winnie’s and John’s bedrooms to be done, and after that a look round just to see that everything was in order. In the bathroom an old memory recurred to her. Will had come in here last night. Perhaps she could see what he came for? But she could see nothing different from usual as she looked round. The locked, glass-fronted cupboard in which Will kept his chemicals hung on the wall beside her. She peered in, as she had done hundreds of times before. The manifold bottles meant nothing to her; but she liked to look at the labels and wonder what they were all about. Some of the bottles were of a mysterious brown, and some were white. They were all very neatly arranged. Except one, and that was a little out of its place on the shelf. (As it might be if a man had put it back there in the dark.) Annie glanced casually at the label. It conveyed nothing to her at all, but it happened that the queer name stuck in her mind﻿—potassium cyanide. She turned away from the cupboard without further thought.
There was a pleasurable little excursion before her. She felt quite thrilled with imagining it as she put on her hat before her bedroom mirror. It was a long time since she had had a lot of money in her pocket as she had now. Not that it would stay there long, as she had to pay off Evans’ bill, but it made her feel very rich and great to go into Evans’ shop and ask, in quite a matter-of-fact way, for her bill, and then to open her bag and produce a roll of notes and hand over the amount as if she were accustomed to such transactions every minute of her life. That would be nice, and then she would go on to Richards’, and walk in there, and Mr. Richards would be so nice to her because she was a new customer, and she would order what she wanted, and he would say “Yes, madam,” and “No, madam,” as if her every word was law. She was glad that Jim had done something for Will. Otherwise she would not be having such a happy day. After all, by contrast with the ordinary day of a woman with a house to look after, it would be a happy day.
She was still feeling happy when Mr. Marble returned from the office in the evening, just after the children had had their tea and were settling down to their homework. He looked very tired, poor soul, and he still walked stiffly, but Mrs. Marble had a nice tea ready for him thanks to her visit to Mr. Richards’ shop. There were nice scrambled eggs, good eggs, not the other kind, and three pieces of toast, and there was fresh tea in the pot. Mrs. Marble was disappointed when he looked at the pleasant tea-table with evident distaste. He flung himself down in an armchair with a sigh.
===Chinese===
说实在的，等安妮着手每日的家务活计时，她要想的事可真不少；连她自己也觉得可惜，脑子偏偏不肯把事情想清楚。就说威尔那一身僵劲吧。那天早晨他僵得几乎迈不开步，打他们结婚以前他踢足球那阵子往后，她还从没见他这样过。可威尔昨晚总不至于去踢足球了吧，是不是？这让她心里发慌。接着，楼上一桩新的意外正等着她。威尔的另一套衣服，那套家常的旧西装，皱作一团堆在卧室地板上。她把它拾起来收好。衣服湿得透透的，还厚厚地糊着一层泥。难怪他早晨没穿它。他是怎么把它弄得这样湿、这样泥的？踢足球的念头又冒了上来，可那当然荒唐。威尔如今不踢足球了；就算踢，也不会深更半夜地踢，还穿着家常衣裳。她叹了口气，把这桩难题撇下，接着收拾屋子。然后还有温妮和约翰的卧室要料理，弄完还得各处转一转，看看一切都归置停当。在浴室里，一段旧事又回到她心头。威尔昨晚进来过。兴许她能瞧出他是来取什么的？可她四下里看，样样都与往常无异。那口上锁的玻璃门小柜就挂在她身旁的墙上，威尔的各色药剂都收在里面。她凑近去瞧，像此前几百回那样。那些五花八门的瓶子，她一个也认不得；可她喜欢看那些标签，猜它们各自是干什么的。有的瓶子透着神秘的棕色，有的是白色。全都排得整整齐齐。只有一瓶例外，在架子上稍稍偏离了原位。（就好像有人摸黑把它放回去似的。）安妮漫不经心地扫了一眼标签。上面的字她全然不懂，可那个古怪的名字偏偏留在了她心里——氰化钾。她没再多想，转身离开了那口柜子。
一趟小小的乐事正在前头等着她。她立在卧室的镜子前戴帽子，一面这么想象着，心里美得直发痒。她已经好久好久没有像现在这样，口袋里揣着这么多钱了。钱当然不会久留，她还得上埃文斯家清账去；可这让她自觉又阔气又神气——走进埃文斯的铺子，不慌不忙地开口讨账单，然后打开手袋，掏出一卷钞票，如数点给他，仿佛这样的交易，她一辈子每分钟都在经手。那才叫体面呢。接下来她还要去理查兹家，往店里一进，理查兹先生见她是新主顾，准会待她客客气气；她要什么便点什么，他一口一个“是，太太”，一口一个“不，太太”，仿佛她说的每个字都是金科玉律。她真高兴吉姆帮了威尔的忙。要不然，她哪来这么快活的一天。说到底，比起一个操持家务的女人平平常常的一天来，这总归是快活的一天。
傍晚，孩子们刚吃过茶点、正安顿下来做功课，马布尔先生下班回来了，她还满心快活着呢。他瞧着累坏了，可怜的人，走路也还僵着；多亏跑了理查兹先生的铺子，马布尔太太给他备下了一顿像样的茶点。有喷香的炒鸡蛋——用的新鲜鸡蛋，不是那种次等的——还有三片吐司，壶里沏的也是新茶。可他望着这满桌可意的茶点，分明一脸嫌恶，马布尔太太不由得失望了。他叹了口气，一屁股跌坐进扶手椅里。

===Original===
“Anybody been?” he demanded.
“No, dear, no one,” replied Annie, surprised.
“Sure?”
“Of course I am, dear. Who is there to come? There was no one besides the milkman, and people selling things. It wasn’t Mr. Brown’s day for the insurance.”
“That’s all right then,” said Mr. Marble, and he began to unwrap the parcel he had brought in with him. The children looked up with interest, but they were disappointed. It was only a stupid old bottle of whisky. But Mr. Marble looked at it very eagerly.
===Chinese===
“有人来过吗？”他问。
“没有，亲爱的，谁也没来。”安妮答道，颇感意外。
“能肯定？”
“当然能，亲爱的。会有谁来呢？除了送奶的，就是几个上门卖东西的，再没别人。今天又不是布朗（Brown）先生来收保险费的日子。”
“那就好。”马布尔先生说。接着，他动手拆开随身带回的那个纸包。孩子们饶有兴味地抬起头来，结果却大失所望——不过是瓶蠢模蠢样的旧威士忌。可马布尔先生却把它瞧得眼巴巴的，热切得很。

===Original===
“Aren’t you going to have your tea, dear?” asked Mrs. Marble.
Mr. Marble looked, hesitated, and looked again.
“Oh, all right, then,” he said grudgingly.
He sat down before his tray and began to eat while his wife began her delightful duty of looking after him, pouring out his tea, refilling the teapot from the kettle on the fire, and seeing that he was comfortable. But Mr. Marble had hardly begun when he rose from the table and hurried out of the room. Annie, hurt and mystified, heard him in the sitting-room next door﻿—for the second time that day, and yet perhaps the second time for months. Almost mechanically she followed him, to find him peering through the window in the half light out into the backyard, where there was a small rain falling. He started as he heard her behind him.
===Chinese===
“你不来用点茶吗，亲爱的？”马布尔太太问。
马布尔先生看了看，踌躇了一下，又看了看。
“唉，那好吧。”他不情愿地说。
他在自己的餐盘前坐下吃了起来，妻子也开始了她那桩乐呵呵的差事——照料他：替他斟茶，用炉上坐着的水壶给茶壶添水，张罗得他舒舒服服。可马布尔先生才刚吃了个头，就从桌边起身，匆匆出了屋。安妮又委屈又纳闷，听见他进了隔壁的客厅——这一天里第二回了，而往常，兴许几个月也摊不上一回。她几乎是身不由己地跟了过去，只见他扒着窗子，就着半明半暗的天色，朝外头下着小雨的后院里张望。听见她跟在身后，他吓了一跳。

===Original===
“What are you following me about for like this?” he snapped.
“Nothing dear. Is there anything you want, dear?”
“Nothing, dear. Anything you want, dear?” he gibed. “Only a wife with some sense. That’s all.”
He pushed past her without apology, back to the dining-room. She found him there seated at the table, but he had pushed his nice tray away, and was staring gloomily at the whisky bottle, which was perched, like some family god, in the exact centre of the table. He could not take his eyes off it. He did not look up as she came in, nor did he speak. For some minutes there was quiet in the room, broken only by the scratching of Winnie’s pen and the whispering of the two children to themselves as they toiled over their homework. Annie ought to have taken away the tray and started the washing up. That was her next duty, but for some reason she did not do it. Mr. Marble’s gaze shifted from the whisky bottle, and fixed itself on the tablecloth. Clearly he was following some new train of thought. Suddenly he moved uneasily in his chair, and then he looked up.
===Chinese===
“你这么步步跟着我，想干什么？”他厉声喝道。
“没什么，亲爱的。你要点什么吗，亲爱的？”
“没什么，亲爱的。你要点什么吗，亲爱的？”他讥诮道，“只想要个多少有点脑子的老婆。仅此而已。”
他连一句歉意也没有，从她身边硬挤过去，回了饭厅。她跟进去，见他已在桌边坐定，却把那好好的餐盘推到一旁，正阴沉沉地瞪着那瓶威士忌——瓶子端踞在桌子正中央，活像一尊供着的家神。他的目光离不开它。她进来，他不抬头，也不作声。有那么几分钟，屋里静悄悄的，只听得温妮的笔尖沙沙作响，还有两个孩子埋头苦赶功课时窃窃的商量声。按说，安妮该撤下餐盘，动手洗碗去了。那是她的下一桩活计，可不知怎么，她没有动。马布尔先生的目光从威士忌瓶上挪开，落到了桌布上。显然，他又顺着一条新的思路想下去了。忽然，他在椅子里不安地扭了扭，随后抬起了头。

===Original===
“Has Mrs. What’s-her-name been here today?” he asked his wife. “Oh, you know who I mean﻿—the washerwoman person.”
“No, dear, of course not. She comes on every other Monday. She won’t be coming until Monday week.”
“Well, she’s not to come at all. You must do the washing yourself if you can’t afford to send it out.”
“Of course we can’t afford to send it out, dear. Laundries are dreadfully expensive now.”
“Then you must do it yourself.”
===Chinese===
“那位什么什么太太的，今天来过没有？”他问妻子，“哦，你知道我指谁——就是那个洗衣婆。”
“没有，亲爱的，当然没有。她隔周一回来。要到下下周一才轮着她呢。”
“哼，叫她干脆别来了。要是花不起钱送出去洗，你就自己动手洗。”
“咱们当然花不起这钱，亲爱的。如今的洗衣店贵得吓人。”
“那你就自己洗。”

===Original===
“But I don’t want to. Why must I, Will? It’s dreadfully hard work.”
“Hard work never killed anyone yet. I’m not going to have strange women in *my* house and hanging up clothes in *my* garden any more. That’s why.”
“But﻿—”
“That’s enough, now. Do what I say and don’t argue.” And Mr. Marble turned his gloomy gaze once more to the whisky bottle.
Poor Annie was almost crying. It had been such a nice day up to now, and now everything was going wrong. To cover her whimpering sniffs she took up the tray and went with it down the stairs into the kitchen.
===Chinese===
“可我不愿意呀。为什么非得我洗，威尔？那活儿可累死人。”
“累活儿还从没累死过人。我不许再有外路婆娘进*我*的家门，在*我的*园子里晾衣裳。原因就在这儿。”
“可是——”
“够了，就这样。照我说的做，不许回嘴。”马布尔先生说着，又把那阴郁的目光转回威士忌瓶上。
可怜的安妮几乎要哭出来了。到方才为止，这一天本来多好啊，如今却事事不顺。为了遮掩她抽抽噎噎的吸鼻声，她端起餐盘，端着它下楼进了厨房。

===Original===
Mr. Marble eyed the whisky bottle. He felt he needed some, despite the fact that he had already had three or four﻿—or was it five or six?﻿—whiskies that day. He was very tired, very, very tired, and his head ached. Just as it ached this time yesterday. No, he didn’t want to think about yesterday. How his arms ached with that digging! He ought to have caught cold, too, seeing how it had been raining, but he hadn’t. Pure Scotch Whisky. Very plain on the bottle, but it was good stuff inside. God, but it was! An indescribably passionate longing to drink came over him, and he scraped his chair back from the table and fetched the corkscrew from the sideboard drawer. He drew the cork rapidly and dexterously. Not a scrap fell into the bottle. Then he found himself a tumbler and stood it beside the bottle. There was no soda-water left after last night, but he didn’t want soda-water. He didn’t want anything besides the relief that he knew a few sips of that yellow liquid would bring him. He fingered the bottle lovingly, still standing by the table. Suddenly he became aware of his children’s gaze upon him. Glad of the distraction from the drudgery of homework, they had been sitting silently watching his every movement. With a gust of fury Marble realized that it was impossible for him to drink with those solemn eyes upon him. He put the bottle down again upon the table with a thump.
“Confound you kids!” he said furiously. “Why on earth aren’t you in bed?”
Neither child spoke. The doom was close upon them again, they knew, and this time it was more than could be hoped for for another new arrival to postpone it, as had happened so miraculously yesterday. But if they kept quiet and pretended to be closely occupied with their work it might be all aright. They buried their noses in their books. Marble could only see the tops of their heads, moving a little as they pored over their writing.
===Chinese===
马布尔先生端详着那瓶威士忌。他觉得他需要喝一点，虽说这一天他已经灌下过三四杯——还是五六杯来着？——威士忌了。他累极了，累得很，很累，头也疼。跟昨天这会儿疼得一个样。不，他不愿去想昨天。那阵挖掘，把他的胳膊都挖酸了！淋了那一场雨，照理他该着凉才对，可他没有。纯苏格兰威士忌。瓶上的字样朴素极了，里头装的却是好货。天哪，可真是好货！一阵难以形容的、火烧火燎的酒瘾涌了上来，他把椅子朝后一蹭，离了桌子，从餐具柜的抽屉里取出开塞钻。他拔瓶塞拔得又快又巧，一丝木屑也没掉进瓶里。然后他给自己寻了一只平底大杯，摆在瓶子旁边。经过昨晚，苏打水一点不剩了，可他不要苏打水。除了那点解脱，他什么都不要——他知道，那黄色酒液呷上几口，解脱便会降临。他仍旧立在桌边，爱不释手地摩挲着瓶身。忽然，他察觉孩子们的目光正落在他身上。巴不得从功课的苦役里分一分神，他们一直闷声坐着，瞧着他的一举一动。马布尔顿时怒火直冲上来：这几双正颜厉色的眼睛盯着，这酒没法喝。他砰的一声，把瓶子重新顿在桌上。
“你们这两个小东西，真是该死！”他狂怒道，“究竟为什么不滚去睡觉？”
两个孩子都没吭声。他们知道，那场厄运又临到头上了，而这一回，再不能指望像昨天那样，奇迹般地再来个生客把它岔开。可只要他们不作声，装出专心用功的样子，兴许就能太平无事。他们把鼻子都埋进了书本。马布尔只看得见两颗头顶心，随着他们伏案书写，微微地晃动着。

===Original===
“Bah!” he said. “Don’t act about like that. Shut up those books and go to bed. At once, now.”
Under more favourable circumstances they would have protested that it was not nearly bedtime yet. They should have pointed out that it was only a little after seven o’clock, and they were entitled to another hour at least. But they knew, in the intuitive manner of children, that this time the less they said the better for them. They began silently to pack up their books.
“Go to bed! Go to bed!” raved Mr. Marble.
“Don’t look at me like that, sir,” thundered Mr. Marble. He had suddenly become half hysterical with rage. He pounded on the table with the whisky bottle, crazy with thwarted eagerness. John turned his scowling face in another direction, but the expression on his face was unchanged, and served to drive his father more frantic than ever, if that were possible. He reached out and struck the boy a heavy blow with his open hand, making him stagger.
===Chinese===
“呸！”他说，“少来这一套装模作样。把书合上，睡觉去。马上，这就去。”
换了平常的情形，他们准要抗议，说离睡觉还早得很呢。他们本该指出，如今才七点过一点，他们至少还该有一个钟头好待。可他们凭着孩子特有的直觉明白，这一回，话说得越少，对他们越有利。他们闷声不响地开始收拾书本。
“睡觉去！睡觉去！”马布尔先生狂喊起来。
“不许那样看我，小子。”马布尔先生雷霆大发。他陡然气得半疯，攥着威士忌瓶砰砰砸桌子，那股想喝又喝不成的急火把他烧得发了狂。约翰把阴沉的脸扭向别处，脸上的神情却分毫不变，反而把他父亲逼得愈发疯癫——倘若还能更疯的话。他伸手抡开巴掌，狠狠一记抽在那孩子脸上，抽得他踉跄了一步。

===Original===
They went, without a word, but the scowl on John’s face had somehow something triumphant about it now. If he was going to be sent to bed in arbitrary fashion, he would at least see that his father lost his temper during the process.
