John always felt he disliked his father during these queer moods of his﻿—and the moods were becoming more and more frequent too.
The children went, and Mr. Marble sighed with relief. He dragged his armchair up to the fire, and put the small table beside it for his glass. He would wait and be quite methodical now that a drink was an immediate certainty. He poured himself out a moderate drink and tossed it off. He felt better at once, more peaceful, more *safe*. He refilled his glass, and set it beside him. Then he sat down comfortably by the fire and gazed at the leaping flames. This was just what he wanted to do yesterday, before that wretched boy turned up and spoilt his evening. But it was even better than yesterday, because then he had only three drinks in the decanter. Now he had a whole bottle full, which would last him for this evening at least, without any thought of stinting. It was fine not having to stint. He wouldn’t have to stint in any direction at all for another two weeks at least, thank goodness, or for a lot more than that, if only he were to change those five-pound notes. And after all, why shouldn’t he? Of course the one-pound notes were as safe as anything, but the fivers ought to be just as safe too. They wouldn’t give anything away, even if they were traced from Medland to himself. And if he took care and only cashed them at places where he wasn’t known they wouldn’t be traced at all. Anyway what did it all matter? It was silly to think that he was going to all the trouble he went to last night just to pay off a miserable thirty pounds of debts. Better to be hung for a sheep than for a lamb. Stop! Why was he thinking about hanging?
Mrs. Marble, reentering from the kitchen, saw her husband reach out greedily for the glass on the table at his side, and swallow its contents in violent gulps. Then she knew that dear Will would be unapproachable for the rest of the evening, and she would not be able to have that pleasant chat with him about what Jim had done for them that she had been looking forward to all day. Mrs. Marble was rather disappointed.
===Chinese===
他们一声不吭地走了，可约翰脸上的阴沉，不知怎么竟带上了一丝得胜的意味。既然要被这般蛮横地打发上床，他至少要叫他父亲在这过程中大发一场脾气，才算捞回一城。每逢这种邪性的脾气发作，约翰总觉得自己恨父亲——何况这发作，也越来越勤了。
孩子们走了，马布尔先生如释重负，长出了一口气。他把扶手椅拖到炉火跟前，又把小桌摆在旁边搁酒杯。眼下这杯酒是稳稳到手了，他要耐下心来，规规矩矩地享用。他给自己斟了适量的一杯，一仰而尽。他顿时觉得好受了些，更平静，也更*安全*了。他又斟满一杯，搁在手边。然后他舒舒服服地在炉边坐下，凝望着跳动的火苗。昨天他图的就是这个，偏偏那个可恶的小子闯上门来，毁了他的夜晚。可今天比昨天还要好：昨天细颈瓶里统共只剩三杯的量，如今他有一整瓶，至少这一晚管够，不必掂斤播两地省着喝。不必省，真好。谢天谢地，至少还有两个星期，他哪一头都不必省了；只要把那几张五英镑的票子兑开，还远不止两个星期呢。说到底，他为什么不该兑？一镑的票子固然万无一失，五镑的照理也该一样稳妥。就算这些票子被追查到是从梅德兰（Medland）手里转到了他名下，也露不出马脚。何况只要他留神，只在不认识他的地方兑，压根儿就查不到他头上。反正，这又有什么要紧？他昨晚吃了那么多苦头，倘若只为清掉区区三十英镑的债，那才冤呢。偷只大羊是绞死，偷只小羊也是绞死。打住！他干吗想绞死不绞死的？
马布尔太太从厨房回来，看见丈夫急不可耐地伸手，抓过手边桌上的酒杯，仰着脖子大口大口地灌了下去。她于是明白了：今晚剩下的时光，亲爱的威尔是谁也休想亲近了；她盼了一整天，想跟他舒心地聊聊吉姆帮了他们什么忙，这场聊天是落空了。马布尔太太好生失望。

## III

===Original===
Yet for some weeks Mr. Marble hesitated about changing those five-pound notes. He was of mixed character. When he had been a rat in a corner he had fought like one, desperately, to the last risk, but now he had escaped he thought of nothing except flight and covering his tracks.
And he was paying heavily enough for those few Treasury notes. That heart of his which had thumped so heavily on that stormy evening thumped just as heavily at other times now. He could not keep his mind from working out possible occurrences in the future, and some new morbid thought of the arrival of the police, instigated by the officials of Medland’s hotel, or of some unexpected inquiry by the Bank as to whence had come the money which had so suddenly come into his possession, would set his heart pounding away until he could only lean back in his chair and gasp. He would wake sweating in the night too, with the fevered blood running hot under his skin, as some fantastic possibility developed in his sleeping mind. Then he would writhe and toss in his bed, muttering faintly to himself, tortured with fears of the known and of the unknown. And sometimes, when he had been sleeping very badly, one or two horrible memories returned to him: memories of a pair of staring eyes, and of a boyish face, the mouth smeared with foam. That was the worst of all.
He was not even happy sitting alone in the dining-room at Malcolm Road, with a full bottle of his only friend beside him, and calm in the knowledge that *no one was poking about in the garden*. The whisky only made him think the more at first. It took a long time before he could stop his mind from thinking about what might happen, and he soon found the worst moments of all were those at the beginning of the evening, when the whisky only bit and did not deaden. At times the prospect so frightened him that he flinched from his preliminary torture, and would go through the evening without drinking at all, although every fibre of his body seemed to be shrieking for it. These were the times when he craved for the company of his wife and children; when he would encourage Annie to long monologues about how she had spent her day. To Marble, sitting back in his chair, with Annie droning away beside him about her encounter with the baker’s boy, who now worked for the butcher instead, and how she went down Rye Lane to buy remnants at the sale, and what Mr. Brown, the insurance agent, had to say, it seemed incredible that anything unusual had happened. It must have all been a wild dream. If it were reality, how then could he be sitting at his fireside so peacefully? Brief monosyllables were sufficient to keep Annie talking and Mr. Marble had ample time to think of all this. It was too like a fantastic nightmare for anything really to have happened in this very room, this poky, stuffy, suburban dining-room, and for a terrible secret to be hidden away in the garden just outside. Drugged by Annie’s talk, or by John’s or Winnie’s chatter about school, Mr. Marble not infrequently succeeded in persuading himself that it was indeed a dream. And Annie was so pleasantly fluttered and flattered by his attention in listening to her like this! But when night came, Mr. Marble paid for his relaxation. Then, on the verge of sleep, he would realize that it was not a dream﻿—and he would spend the night turning and tossing in fevered anxiety.
Security came back gradually. It was not a feeling of complete security; rather was it a feeling that anything would be welcome if only it would happen. No policeman came prying round 53 Malcolm Road; no official from the hotel came to ask as to the present whereabouts of one James Medland. Marble had paid his debts, every one of them, like a sensible man, and that had taken all the slender store of one-pound notes. But his personal expenses were much higher nowadays﻿—they were bound to be while he spent half a sovereign a day on whisky﻿—and nothing he could say to Annie had any effect in reducing household expenditure. The old rule became well established of spending a little more than his income. Under such circumstances it was inevitable that the five-pound notes should begin to melt away. He was very careful about it, as his bank clerk experience taught him to be. Not one of those rustling pieces of paper ever passed through the hands of one of the local tradesmen, and most certainly never did one go through Mr. Marble’s account at the Bank.
Occasionally at the big, gilded, popular Corner Houses was to be seen a little round-faced man sitting alone. He ordered an expensive dinner, as expensive as the limited menu allowed, and he would eat it hurriedly and furtively, with never a glance from his pale-blue eyes at the people at the other tables, and having eaten he would call for his bill and hurry out. He paid the bill by means of a five-pound note, and, thrusting the change into his pocket, he would rush away as though pursued. So he was pursued, too. One pursuer was an impossible fear of one of the frock-coated floorwalkers stopping him and asking him whence came the note﻿—with a meaning glance to an attendant to fetch the waiting detective﻿—and the other was the haunting fear lest there might be someone casually finding out something in a desolate back garden in a dreary street in a southern suburb. Mr. Marble learned what it meant to walk down that street with that foolish heart of his pounding away in his breast, hot with impatience to reach home to ensure that all would be well, and yet standing hesitating at his front gate unable to go in to face what might be there﻿—police, or, what would be nearly as bad, a look in the eyes of his wife and children and words unspoken that meant that they *knew*. And still nothing happened.
===Chinese===
然而接连几个星期，马布尔先生迟迟不敢把那几张五英镑的钞票兑开。他这个人性格本就驳杂。当初被逼进死角时，他当真像一只困鼠那样搏斗过，不顾死活，不惜押上最后一注；如今既然脱身出来，他心里盘算的便只剩下一件事——远走高飞，抹去踪迹。
而为了区区几张国库券，他付出的代价也着实不轻。他那颗心，在那个风雨交加的夜晚擂得那样凶，如今在别的时辰也照样擂得厉害。他管不住自己的脑子，由着它一遍遍推演日后可能的种种变故——一会儿是梅德兰下榻旅馆的职员说动了警察找上门来，一会儿是银行突然查问那笔凭空落到他手里的钱财来路何方——每有这类病态的新念头冒出来，他的心便狂跳不止，直跳得他只能仰靠在椅中大口喘气。夜里他也会一身汗地惊醒，滚烫的血在皮肤底下奔涌，睡梦中的头脑正演化出某种荒诞不经的可能。于是他在床上翻来覆去，浑身扭动，气若游丝地喃喃自语，被已知的和未知的恐惧轮番煎熬。有时，本就睡得极坏的他，一两幅骇人的景象会重新浮上眼前：一双瞪圆的眼睛，一张少年的脸，嘴角糊满白沫。那才是最难熬的。
就连独个儿坐在马尔科姆路的饭厅里，他也不得安生——身旁立着他唯一的朋友，满满一瓶威士忌，心里又笃定*花园里没有人翻寻*。起初，威士忌反倒叫他念头更多。得过上好一阵子，他的脑子才肯罢休，不再去想那些可能临头的祸事；而不久他便发现，最难熬的莫过傍晚开头那段时光，那时酒劲只会咬人，还麻醉不了。有时前景吓得他连这场入夜的先刑都不敢领受，宁可整晚滴酒不沾，尽管浑身上下每一根筋都像在尖声讨酒喝。偏是这种时候，他渴望妻儿做伴；会怂恿安妮作冗长的独白，絮絮讲她这一整天是怎么过的。马布尔靠在椅背上，听安妮在旁边嗡嗡絮语——说撞见了面包店的伙计，那伙计如今改去肉铺帮工了；说她怎么逛到赖巷，在大减价上买零头布料；说保险经纪人布朗（Brown）先生又讲了些什么——在他听来，竟难以相信出过什么非常之事。那一切想必只是一场荒唐大梦。若果有其事，他此刻又怎能这样安然坐在自家炉边？偶尔吐出一两个单音节的字眼，便够让安妮接着讲下去，马布尔先生有的是工夫细细思量这一切。这一切太像一场光怪陆离的噩梦了——就在这间屋子里，这间局促憋闷的郊区小饭厅里，真出过事；一个可怕的秘密，就藏在外头的花园里——这教人如何信得。被安妮的絮语、或是约翰温妮关于学校的闲话所麻醉，马布尔先生往往还真能说服自己：那的确是场梦。而他这样肯侧耳听她说话，安妮又是何等受宠若惊、心里发甜！可是夜幕一落，马布尔先生就得为这份松快还账。临到睡意将临的一刻，他会恍然醒悟：那不是梦——于是整夜焦灼，翻腾不休。
安全感是渐渐回来的。那并不是十足的安全感；倒不如说是一种“事情只要肯来，来什么都好”的心情。没有警察到马尔科姆路53号左近窥探；旅馆方面也没有人来打听一个叫詹姆斯·梅德兰的人眼下身在何处。马布尔像个明白人似的，把欠账一笔一笔还了个干净，那薄薄一叠一英镑的钞票也就此用罄。可如今他个人的花销大增——每天灌下半沙弗林金币（half a sovereign）的威士忌，想省也省不成——而无论他说什么，安妮也毫不见效，家用一文不减。那句老规矩又稳稳当当立住了：花的总比进的多出一星半点。处在这种境况下，那几张五英镑的钞票开始消融，原是势所必然。他办这事极为小心，银行职员的历练教会他非如此不可。那些窸窣作响的纸片，没有一张经本地商人的手转过一道；更万万不会有一张，走进马布尔先生在银行的账户。
偶尔，在那些金碧辉煌、宾客盈门的大角屋餐厅（Corner Houses）里，可以见到一个圆脸的小个子男人独坐一桌。他点一份昂贵的晚餐——价钱之高，以那份有限的菜单所能容许为度——吃得匆匆忙忙、偷偷摸摸，一双淡蓝色的眼睛从不朝邻桌的人瞥一下；吃罢便唤账单，匆匆离席。他用一张五英镑的钞票会账，把找头往衣袋里一塞，便仓皇而去，仿佛身后有人追赶。他的确也有人追赶。一个追者，是那种绝无可能的恐惧：生怕哪位穿长礼服的巡场管事拦住他，盘问这张钞票来路何处——一面意味深长地递个眼色，叫侍者去请那位候着的侦探；另一个，是那种阴魂不散的恐惧：生怕在南郊某条灰暗的街上，一座荒凉的后花园里，正有人无意间刨出些什么。马布尔先生由此领教了那是什么滋味：走在那条街上，那颗愚蠢的心在胸腔里擂鼓般狂跳，急煎煎只想一步跨进家门，好确知一切无事；可站到自家院门前，却又踌躇却步，不敢进去面对里头可能有的一切——也许是警察，也许是那几乎同样糟糕的东西：妻子儿女眼中的一种神色，和那些没说出口、却分明表示他们*已经知道*的话语。然而，仍旧什么事也没有发生。

===Original===
The thing that came in the end was not in the least what he expected. It was nine o’clock in the evening, and Mr. Marble was sitting in the same frowsty dining-room, from minute to minute gauging with his eye the two most important things of the moment﻿—the height of the whisky in the bottle and the distance the hands of the clock had moved round. His wife was in the room too, but she made no sign of her presence save for her whispered conversation with herself. A smart rebuff from her husband earlier in the evening had shown her that it was not one of his “nice” evenings. But when the postman knocked she rose and went to fetch what he had brought. It was only a single letter, and she gave it to Will. He fumbled it open with whisky-tangled fingers, and read the enclosed typewritten note with whisky-dazzled eyes. He read it three times before he understood the words, and for five minutes following he sat quite still as their significance came slowly home to him. It was formal notice to quit the house known as 53 Malcolm Road.
It was next morning before he could convince himself that the peril was not as real as his shrinking mind had believed last night. He was safe enough at present, of course. The Rent Act confirmed him in his tenancy for as long as it endured. This formal notice to quit was merely a necessary preliminary to raising his rent. But it gave him real cause for anxiety. It showed him a concrete thing to be afraid of, instead of the wild nightmares of prying neighbours or stray dogs, which might dig in that disused flowerbed. Some time or other he might be compelled to leave the house, and what would happen then? He did not know.
The next evening the librarian at the local Public Library had an interview with a little man in a seedy blue suit, with a ragged, red moustache and weak blue eyes, who went through the formalities to obtain a ticket and then demanded a selection of books on crime.
“I am afraid we have very few books on the subject,” said the librarian, surprised.
===Chinese===
最后来的那桩事，与他所料的毫不相干。晚上九点，马布尔先生照旧坐在那间霉闷的饭厅里，一分一秒地用眼睛估量着眼下最要紧的两样东西——瓶里威士忌的液面，和钟面上指针走过的刻度。他妻子也在房里，只是除了自顾自的低声絮语，全无声息。傍晚早些时候丈夫的一声抢白，已让她明白，今儿晚上算不上他“和气”的日子。可邮差一敲门，她还是起身去取来件。不过一封信，她交给了威尔。他用被威士忌缠得不利索的手指拆开信封，用被威士忌晃花了的眼睛，读那张打字机打出来的便函。字句读了三遍他才看懂；此后足足五分钟，他一动不动地坐着，那字句的分量一点一点沉入心底。那是一纸正式的迁出通知，限他腾退名为马尔科姆路53号的这所房子。
直到第二天早晨，他才总算让自己相信，这危险并不像昨夜那颗瑟瑟发抖的心所以为的那样真切。眼下他当然够安全。《租金法》（Rent Act）保障他的租住权，法令一日不废止，就一日撵不走他。这纸正式的迁出通知，不过是加租之前必经的一道手续。可它确实给了他忧惧的实据。它给了他一件具体的可怕之物，来取代先前那些凭空乱想的噩梦——什么爱打听的邻居啦、乱刨土的野狗啦，都可能在那块废弃的花坛里下爪。迟早有一天，他说不定不得不搬出这所房子，到那时可怎么好？他不知道。
第二天晚上，本地公共图书馆的管理员接待了一个小个子男人：一身寒酸的蓝西装，一撇乱蓬蓬的红胡子，一双怯弱无神的蓝眼睛。此人办妥了领借书证的手续，便开口索要一批讲犯罪的书。
“这个题目的书，恐怕我们没几本。”管理员说，颇觉意外。

===Original===
“Never mind, let’s see what you have got,” replied Marble.
The librarian brought him an armful of books. There were two of Lombroso’s works, a volume of medical jurisprudence, two or three stray works on prison reform and kindred subjects, while from the bottom of the heap the librarian produced apologetically a rather lurid volume on *Crimes and Criminals: Historic Days at the Assizes*. Marble took the pile into his trembling hands, and peered at them anxiously.
“I’ll have this one,” he said, firmly, *Crimes and Criminals* in his hand.
The librarian dated it and handed it over unwillingly. For one class of book borrower he had a friendly feeling; for another he had toleration, seeing that it was the main support of the library. The one class was that of the humble clerk and mechanic, anxious for self-improvement, the other was that of the diligent reader of fiction. For the omnivorous browser who read all sorts of things, he had a virulent hatred, suspecting him of a prurient desire to lay hold of those books which even a Public Library has to a small extent, the gift of some careless donor, or (dangerously, in his opinion) termed “classical.” And this newcomer was evidently a bad example of this type. His perverted taste was obviously sated even with Public Library fiction, and the craving for sensation was worse, to his mind, even than the physiological researches of spotty-faced adolescents. He had taken the book of all the library of which the librarian was most ashamed. He shook his head sadly after the receding, bent-shouldered figure.
===Chinese===
“不妨事，你们有什么，先拿来瞧瞧。”马布尔答道。
管理员给他抱来一摞书。有两本龙勃罗梭的著作，一册法医学，两三本论监狱改良及同类题目的零散书；管理员又带着歉意，从书堆底下抽出一本颇为耸人听闻的书——*《罪案与罪犯：巡回法庭上的历史性日子》*（*Crimes and Criminals: Historic Days at the Assizes*）。马布尔把那一摞书接进颤抖的双手，急切地逐本端详。
“我就要这本。”他说，语气坚决，手里攥着那本*《罪案与罪犯》*。
管理员在借出单上填了日期，老大不情愿地把书递了过去。对一类借书人，他心存好感；对另一类，他姑且容忍——毕竟人家是图书馆的顶梁柱。前一类，是那些一心上进的卑微职员和工匠；后一类，是那些手不释卷的小说读者。至于那种什么都啃、样样都翻的杂食性书虫，他则怀有切齿的痛恨，疑心这种人藏着贪淫的邪念，专想染指连公共图书馆也只备得少许的那类书——或是哪位漫不经心的捐赠人送来的赠品，或是（在他看来，危险得很）被冠以“古典”之名的东西。而这个新来的，显然正是此辈中的劣等货色。他那副歪曲的胃口，显然连公共图书馆的小说都吃腻了；这种渴求刺激的瘾，在他看来，比满脸粉刺的少年偷偷摸摸的生理研究还要等而下之。他借走的，是全馆藏书之中最令管理员羞于示人的一本。他望着那佝偻着肩、渐渐远去的背影，悲哀地摇了摇头。

===Original===
But that was the first night for months that Mr. Marble did not drink more than was good for him. *Crimes and Criminals* held him fascinated. It was not a subject of which he knew very much; he was even ignorant of the details of ordinary criminal procedure. In this book he found criminals labelled as “great”; he read ghastly stories of passion and revenge; he read of the final scenes on the scaffold with the dreadful concentration of a bird on a snake.
And the hair bristled on his head, and he experienced a horrible sensation of despair as he gradually realized that two out of every three of the criminals mentioned came to grief through inability to dispose of the body. There was a tale of a woman who had walked for miles through London streets with a body in a perambulator; there was an account of Crippen’s life in London with the body of his murdered wife buried in his cellar; but the police laid hold of them all in time. On this damning difficulty the book dwelt with self-righteous gusto. At midnight Mr. Marble put the book aside sick with fear. He was safe at present; indeed, he was safe altogether on many accounts. As long as he could see that that flowerbed was undisturbed no one would know that there was any cause for suspicion at all. That nephew of his had vanished into the nowhere that harbours so many of those whose disappearance is chronicled so casually in the papers. There was nothing, absolutely nothing, to connect him, the respectable Mr. Marble, with young Medland’s annihilation. But once let some fool start investigations, even involuntary ones, in that flowerbed, and the fat would be in the fire. Mr. Marble did not know whether identification would be possible at this late date﻿—he made a mental note that he must get another book from the library in which he could look that up﻿—but even if that were impossible there would be unpleasant inquiries, and he would be in trouble for certain. Come what may, either he must maintain complete control over that flowerbed, or else he must make some other adequate arrangements. And from those “arrangements,” whatever they might be, his soul shrank in utter dread. They were certain to be his ruin. There would be some unforeseen mishap, just as there had been to the cart in which that man in the book tried to carry his victim’s remains along Borough High Street. Then he would be found out and then﻿—? Prison and the gallows, said Mr. Marble to himself, with the sweat pouring in torrents down his face.
One thing would give him security, and that would be the purchase of his house. That would give him security against disturbance for the rest of his life. Mr. Marble did not mind what happened after his death, as long as that death was not accelerated by process of law.
But how could Mr. Marble possibly purchase his house? He was living beyond his income as it was, he told himself, with a grim recollection of five-pound notes changed in Popular Corner Houses. Yet he must, he must, he must. The blind panic of the earlier months had changed to a reasoned panic now; the one object of Mr. Marble’s life now was to raise enough money to buy that house. The covering letter to yesterday’s notice to quit had hinted that perhaps Mr. Marble would find it to his advantage to buy instead of continuing to rent; Mr. Marble went to bed, to toss and mutter at his wife’s side all night long as he framed impossible schemes for acquiring money, a great deal of money, enough to buy the freehold of 53 Malcolm Road.
===Chinese===
然而那一夜，几个月来头一回，马布尔先生没有喝多。*《罪案与罪犯》*把他迷住了。这个题目他原本所知寥寥；连寻常刑事诉讼的种种细节，他也是懵然无知。在这本书里，他见到了被冠以“伟大”之名的罪犯；读到了一桩桩情杀与复仇的骇人故事；读到绞刑架上的临终一幕时，他专注得可怕，宛如小鸟盯住了蛇。
他头上的头发根根倒竖，一股骇人的绝望攫住了他——他渐渐看清，书中提到的罪犯，三个里倒有两个，栽就栽在尸体无法处置上。有一个故事，讲一个女人用婴儿车推着一具尸体，在伦敦街头走了好几里地；有一篇记叙，讲克里平（Crippen）在伦敦的日子，被害妻子的尸身就埋在他家地窖里；可警察到头来把他们一个个都及时拿住了。书中以道貌岸然的兴味，对这个足以定罪的难题大书特书。午夜时分，马布尔先生把书撂到一边，心里发呕，怕得厉害。他眼下是安全的；说实在的，从好些方面看，他都是安然无事的。只要他能看住那块花坛，不容它受一点惊动，就没有人会想到世上竟还有任何可疑之处。他那位外甥已经消失得无影无踪——报纸上用寥寥数行便打发了的那些失踪者，正是消失在这同样的无影无踪里。没有任何东西，绝对没有任何东西，能把体面的马布尔先生同小梅德兰的毁灭联系起来。可是，一旦有哪个蠢货——哪怕是出于无心——在那块花坛里动起挖掘的念头，可就要捅破天了。马布尔先生不知道事到如今，尸身还辨认不辨认得出来——他心里暗暗记下一笔，回头得从图书馆另借一本书，把这一点查个明白——可就算辨认不出，也免不了要来一番不痛快的盘查，他准得陷进麻烦里去。无论如何，他要要不然就得把那块花坛牢牢攥在手心，要不然就得另作一番妥善的“安排”。而一想到那些安排——不管是什么样的安排——他的灵魂便吓得整个儿缩紧。那些安排准保毁了他。总会出点什么意料不到的岔子，就像书里那个人，赶着大车载受害人的残骸沿博罗高街（Borough High Street）走时出的那场意外一样。到那时他就会败露，然后呢——？监狱和绞架，马布尔先生对自己说，汗如泉涌，顺着脸颊往下淌。
只有一件事能给他安全，那就是把这所房子买下来。买下它，他下半辈子就不怕任何惊扰了。马布尔先生并不在乎死后如何，只要那死期不至于被法律程序提前。
可马布尔先生哪里买得起这所房子？他如今过的本就是入不敷出的日子，他对自己说，一面咬牙想起那些在角屋餐厅里兑开的五英镑钞票。然而他必须买，必须，必须。前几个月那种盲目的恐慌，如今已改头换面，成了一种有理有据的恐慌；马布尔先生此生唯一的目标，便是凑足一笔钱，把那所房子买下来。昨天那纸迁出通知所附的信函里暗示，比起继续租住，马布尔先生或许觉得干脆买下更合算。马布尔先生上了床，在妻子身边翻腾、咕哝了整整一夜，一门心思盘算着弄钱的路数——全是行不通的路数——要弄到很多很多钱，多到足够买下马尔科姆路53号的永久业权。

## IV

===Original===
At the Bank, as was only to be expected, they had noticed a slight change in Mr. Marble’s manner of late. He always looked worried, and clearly often he had been drinking. The head of the department to whom Mr. Marble acted as second-in-command noticed it frequently, but he took no drastic action. For one thing, he was glad to have an inefficient second, as that enabled him to keep the work more closely under his own hand, thereby making his retention of his job more probable, and for another he had a queer sort of liking for “poor old Marble,” with his worried expression, and his worried eyes, and his worried moustache. In fact, Mr. Henderson was heartily glad that Marble had apparently struggled out of the financial difficulties which had for many months before made him come to Henderson borrowing a fortnight before payday. What Henderson did not realize was that for all Marble’s looseness of fibre, and seeming incompetence to grapple with anything worth while, somewhere in Marble there was a keen mind﻿—razor keen still, despite too much whisky of late﻿—and a fount of potential fierce energy which might contrive wonderful things if only something were to rouse him sufficiently. Mr. Henderson, of course, knew nothing of a piece of work that Marble had performed marvellously efficiently some months ago.
So far Mr. Marble had found no opportunity of making the money that he hankered after so pathetically. Yet money was in the air all the time that he was working, more so even than is usually the case in a bank department. For the department of the County National Bank, of which Mr. Henderson was executive chief, with Mr. Marble as his chief assistant, dealt solely with foreign exchange, buying and selling money all day, dollars for cotton spinners, francs for costumiers, pesetas for wine merchants, and dollars, francs, pesetas, and, above all, marks, for speculators of every trade or none. Gambling in foreign exchange was becoming a national habit by which the National County Bank profited largely. Here, if anywhere, would Mr. Marble win the money for which he thirsted. But Mr. Marble knew too much about foreign exchange operations, and he was afraid. There had been times when he had made a pound or two by well-timed buying and selling, but not many. He had the example before him of those who had bought marks at prices seemingly lower than they could ever be again, only to see them change from the absurd thousands to the still more absurd hundred thousands which meant the loss of nine-tenths of their investment. The wise man who speculated in foreign exchange would do well to sell, not buy, in most cases. And one could not sell without first buying unless one could arrange a “forward operation” with a bank. One can only profit by a falling market if one sells what one has not got. And no bank would allow anyone to arrange a forward operation unless he could show some sort of reason for wanting to do so. Not much reason, of course, but a good deal more than a mere bank clerk with sixty pounds only in his account could possibly show. There was another advantage about the forward operation, too. It was only necessary in this case to put up a margin of ten percent of one’s investment. In that case an increase of five percent in the value of the sum controlled meant an actual profit on the sum invested of fifty percent﻿—if one were lucky enough to have bought; if one had sold instead it meant a loss of fifty percent. If a man were to buy on a ten percent margin, and the money bought were to double in value, he would multiply the money invested not by two, but by twenty. Mr. Marble thought of the sixty pounds he had to invest, and his mouth watered.
But week followed dreary week and nothing could be done. The foreign exchange market seemed to have gone mad. The mark had fallen away to millions, as the Austrian crown had done two years before. The lira seemed to be going the same way. Sterling exchange on New York alone was struggling back to its prewar position. Even the franc was dropping steadily. Before the war it was little more than twenty-five; now it was over a hundred, and it was being hammered slowly lower and lower. Mr. Marble watched the market and hesitated. If the franc and the lira were to drop with a crash as the mark had done, and a wise man had sold in advance, he could look for a profit to be reckoned in terms of thousands percent. And the brokers with whom Mr. Marble in the course of his daily duties conversed over the telephone all seemed to think it likely. In the foreign exchange department of the National County Bank (Head Office) all the clerks were convinced of the same thing. Some of them had already made minute sums of money by cautious speculation, helped by their friends the clerks in the brokers’ offices. They had sold tiny sums in advance, got out hurriedly immediately afterwards, and then cursed their faint-heartedness as the franc still went on dropping. Mr. Marble saw all this, and was tempted. Twice he nearly ventured, but each time that keen judgement of his which was always somewhere in the background ready to be called upon held him back. There was something doubtful somewhere.
===Chinese===
银行里，大家不出所料地注意到，马布尔先生近来的举止有些变了。他总是一副愁容，而且明摆着常常喝过了量。马布尔先生在他手下担任副手的部门主任对此屡有所见，却没有采取任何断然措施。一来，副手无能正中他下怀——如此一来，工作便能更紧地攥在自己手心，他保住职位的把握也随之更大；二来，他对“可怜的老马布尔”怀有一种说不清道不明的喜爱——那副愁苦的表情，愁苦的眼睛，愁苦的小胡子。事实上，先前一连数月，马布尔总在发薪日前两周便跑来找他借钱，如今显然已从经济窘迫中挣扎脱身，亨德森先生为此由衷高兴。亨德森不曾意识到的是：尽管马布尔生性疲沓，看上去连一件像样的正经事都对付不了，可在他身上的某个地方，藏着一副敏锐的头脑——尽管近来威士忌灌得太多，依旧锋利如剃刀——还蓄着一泓凶猛的潜在精力，只要有什么东西将他充分激发起来，这股精力便可能干出一番了不起的事业。至于几个月前马布尔曾何等干净利落地办成一桩活计，亨德森先生自然一无所知。
到那时为止，马布尔先生始终没有找到机会，去弄到他那般可怜巴巴渴求着的钱。然而他办公的时时刻刻，空气里都浮着钱味，甚至比一般银行部门里的还要浓。因为国民县银行（National County Bank）里由亨德森先生任主管、马布尔先生任首席助手的这个部门，专营外汇，成天买进卖出各国货币：替纺纱商买美元，替服装商买法郎，替酒商买比塞塔，还替各行各业的投机客——有正业的和没正业的——买美元、法郎、比塞塔，尤其是马克。外汇投机正蔚为一种举国风气，国民县银行从中获利甚丰。若说天下还有一处能让马布尔先生赢到他渴求的那笔钱，那便是此地。可是马布尔先生对外汇业务懂得太多，所以他心里发怵。他也有过把握时机买进卖出、赚上一两英镑的时候，但为数寥寥。他眼前便有前车之鉴：有人买进马克时，价钱看似已低到再不可能更低，结果却只能眼睁睁看着它从荒唐的几千跌到更加荒唐的几十万——十分之九的本钱就此打了水漂。投机外汇的聪明人，多数场合以卖出为宜，而非买进。可若不先买进，便无从卖出，除非能同某家银行安排一桩“远期交易”（forward operation）。只有卖自己手里没有的东西，才能在跌市中获利。而任何银行都不会容许谁去安排远期交易，除非那人能摆出几分要这样做的理由。当然用不着多大理由，但总要比一个账户里仅有六十英镑的普通行员所能摆得出的多得多。远期交易另有一个好处：这种场合只消缴付投资额一成的保证金。如此一来，所控金额的价值每上涨百分之五，投入的本金便实赚百分之五十——前提是运气好做了买进；若是做了卖出，那便是实亏百分之五十。倘若一个人以一成保证金买进，所买货币的价值又翻上一番，他投入的本钱就不是翻一倍，而是翻上二十倍。马布尔先生想着自己可以投进去的六十英镑，口水都快要流出来了。
可是沉闷的星期一个跟着一个过去，仍是无事可做。外汇市场像是发了疯。马克已经跌到以百万计的地步，一如两年前的奥地利克朗。里拉似乎正循着同一条路走。唯独对纽约的英镑汇价，还在挣扎着往战前的位置爬。连法郎也在稳步下挫。战前它不过二十五挂零，如今已过百，还在被人一锤一锤、慢慢往低处砸。马布尔先生盯着行情，踌躇不定。倘若法郎和里拉也像马克那样轰然崩塌，而哪个聪明人预先卖出了，那他便可坐享一份须以百分之几千来计算的利润。而马布尔先生每日公务中在电话里交谈的那些经纪人，似乎个个都认为这大有可能。国民县银行（总行）外汇部的全体行员也对此深信不疑。其中几人已经在经纪人营业所里那些朋友行员的协助下，靠谨慎投机赚过几笔蝇头小利。他们预先卖出小小一笔，随即仓促脱手，之后眼看法郎照跌不误，又痛骂自己胆子太小。马布尔先生看在眼里，心痒难耐。有两回他几乎要冒险出手，但每一回，那副始终退居幕后、随传随到的敏锐判断力都把他拽住了。总有什么地方，隐隐靠不住。

===Original===
And then one morning it happened. Chance flung a fortune within Mr. Marble’s reach, and only asked from him in return the effort to grasp it.
It was ten o’clock in the morning. Mr. Marble was at his desk farthest from the entrance to the room, and close beside the partitioned cubbyhole that housed the superior majesty of Henderson, chief of the department. In front of Mr. Marble lay the opened letters sent across by the correspondence department, and he was casually glancing through them to see that there was nothing that the department could not cope with without troubling Mr. Henderson, or even higher authority still. They were all just ordinary routine letters; notifications mainly, from the various continental branches, of credits given and of drafts honoured. Nothing of any import; the staff of the department could handle them nearly all. Four of them he picked out that he would have to answer; two must go in to Henderson. The last letter was the most ordinary of them all. It was the biweekly letter from the Paris branch confirming the actions reported already by cable and telephone. Mr. Marble looked through it. Still there was nothing of any interest. For a wonder the Paris staff had made no mistake in decoding cables. Everything had been done as ordered, and no hasty cable would have to be sent countermanding some ridiculous blunder. No forgeries, no failures. Yet Mr. Marble read the letter to the end, because the man who had written it was known to him personally; it was Collins, who had worked under him in that very department for a couple of years before being sent out to Paris. He was a voluble fellow﻿—“chatty,” Mr. Marble described him to himself﻿—and his volubility was noticeable even in this official correspondence, for at the end of the letter he had inserted a paragraph that was quite unnecessary by all rules of routine. It was quite brief for Collins, and merely said that more steadiness might be noticed in the French franc in future, as it was rumoured that the Government were taking the matter in hand, and might even resort to “pegging” on the London exchange.
Mr. Marble laid the letter down and gazed through the dusty window at the uninviting view of other dusty windows across the ventilation shaft. There might be something in it. The franc was a point higher this morning than at the close of yesterday. So much the trained foreign clerk’s brain told him﻿—Mr. Marble could at two minutes’ notice give the rate of exchange on anywhere at any time within the last two years﻿—but Mr. Marble was capable, strange though it may seem, of doing more than that if sufficiently stimulated. If the Republic took a hand in restoring its foreign credit, it would do more than merely keep it steady. Mr. Marble suddenly recollected a nebulous idea he had framed some time ago as to the best means that would bring this about. He turned this idea over in his mind. If they were to do *that*, the franc would recover like a skyrocket. It would easily go to sixty; it might even reach fifty, or perhaps even forty. It would never be better than forty, decided Mr. Marble, weighing the chances with an acuteness of perception that would have astonished him had he only thought about it. Most likely it would be about sixty.
===Chinese===
后来，一天上午，事情终于来了。机缘把一笔巨富甩到了马布尔先生伸手可及的地方，作为回报，只要求他出一把抓住它的力气。
时间是上午十点。马布尔先生坐在离房门最远的那张办公桌前，紧挨着一间隔板隔出的小格子间，部门主任亨德森那高人一等的威仪便端坐其中。马布尔先生面前摊着文书科送来的、已拆开的信件，他信手翻阅，看看其中有没有什么事，是部门不打扰亨德森先生、乃至更高层就应付不了的。全都是普普通通的例行公函；主要是欧洲大陆各分行发来的通知，报告放出的信贷与兑付的汇票。无一紧要；部门职员几乎全能处理。他从中挑出四封需要作复的；两封必须呈送亨德森。最后一封信是全部信里最寻常的一封。那是巴黎分行每半月一封的公函，确认先前已用电报和电话呈报的各项业务。马布尔先生通读一遍，依旧没有什么引人注意的东西。难得的是，巴黎的职员这回破译电报没有出一处错。事事均奉命而行，不必再拍急电去撤销什么荒唐的纰漏。没有伪造，没有闪失。然而马布尔先生还是把这封信读到了底，因为写信人他私下认识；是科林斯（Collins），从前就在这个部门、在他手下干过两年，后来才外放巴黎。此人是个话篓子——照马布尔先生自己心里的说法，“话多得腻人”——而他的饶舌连这等公务函件里都显露无遗：信的末尾，他插了一段按一切公文常规都纯属多余的话。就科林斯而言，这段话写得已算简短，大意不过是：今后法国法郎或许可望稳定一些，因为有传言说政府即将过问此事，甚至可能不惜在伦敦外汇市场上实行“钉住”（pegging）。
马布尔先生把信放下，透过积尘的窗玻璃，凝望通风井对面那一排同样积尘的窗户，一片乏善可陈的景致。这信里也许有点名堂。法郎今晨比昨日收盘高了一个点。受过科班训练的外汇行员头脑告诉他的，便是这么多——随便哪个地方、过去两年内任一时刻的汇价，马布尔先生都能在两分钟内随口报出——然而说来奇怪，只要刺激足够，马布尔先生所能做的远不止于此。倘若共和国出面恢复本国的对外信用，那它要做的绝不只是维持汇率安稳而已。马布尔先生猛然记起自己先前酝酿过的一个模糊念头——关于促成此事的最佳途径。他在心里把这念头翻来覆去掂量。倘若他们当真*那么*干，法郎便会像冲天炮一样蹿起来。轻而易举就能涨到六十；说不定到五十，兴许还到四十。绝好不过四十，马布尔先生断定，一面权衡着种种几率，那份眼光之敏锐，只要他自己细想一下，准会大吃一惊。最可能的，是停在六十上下。

===Original===
“What’s Paris now, Netley?” he asked a passing subordinate.
“One-nineteen, one-seventeen,” replied Netley over his shoulder as he hurried on hoping that Mr. Marble would notice that he deliberately did not say “sir.”
Two points rise again, thought Mr. Marble. There might be some truth in the wild theories that had been flashing through his brain. On the other hand, it might be only one of the periodic and temporary recoveries that were usual. The price might sag at any minute again. If Mr. Marble were to buy, he might find that he had bought at the beginning of a fall instead of the beginning of a rise. And if he were to buy on a margin﻿—Mr. Marble had already in his mind a scheme to enable him to do so﻿—then the inevitable ten point fall would deprive him of ninety percent of his capital. But then﻿—Mr. Marble’s silly heart was already bumping heavily against his ribs﻿—the sort of rise he thought possible would bring him in nearly a hundred times the amount invested. Mr. Marble managed to keep his brain deadly clear despite the thumping of his heart, just as he had done that memorable evening some months ago. It was a chance. Mr. Marble’s brain sorted out all the tiny indications it had collected and stored up unbidden for weeks past. It was more than a chance. It was a certainty, decided that clear brain.
Yet Mr. Marble felt the flush of blood in his cheeks, and his heart beat unbearably as he rose from his seat and walked out of the office on what was to be one of the momentous occasions of his life. Even his gait was a little unsteady so great was the strain upon him, and the younger clerks whom he had to pass on the way to the door nudged each other merrily as he went by.
“Old Marble going out for his usual,” they said. “Bit early even for him. Must have been properly soused last night.”
===Chinese===
“巴黎现在什么价，内特利（Netley）？”他问一个路过的下属。
“一一九，一一七。”内特利头也不回地答道，一面匆匆走开，心里巴望着马布尔先生会察觉，他是故意没称“先生”的。
又涨了两个点，马布尔先生想。方才在他脑际电光石火般闪过的那些狂想，或许并非全无道理。可话说回来，这也可能只是行情惯常的周期性短暂回涨，价格随时会再塌下去。倘若马布尔先生此刻买进，他也许会发现，自己买在了跌势的开头，而不是涨势的开头。倘若他是凭保证金买进——马布尔先生心里已经揣着一个使他得以如此行事的点子——那么不可避免的十个点跌幅，就会夺走他九成的本金。可是——马布尔先生那颗愚蠢的心已在他肋骨上重重捶击——他认为可能的那种涨幅，会给他带来将近本金一百倍的进项。马布尔先生不顾心脏擂动，硬是使头脑保持着极度的清明，一如几个月前那个难忘的夜晚他所做到的。这是个机会。马布尔先生的头脑把几个星期以来不请自来、自行积攒的种种细微信号一一理清。这不止是机会。这是定局，那颗清明的头脑断定。
然而马布尔先生仍觉出热血涌上面颊，心口跳得难以忍受。他从座位上起身，走出办公室，去赴他一生中最重大的时刻之一。重压之下，他连步子都有些不稳，往门口去的一路上，年轻的行员们在他经过时喜滋滋地彼此用胳膊肘乱捣。
“老马布尔又出去照例来一杯了，”他们说。“就算是他，这也早了点。昨晚准是灌了个十足十。”

===Original===
There was a public-house round the corner, and it was there that Mr. Marble stayed his steps, just as, be it admitted, he had often done before. The girl at the bar knew him, and the double Scotch was ready for him before even he was within reach of it. But Mr. Marble did not, as was usual, drain it off and demand another. Instead, he withdrew, glass in hand, to a bench behind a table, and sat waiting, waiting inexorably, while his heart beat in his finger tips so that his hands shook. It was only just opening time, and there was a steady stream of entrants, stockbrokers and clerks, street-betting agents, all the curious mixture that is to be found in a public-house fifty yards from Threadneedle Street on a weekday morning.
Once or twice men he knew entered, and nodded, but Mr. Marble was not cordial. His return nod carried with it no invitation to share his bench and sit at his table with him. Most probably none of them would have accepted such an invitation anyway, unless it were unavoidable. Mr. Marble continued to stare at the swing doors.
And then Mr. Marble’s heart gave a convulsive throb. He suddenly felt sick with fear, fear of the future. At last his scheme was presented to him in the concrete, instead of the comfortably abstract. For a wild moment he thought of flight, of abandoning the whole business. He could, he knew. He might struggle along for years without the aid that improbable success in this venture would bring him. But Mr. Marble thrust the temptation on one side, and proceeded with the business with bitter determination. He beckoned to Saunders, the man whose entrance had caused this last internal commotion.
===Chinese===
拐角处有一家酒馆，马布尔先生便在那里停住了脚步——不瞒人说，从前他也常这样。柜台后面的姑娘认得他，人还没走到跟前，那杯双份苏格兰威士忌已替他备好了。可这一回，马布尔先生没有照惯例一饮而尽再叫一杯，而是端着酒杯退到一张桌子后面的长凳上，坐着等待，铁了心地等待，心跳直撞指尖，震得双手发颤。此时刚过开门营业的时刻，客人络绎不绝地进来：证券经纪人、公司职员、街头揽赌的掮客——工作日上午，离针线街（Threadneedle Street）五十码的一家酒馆里，见得到的正是这般光怪陆离的杂拌儿。
有一两回，熟识的人走进来，朝他点头，马布尔先生却并不热络。他回敬的那一点头，绝无请人来同坐一条长凳、共用一张桌子的意思。不过十有八九，那些人本来也不会领这份情，除非推辞不掉。马布尔先生只管盯着那扇弹簧门。
就在这时，马布尔先生的心猛地痉挛了一下。他突然感到一阵恐惧，直叫人发呕——对未来的恐惧。他的计划终于以具体的形态摆到了他面前，不再是舒舒服服的抽象之物。有那么疯狂的一瞬，他想到逃走，想到把整桩事弃之不顾。他办得到，这他知道。没有这桩冒险万一成功所能带来的那笔救助，他也可以再苦熬许多年。但马布尔先生把诱惑推到一旁，怀着狠厉的决心着手办事。他朝桑德斯招了招手——正是此人进门，方才搅起了他心里这最后一场骚乱。

===Original===
Saunders had his drink in his hand; he had greeted those men at the bar whom he knew, and was glancing round the room to make sure that he had missed no one.
He was a plump man of middle height, rosy and prosperous looking. He knew Marble slightly; that is to say, he had talked to him a dozen times in that public-house. He was acquainted with the fact that Marble worked for the National County Bank, which he himself employed, and that was all. Naturally he was a little puzzled at Marble’s beckoning to him, but Saunders took care to be friendly to everyone who spoke to him, seeing that his livelihood depended on the goodwill of the public. Saunders was a bookmaker, in a tiny office five stories up near Old Broad Street, conducting his business almost entirely by telephone and by the men who waited about at lunchtime near the corners of the streets.
Glass in hand, Saunders came across to the table just as Marble had done before him, and almost without knowing it he sat down opposite him. There was that in Marble’s manner which seemed to make it inevitable that he should sit down.
“Well,” said Saunders cheerily, “how’s things?”
===Chinese===
桑德斯手里已经端上了酒；他同吧台边认识的人一一打过招呼，正环顾室内，确信没有漏掉谁。
他是个中等个子、白白胖胖的男人，面色红润，一副兴旺发达之相。他同马布尔只有几分头之交；也就是说，他在这家酒馆里同马布尔搭过十来次话。他知道马布尔在国民县银行做事——那家银行他自己也有往来——所知尽于此。马布尔朝他招手，他自然有点摸不着头脑，但桑德斯对每一个同他搭话的人都刻意热络，须知他的生计全系于公众的好感。桑德斯是个赛马赌注登记员，在老布罗德街附近一间五层楼上的小办公室里开业，生意几乎全凭电话，外加午间守候在街角附近兜转的那班人。
桑德斯端着酒杯，朝那张桌子走去，一如先前的马布尔，而且几乎不知不觉地在他对面坐了下来。马布尔的神态里自有一股东西，使他这一坐仿佛在所必然。
“哟，”桑德斯喜眉笑眼地说，“近况如何？”

===Original===
“Not too bad,” said Marble. “Got ten minutes to spare?”
Saunders supposed he had, a little ruefully, though, for he had at first imagined that this was merely evidence of a desire to open a credit account with him, and, since Marble’s manner did not indicate this, he came to the other conclusion, that he was about to hear a hard luck story. As far as Saunders’ experience went, men between themselves did nothing but bet or borrow.
“You do bank with the National County, don’t you?” began Marble.
“You’re right.”
“And you know I work there?”
“Right again. What’s the matter? Firm going bust? My account overdrawn?” Saunders was a witty fellow; there never was the slightest chance of the National County suspending payment, and Saunders’ account was never lower than four hundred pounds. But Marble hardly smiled. Instead, he turned his bleak eyes full upon Saunders’ brown ones.
“No,” he said; then slowly, “I want to do a deal, and I must have the help of a customer of the bank. You’ll do, or, of course, if you don’t want to come in, I’ll have to get someone else.”
===Chinese===
“还过得去，”马布尔说。“能腾出十分钟吗？”
桑德斯心想，腾是腾得出，只是有点无奈：起初他还当这是对方想在他那儿开个赊账户头的表示，可马布尔的神情又不像，于是他得出另一个结论——自己马上要听人倒一肚子苦水了。照桑德斯的经验，男人凑在一处，无非干两件事：不是打赌，就是借钱。
“你的钱是存在国民县银行的，对吧？”马布尔开了腔。
“说得对。”
“你也知道我在那儿做事？”
“这也没说错。怎么，出事了？银行要倒了？我的户头透支了？”桑德斯是个爱耍嘴皮的人；国民县银行绝无分毫停业之虞，桑德斯的户头也从没低于四百英镑。可马布尔几乎没有笑。他只把自己那双阴冷的眼睛，直直地对上了桑德斯的棕色眼睛。
“不是，”他说；随后缓缓地说：“我要做一笔交易，得有一位银行客户帮忙。你就行。当然，你若不想入伙，我只得另找别人。”

===Original===
“I should think you were right again,” said Saunders, but he was not feeling quite so full of levity now. His puzzled mind was searching for Marble’s motives. Either this seedy old boy was balmy or else he was on some illegal lay. In either case Saunders was not having any. Saunders was a law-abiding man, save in the matter of inciting others to street-betting. And yet﻿—and yet﻿—
“Do you want to hear about it?” said Marble, grimly. He had himself as well in hand as when he had offered Medland that glass of whisky, but the effort was frightful. His confidence overbore Saunders’ easy scepticism.
“Right-ho. I don’t say I’ll buy it, though,” added Saunders hastily.
“No. But I want your word that you’ll keep quiet if you don’t.”
Saunders agreed. And the word of a bookmaker is accepted all over England.
“I’ve got some information. It might mean a lot of money if it were used properly.”
===Chinese===
“我看这回你又没说错，”桑德斯说，但他此刻已不那么满心轻佻了。他那颗犯疑的脑子正在搜寻马布尔的动机。这位寒碜的老兄要么脑子有毛病，要么就在干非法的营生。无论哪种情形，桑德斯都不奉陪。桑德斯是个奉公守法的人——唯独在怂恿他人街头聚赌一节上例外。然而——然而——
“你想不想听？”马布尔冷冷地说。他把自制力攥得同递给梅德兰那杯威士忌时一样紧，只是这份苦刑可怕至极。他的自信压倒了桑德斯随口而出的怀疑。
“行啊。不过我没说我一定信。”桑德斯赶紧补上一句。
“不必信。但你要是不信，我要你一句话：一字不外传。”
桑德斯应允了。而赛马赌注登记员的一句话，在整个英格兰都是作数的。
“我手头有个消息。运用得当，可能意味着一大笔钱。”

===Original===
“Racin’ information?” There was a suspicion of a sneer in Saunders’ voice: he obtained half his income from people with racing information.
“No. Foreign exchange.”
“Don’t hardly know what that is.”
“No,” said Marble. “Lots of people don’t.”
“Oh, I know a bit,” said Saunders, anxious to justify himself. “I know about how the mark has fallen to half nothing and﻿—and﻿—all that sort of thing, you know.”
Somehow the moral tables had turned during those few minutes. Undoubtedly Marble was now in the ascendant. Largely, of course, this change was due to the fact that the conversation was about a subject of which he knew much and the other nothing. But on the other hand it must be admitted that the vague thing known as “Personality” was affecting the case, too. Marble was putting out all his strength to influence Saunders unobtrusively, and he was succeeding. Men can do much when they have to.
===Chinese===
“赛马滴消息？”桑德斯的语声里含着一丝讥诮：他半数的进项就来自那些揣着赛马消息的人。
“不是。是外汇。”
“外汇嘛，那是个啥玩意儿，咱就不大知道了。”
“可不是，”马布尔说，“不知道的人多着呢。”
“哦，多少懂一点，”桑德斯急着想给自己找补，“我知道那个马克，跌得差不离儿白送了，还有——还有——那一档子事，你晓得。”
不知怎的，短短几分钟里，两人间的高低之势已倒转过来。无疑，如今占了上风的是马布尔。这变化自然大半因为，话题成了一门他精通而对方一无所知的学问。但另一方面也得承认，那个叫作“人格”的玄乎东西也在其中起作用。马布尔正使出全部力气，不着痕迹地去影响桑德斯，而且正在得手。人到了非做不可的当口，能耐大得很。

===Original===
“Well,” said Marble, bleak eyes set like stones, “the franc is going to rise, and now is the time to buy.”
The secret was out now. Saunders could betray him now at leisure. But Marble, never taking his eyes off Saunders’, was sure he would not.
“I don’t say you’re not right,” said Saunders, “but what’s the little game? Where do I come in? And where do you come in?”
“It’s like this,” said Marble, showing his whole hand, so sure was he. “It isn’t much good buying francs outright. I could do that myself next door. But you don’t stand to make much that way. This time you might make a hundred percent, but of course that’s not good enough.”
“Of course not,” said Saunders submissively.
===Chinese===
“是这样，”马布尔说，那双阴冷的眼睛定得像两块石头，“法郎要涨，眼下正是买进的时机。”
底牌如今已经亮出。桑德斯尽可以从从容容去出卖他。但马布尔一眼不眨地盯住桑德斯的眼睛，笃定他不会。
“我没说你不对，”桑德斯说，“可这葫芦里卖的什么药？我算一份什么？你又算一份什么？”
“是这么回事，”马布尔说着摊出了整副底牌，他有这般把握。“现钱直购法郎没什么大意思。那种事我在隔壁自己就能办。可那么干赚头有限。这一回你或许能赚一倍，不过那当然不够劲。”
“当然不够。”桑德斯服服帖帖地说。

===Original===
“The best way to do it is to make a ‘forward operation’ of it. That means you must put up ten percent.”
“On a margin,” put in Saunders, proud of this little bit of argot picked up during much frequenting of City public-houses.
“Exactly. On a margin. That means that a five percent rise in the franc gives you fifty percent profit. As I said, it might be a hundred percent rise this time. That would be a thousand percent profit﻿—ten to one, in other words,” added Marble, countering Saunders’ effort.
Saunders only dimly understood.
“But I don’t see now why you’re telling me all this,” he said. “Why don’t you get along and do it? Why tell anyone else at all?”
“Because I’m not allowed to put a forward operation through for myself in the Bank. You have to have some legitimate reason for wanting to.”
===Chinese===
“最好的办法，是把它做成一桩『远期交易』。那就是说，你只消缴一成。”
“缴保证金，”桑德斯插嘴道，对这句行话暗自得意——那是他在金融城各家酒馆里泡出来的。
“一点不错。缴保证金。这就是说，法郎涨百分之五，你就赚百分之五十。我方才说了，这一回可能涨上一倍。那便是十倍的利——换句话说，一赔十。”马布尔补了一句，把桑德斯的招架顶了回去。
桑德斯只懂得个模模糊糊。
“可我倒不明白了，你干吗把这些一股脑儿告诉我？”他说。“你自个儿去办不就得了？干吗还要告诉旁人？”
“因为银行不许我替自己经手远期交易。要办这种交易，总得有个说得过去的正经理由。”

===Original===
“And what reason would *I* have?” Saunders was being rapidly drawn into the net.
“Oh, that’s easy. You can easily have some business in France, can’t you? Don’t you ever bet on French races?”
“Of course I do, sometimes.”
“And don’t you ever send money to France?”
“Well, I have done, once or twice.”
“Right, then. If you were to tell the Bank that you wanted to buy francs they’d believe you all right. And they love you in the Bank, anyhow. You keep a big current account, and they love anyone who does that.”
===Chinese===
“那*我*又能有什么理由？”桑德斯正被飞快地拖进网里。
“哦，那好办。你在法国随便有点生意往来不就有了？你没押过法国的赛马吗？”
“当然押过，有时。”
“那你不也往法国汇过钱？”
“嗯，汇过，一两回。”
“这就成了。你去对银行说想买法郎，他们保管信。再说，银行里的人本来就喜欢你。你的往来户头数额不小，银行最疼这样的主顾。”

===Original===
Saunders was still struggling against the mesmeric influence that was beginning to overpower him.
“Tell me some more about this ‘forward operation’ stunt,” he pleaded, wavering, conscious that in a minute he would have to decide one way or the other, conscious that he would most probably fall in with Marble’s plans, and conscious, too, that he did not really want to. “Tell me what I should have to do.”
Marble explained carefully, drilling it into his man with meticulous care. Then he threw his last bait. He showed how, if a man were to take his profit when it amounted to a mere five times his stake, and had the hardihood to fling both profits and stake into the business again, by the time the currency purchased stood at twice the figure it stood at at the start, the profit would not be ten, but thirty-five units.
Saunders scratched his head wildly.
“Here, what are you drinking?” he asked, signalling agitatedly to the barman, and then leaning forward again to go over the details once more. Marble’s cold judgement had chosen his man well. A bookmaker earns his living by other people’s gambling, but even with this practical example always before him there is no one on earth so ready to gamble﻿—in anything not pertaining to the turf.
Then Saunders made a last despairing effort to writhe out of the business. He said that, after all, he was unacquainted with Marble.
===Chinese===
桑德斯还在抗拒那股正开始压倒他的催眠般的力量。
“再给我讲讲这个『远期交易』的门道吧，”他央求道，摇摆不定，心里明白再过一分钟，自己就得或进或退做出决断，明白自己十有八九会依着马布尔的计划办，也明白自己实在并不情愿。“告诉我，我都得干些什么。”
马布尔细细讲解，一丝不苟地把这门道往他物色的这个人脑袋里凿。随后他抛出最后一枚钓饵。他演算给他看：一个人若在利润才到本金五倍时便见好就收，又有胆量把本金利润一并再掷回这桩买卖里，那么待到所购货币的价格涨到起手价的两倍时，所得便不止十份，而是三十五份。
桑德斯没命地挠起头来。
“哎，你喝的什么？”他问，一边心浮气躁地朝酒保打手势，随即又俯身向前，把细节从头再过了一遍。马布尔那冷静的判断力，人挑得着实高明。赛马赌注登记员靠别人的赌瘾谋生，可即便这一活教材终日在眼皮底下，天底下再找不出比他更好赌的人了——只要赌的不沾赛马的边。
接着桑德斯做了最后一次绝望的努力，想从这桩买卖里挣脱出去。他说，毕竟，他同马布尔素昧平生。

===Original===
“How do I know it’s straight?” he asked pitifully.
“It can’t be very well anything else, can it?” replied Marble, and the condescension in his tone was an added spur to Saunders. “*I* won’t be able to pinch your money, will I? It will all be in your account, won’t it? If it isn’t straight I can’t make anything out of it.”
Saunders had realized this as soon as the words had left his mouth, and he apologized. Mr. Marble, hope boiling in his veins, was very gracious.
“Well, what is it you want out of it?” asked the wretched Saunders.
“Ten percent of what you make,” said Marble uncompromisingly, “and, of course, I’m going to have a bit on, too.”
“How much?”
===Chinese===
“我怎么知道这买卖干净？”他可怜巴巴地问。
“它还能不干净到哪儿去，不是吗？”马布尔回答，语气里那份屈尊俯就，反倒又给了桑德斯一鞭。“*我*又偷不着你的钱，对吧？钱全在你的户头里，不是吗？这买卖若不干净，我什么好处也捞不着。”
这话一出口，桑德斯自己也想明白了，连忙赔不是。马布尔先生血脉里希望滚滚沸腾，态度极其雍容大度。
“那么好吧，你到底想从中得什么？”狼狈不堪的桑德斯问。
“你赚的抽一成，”马布尔寸步不让地说，“当然，我自己也要押上一注。”
“押多少？”

===Original===
“Sixty quid.” Mr. Marble produced a roll of five-pound notes﻿—the last of those he had been so careful about changing. He was throwing discretion to the winds now. If ever the notes were traced to him, it would be his own fault, but there was no time for complicated manoeuvres to change them.
Saunders took them half involuntarily.
“In fact,” said Mr. Marble, “I should like to have more than that on. Only I haven’t got it with me. I could raise it by tomorrow. But tomorrow will be too late.”
With those baleful eyes upon him, and the reassuring feel of those five-pound notes in his hand, Saunders could do nothing except make the inevitable offer.
“Thanks,” said Marble. “Look here, I tell you what we’ll do. Put four hundred in. Two hundred of that will be yours. There’s another sixty. Then you lend me the odd one-forty. That makes us just even.”
Saunders agreed helplessly.
===Chinese===
“六十英镑。”马布尔先生掏出一卷五英镑的钞票——那是他一直小心翼翼兑散着花的最后一批。此刻他已把谨慎抛到了九霄云外。这些钞票他日若被追查到他头上，只能怪他自己，可已没有工夫再绕弯子把它们兑开了。
桑德斯半是不由自主地接了过来。
“其实，”马布尔先生说，“我倒想多押一些。只是没随身带着。到明天我凑得出来。可明天就太迟了。”
那双含着凶光的眼睛盯着他，掌心里又攥着那沓叫人心里踏实的五镑钞票，桑德斯除了做出那个注定要做的提议之外，什么也干不了。
“多谢，”马布尔说。“听我说，咱们这么办。你投四百进去。其中两百算你的。再有我这六十。剩下的那一百四，你借给我。这样咱们俩正好扯平。”
桑德斯无可奈何地应承了下来。

===Original===
“Time’s getting on,” said Mr. Marble, with a glance at the clock. “We’d better get a move on. I can tell you what to do all over again as we walk back.”
As though in a dream Saunders rose from the table and followed him out. The stimulus of the fresh air outside revived him sufficiently to remember to ask Marble how it was he was so certain that the franc was going to rise.
“I know all right,” said Marble casually. He could afford to be casual, so sure was he of himself, and above all, so sure was he of Saunders.
And Saunders weakly yielded to the man with the superior knowledge. He would have hooted with derision at a man who proposed to back a stray acquaintance’s favourite horse to the tune of two hundred pounds; he would have rolled on the ground with mirth if he heard that the same man was going to risk another one hundred and forty by backing that horse for his friend; but this was not horse-racing, about which he was thoroughly well informed. It was business﻿—Big Business﻿—and he was awed and submissive.
Marble ended his instructions just as they reached the main door of the National County Bank.
“Go in there and say you want to buy francs as a forward operation. They’ll send you along to my department, so don’t worry. I’ll be there. I may even do the business for you. But I expect it’ll be Henderson that you see. Oh, yes, and don’t forget, whatever you do, to ring me up two or three times today and tomorrow. Get through to Foreign Exchange and then ask for me. It doesn’t matter what you say. Just say﻿—what is that you always say when you see anyone in the bar?﻿—‘Hallo, old bean, how’s things?’ Keep it up for a bit; say ‘Doodle-oodle-oodle’ if you can’t think of anything else. That’s just to give me authority to move the account about when it’s necessary. Got it all? All right, then. Goodbye.”
===Chinese===
“时候不早了，”马布尔先生看了一眼钟说。“咱们最好抓紧。走回去的路上，我可以把该怎么办理再从头教一遍。”
桑德斯如同身在梦中，从桌边起身，随他走了出去。门外的新鲜空气一激，他总算缓过点儿神，想起要问问马布尔，凭什么这样笃定法郎必涨。
“我就是知道。”马布尔轻描淡写地说。他有本钱这样轻描淡写——他对自己如此有把握，尤其对桑德斯，更是十拿九稳。
于是桑德斯软软地屈服于这个学问高出自己一头的人。若是听说有谁打算押两百英镑，去赌一个萍水相逢者的心爱马匹，他准会报以一片嗤笑；若是听说此人还要再拿一百四十英镑替朋友押那匹马，他更要笑得满地打滚；可这不是赛马——赛马这一行他烂熟于心。这是生意——大生意——于是他肃然生畏，俯首帖耳。
两人走到国民县银行正门前，马布尔的吩咐恰好说完。
“进去就说，你要做远期交易买法郎。他们会把你送到我们部门，所以不用发愁。我会在那儿。这单生意说不定就由我亲手替你办。不过我料想你见到的是亨德森。哦，对了，千万别忘了，今天明天，无论如何要给我打上两三个电话。接通外汇部，然后指名找我。说什么都无所谓。就说——你们在酒馆里见了人常说的那句是怎么说的？——『喂，老伙计，近况如何？』多念叨几句；实在想不出词儿，就来一句『嘟噜—嘟噜—嘟噜』。这不过是要给我个由头，必要时好把那个户头挪动腾转。都记下了？好，就这样。回见。”

===Original===
Mr. Saunders, dazed and mazed, walked weakly into the National County Bank. Mr. Marble walked on to the side entrance where dwelt his own among other departments. The sweat was running off him in streams; for a brief space he had been a master of men; he had swayed a hardheaded man into doing something totally unexpected; he had gambled with fate and he had won; for a while he had known the wild exultation of success. He had done something that he would certainly not have done without the urgent impulse due to﻿—a slight indiscretion one stormy night some months ago, but reaction closed upon him with terrible swiftness. His steps dragged as he came into the department of Foreign Exchange. He felt, and he looked, inexpressibly weary. The junior clerks nudged each other again as he walked by.
“Old Marble’s had as much as he can carry already. He’ll be getting the sack one of these days, just see if he doesn’t.”
And Marble, tired to death, weary with fear, worn out by the thumping of his heart, crept brokenly to his desk and buried his face in his hands.
===Chinese===
桑德斯先生晕头转向、如堕五里雾中，脚下发虚地走进了国民县银行。马布尔先生则继续走向侧门，他自己的部门同别的部门都在那头。汗水正顺着他的身子滚滚而下。短短片刻，他曾做过支配他人的人；他曾摆布一个精明的人，去干一件全然出于意外的事；他曾同命运赌了一局，而且赢了；他曾一度尝到成功那种狂野的欢腾。他做成了一件事——若非几个月前一个雷雨夜里一桩小小失检所催出的急迫冲动，他是万万不会去做的——然而颓唐的反作用，以可怕的速度将他罩住了。走进外汇部时，他的脚步拖沓。他觉得自己，看上去也是，疲惫得难以言表。他走过时，年轻的行员们又彼此用胳膊肘乱捣。
“老马布尔又喝够量了。早晚有一天要吃炒鱿鱼，不信就走着瞧。”
而马布尔，累得要死，怕得发慌，心跳得筋疲力尽，颓然挪到自己的办公桌前，把脸埋进了双手。

## V

===Original===
Mr. Marble was paying. He was paying by the feeling of weary misery from which he suffered as he walked that day across London Bridge, as he stood exhausted in the train, and in the bus which brought him from the station, and as he sat in the back room at 53 Malcolm Road.
It was a new habit this of sitting in the tiny “drawing-room” instead of the dining-room. In the drawing-room the light was bad and the furnishing even more dreary than in the dining-room, while the fact that it was in the dining-room that during the winter they had their fire had habituated the family to passing all their time there. But Mr. Marble now sat in the drawing-room. He did little enough there. He read, it is true, in the books that he now chose regularly from the Free Library﻿—crime books, even the interminable *Lombroso*﻿—but he only read at intervals. Quite half the time he spent in looking out of the window across the barren flowerbed. That way he felt more comfortable. He did not have to worry then in case some stray dog from one of the neighbouring houses were there. Mr. Marble had read how dogs are employed to find truffles in Perigord, and he was afraid.
There were in addition various children from neighbouring houses who had been known to climb into the garden after balls which they had knocked over. They had left off doing that now. Once upon a time Mr. Marble had not shown any active objection, but two or three times lately he had caught them at it, and had rushed out in blind, wordless fury. The children had seen his face as he mouthed at them, and that experience was enough for them. Children know these things more clearly than do their elders, and they never came into Mr. Marble’s garden again. The neighbours were at a loss to understand Mr. Marble’s jealous guardianship of his garden. As they said, he never grew anything there. Gardening was hardly likely to be a hobby of a man with Mr. Marble’s temperament, and the garden of Number 53 had always been in its barren weediness an unpleasing contrast to those near it.
===Chinese===
马布尔先生正在偿付。那天，他走过伦敦桥，在火车里疲惫地站着，坐上从车站开来的公共汽车，又坐进马尔科姆路53号的后屋——自始至终，他都被一种倦怠的愁苦折磨着：这，便是他的偿付。
不坐饭厅，改坐那间狭小的“客厅”，这是桩新养成的习惯。客厅里光线不好，陈设比饭厅还要凄凉，而一家人冬天生火一向生在饭厅，早就过惯了把全部时间都消磨在那儿的日子。可如今马布尔先生却坐进了客厅。他在那儿其实没有多少事可做。不错，他也读书——如今他定期从免费图书馆（Free Library）选些犯罪的书来读，连那部没完没了的《龙勃罗梭》（*Lombroso*）也不放过——不过只是断断续续地读。足足有一半工夫，他都花在凭窗眺望那片光秃秃的花坛上。这样他觉得自在些。那样他就用不着担心，怕邻家哪条野狗跑到园子里去。马布尔先生读到过，在佩里戈尔（Perigord），人们就是用狗来寻松露的，所以他害怕。
此外，邻家还有几个孩子，往常球踢过了墙，就会爬进园子去捡。如今他们不再这么干了。从前马布尔先生对此并没露出多少激烈的反对，可近来他有两三次把他们撞了个正着，无名火起，一句话也骂不出，人已经冲了出去。孩子们见过他冲他们咬牙啐骂的那张脸，领教这一回就够了。这种事情，孩子们比大人看得更透，从此再没人踏进马布尔先生的园子。邻居们实在纳闷，马布尔先生怎么把园子守得这么紧。照他们的说法，他在那园子里从来什么也不种。以马布尔先生的脾性，园艺怎么看也成不了他的爱好，而53号的园子也一向荒得满地是草，跟左近各家的园子一比，总是一处碍眼的败景。

===Original===
It gave at least some cause for a feeling of superiority to the neighbours. They all thought Mr. Marble unbearably snobbish. He sent his children to secondary schools﻿—on scholarships, it is true, and after some years at public elementary schools﻿—while their children began to work for their livings at the age of fourteen, and he wore a bowler hat, while the neighbouring menfolk wore caps. They none of them liked Mr. Marble, although they all had a soft corner in their hearts for Mrs. Marble. “Poor thing, he treats her like the dirt beneath his feet, he does.”
It was a comforting feeling that this monster suffered from the same troubles as they did, and was at times unable to pay his rent, even as they, for so the collecting clerk told them.
Mr. Marble spent the evening sitting in the drawing-room of 53 Malcolm Road; on his knee was the last of the Free Library books on crime. It was very interesting﻿—a *Handbook to Medical Jurisprudence*. Until he had begun it, Mr. Marble did not know what Medical Jurisprudence was, but he found it more and more absorbing. The periods spent in gazing out of the window grew shorter and shorter as he read all about inquests, and the methods for discovering whether a dead body found in the water had been put there after death or not, and the legal forms necessary for certifying a person insane. Then he passed on to the section of Toxicology. He read all about the common domestic poisons, spirits of salt, lead acetate, carbolic acid; from these the book proceeded to the rarer poisons. The first ones mentioned, perhaps given pride of place because of infinitely superior deadliness, were hydrocyanic acid and the cyanides. The comments on the cyanides were particularly interesting:
===Chinese===
这至少给了邻居们几分优越感。他们个个觉得马布尔先生势利得叫人受不了。他送孩子上中学——虽说靠的是奖学金，而且是在公立小学念过几年之后——他们自己的孩子却十四岁就得出去挣钱谋生；他戴圆顶硬礼帽，街坊的男人戴的却全是便帽。他们没有一个喜欢马布尔先生，不过对马布尔太太，人人心里都替她留着一角软处。“可怜的人儿，他拿她当他脚底下的泥踩，可不就是。”
一想到这个恶人也受着同他们一样的难处，有时竟连房租都付不出——同他们一模一样——大家心里就熨帖了；这话可是收租员亲口对他们说的。
这天晚上，马布尔先生就坐在马尔科姆路53号的客厅里消磨时光；膝头摊着从免费图书馆借来的最后一本犯罪学的书。这书有趣极了——一部《法医学手册》（*Handbook to Medical Jurisprudence*）。没动手读之前，马布尔先生压根不知道法医学是什么，可他越读越放不下。读着读着，凭窗望园子的工夫就越来越短了：他读到验尸审讯的种种，读到如何查明水里捞起的尸体究竟是生前落水还是死后被抛，读到宣布一个人精神失常须办哪些法律手续。接着他读到了毒物学部分。家常毒物他读了个遍：盐精（spirits of salt）、醋酸铅（lead acetate）、石炭酸（carbolic acid）；由这几样，书又讲到更罕见的毒物。最先列出的几种——许是致命的效力远胜群侪，才占了头筹——正是氢氰酸和氰化物。讲氰化物的那些文字尤其有趣：

===Original===
> “Death is practically instantaneous. The patient utters a loud cry and falls heavily. There may be some foam at the lips, and after death the body often retains the appearance of life, the cheeks being red and the expression unaltered.
> “Treatment﻿—”
But Mr. Marble did not want to know anything about the treatment. Anyway, it was easy to see that there would rarely be an opportunity of treating a sufferer from cyanide poisoning. Besides, he did not want to read the book at all after that. It made his stupid heart beat too fast again, so that he had difficulty in breathing while his hand shook like the balance-wheel of a watch. And the book had started an unpleasant train of thought, that set him once again gazing out into the garden, only twilight now, at the end of the day, while he thought about blank horrors.
He knew much more about crime than when he had first become a criminal. He knew that nine murderers out of ten were only discovered through some silly mistake. Even if they were very careful about planning the deed, and carried it out successfully, they still made some ridiculous blunder that betrayed them. But in some cases they were found out by some unfortunate mischance. It was generally through the gossip of neighbours, but sometimes it was through the insatiable curiosity of some really uninterested person. Now Mr. Marble could rely upon there being no gossip. No one knew that young Medland had come to his house that night. And he had made no blunder. It was only some event beyond his control that could betray him. Such as? The answer came pat to mental lips﻿—someone else moving into the house after he had been turned out, *someone with a taste for gardening*. Come what may, he must not be turned out of 53 Malcolm Road. But he might be at any minute now. His tortured mind raced like a steamship propeller in a rough sea. Supposing the franc should fall! He would lose his money, but that would be only part of his loss, and the least part, too. For Saunders would complain about his loss, perhaps even to the management of the Bank, certainly in a way that would come to the ears of the authorities. Then Marble would lose his job﻿—not possibly, but certainly. Then﻿—a few weeks of grace perhaps, and then, rent unpaid, out of the house he would go. After that it was inevitable. Mr. Marble shuddered uncontrollably. It all depended on the franc. One part of Mr. Marble’s feverishly active mind began to toil once more through all the data accumulated that had made him decide that the franc would rise; another part began to regret bitterly that he had ever entered into such an absurd venture, rashly leaving his temporary safety﻿—which already he began to long for again﻿—in a wild search for permanency. Perhaps this was his blunder, like Crippen’s flight to the Continent. Perhaps it was because of this that he was going to be hanged by the neck till he was dead. That other book, the one about Famous Criminals, had been disgustingly fond of that expression. Mr. Marble shuddered again.
===Chinese===
> “死亡几乎是顷刻间的事。中毒者发出一声高叫，沉沉栽倒。唇边可能涌起一些泡沫，死后尸身往往还保留着生前的模样：双颊红润，神情如故。
> “救治方法——”
可是救治的方法，马布尔先生一点儿也不想知道了。反正一看就明白，中了氰化物毒的人，几乎轮不到人来救。何况读过那一段之后，这本书他压根儿不想再翻了。它又叫他那颗愚蠢的心跳得飞快，逼得他喘不上气，手抖得像手表的摆轮。这本书还勾起了一串不痛快的念头，害他再一次凭窗望向园子——此刻已是日暮黄昏，一天将尽——心里翻来覆去，尽是空白的恐怖。
关于犯罪的门道，他可比当初头一回做罪犯时懂得多得多了。他知道，十个杀人犯里有九个，是栽在什么蠢头蠢脑的疏忽上的。哪怕那桩事谋划得再周密、干得再利落，到头来仍会犯下哪个荒唐的错误，把自己招供出来。可也有些案子，坏就坏在倒霉的意外上。走漏风声的通常是邻人的闲话，可有时，偏偏是哪个八竿子打不着的人，那股不知餍足的好奇心坏了事。这一层，如今马布尔先生倒不必担心。谁也不知道那晚小梅德兰到过他家。疏忽呢，他一处也没有。能出卖他的，只剩下他管不着的事变了。比如说？答案立刻就到了心边——等他被撵出家门之后，另有别人搬了进来，*一个偏爱摆弄园子的人*。无论出什么事，他也万万不能被撵出马尔科姆路53号。可如今他随时都可能被撵出去。他那受尽折磨的头脑，像风暴海面上的轮船螺旋桨一样狂转。万一法郎跌了呢！钱是要蚀的，可蚀钱不过是损失的一部分，还是最小的一部分。因为桑德斯必要抱怨自己的亏损，说不定一直抱怨到银行的管理层，反正迟早要传进当权者的耳朵。那时候马布尔就要丢饭碗——不是可能丢，是一定丢。然后——也许宽限几个星期，然后房租一欠，就得滚出这所房子。再往后的事，就万难幸免了。马布尔先生不由自主地打了个寒噤。一切都拴在法郎上。他那颗烧得发烫、转个不停的头脑，一部分又埋头去磕那一堆旧账——当初就是这堆账叫他断定法郎必涨；另一部分却在一味痛悔，悔自己不该一头扎进这样一桩荒唐的买卖，鲁莽地丢下暂时到手的安全——这安全他如今又开始渴望了——去疯疯癫癫地追逐万全。也许这正是他的败笔，正像克里平（Crippen）当年逃往大陆。也许就为了这个，他就要被套上绞索，吊到气绝为止。另外那本书，那本讲著名罪犯的书，偏偏最爱用这个说法。马布尔先生又是一阵寒噤。

===Original===
Mr. Marble sat till late that night﻿—indeed, he sat until early the next morning, disregarding, hardly hearing the appeals of his wife, working out with one part of his mind the chances of the rise of the franc, with the other the chances of escaping detection. Mr. Marble found some ghoulish details at the end of the *Handbook to Medical Jurisprudence* which interested him as well as appalled him. They dealt with the possibilities of identifying bodies after prolonged burial.
At half-past seven the next morning, Mr. Marble, who was already awake﻿—he hardly seemed to sleep nowadays﻿—heard the newspaper pushed through the letterbox of the front door downstairs. He climbed out of bed and padded down barefooted and in his pyjamas. The house was very still, and it seemed as if the beating of his heart shook it. It was unfortunate that it should start again now when it had taken him all the time he was in bed to quiet it down. But there was no help for it. Mr. Marble wondered whether the paper had anything to say about the franc, and of course that was enough to start it.
With the fibre doormat scratching his bare feet Mr. Marble stood and read the financial columns of the newspaper. It was unenlightening. It mentioned the closing price of the franc﻿—118﻿—one point better than he had bought at. Mr. Marble knew that already. Nowhere was there any mention of drastic action by the French Government. Everything seemed as it was yesterday. Mr. Marble realized that he might perhaps be able to get out of the transaction even now with safety and a small profit. That would perhaps keep Saunders’ mouth shut. But Mr. Marble only dallied with the idea for a moment. Then his eyes narrowed, and his weak, nubbly chin came forward an eighth of an inch. No. He would stick it out now. He would carry the business through, cost what it might. He was sick of being afraid. There was some quite good stuff in the makeup of Mr. William Marble. It was a pity that it took danger of life and death to stir him up to action.
===Chinese===
那天夜里，马布尔先生一直坐到很晚——其实是坐到了第二天凌晨，妻子的一再央告他全不理会，几乎充耳不闻，脑子里一半在盘算法郎上涨的成算，另一半在盘算逃脱查缉的成算。在《法医学手册》的末尾，马布尔先生还读到一些阴森森的细节，既叫他胆寒，又叫他着迷。那些内容讲的是：人葬下去许久之后，尸身还认不认得出来。
第二天早上七点半，马布尔先生——他早就醒了；近来他简直仿佛不用睡觉——听见楼下的报纸从前门信箱里塞了进来。他爬下床，睡衣裤也不换，光着脚下楼去。屋子静极了，仿佛他的心跳震得整座房子都在晃。真倒霉，在床上费了整夜的工夫才把心跳压下去，这会儿它又要闹起来。可这没法子。马布尔先生寻思着，报上会不会说法郎的事——这一寻思，就够它跳起来的了。
马布尔先生站在门口，粗硬的纤维门垫扎着他赤裸的脚底，他就这么站着读报上的财经版。读不出什么名堂。法郎收盘价118——比他买进时好上一个点。这个马布尔先生早就知道了。报上哪儿也见不着法国政府有什么大动作的风声。一切都同昨天一个样。马布尔先生明白，就是此刻脱手，他说不定也能安然退出这笔交易，还落点小利。那也许能堵住桑德斯的嘴。可这个念头，马布尔先生只转了一霎。跟着他眯起眼睛，那软弱、疙疙瘩瘩的下巴朝前凸出了八分之一英寸。不。这一回他要硬到底。不管代价多大，这桩买卖他都要做到底。担惊受怕，他受够了。威廉·马布尔先生的骨子里，原也有几块相当好的料。只可惜，非要生死攸关的危险，才激得他行动起来。

===Original===
Yet Mr. Marble was so anxious that he called up to his wife, “Aren’t you *ever* coming down, Annie?” and he bustled hastily through his dressing and breakfast and rushed off to the City a good half-hour before his usual time. No one in the crowded railway carriage guessed that the little man in the blue suit perched in the corner, his feet hardly touching the floor, who read his paper with such avidity, was hastening to either fortune or ruin, although perhaps a closer glance than ever Mr. Marble received might have raised some strange speculations, considering his white face and his tortured light-blue eyes. He did not walk across the bridge from the station. Instead he scampered, breathlessly.
At the Bank he hung up his hat and coat carelessly, and dashed upstairs to the department of Foreign Exchange. The few clerks already there stared in wonder at his unwontedly early arrival. Straight to Mr. Henderson’s room went Mr. Marble, to that private sanctum which only he and Henderson had the right to enter. He looked at the tape machine. Fool that he was! Of course, there would be no quotations through yet. He might as well have stayed at home.
He came back to his desk, and sat down, making a pretence of being busy, though this was difficult to maintain, as the letters had not yet arrived. He waited for twenty minutes while the room filled with late arrivals of the one timetable and the early arrivals of the other. The customary din of the office began to develop. The telephones began to ring, and the clerks began to call to each other from desk to desk. Mr. Marble became conscious that young Netley was speaking into the telephone opposite. He knew from Netley’s greeting to the unknown at the other end that he was talking to the exchange brokers in London Wall.
===Chinese===
然而马布尔先生心里急得不行，竟朝楼上妻子喊道：“安妮，你*到底*还下不下楼来？”他又急匆匆穿好衣裳、匆匆咽下早饭，比平常足足提前半个多钟头，就赶往伦敦金融城（the City）去了。拥挤的铁路车厢里，谁也没有猜到，那个缩在角落里的蓝衣小个子——双脚几乎够不着地板，正如饥似渴地读着报纸——正奔走在或发财或毁灭的路上；不过，倘若有谁肯多瞧他一眼——比马布尔先生从来领受过的任何一瞥更仔细的一瞥——看看他那张苍白的脸和那双饱受折磨的浅蓝色眼睛，说不定会生出些奇怪的猜想来。出了车站，过桥那一段他不是走着过的。他是一口气小跑过去的，连大气也喘不匀。
到了银行，他把帽子外套随随便便一挂，便直奔楼上的外汇部。先到的几个职员见了他这反常的早到，个个惊异。马布尔先生径直进了亨德森先生的房间——那间只有他和亨德森有权踏进的内室。他看了看行情收录机（tape machine）。真是个傻瓜！行情这会儿当然还没过来。他还不如待在家里。
他回到自己的办公桌前坐下，装出一副忙碌的样子，只是这很难维持，因为信件还没有送到。他等了二十分钟，眼看屋子里渐渐坐满了人——有踩着这一张时刻表迟到的，也有踩着另一张早到的。办公室惯常的喧闹渐次升起来。电话铃此起彼伏，职员们隔着一张张桌子相互喊话。马布尔先生觉察到，年轻的内特利（Netley）正在对面打电话。听内特利对电话那头那个陌生人的招呼，他就知道，对方是伦敦墙（London Wall）的外汇经纪人。

===Original===
“Yes,” said Netley, “yes, no, what, really? No, I hadn’t heard, yes, yes, all right.”
Marble knew instinctively what he was talking about.
“What’s Paris now, Netley?” he asked.
Netley was so full of his surprising news that he did not notice the coincidence, and also actually added the hated “sir.”
“Ninety-nine, sir,” he said. “It’s gone up twenty points in the night. They don’t know why, yet.”
Marble knew. He was right, of course. He had a good head for finance when he chose to use it.
===Chinese===
“对，”内特利说，“对，不，什么？真的吗？不，我还没听说，是，是，好嘞。”
马布尔本能地就知道他在说什么。
“巴黎现在什么价，内特利？”他问。
内特利满心装着他那惊人的消息，竟没有留意这个巧合，甚至还当真添上了那声他平日最不肯叫的“先生”。
“九十九，先生，”他说，“一夜之间涨了二十个点。什么缘故，眼下还没人知道。”
马布尔知道缘故。他当然是对的。他那颗脑袋盘算金融原本不坏，只要他肯用它。

===Original===
Henderson came in and passed through to his own room. Marble did not notice him. He was busy thinking. He was nerving himself to go on with the venture. If he sold now he could give Saunders some three hundred pounds profit﻿—enough to satisfy him most probably. Anyway, he was safe for a bit. Keeping in close touch with the market as he naturally did, he could sell at the instant a decline seemed likely. But if he did what he had suggested to Saunders yesterday﻿—sold out and then reinvested, he would be much less safe. A ten percent drop would wipe out profit and capital as well, and Saunders would think he had been swindled. But all Marble’s judgement told him that the rise was bound to continue. There was a huge gain to be made if only he was bold enough﻿—or desperate enough﻿—to risk it. Henderson appeared at the door of his room.
“Mr. Marble,” he said, “someone wants you.”
Marble went in and picked up the receiver.
“Hullo,” he said.
“That Mr. Marble?” said the receiver.
“Hallo, old bean, how’s things?” said the receiver.
===Chinese===
亨德森走了进来，径直进了自己那间屋。马布尔没有留意他。他正忙着盘算。他正给自己打气，要把这场投机接着做下去。此刻卖出，他能给桑德斯挣下三百来英镑——多半足够叫人家满足了。无论如何，眼下他是稳当的。行情他自然时刻盯着，一见跌势露头，立时便能出手。可要是照他昨天向桑德斯提过的那套——卖出再另行买进——那他就远没有这么稳当。跌上百分之十，利润连同本钱就会一齐化为乌有，桑德斯就会认定自己叫人坑了。可马布尔全部的判断都告诉他：这轮涨势必定继续。只要他胆子够大——或者够亡命——敢担这场风险，便有一大笔横财可发。亨德森出现在他房间的门口。
“马布尔先生，”他说，“有人找您。”
马布尔走进去拿起听筒。
“喂，”他说。
“是马布尔吗？”听筒里说。
“喂，老伙计，近来可好？”听筒里说。
===Original===
It was Saunders. He had begun to regret his transaction of the previous day long ago, but he was determined on playing the game to the last. Marble might have got four hundred pounds out of him by some nefarious means, but he was not going to get a rise out of him as well. He would see the thing through to the bitter end.
“Going well,” said Mr. Marble.
He had to pick his words, for Henderson was within earshot, and it would never do for him to know that he was acting in collusion with one of the Bank’s customers.
“They’ve begun to rise,” said Mr. Marble. “Look at your tape machine.”
Mr. Saunders was unable to retain an exclamation of surprised unbelief.
“You can get out now with a bit of profit,” said Mr. Marble. His tone was cold and sincere, as he was striving to make it, and carried conviction.
===Chinese===
是桑德斯。昨天那笔交易他早就懊悔了，可他打定主意，这场游戏非玩到底不可。马布尔也许使了什么邪门歪道，从他身上抠走了四百英镑，可他休想再撩起他的火气、看他发作。他要硬着头皮，把这局撑到最后。
“还顺当。”马布尔先生说。
他说话得掂掂字眼，因为亨德森就在听得见的地方，要是让他知道自己同银行的客户串通一气，那可万万使不得。
“已经开始涨了，”马布尔先生说，“您看看您的行情收录机。”
桑德斯先生憋不住，发出一声又惊又疑的叫喊。
“您现在出场，还能带走一点利润。”马布尔先生说。他刻意让语气又冷淡又诚恳，这语气也确实叫人信服。

===Original===
“Sorry, I can’t hear what you’re saying,” said Mr. Marble.
“Doodle﻿—oodle﻿—oodle,” said the receiver, as Mr. Saunders realized that this was his cue, and all his old gambling spirit came back to him.
“Right. I think you’re wise,” said Mr. Marble, replacing the receiver.
“That Mr. Saunders,” said Marble to Henderson. “Bought some francs yesterday﻿—lucky devil﻿—wants to sell and reinvest.”
In the outer office the usual nerve-racking bustle was at its usual height. Mr. Marble sat at his desk, where the letters had now been put, and rallied himself. For nearly five minutes he fought with himself before he could turn to the telephone at his elbow and give the necessary orders to increase Saunders’ holding﻿—and the risk as well.
There was apparently no need to worry. Mr. Marble sold at 95; he bought again at 93. Half an hour later the franc stood at 87, and the risk was past. It is an old story now, how the French Government had quietly appropriated other people’s credits the night before, how the franc rose all day long, while puzzled exchange brokers racked their brains to explain the mystery, and cursed their gods that they had not foreseen this action and bought all the francs that their credit would stand. And all day the franc rose, as men who had been caught hurried in to cover their losses, as the German speculators who had been hammering away so joyfully gave up the struggle in despair, as the small investors who follow the movements of the market far to the rear came panting in to steal a slice of profit as well. The men in the office who only a few days before had been confidently predicting that the franc would go the way of the mark had already changed their minds completely and were now saying that it would climb to its prewar value of twenty-five and a quarter. But Mr. Marble kept his head, just as he had kept it that fatal night when he knew that a single mistake meant ruin and violent death. The cold fear that had succeeded to the initial feeling of elated success vanished completely, and he was left calm, deadly calm. He watched the market with a fierce intensity. Once or twice it wavered, as the faint hearts took their profits, but each time it recovered, as it was bound to do with a genuine demand behind it. At 75 he resold and reinvested again, sitting lunchless in the office all day, so as to keep the business under his own hand, and when the franc touched 65 he sold out for good. It might well go a little higher, as indeed it did, reaching 60 for a brief space, but he had done all that was necessary and a good deal more.
===Chinese===
“对不住，您说什么？我听不清。”马布尔先生说。
“嘟——嘟——嘟，”听筒里说道；桑德斯先生这才醒悟，这是给他的暗号，他那一身老赌徒的劲头立时全回来了。
“好。您这么做是明智的。”马布尔先生说，把听筒搁了回去。
“是桑德斯先生，”马布尔对亨德森说，“昨天买了点法郎——走运的家伙——如今想卖掉，另换一桩做。”
外间办公室里，惯常那阵磨人心的忙乱正闹到惯常的高峰。马布尔先生坐在办公桌前——信件这会儿已经摆上桌了——给自己鼓劲。他同自己较了将近五分钟的劲，才终于转向手边的电话，下达必要的指令，给桑德斯的头寸加码——连同风险一齐加码。
看来是白操心了。马布尔先生在95卖出，又在93买进。半小时后，法郎升到87，风险过去了。法国政府头天夜里不动声色地把别人名下的存款划归己有；法郎一整天一路上扬，摸不着头脑的外汇经纪人绞尽脑汁想解开这个谜，一面又咒骂自家的财神爷，恨自己没料到这一手、没把信用担得起的法郎一网买尽——这些如今都已是旧话。法郎涨了一整天：叫套住的人争先恐后进场补空；一路兴高采烈猛砸法郎的德国投机客绝望地歇了手；远远缀在行情后面的小散户，也气喘吁吁地赶进来，想偷分一瓢利润。办公室里那几位，几天前还满有把握地断言法郎会走上马克那条老路，如今心思已经整个儿翻转过来，说法郎要一路爬回战前二十五点二五的价位。可马布尔先生沉得住气，正如在那致命的一夜，他明知一着失手便是身败名裂外加横死，也照样沉得住气。初战得手那阵欢腾过后继之而来的冰冷恐惧，此刻烟消云散，他心里只剩下平静，死一般的平静。他以炽烈的专注盯住行情。行情有一两回晃了晃，那是胆小的人落袋为安；可每回它都重新站稳，背后有实打实的需求托着，它不能不站稳。到75，他再度卖出、再度买进；为了把买卖攥在自己手心里，他一整天坐在办公室，午饭也没吃；待法郎一摸到65，他便尽数出清，就此收手。行情很可能还会再涨一点——后来也果真涨了，一度摸到60——可该做的他都已做完，还多做了不少。

===Original===
There was no need to work out the profit. He knew that already, counting with painful eagerness every penny that every point meant to him. He called to the department stenographer, and began the official Bank letter to Saunders reporting the progress made:
> Dear Sir,
> In accordance with your instructions received today by telephone at 9:45 a.m. and 4:51 p.m. we have﻿—
and all the rest of the business. It was cold and dry and formal enough, heaven knew. Bank letters are usually cold and dry and formal, even when they embody a paean of praise. This one told, with an air of supreme detachment, how Mr. Saunders had originally bought rather more than forty-five thousand francs with the four thousand pounds represented by his margin of four hundred; how they had been sold at 95, and then represented nearly five thousand pounds (a thousand pounds profit); how this thousand pounds, and the original four hundred, had purchased francs at 93, and thus, thanks to the fact that each pound did the work of ten, controlled nearly a million and a quarter of francs; this million odd had been sold at 75, bringing in sixteen thousand pounds and more. Mr. Saunders’ profit now stood at over four thousand pounds, and it and the good old original four hundred had gone back once more into francs, purchased at 75 still, thanks to Mr. Marble having taken advantage of an eddy in the market. Forty-five thousand pounds’ worth of francs did that sum control﻿—three millions of francs and a few thousand odd ones.
===Chinese===
利润不必再算。他早知道了：每个点在他算来合几便士，他都带着揪心的急切，一便士一便士地点过了。他唤来部里的速记员，开始口授银行致桑德斯的正式公函，报告交易的进展：
> 敬启者：
> 兹遵照您今日上午九时四十五分及下午四时五十一分以电话发来的指示，我行已——
还有诸如此类的官样文章。这封信够冷、够干、够板正的了，天知道。银行的信函向来又冷又干又板正，哪怕字字句句是一篇颂歌也照样。这封信以十足置身事外的口气写道：桑德斯先生最初以四百英镑的保证金作抵，按其所折合的四千英镑，买进了四万五千有余的法郎；这批法郎在95卖出，当时已值将近五千英镑（获利一千英镑）；这一千英镑，连同原先那四百，又按93买进法郎，于是——托每一英镑顶十英镑使的福——握有了将近一百二十五万法郎；这一百万挂零在75卖出，回收一万六千多英镑。至此，桑德斯先生的利润已逾四千英镑；这笔钱，连同那笔劳苦功高的老本四百英镑，又一头扎回法郎，买价仍是75——这要归功于马布尔先生看准了行情里的一阵小旋涡。这笔款项此刻握着的，是价值四万五千英镑的法郎——整整三百万法郎，还带几千零头。

===Original===
When they were finally sold at 65 Mr. Saunders’ credit balance stood at a paltry fifty-one thousand pounds. He probably was unable to do the simplest sum in foreign exchange; at the moment he did not have the least idea of what profit he had made; Marble’s forethought had earned it for him; most of the money would come eventually out of the pockets of less fortunate speculators, which only served them right, but some would come from the myriad firms which had to have francs at any price. Above all, if the Bank had had anything to say in the matter they would probably have cut short the speculation at the earliest opportunity, but they had never been consulted after the first interview. Mr. Marble had a specious plea of justification for having done all this on his own responsibility, in that Henderson had acquiesced in the first expansion of the deal, but he did not think he would have to use it. No bank really objects to having its clients enriched by the enthusiasm of its staff.
When the letter was finished Mr. Marble slipped out of the office. He had done no work that day, and he would not have been able to even if he had tried. He was too exhausted by the emotional strain under which he had been labouring all these hours. Instead, he walked quietly round to Saunders’ office. The hurrying crowds round him, hastening to catch the 5:10 at Fenchurch Street, or the 5:25 at London Bridge, did not pay him the tribute of a glance. They did not realize that this shabby man in blue was a capitalist﻿—a man possessed of over ten thousand pounds, *if he could be sure of making Saunders pay up*. They paid no heed to him, save to shoulder their homeward progress. He was rich almost to the full extent of his wildest dreams, and yet they pushed him into the gutter. Mr. Marble did not resent it. They had likewise paid no heed to him when he was only a murderer.
Saunders in his office, the last race of the day over, was glancing through some trial totals of the day’s figures when one of his two clerks showed in Mr. Marble.
===Chinese===
待最后在65卖出时，桑德斯先生的贷方余额不过区区五万一千英镑。他多半连最简单的外汇账都算不上来；此刻他赚了多大的利钱，自己心里连个大概也没有；这是马布尔的深谋远虑替他挣下的；这笔钱大部分终归要从运气差些的投机客口袋里掏出来——那也叫他们活该——但还有一部分，会出自那千百家不论什么价都必须弄到法郎的商号。最要紧的是：这件事银行若有权过问，多半一有机会就把这场投机掐断了；可初次面谈之后，就再没有人征询过他们。马布尔先生独断独行做下了这一切，却备好了一套冠冕堂皇的说辞——亨德森当初毕竟默许过这笔交易的第一次扩张——不过他估摸着用不上。自家职员一片热忱，叫客户发财政富，天底下没有哪家银行会当真见怪。
信写完，马布尔先生悄悄溜出了办公室。这一天他一点正经事也没做，就是想做也做不进去。这许多个钟头，他一直在心弦紧绷底下苦撑，此刻早已筋疲力尽。他没有回家，却安安静静地绕到桑德斯的办公室去。身边匆匆的人流——赶着去芬乔奇街（Fenchurch Street）搭5点10分车的，赶去伦敦桥搭5点25分车的——谁也不肯赏他一瞥。他们不知道，这个穿蓝衣裳的寒酸人是一位资本家——一位坐拥一万多英镑的人，*只要他拿得稳桑德斯肯付账*。他们全不把他放在心上，至多用肩膀把他一拱，自顾自地往家赶。他已经富得几乎到了自己最狂的梦的顶点，他们却照样把他挤进阳沟。马布尔先生并不见怪。当初他还不过是个杀人犯的时候，他们同样不曾把他放在心上。
桑德斯正在自己办公室里，当天最后一场赛马已经收场，他正翻看着当日账目数字的试算合计，手下一个职员——他一共雇着两个——把马布尔先生领了进来。

===Original===
“Hallo?” he said, glancing up, “so you’ve made a bit?”
Mr. Marble sank wearily into the chair indicated and took the cigarette that Saunders offered.
“What did you get? Six to one?” asked Saunders. He was half joking, half serious. He had determined that Mr. Marble’s final bait of yesterday of three thousand percent was a mere piece of bluff. Obviously Marble had taken a chance and it had come off, and he, glad to see his money back, let alone with profit attached, would not press him too hard for the fulfilment of all his promises.
“Don’t know,” said Marble. “Haven’t worked it out like that. But the total comes to something like fifty thousand.”
“What?” gasped Saunders. “Fifty thousand? Or it’s francs, I suppose you mean?”
“No,” said Marble expressionlessly, “pounds.”
===Chinese===
“哦？”他抬眼一看，说，“赚着一点了？”
马布尔先生疲惫地陷进人家指给的那把椅子，接了桑德斯敬来的香烟。
“赚了多少？六赔一？”桑德斯问。他半是玩笑，半是当真。他已断定，马布尔先生昨天临了抛出的那记百分之三千的香饵，纯属唬人。明摆着马布尔是赌了一把，赌赢了；他呢，眼见本钱回来已是欢喜，何况上面还附着利钱，那些承诺也就不必逼他条条兑现了。
“不知道，”马布尔说，“没那样算过。不过总数差不多有五万。”
“什么？”桑德斯倒抽一口气，“五万？我说，你指的是法郎吧？”
“不，”马布尔不动声色地说，“英镑。”

===Original===
“D’you mean it?”
“Oh, of course I do. You’ll get the official notification from the Bank tomorrow.”
Saunders said nothing. Nothing in his limited vocabulary was equal to the situation.
“Fifty thousand pounds,” said Mr. Marble, still expressionless, but bracing himself unobtrusively for the final effort. “Let’s work out what my share of it is.”
It was astonishing to him to find that Saunders agreed without any difficulty at all. He would not have been surprised to find him refusing to render any account whatever; he could have retained the whole and nothing could have been proved against him. But Marble, when he feared this, allowed his fear to overbalance his estimation of several important items in Saunders’ makeup. In the first place, Saunders was an honest man. In the second place, he was so dazzled by the magnitude of the profit that he did not grudge the fair share of the man who had earned it for him. In the third place, he was a bookmaker, and he was accustomed to handing over large sums on account of transactions of which no law in the United Kingdom took the slightest notice.
“Rightio,” said Saunders. “How much is it exactly?”
===Chinese===
“你当真？”
“哦，自然当真。明天你就等着收银行的正式通知吧。”
桑德斯没言语。他那一副有限的词汇里，找不出一句话配得上眼前的场面。
“五万英镑，”马布尔先生说，依旧不动声色，人却在暗暗攒劲，预备最后一搏。“咱们来算算，我那一份是多少。”
