马布尔太太的心思也在转。上个星期六买下的东西，头一批当天到货了——货运行送来一只只大箱子，装满了她的想象力从未描画过的、最最可心的物件。她恋恋不舍地一件件摩挲过去。有帽子，妙不可言的帽子，戴在她头上合适极了——虽然她私心承认，自己并不大喜欢时兴的钟形帽式样，线条素净得一丝装饰也不留。有套头毛衣，主妇味道的套头毛衣，她惊讶地发现，穿在自己身上竟很好看，虽说在那之前，她一直只当套头毛衣是年轻姑娘穿的。还有一盒又一盒的内衣，价钱起初吓了她一跳，直到想起家里有的是钱好花，才又壮起胆来。至于那身定做的套装和外套式连衣裙，自然还没到手——替她量尺寸的是位女裁缝，那人是她刚买完别的东西时，冷不丁出现在铺子里的；说“冷不丁”，是对马布尔太太而言——她还不知道女士商店那套瓜分回佣的门道，也没领略过电话的便利呢。

===Original===
But that costume and the coatfrock would not have been any good to her even if she had had it. Mrs. Marble had indeed roused herself to put on some of the underclothing, priceless things, warm and weightless, costing as much as her husband had earned in a month before all this started happening, but she had not the heart to put on a pair of the thick silk stockings while she had so much housework still to be done, and she wore over the wonderful underclothing her usual draggled housefrock. With the other things arriving shortly she might as well have put on her best frock, but she had not the heart to do that with so much washing-up to be done that evening. Mrs. Marble felt aggrieved. Besides, she was tired, and her back really did hurt.
A day or two ago she had pictured herself sitting at her ease in the evenings, wearing a wonderful frock, with her skin deliciously thrilled by the contact of the silken underclothing. As it was, she wore a shabby frock and there was a bowlful of washing-up clamouring for her attention in the kitchen. It was this that roused her to surprising rebellion﻿—very mild rebellion, but any sort of rebellion was surprising in Mrs. Marble.
“I’ll have Mrs. Summers in during the daytime when you don’t know anything about it,” she said.
===Chinese===
不过，那身套装和外套式连衣裙就算到了手，眼下她也穿不成。马布尔太太确实打起精神，换上了几件内衣——件件都是无价之宝，又暖又轻，一件的价钱抵得上这一摊事起来之前丈夫一个月的薪水——可家里还有那么一大堆活计等着干，她舍不得穿那双厚丝袜，只在那身美妙的内衣外头，罩上平日那件拖沓的家常旧衣。别的东西反正转眼就到，她本不妨干脆穿上自己最好的连衫裙，可那天晚上还有满满一大盆碗碟等着洗，她终究还是不忍心。马布尔太太觉得满腹委屈。再说，她也累了，腰也真真切切地疼着。
一两天前，她还想象过晚间的光景：自己穿着一身漂亮的连衫裙，舒舒服服地坐着，丝绸内衣贴着肌肤，激起一阵阵惬意的酥麻。可眼下，她穿着一身寒酸的旧衣，厨房里一大盆待洗的碗碟正吵着要她去伺候。正是这个，把她激出了惊人的反叛——极其温和的反叛，可在马布尔太太身上，任何一点反叛都足以惊人。
“那我就趁你不知情的白天，让萨默斯太太过来帮工。”她说。

===Original===
The words brought Mr. Marble out of his chair in a panic. That would be worse than the other thing; it would start gossip of a more poisonous sort than ever, and gossip better directed towards the real food for suspicion, for Mrs. Marble would have to tell Mrs. Summers that he did not like having other people about the house. He glared at her with frightful intensity.
“You mustn’t, you mustn’t ever do anything like that,” he said, and his voice was cracked and shrill. His clenched fists shook in his agitation. Mrs. Marble could only stare at him, surprised and speechless.
“You mustn’t do it. Do you hear me?” he shrieked.
His agitation infected his wife, and she fumbled nervously with the sewing on her lap.
===Chinese===
这一句话吓得马布尔先生从椅子上弹了起来。那可比头一桩还要糟糕；那会惹出比什么都毒的闲话，而且闲话偏偏直奔真正惹疑的实处——因为马布尔太太总得跟萨默斯太太交代，他不喜欢家里有外人走动。他双眼凶光毕露，死死瞪住她。
“你不许，你永远不许干这种事。”他说，嗓音又劈又尖。他攥紧的双拳激动得直抖。马布尔太太只能瞪着他，又惊又诧，说不出话来。
“你不许干。听见没有？”他尖声喊道。
他的激动传染给了妻子，她攥着膝上的针线活，手指慌乱地摆弄个不停。

===Original===
“Yes, dear.”
“Yes, dear! Yes, dear! I don’t want any of your ‘yes, dears.’ You must promise me, promise me faithfully, that you won’t ever do that. If ever I find out, I’ll﻿—I’ll﻿—”
Mr. Marble’s high-pitched scream died away as the door swung open. John had come running down as soon as he heard his father’s voice at that hysterical pitch. It was not very long since the last time he had heard it like that, and then he had had to carry his mother up to bed, and she had a bruise on her face.
John stood by the door with the light on his face. Mr. Marble shrank back a little, and his lips wrinkled back from his teeth. He was the rat in the corner once more. Hatred flashed from the father to the son. It was not John’s fault, nor, by that time, was it Mr. Marble’s. For James Medland, he who had come in that memorable evening, nearly a year ago now, was John’s cousin when all was said and done, and there was a considerable family likeness. As John stood by the door he was in the same attitude and in the same light as Medland had been in, that evening, when he had entered the dining-room after Winnie had opened the door to him. No wonder that Mr. Marble hated John, and had hated him ever since he first noticed the likeness, that evening when he had struck his wife.
===Chinese===
“是，亲爱的。”
“是，亲爱的！是，亲爱的！我不要你这些‘是，亲爱的’。你必须答应我，老老实实答应我，你永远不干那种事。一旦叫我发现了，我就——我就——”
门猛地荡开，马布尔先生尖利的喊声随之咽了下去。约翰一听父亲的声音拔到这般歇斯底里的调门，便一路奔下楼来。上回听见这个调门还是不久以前的事，那一回，他不得不把母亲抱上楼安置到床上，她脸上还带着一块青伤。
约翰站在门口，灯光正照在他脸上。马布尔先生往后缩了缩，嘴唇皱起，龇出牙齿。他又成了被逼进墙角的那只老鼠。憎恨从父亲身上直闪向儿子。这不是约翰的错，而事到如今，也算不得是马布尔先生的错了。因为詹姆斯·梅德兰（James Medland）——那个在难忘的傍晚登门的人，如今已快一年了——归根结底是约翰的表哥，两人眉眼间颇有几分家族的相像。约翰站在门口的那副姿势、那片灯光，同梅德兰当初一模一样——就是那个傍晚，温妮给他开了门，他随后走进饭厅。怪不得马布尔先生恨约翰，而且打头一回看出这层相像的那晚起就恨他——正是他动手打了妻子的那一晚。

===Original===
Father looked at son, son looked at father. The room was all aglare with gilded furniture. The diamond in Mr. Marble’s tiepin winked and glittered as he shrank slowly back before John’s slow and menacing advance. John had come to protect his mother, but the desperate challenge in his father’s attitude had roused him to the limit of his self-control. It was Mrs. Marble who saved the situation. She glanced terror-stricken at her husband’s snarling countenance, and at the wrinkled scowl of her son. In utter fear she flung herself into the breach.
“John, go away,” she said. “Go﻿—quickly﻿—it’s all right.”
John checked himself, and his hands unclenched. Mrs. Marble’s hand was on her heart; for in that same second she had seen what her husband had seen long before, and she had guessed that it was this that had called up that ferocity into his face. She was frightened, and she did not yet know why.
“Go, go, go,” wailed Mrs. Marble, and then, with a supreme effort, “There’s nothing to worry about John. You had better go to bed. Good night, sonny.”
When he had gone, silent, wordless as when he had entered, Mrs. Marble sank into a chair and laid her face on her arms on the gilded table and sobbed and sobbed, heartbroken, while her husband stood morosely beside her, hands in pockets, the gaudy glare of the ornate furniture mocking him, mocking his hopes of the future, mocking his submerged, sensual dreamings of Madame Collins.
===Chinese===
父亲望着儿子，儿子望着父亲。满屋的镀金家具光焰逼人。约翰缓缓进逼，步步行前，一脸威胁；马布尔先生在他面前节节后退，领带别针上的钻石随之明明灭灭、闪闪发亮。约翰本是来保护母亲的，可父亲那副破釜沉舟的挑衅架势，把他逼到了自制力的极限。出面收拾危局的还是马布尔太太。她惊恐地看一眼丈夫龇牙咆哮的脸，又看一眼儿子紧锁的怒容，在极度的恐惧里挺身扑进了那道裂口。
“约翰，走开。”她说，“走——快走——没事的。”
约翰收住脚，攥紧的手松开了。马布尔太太一只手按在胸口；因为就在同一瞬间，她也看见了丈夫早先看见的东西，并且猜到，正是这个，把那副凶残唤上了他的脸。她害怕了，可她还不知道缘由。
“走吧，走吧，走吧。”马布尔太太哀声催着，随即又拼尽全力添了一句：“没事的，约翰，没什么可担心的。你上楼睡去吧。晚安，好孩子。”
他走了，同进来时一样，一言不发。马布尔太太跌坐进一把椅子，把脸伏在臂弯里，趴在那张镀金桌子上，伤心欲绝地呜咽了又呜咽；她丈夫阴沉沉地立在旁边，双手插在口袋里——满屋富丽家具射出的俗艳光焰嘲弄着他，嘲弄他对前程的指望，嘲弄他心底沉浮的那些对科林斯夫人的肉欲绮念。

## IX

===Original===
After this episode matters went for some time exactly as Mr. Marble would have wished. John’s application to enter Sydenham College was favourably received, and he went there without further demur. He was not quite sixteen. There was more trouble about Winnie. Mr. Marble obtained from scholastic agents a list of all the more expensive girls’ schools, but his endeavours to enter Winnie in one of them were baulked for some time. They displayed a not unnatural reluctance to receive in their midst a girl of nearly fifteen who hailed from an address in a dubious street in a south London suburb, and who had been educated so far at a Council school and at a secondary school. But at last a Berkshire school accepted her﻿—it was incidentally the most expensive of all﻿—and then there was a flurrying and a scurrying to get ready the vast outfit that the rules of the school demanded. There was a special type of gymnasium frock to be obtained, and day clothes and evening clothes and, crowning glory of all, a riding-habit and boots. Mr. Marble was delighted. He certainly seemed prouder of Winnie’s outfit than she was herself.
And so, after Easter, the same day that Mr. and Mrs. Marble, he dressed in his very best to impress the other girls’ parents and the other girls, she rather tearful, with her appearance not justifying the amount spent on it, saw Winnie off from Paddington, John put on the blue and black cap of Sydenham College and set off on his two-mile walk thither, not feeling at all happy, with all the mysteries of Rugby football and the prevailing etiquette of the new school before him.
True, his father had been jolly decent to him lately. He had given him nearly all the pocket-money he wanted, while in a tiny lockup garage at the end of Malcolm Road there reposed the giant two-cylinder motor-bicycle that his heart had yearned for. John had averaged a hundred miles a day on it for the last week, delighting in learning its mechanical whims, making gallant and occasionally successful attempts to climb hills in “top,” and finding all the wonderful bits of country that lie near London just out of reach of the ordinary bicycle. It was a good way of forgetting saying goodbye to the old school, where he had been happy for nearly five years﻿—and saying goodbye to his old friends, too.
John was violently unhappy. It was not altogether the new school that made him so, not by any means. It was affairs at home. His father was drunk five nights out of seven, and that was the least of his troubles. Most times Mr. Marble’s drunkenness did not disturb the rest of the family as much as might be expected, for on those occasions he kept himself very strictly to himself﻿—shut up in the drawing-room gazing out over the backyard. Only twice had John to interfere actively between his mother and his father, fearing that he would do her an injury, but John knew in his soul that there was a far worse trouble than drunkenness in his house. His mother was looking pinched and thin, and he guessed repeatedly that she had been crying during the day. His father’s unaccountable moroseness probably caused that, combined with the fatigue resulting from his unreasonable refusal to allow her to have any help about the house. Yet John could not find any definite beginning to this moroseness and cross-grainedness, for Mr. Marble had drunk more than was good for him and had neglected his wife long before James Medland had made his solitary visit to 53 Malcolm Road. John looked upon the unpleasing traits in his father’s character as plants of slow growth and poisonous flowering.
===Chinese===
这场风波过后，有一段时间，诸事都正合马布尔先生的心意。约翰申请入读西德纳姆学院（Sydenham College）得到了顺利的答复，他没有再推辞，便入学了。他还不到十六岁。温妮的事却费了些周折。马布尔先生从教育代理那里弄到一份收费较高的女子学校名单，可想把温妮送进其中一所的努力，却一度连连受挫。那些学校对于接收一个年近十五、家住伦敦南区一条名声可疑的街上、此前只在地方议会学校（Council school）和一所中学念过书的女孩，表现出一种不算不近人情的勉强。不过伯克郡（Berkshire）终于有一所学校肯收下她——顺带说一句，那恰恰是所有学校里收费最贵的一所——于是全家一阵手忙脚乱，赶置校规所要求的那一大套行头。要添置特制样式的体操裙，还有日间穿的、晚间穿的各色衣裳，而压轴的荣耀，是一套骑装和马靴。马布尔先生欢喜得很。说起温妮这身行头，他那股得意劲儿，简直比温妮本人还足。
就这样，复活节过后，马布尔先生一身最好的衣裳，好震一震其他女生的家长和其他女生，马布尔太太则眼里含着泪——温妮那副模样，实在对不起花在她身上的那些钱——两口子到帕丁顿车站（Paddington）给温妮送了行。就在同一天，约翰戴上西德纳姆学院蓝黑两色的帽子，步行两英里上路去学校，心里一点也不痛快，眼前横着英式橄榄球的种种玄妙，还有新学校里通行的种种规矩。
诚然，父亲近来待他实在不坏。他要的零花钱，父亲差不多有求必应；马尔科姆路尽头一间小小的封闭车库里，还停放着他心心念念的那辆双缸大型摩托车。过去一个星期，约翰骑着它平均每天跑一百英里，津津有味地摸透它的种种机械小脾气，英勇地——偶尔也能成功——尝试挂最高挡爬坡，还把伦敦近郊那些普通自行车够不着的绝妙乡野寻访了个遍。这是个忘掉告别旧学校的好法子——在那所学校里，他快快活活过了将近五年——也忘掉告别那些老朋友。
约翰心里苦得很。这绝不全是新学校的缘故，绝不是。是家里的事。父亲七天里倒有五个晚上喝得烂醉，而这还算他烦恼里最轻的一桩。大多数时候，马布尔先生的醉态并不像旁人料想的那样搅得全家不安，因为那些晚上，他总是把自己关得严严实实——锁在客厅里，望着后院出神。只有两次，约翰生怕父亲会伤着母亲，不得不出面强行隔开。可约翰心底清楚，他家里藏着一个比酗酒糟糕得多的祸根。母亲看上去一天天瘦削憔悴，他不止一次猜到她白天哭过。这多半是父亲那没来由的阴沉所致，再加上他不肯让母亲雇人帮佣、害她积劳的缘故。然而约翰理不出这份阴沉乖戾有个什么确切的起点，因为早在詹姆斯·梅德兰（James Medland）独自登门造访马尔科姆路53号之前很久，马布尔先生就已经饮酒过度、冷落妻子了。在约翰眼里，父亲性格中这些惹人厌的品性，就像生长缓慢、终而开出毒花的植物。

===Original===
All John knew was that there was trouble in the family, terrible trouble, too, and he guessed, childlike, at his father’s hatred for him and resented it with a hatred equally bitter. The gifts that his father had showered on him so profusely lately he had accepted because there was hardly any other course open to him; and he had offered no thanks, because he had seen that what Mr. Marble desired more than anything was to dazzle him with his profusion, and﻿—somewhere﻿—there was a lurking suspicion that these gifts were a sort of bribe, offered to keep him in a good temper.
But at fifteen﻿—nearly sixteen﻿—John had not thought out all these things in the clear-cut fashion of print. He still thought child fashion, all instinct and intuition, but that did not make him any the less unhappy. In fact, it tended rather in the contrary direction.
It was hardly surprising that during that term John found himself very much to himself, and that his already well-developed taste for being solitary increased under stress of circumstances. At school he was that most uncomfortable of beings, the old new boy. The thirteen-year-old at a new school enters a low form, where he finds others of his kind with whom to congregate; he is not expected to know the little points of etiquette that are so vastly important; friends come to him automatically. But John found himself in the Remove, only one form below the Sixth. The others in his form had long ago formed their own particular sets and cliques, and not one of them had room for John. They had been at no pains to conceal their amusement at the one or two blunders into which he unwittingly fell, and, if the truth must be told, their opinion of him was not increased by the knowledge that he had come from the secondary school a mile away for which they had an unreasoning contempt. John resented their attitude towards him, resented it bitterly, and was unwise enough to show it. That raised the baiting of “young Marble” in the eyes of his schoolfellows from the level of a pastime to that of a duty. His very name, of course, gave them endless opportunities for being witty. It ended, as was inevitable, in John’s flinging away from them in disgust, thanking God he was a day boy, and only coming into contact with his fellows when the elastic rules of compulsory games compelled him to.
===Chinese===
约翰只知道家里出了事，而且是可怕的事；他凭着孩子气的直觉，猜到父亲恨他，便也用同样刻毒的恨回报。父亲近来大把大把撒给他的那些礼物，他收下了，因为此外几乎无路可走；他一句谢也没道，因为他看得分明，马布尔先生最巴望的，莫过于用这份挥霍镇住他，而且——在心底某个角落——还潜着一丝疑心：这些礼物是一种贿赂，买他一个好脾气。
不过，十五岁——快十六岁——的约翰，还不曾把这一切像印在书页上那样条理分明地想清楚。他仍旧用孩子的方式想事，全凭本能和直觉，可这并不因此就让他少受一分苦。说实在的，反倒更苦了。
那一学期里，约翰发觉自己大大地落了单，他那早已相当发达的孤僻性子，又在境况的重压之下愈发滋长，这倒不足为奇。在学校里，他成了众生之中最不自在的一种——年纪偏大的新生。十三岁的孩子进新学校，插在低年级，身边自有一批同类可以扎堆；没人指望他懂得那些天大地大的细碎规矩；朋友自会找上门来。可约翰却被安在只比六年级低一级的班里。同班的同学早就各自结成了固定的小圈子和团伙，没有一个还容得下约翰。他不留神栽过一两回岔子，他们毫不掩饰地拿这个取乐；而且说句实话，听说他是从一英里外那所中学转来的，他们对他的评价又打了折扣，因为对那所学校，他们怀着一种没道理的轻蔑。约翰怨恨他们这种态度，恨得咬牙，又不够老成，居然露在了脸上。这么一来，在同学们眼里，作弄“小马布尔”就从消遣升格成了义务。他的姓氏本身，当然给了他们无穷无尽卖弄俏皮的机会。事情到了头，也无非是约翰满心厌恶地同他们甩手决裂，谢天谢地自己是个走读生，只在必修体育课那些富于弹性的规矩强逼之下，才不得不和同窗们碰面。

===Original===
But unhappily it was just as bad with the fellows with whom he had been friendly at the other school. He went to some pains to look them up and to keep in touch with them, but he was soon conscious of how far they had fallen apart. There was just a trace of suspicion in their attitude towards him﻿—they were always ready to note, and resent, any hint of condescension on his part. Their holidays were different now, for the secondary school had all day on Saturday free, while John was at school in the morning, receiving Wednesday afternoon instead, and the long excursions they were in the habit of making could not be cut down by half to allow him to join them. Besides, did he not have a motor-bicycle, so that he would see no point in sweating with them on a push-bike? And in honest truth John soon found that the savour had gone out of pedal-cycling now that he knew the joys of forty miles an hour on the Giant Twin. Once or twice, at John’s urgent invitation, they had come to 53 Malcolm Road, but John had found himself regretting the invitation as soon as it had been accepted. Those rooms full of gaudy furniture did not make them feel at home. Mr. Marble had been barely civil to them, and had made obvious the fact that he was not entirely sober; John, desperately sensitive, had suspected them of finding much to joke about among themselves at the Marble ménage, and had hated himself for his disloyalty at thinking them capable of such a thing and yet had still gone on thinking it.
All things considered, it was as well that John had the consolation offered him by the Giant Twin. That bulky machine became as good as a brother to him, sharing his troubles and giving him, in the very few mechanical defects it developed, other things to think about than the drunkenness of his father and the disorder of his home.
It is only doing Mr. Marble bare justice to admit that he was happily unconscious of the turmoil in his son’s life. He had other things to think about, too, matters of life and death. The old obsessions were gripping him hard, despite all the distractions offered him in the fact of his having a son at the College, and a daughter at the most expensive school in Berkshire, and a new interest in his life centring in a house in the next road whose gate was adorned with a brass plate bearing the legend “Madame Collins, Modes and Robes.” The evenings were many when the lure of that house was not sufficient to drag him away from his steady watching over the backyard from his coign of vantage in the drawing-room.
===Chinese===
不幸的是，同老学校里那帮要好的伙伴，处得也一样糟。他费了不少事去寻访他们、同他们保持往来，但很快就觉出彼此隔了多远。他们对他的态度里带着一丝戒心——只要他言语间露出一星半点屈尊的意味，他们立刻就会察觉，并且记恨。两边的假期如今也合不上了：中学星期六全天放假，约翰却要上一上午课，改放星期三下午；他们习惯搞的长途远足，没法砍去一半来迁就他入伙。再说，他不是有摩托车了吗？让他跟他们一道蹬自行车挥汗如雨，他还看得上吗？而且说句实话，自从尝过“巨人双生”（Giant Twin）上时速四十英里的痛快，约翰很快便觉得蹬脚踏车索然无味了。有一两回，经约翰再三热切相邀，他们到马尔科姆路53号来过，可邀请刚被接受，约翰就自悔失算了。那几间堆满俗艳家具的屋子，让他们浑身不自在；马布尔先生对他们勉强称得上客气，还让人一眼看穿他并未全醒。约翰敏感得要命，疑心他们背地里正拿马布尔家的居家光景大编笑话，一面又恨自己不念旧谊，竟把他们想得这般不堪——可这念头偏偏还是挥之不去。
通盘想来，亏得“巨人双生”还能给约翰一点安慰。那台笨重的机器简直抵得上他的一个兄弟，分担他的烦恼，还凭它出的那几样寥寥可数的机械毛病，让他有旁的事可以惦记，不必总想着父亲的醉态和家里的乱局。
平心而论，得承认马布尔先生有一点是侥幸的：儿子生活里的这番风波，他全然不知。他自己也有别的事要想，还是生死攸关的事。那些盘踞心头的旧执念照旧把他攥得死紧，尽管眼前有的是排遣——儿子上了学院，女儿进了伯克郡最贵的学校，生活里又添了一样新乐趣，中心在隔壁那条街上的一幢房子，大门上装点着一块黄铜招牌，题着“科林斯夫人，时装女服”（Madame Collins, Modes and Robes）字样。有许多个晚上，那幢房子的吸引力都不足以把他从客厅里他那个瞭望的良位上拽走，他仍旧一动不动地守着后院。

===Original===
Marble had more to lose now: security of income, a house full of Empire furniture, a rapidly-expanding and catholic library of books on criminal matters, as much whisky as he was able to drink, a woman who took a more than friendly interest in him. And the irony of it lay in the fact that the more he had to lose the more anxious he was not to lose it, and the more difficult it became in consequence to enjoy all these priceless possessions. Those summer months fled past him like a maelstrom; he was hardly conscious of what was happening around him. The sweets of life were bitter to his palate, in that they were poisoned by the girding worry that was ever present and ever increasing.
The summer term flashed by. It hardly seemed a week since he had seen Winnie off from Paddington before his wife began to make arrangements for her return. Then she spoke to Mr. Marble about holidays.
“Holidays,” said Mr. Marble, vaguely.
===Chinese===
如今马布尔输不起的东西多了：一笔稳当的收入，一屋子帝政式家具，一批与日俱增、五花八门的犯罪学藏书，敞开供应的威士忌，还有一个对他关怀得超出一般友谊的女人。而讽刺恰恰在此：怕失去的东西越多，就越怕失去，随之也就越难享用这一切无价之宝。夏天那几个月像一个大漩涡，从他身边呼呼卷过；周围在发生什么，他几乎浑然不觉。生活的甜，落到他舌头上全是苦的，因为那无时不在、有增无已的绞心忧虑，早已把甜里渗足了毒。
夏季学期一闪而过。他到帕丁顿给温妮送行，仿佛还不到一个星期，妻子已经张罗起接她回家的事了。接着，她跟马布尔先生提起度假。
“度假？”马布尔先生迷迷瞪瞪地说。

===Original===
“Yes, dear. We’re going away this summer, aren’t we?”
“I don’t know,” said Mr. Marble. “Are we?”
“We didn’t go away at all last summer,” said Mrs. Marble, “and the one before that we only had those few days at Worthing. We can afford it, can’t we?”
“M’yes. I suppose so. But I don’t know what arrangements the office are making.”
===Chinese===
“是啊，亲爱的。今年夏天咱们要出去走走，对不对？”
“我不知道，”马布尔先生说，“要出去吗？”
“去年夏天咱们压根儿哪儿也没去，”马布尔太太说，“前年也不过在沃辛（Worthing）待了那么几天。咱们出得起这钱，不是吗？”
“唔……嗯，大概出得起吧。可我不知道行里打算怎么安排。”

===Original===
“Oh, dear,” said Mrs. Marble. She had looked forward to a holiday this year, if only to get away from having to look after that house and to give her a chance of wearing all the wonderful clothes she had bought.
“I suppose you had better go anyway,” said Mr. Marble, who was absolutely determined that he, for one, was not going to leave that house unguarded. “I’ll look up some nice hotel for you and the kids to go to. I might come down for a bit if I can get away from the office.”
Annie Marble drew her breath in sharply. A hotel! No washing-up, no bother about food, servants to do her bidding; it seemed like a prospect of heaven. There was just a slight momentary fear in her bosom as she thought of possible motives in Mr. Marble’s handsome offer, but her suspicions were too unformed for her to be very worried, and further, there were two possible motives for Mr. Marble wishing to be left in the house, and Mrs. Marble was a little too muddleheaded to disentangle them. Instead, she accepted gratefully.
“But are you sure you’ll be all right, dear?” she said, perforce.
===Chinese===
“唉，”马布尔太太说。她今年一心盼着出去度假，哪怕只为逃开那座得由她照管的房子，也好有机会把买下的那一堆漂亮衣裳穿一穿。
“依我看，你们还是照去不误，”马布尔先生说。他打定了主意，反正他自己绝不肯让那房子没人守着。“我去给你们娘儿仨寻一家好旅馆。要是行里走得开，兴许我也能下来住几天。”
安妮·马布尔猛吸了一口气。旅馆！不用洗洗涮涮，不用为饭菜操心，仆人听凭使唤——这前景简直像天堂一样。想到马布尔先生这份慷慨提议背后说不定另有用心，她心头掠过一丝转瞬即逝的害怕，可她的疑心太不成形，还够不上让她真正发愁；再一说，马布尔先生想独自留在家里，可能的用心理当有两种，马布尔太太的脑子又太糊涂，哪里理得清楚。于是她千恩万谢地应下了。
“可你一个人真的行吗，亲爱的？”她不得不问一句。

===Original===
“Of course I will.” And that settled the matter.
Soon after Winnie came back from school, strangely mature, and rapidly fulfilling her early promise of beauty. She seemed a different girl, somehow. There was an alteration in her speech. Not that she had ever talked broad cockney﻿—her other school friends had always considered her “refined,” but the slight trace of a twang when she used the higher registers had now disappeared, in fact, she did not employ those higher registers at all now. She talked more throatily﻿—“pound-notey” was the terse descriptive phrase popular in that district﻿—and she was much more self-possessed and placid than she had been when she went away. Mr. Marble was pleased, as pleased as he could be at that period﻿—he was going through rather a bad time just then﻿—and Mrs. Marble, as was inevitable, was heartily sorry. Winnie had grown away from her.
But neither of her parents, much as they fussed about her when she arrived, noticed her tiny lift of the eyebrows as she entered their wonderful dining-room, with its wonderful mosaic table. Winnie had now had experience of what good rooms look like, and to her, the half-forgotten gaudiness of the gilt furniture in terrible contrast to the faded wallpaper was indescribably vulgar.
===Chinese===
“当然行。”事情就这么定了。
不久温妮就从学校回来了，出奇地懂事成熟，小时候透出的那点美人底子，正飞快地长成真正的姿色。不知怎么，她像换了一个人。她说话也变了调。倒不是她从前满口伦敦土腔——从前学校的伙伴们一向认为她“文雅”——而是她一提高声调便隐隐带出的那点儿腔没了；实际上，如今她压根儿不用那种高声调了。她说话嗓音更沉了——用那一带流行的一句简截考语，叫“镑票腔”（pound-notey）——人也比离家时沉着淡定多了。马布尔先生很满意——就他当时的心境而言已算难得，那阵子他正熬着一段颇不好过的日子——而马布尔太太呢，不出所料，打心眼里难受。温妮长大了，跟她疏远了。
可是她到家的时候，父母尽管围着她忙前忙后，却谁也没留意，她走进那间漂亮的饭厅、见到那张漂亮的马赛克桌面时，眉毛轻轻往上一挑。温妮如今见识过真正的好屋子是什么样了；在她眼里，那半已被淡忘的镀金家具的俗艳，衬着褪色的墙纸，俗气得没法形容。

===Original===
Later she hinted as much to her mother, but her comments were not received very gratefully. Mrs. Marble at once began to fidget with her sewing﻿—a sure sign that she was embarrassed.
“Your father has one or two odd fancies, dear,” she said, fumbling, blushing and stammering. “I shouldn’t mention it to him if I were you. He doesn’t like the idea of having a lot of people about the house, as we should have to have if we had any decorations done. Besides,”﻿—she bridled a little, for she was as proud as was her husband of the gilt furniture.﻿—“I’m sure this room looks very well indeed. I’m sure nobody in this road has anything half as good in their house. I don’t expect there are many rooms like this in London, even. And, of course, every room in the house is done in the same way. Madame Collins says it’s as good as the Louvre, and she ought to know, seeing she’s been there.”
And that at once placed the matter beyond argument, for Madame Collins was by now a great friend of the family’s. But Winnie would not have argued about it, anyway. She merely made a mental note of the fact and said no more about it. That was typical of Winnie.
===Chinese===
后来她就此向母亲做了些暗示，可她的话没换来多少感激。马布尔太太立刻摆弄起针线活来——这是她发窘的可靠标志。
“你父亲有一两样怪念头，亲爱的，”她说着，手足无措，红着脸，结结巴巴。“我要是你，就不去同他提。他不喜欢家里围一大堆人，可要是重新装修，就少不了人进进出出。再说呢，”——她微微扬起下巴，因为对那套镀金家具，她的得意不亚于她丈夫——“我敢说这间屋子瞧着确实很好。这条街上谁家的东西也及不上咱家的一半好。我看整个伦敦，像这样的房间也找不出几间。还有呢，家里每一间屋子都是照这个样子布置的。科林斯夫人（Madame Collins）说，这儿跟卢浮宫（Louvre）一样气派，她可去过卢浮宫，她说这话作得了数。”
这话一出，事情便再没有争论的余地，因为科林斯夫人如今已是这一家的大好友。不过温妮原本也不打算争论。她只在心里记下一笔，别的不再提了。温妮就是这样的人。

===Original===
But Mrs. Marble had by now become well started on the subject that absorbed so many of her few thoughts.
“You mustn’t think any the less of your father, dear, because﻿—because﻿—he is a little odd at times. He has a great deal to worry him, you know, and I’m sure you ought to be grateful to him for all he’s done.”
“Of course I am,” said Winnie, sweetly. It had never occurred to her to be grateful.
===Chinese===
可马布尔太太这时已经顺着那个话题开了头——她那点本来就少的心思，倒有大半叫它占了去。
“你可不能因为你父亲——因为——他有时候有点儿怪，就看轻了他，亲爱的。他操心的事多着呢，你知道的。他为你出了这么多力，我看你该感激他才是。”
“我当然感激。”温妮甜甜地说。感激这个念头，她脑子里从来没闪过。

===Original===
“I’m so glad. I﻿—I was a little afraid that when you came back from your fine school you might find that﻿—that﻿—everything was not quite as you would like it.”
“Do you mean because father drinks?”
“Winnie!” Mrs. Marble showed in her expression how shocked she was at this calling of a spade a spade.
===Chinese===
“那就好了。我——我还担心，你从你那好学校回来，会觉得——觉得——家里样样都不合你的意呢。”
“你是说爸爸喝酒的事？”
“温妮！”马布尔太太满脸的神情，显出这句直捅捅的实话叫她多么吃惊。

===Original===
“But he does, mother, now doesn’t he?”
“Y﻿—es, I suppose he does. But not very much, dear. Not more than you could expect, seeing how much his business worries him. And you didn’t ought to speak about it like that, Winnie. It doesn’t sound nice.”
Poor Mrs. Marble had by now nearly as much to worry her as her husband had. It might almost be worse in her case, for she did not know really what it was she had to worry about, and her not over-exuberant imagination could not allow her even to guess. And the worry of defending her husband to her children on unnamed and unguessed-at charges was nearly as great as everything else put together.
===Chinese===
“可他就是喝了嘛，妈妈，是不是？”
“是——是啊，喝了是喝了。可喝得不多，亲爱的。他行里烦心的事那么多，喝这点儿也算不得过分。还有，你不该那样说话，温妮。不好听。”
可怜的马布尔太太，如今要操心的事几乎同她丈夫一样多了。她的情形说不定还更糟，因为她压根儿不知道自己操心的究竟是什么，而她那本就不丰沛的想象力，连让她猜一猜都够不上。这份煎熬——对着儿女为丈夫辩白，罪名却说不出、猜不着——几乎抵得上其余一切加在一起。

===Original===
For her children were small comfort to her now. John was gawky and shy, and she could not know of the love he bore her, especially as the memory of the times when he had come to defend her from her husband stood as a barrier between them which neither of them had the moral courage to surmount at a rush. And Winnie﻿—even Mrs. Marble felt this﻿—was just a little bit *superior* nowadays.
Yet even Winnie was for the moment mollified by the announcement that they were going to stay for a month at the Grand Pavilion Hotel at a very fashionable south coast resort. That would be much nicer than staying all the holidays at Malcolm Road, and it would be something to tell the girls about when she went back to school. Some of these might go to France, some to Italy, but there would be few who would spend their holidays at such a place as the Grand Pavilion Hotel. Their parents would have more sense.
And the packing, and the preparation! Winnie helped her mother sort out her astonishing wardrobe. In the tiny cupboards of the bedroom upstairs, where the gilt cupids climbed eternally about the big bed, there were heaps of the most assorted clothes it was possible to conceive. Mingled with the frightfully expensive costumes, there were old rags dating back from the dark ages before the rise of the franc. Mrs. Marble, apparently, wore indifferently the pastel shade silk underclothes bought in Bond Street and the wool and cotton mixture garments, neither decorative nor useful, which she had had before the arrival of these others. The explanation was really simple. Never in her life before had Mrs. Marble had any clothes to give away; they were all worn to rags long before she could spare them. She could not get into the habit of disposing of her older wardrobe. In fact, it is much to be doubted whether she had ever contemplated giving away garments in which there still remained six months’ wear, according to earlier standards of economy. And all the clothes, worn and unworn, were badly looked after, unbrushed and hung on hooks without hangers.
===Chinese===
因为如今儿女给她的安慰也有限。约翰笨手笨脚，见了人怯生生的，她无从知晓儿子心里爱她；更何况，那几回他挺身护住她、对抗父亲的记忆，横在母子中间，成了堵墙，谁也没有那股道德勇气，能一口气翻过去。而温妮——这一点连马布尔太太自己都觉出来了——如今就是有那么一点儿*自命不凡*。
然而，一听说全家要去南部海岸一处非常时髦的海滨胜地，在大亭阁酒店（Grand Pavilion Hotel）住上一个月，连温妮也暂且消了气。这可比整个假期都待在马尔科姆路强多了，而且回学校以后，也有得跟女同学们讲了。她们有的也许去法国，有的去意大利，可假期住得进大亭阁酒店这等地方的人，怕是没有几个。人家的父母更有头脑。
还有那装箱打包、那一番筹备呢！温妮帮母亲归置她那惊人的衣橱。楼上卧室的小柜子里——镀金的小爱神绕着那张大床攀爬了不知多少时日——堆着所能设想的五花八门、各式各样的衣裳。同那些贵得吓人的衣裙混在一处的，还有法郎腾贵之前那黑暗年代传下来的破衣旧衫。看那情形，邦德街（Bond Street）买来的淡彩丝绸内衣，同早年间那些既不中看又不中用的毛棉混纺衣裳，马布尔太太穿起来全无所谓。缘由其实简单：马布尔太太这辈子从没有过穿不完、可以送人的衣服；从前每一件都是早早就磨成了破布条，根本轮不到她匀出来。处理旧衣的习惯，她怎么也养不成。说实在的，按她早年那套节俭标准，一件还剩六个月可穿的衣裳就送人——这种事她想只怕都没想过。而且所有的衣服，穿过的没穿过的，都没人好好照管，从不清刷，就那么挂在钩子上，连个衣架都不用。

===Original===
Even one term at a boarding-school had taught Winnie to do better than this, and for two hectic days she took her mother’s clothes in hand, sorting and folding, ruthlessly relegating some to the rag-basket, wondering over others. It was beyond her powers to imagine her mother in underclothing of orange silk or eau de nile; it was almost beyond it to imagine her well dressed at all, but Winnie contrived to arrange that her mother looked more like the mothers whom she had seen at the school occasionally. Mrs. Marble was almost tearfully grateful.
“I don’t seem to have time to do all this,” she said. “And﻿—and﻿—it doesn’t seem worth it, sometimes. Your father is a very busy man, you know, dear.”
The cupids that climbed about the big Empire bed had climbed in vain for months now, that was what Mrs. Marble implied, although she would never have dreamed of hinting as much to her daughter.
===Chinese===
哪怕只在寄宿学校待了一个学期，也让温妮学会了比这高明的打理。整整两天，她忙得团团转，把母亲的衣服全数接手过来，分拣、叠放，毫不留情地把一些发落到碎布篮里，对着另一些啧啧称怪。要想象母亲穿上橙色或尼罗河水色（eau de nil）的丝绸内衣，超出了她的本事；要想象母亲穿得体面，也几乎同样超出了她的本事。可温妮还是设法张罗得让母亲看上去像样了些，像她偶尔在学校里见到的那些母亲了。马布尔太太感激得几乎要落泪。
“我好像总腾不出工夫弄这些，”她说，“再说——再说——有时候，好像也不值当。你父亲是个大忙人呢，你知道的，亲爱的。”
绕着那张帝政式大床攀爬的小爱神们，如今已经白白爬了好几个月——马布尔太太话里就是这个意思，尽管她做梦也想不到同女儿把话点到这一步。

===Original===
And in all this rush to put Mrs. Marble’s wardrobe in order Winnie herself was not forgotten. She had to have new frocks as well, for her stay at the Grand Pavilion Hotel, and Winnie’s taste, which was allowed almost complete freedom, was at its best a little juvenile. Mrs. Marble was a shade frightened when she saw some of the things Winnie had chosen, but it was beyond her to protest. She had only the haziest idea as to what was the correct wear for a girl of fifteen at the Grand Pavilion Hotel.
“There’s a lot in being bobbed,” said Winnie to herself in the glass, attentively studying her features while her mother was safely out of the way. “No one can ever really tell how old you are. If I weren’t bobbed, I wouldn’t have my hair up, and then anyone could tell. But as it is, with my new frocks and everything, I think I’ll have rather a nice time this holiday. And there’ll be something to tell them about when I get back to school.”
Winnie and her mother were pleasantly excited when the cab came to take them to Victoria. John was not with them. He had decided to go on alone on the Giant Twin, despite Winnie’s faint protest that a motor-bicycle was highly vulgar.
Mr. Marble, too, was pleasantly excited when he waved goodbye to them. For reasons of his own he was glad to know that his daughter was out of the way. He had felt uncomfortable in her presence. That three months, three months of good food and of close intimacy with people who never had the slightest difficulty with their aitches, had caused her to grow away from her family at a surprising rate. Mr. Marble was not even comfortable with his daughter when he was drunk. And every moment he feared lest she should demand that they should move to a bigger and better house, or failing that, that they should have the present house redecorated and arranged as nearly as possible like the houses of the girls she had known at school. It was not the expense that Mr. Marble feared. Instead, he was thrown into agonies of terror at the thought of workmen nosing around his house, and of piles of ladders and boards heaped up in his backyard. The foot of a ladder might easily dig some inches deep into the soft soil of the barren flowerbed.
When he was sober, Mr. Marble had his suspicions, too, that his children were neither grateful for nor impressed by the benefit he had heaped upon them. He even suspected them of not admiring the mosaic table as much as they should have done. In a ferment of self-pity he realized that he was not getting full return for all his outlay. When he could blame it on to circumstances he did not mind so much; but there were a few aching moments, when the whisky had failed to bite as it should, when it was forced home on him that it was his fault. There were times when he could not visualize himself, as he usually could, as the triumphant criminal, surmounting all difficulties, overriding all obstacles, tearing success out of the very teeth of failure. Instead, there came moments when he saw himself in his true colours, as the cornered rat he was, struggling with the courage of desperation against the fate that would inevitably close upon him sooner or later. When these black periods came he would clutch hurriedly at his glass and drain it thirstily. Thank God, there was always whisky in exchange for his money﻿—and Marguerite Collins.
===Chinese===
在这阵替马布尔太太归置衣橱的忙乱里，温妮自己也没被忘掉。去住大亭阁酒店，她也得添置新衣裳，而温妮的品位几乎被放任全权，它发挥到极致，也总带几分孩子气。看见温妮挑中的某些物件，马布尔太太微微有些发怵，可抗议超出了她的能力。十五岁的姑娘住大亭阁酒店，怎样穿戴才算得体——她心里只有一团顶模糊的影子。
“剪短发的好处可多着呢，”温妮对着镜子自言自语，趁母亲走得远远的，端端正正细瞧着自己的眉眼。“谁也说不出你到底多大年纪。要不是我剪了短发，就得把头发盘起来，那谁看了都心里有数。可如今这样，配上我的新裙子，再加上种种，我看这个假期会过得挺痛快。回学校以后，也有得跟她们讲的了。”
马车来接她们去维多利亚车站（Victoria）的时候，温妮和母亲都怀着一种快活的兴奋。约翰没有同行。他决定独自骑“巨人双生”去，尽管温妮轻轻抗议了一句，说摩托车俗气得很。
马布尔先生朝她们挥手道别的时候，心里也怀着一种快活的兴奋。出于他自己的缘故，得知女儿走开了，他很高兴。有她在跟前，他就浑身不自在。那三个月——三个月的精美伙食，三个月同一帮咬字吐音、连一个 h 都从不吞掉的人们亲密过从——使她以惊人的速度同这家人疏远了。就是喝醉的时候，马布尔先生在女儿跟前也不自在。他无时无刻不提心吊胆，唯恐她开口要求搬进一幢更大更好的房子；退一步，也唯恐她要求把眼下这幢重新装修，布置得尽量像她在学校认识的那些女生家里的样子。马布尔先生怕的不是这笔开销。叫他吓到魂飞魄散的，是想到工匠们在他房子里东嗅西探，想到一堆堆梯子和木板垛在后院里。梯子脚往那块寸草不生的花圃松软的泥土里一戳，就是好几英寸深。
清醒的时候，马布尔先生也犯嘀咕：儿女们对他堆到他们头上的种种好处，既不感激，也并不觉得了不起。他甚至疑心，他们连那张马赛克桌子都欠着应有的叹赏。他一阵阵自怜得心里翻腾：这一番抛掷，竟没收回十足的回报。能把这笔账推给境遇的时候，他还不太在意；可总有那么几个酸痛的时刻——威士忌下了肚，却没有像它应该的那样咬得上来——他被迫认清：错在他自己。平日里，他总还能把自己想象成得胜的罪犯，翻越一切困难，踏平一切障碍，从失败的牙缝里生生撕出成功来；可这样的时刻，有时竟想象不成了。取而代之的是另一些时刻，他看见了自己的真面目——一只被逼进绝境的老鼠，凭着亡命之勇，同那个迟早要罩落下来的命运挣扎。每逢这些黑暗的时刻袭来，他就慌忙抓过酒杯，渴也似的灌个见底。感谢上帝，他的钱总能换来威士忌——还有玛格丽特·科林斯（Marguerite Collins）。

## X

===Original===
Madame Collins was a highly successful intriguer, now that she had gained experience and the poise given by experience. To no one in the suburbs, not even to the milk roundsman, is given the opportunity for gossip which the suburban dressmaker enjoys. After the costume has been fitted, and changing naturally from the easy and fertile subject of clothes, there comes a time in every interview with every customer when local affairs must be discussed. Some merely talk parish shop, and with these Madame Collins had to walk warily, but most are only too willing to discuss neighbours, especially to a sympathetic audience of one, and that a woman. Madame Collins heard all about Mr. Marble’s newly acquired wealth almost as soon as he acquired it. She had noted the information mentally; rich men were always desirable acquaintances, especially to a woman utterly wearied of life in a suburb on very little money after the varied experiences she had enjoyed as a girl in a district occupied by English troops during the war.
The historic meeting with Mr. Marble on the day that the furniture arrived was only partly planned. Madame Collins had been walking along Malcolm Road on perfectly legitimate business when she had seen all the massy gilt being carried in, and she had been impressed. They must have cost a great deal of money, even if they were in abominable taste, and then when she had seen Mr. Marble himself, tiepin, wristwatch, cigarette case, well-cut clothes and all, her mind was at once made up. There must be a great deal of truth in what she had heard about his money. It was the easiest thing in the world to scrape acquaintance with him after that.
Then in a week’s time what Madame Collins did not know about the Marble ménage was hardly worth knowing﻿—save for the, to her, unimportant detail about a certain transaction consummated twenty months before in the Marbles’ dining-room. Neighbours had already hinted that all was not well between Mr. Marble and his wife, and that was all the knowledge Madame Collins asked for. A rich man estranged from his wife, and that wife simple enough to be easily hoodwinked, living conveniently near, meant all the colour and all the money that Madame Collins’ drab life demanded﻿—especially seeing that he was obviously a raw hand in dealings with women, and had not yet had his money long enough to be spoilt by it.
===Chinese===
科林斯夫人（Madame Collins）如今算得上手段老到的猎手，既有了阅历，也有了阅历带来的从容。在郊区，谁也得不到郊区女裁缝那样的闲话机会——连挨家送奶的都不成。衣裳试定之后，从衣服这个轻松而源源不绝的话题自然而然一转，每一次会面、每一位主顾，总有一个时刻非谈谈本地的事不可。有些人张口闭口无非教区里的那点子事，对这类人科林斯夫人只得步步留神；但大多数人巴不得议论议论邻居，尤其是对着一位善解人意的听众，何况还是个女人。马布尔先生新近到手一笔钱，这事的前前后后，科林斯夫人几乎在他到手的同时便听了个遍。她把这条消息记在心里：有钱人永远值得结交——尤其对这样一个女人——战争年代她还是姑娘，在英军占领下的地区享过种种热闹世面，如今却困在郊区靠一点点钱度日，早把这种日子腻烦透了。
家具进门那天与马布尔先生那场历史性的会面，只有一半出于蓄谋。科林斯夫人本是为一桩正经营生走在马尔科姆路（Malcolm Road）上，恰逢那些沉甸甸的镀金物件一件件往宅子里抬，她不由得看在眼里、动在心头。这些东西纵然俗恶得可以，价钱必定吓人；待见了马布尔先生本人——领带夹、腕表、烟盒、剪裁合体的衣裳，一应俱全——她当即拿定了主意。传闻说他有钱，这话多半不是空穴来风。打这以后，同他攀交情便是天下顶容易的事了。
不出一个星期，马布尔家的内情，凡值得一知的，科林斯夫人已尽知无遗——唯独一个细节除外：二十个月前在马布尔家饭厅里办妥的一桩“交易”；不过在她看来这细节无关紧要。邻居们早有暗示，马布尔先生同太太不睦；而科林斯夫人想知道的，也尽在于此。一个同妻子离心离德的有钱人，那妻子又老实得可以任人蒙蔽，住得还这么近便——这便意味着科林斯夫人那灰扑扑的生活所需要的一切色彩与一切金钱——何况他分明是个周旋女人的生手，钱到手的日子又短，还没被钱养骄。

===Original===
To Mrs. Marble, Marguerite Collins had borne Greek gifts. She had offered her a friendship which the lonely woman had eagerly accepted. She had invited her to her little house in the next street, and there had introduced her to her husband, proving that she was a perfectly respectable married woman. Annie Marble did not appreciate what a cipher Collins himself was.
For Collins was a dull and a tragic figure. He was cursed with an intense sensitiveness for music combined with complete absence of creative talent. All his life, with the exception of a violent interval in France during the last year of the war culminating in his marriage to Marguerite, he had earned his living tuning pianos. He was a very good piano tuner, and highly prized by the firm that employed him. Therein lay the tragedy. For the perfect piano tuner must never play the piano. If he does, he loses half his worth as a tuner. His ear loses that shade of anxious accuracy that makes him the perfect tuner. So Collins, thirsty for music, inexpressibly moved by music, spent his life in a piano factory tuning pianos, eternally tuning pianos. No wonder that Marguerite Collins found life drab.
Collins accepted the advent of the Marbles into his wife’s life with the lack of interest that he evinced towards everything. He talked with weary politeness to Marble himself on the one or two occasions that the latter had accompanied Annie there. But he probably did not know their names. After all these years of married life he had ceased to be interested in his wife’s doings. Marguerite, red-haired, brown-eyed, tempestuously passionate, and with her native peasant craftiness to guide her, was by no means the ideal wife for him. By that time they both knew it.
===Chinese===
对马布尔太太，玛格丽特·科林斯（Marguerite Collins）献上的是希腊人的礼物。她向这位寂寞的女人奉出一段友谊，那女人求之不得地领受了。她请她到自己隔街的小宅里做客，又在那儿把她引见给丈夫，证明自己是个规规矩矩的已婚妇人。至于科林斯（Collins）本人是何等无足轻重的一个影子，安妮·马布尔（Annie Marble）是领略不到的。
因为科林斯本是个沉闷而悲惨的人物。老天爷咒罚他，给了他一副对音乐极度敏锐的耳朵，却半点创造的才分也不给。他一辈子——除了战争最后一年在法国那段狂暴的插曲、以娶玛格丽特告终之外——都靠给钢琴调音谋生。他是位顶好的调音师，深受雇用他的那家琴行的器重。悲剧恰在于此。因为完美的调音师万万弹不得钢琴。一旦弹了，他作为调音师的身价便折了一半；他的耳朵便失掉那一丝战战兢兢的精确，而正是那一丝精确成就了他完美的调音师。于是科林斯，渴着音乐，被音乐感动得说不出话，就在钢琴厂里调琴，调了一辈子，永远永远地调下去。无怪玛格丽特·科林斯只觉得生活灰暗。
马布尔一家走进他妻子的生活，科林斯以他对万事万物一贯的淡漠听之任之。有一两回马布尔亲自陪安妮上门，他也只带着倦怠的客气同马布尔寒暄几句。但他多半连人家的名字都记不得。婚龄这么多年，他早不关心妻子的行止了。玛格丽特，红头发，棕眼睛，情热如暴风骤雨，又有乡下农妇的狡黠给她领路，怎么也算不得他的理想妻子。到这时，两口子心里都明白了。

===Original===
Marguerite played her new capture with dexterous skill﻿—not that there was much skill needed, seeing that Marble’s greatest wish at the moment was to be her captive, provided no one else knew about it. There had been glances from her hot brown eyes into which Mr. Marble was at liberty to weave all sorts of meanings. There had been strange coincidences when she had happened to be out shopping just when Mr. Marble was walking home from the bus that brought him from the station. There had been times, anxiously awaited, when he had escorted her home after she had been to Malcolm Road calling in the evening. Then, in the comforting darkness, she had walked close at his side so that he could feel her warmth against his. She had long ago decided that she would yield to him, but she was not going to yield too readily. She wanted money as well as intrigue, money that she could put into the bank account that stood in her name alone, in which her husband had no share. Peasant avarice was in her blood, the avarice that demands hard money and plenty of it﻿—enough too, to enable her to forsake her spiritless husband and live her own life in Rouen or even Paris.
But she nearly miscalculated, owing to her not being in possesion of all the data. There came a time when in place of the pleasant lunches in town﻿—when she had to be up buying her materials﻿—a little dinner was suggested. Marguerite had the whole scene in her mind’s eye. There would be a private room, and a discreet waiter, and plenty of good wine﻿—Burgundy, she thought would be best. Then, with Mr. Marble well warmed and comforted, would come the tale of unexpected business losses and of pressing debts. Mr. Marble might not believe it; he would be welcome not to. But he would offer a loan, nevertheless, and when she had it safely in her purse, she would melt with gratitude towards him. She would be overcome, yielding, tender. Then she would hear no more about that “loan.” But the farce would be necessary, nevertheless. Otherwise Mr. Marble might get unwelcome ideas into his head﻿—that it was solely by his charms that he had overcome her resistance. Marguerite preferred to have matters on a sound business footing.
At first everything went according to plan. Marguerite arrived only ten minutes late, just enough to make Mr. Marble anxious and yet not long enough seriously to annoy him. And at the sight of her all anxiety vanished. She was in splendid evening dress, low-cut and dazzling, so that Mr. Marble caught his breath as he looked at her. He himself was in a lounge suit, as was necessary with a wife at home who would look for some explanation should he do anything so extraordinary as to go out in dress clothes.
===Chinese===
玛格丽特摆布她这个新到手的猎物，手法轻车熟路——其实也用不着什么手法，因为马布尔先生此刻最大的心愿就是做她的俘虏，只要旁人不知情便好。她那双滚烫的棕色眼睛一瞥又一瞥，任凭马布尔先生往里头织进千般意思。总有些奇巧的凑合：她偏偏出门购物，偏偏马布尔先生正从火车站搭公共汽车回来，徒步走回家去。也有些叫他望眼欲穿的时辰：她傍晚到马尔科姆路串完门，由他护送回家。于是，在令人心安的夜色里，她紧紧傍着他走，教他觉得出她的暖意贴着自己。她早已打定主意要委身于他，却不想委身得太快。她要的不止是偷情，还要钱——存得进她独自立户的银行账户的钱，丈夫分不着半个子儿的钱。庄稼人的贪欲在她血里流着，那种贪要的是硬邦邦的现钱，还得要多多的——多到够她甩下那没精打采的丈夫，去鲁昂（Rouen）、甚至巴黎过自己的日子。
可她险些失算，因为她没有掌握全部底数。到了某一天，城里那些惬意的午餐——那时节她得趁白天进城采买衣料——被一顿小小的晚餐顶替了。玛格丽特心里的整场戏早排演停当。要有一间雅座，一名嘴严的侍者，还要足量的好酒——她想，勃艮第（Burgundy）顶相宜。待到马布尔先生酒酣身暖、通体舒泰，便有一番生意上飞来的亏空、债主的紧逼可讲。信不信由他；不信也无妨。但他总归会提出借一笔钱，而等那笔钱稳稳落进她的手袋，她便会对他感激涕零。她会情不自禁、温顺可人、柔肠百转。此后那笔“借款”自然再无音信。但这出戏仍旧非演不可。不然马布尔先生脑子里怕会钻进些不中听的念头——以为全凭他的魅力才降伏了她的抗拒。玛格丽特做事，宁肯一切都立在稳稳当当的生意根基上。
起初一切都照着计划走。玛格丽特只迟到了十分钟——恰好够叫马布尔先生心里发急，又不至于叫他当真着恼。待她一露面，焦灼登时烟消云散。她一身华彩的晚装，低胸敞领，顾盼生辉，马布尔先生一望之下，气都屏住了。他自己穿的却是一套寻常便服——也只能如此，家里有个太太，他若做出穿礼服出门这样破天荒的事，势必要被追问个所以然。

===Original===
There had been no difficulty about obtaining a room to themselves; the waiter had been discreet, the wine had been good, the dinner excellent. Marguerite noted with pleasure that Marble ate hardly anything. He seemed to be in a fever.
Marble sat at the table. He was paying no attention to the woman opposite him. Coffee and liqueur brandy stood awaiting his leisure. The waiter had gone for good, with his bill paid. Marguerite was about to bring her carefully-rehearsed story into action, when she noticed the look on his face. He was staring, staring hard, past her at the opposite wall. In that direction lay the door that led into the tawdry bedroom, but clearly he was not thinking about that. The stare was a stare of agony.
Marble had felt uneasy almost as soon as the arrival of Madame Collins had set free his thoughts to wander where they would. He had a sudden awful suspicion that while he was dallying here someone was interfering with the flowerbed in his backyard. It would be poetic justice if it were so﻿—newspaper poetic justice. He could imagine their baldly moral comments in the flaming reports that would appear in the next day’s papers. Cases of that kind, where there had been long concealment of the body, were nearly as popular in the newspapers as cases where the body had been cut up or burnt. And he would be dragged away. Then﻿—his thoughts jumped back to half-remembered fragments of a stray copy of “The Ballad of Reading Gaol” which had come into his possession. There was something about “the black dock’s dreadful pen.” His mind hovered there for a space, and then fled on through all the horrible verses about “the silent men who watch him night and day” and about crossing his own coffin as he moves into the hideous shed. Then writhing into his mind came the lines about having a cloth upon his face and a noose about his neck. Marble’s breath came croakingly through his dry, parted lips. In imagination he already had a cloth upon his face. He could feel it, stifling, blinding him, while the officials padded warily about him making all ready. Marble struggled in his chair.
===Chinese===
单要一间雅座毫不费难；侍者嘴严，酒是好酒，菜也极佳。玛格丽特满意地看在眼里：马布尔几乎什么也没吃。他像是在发着烧。
马布尔坐在桌边，全没把对面的女人放在心上。咖啡和利口白兰地摆在手边，只等他从容受用。侍者收清了账，早已一去不回。玛格丽特正要把那套精心排练的说辞搬上场，忽然留意到他的脸色。他正瞪着眼，死死地瞪着，越过她直望对面的墙。那个方向有一扇门，通向那间俗艳的卧室，可他分明想的不是这个。那瞪视是万分痛楚的瞪视。
科林斯夫人一驾到，马布尔的思绪便得了自由，爱往哪儿飘就往哪儿飘，他几乎立刻就不安起来。他忽然起了一个可怕的疑心：自己在这儿温存厮混的当口，会不会正有人在他后院的花坛底下刨弄。倘若果真如此，那倒真是诗意的报应——报纸上的诗意报应。他都能想见第二天各报那火爆的报道里，一行行光秃秃的道德训诫。这一类案子，尸身藏匿经年的，在报纸上几乎同碎尸案、焚尸案一样叫座。然后他就会被拖走。跟着——他的思绪跳回到一册偶然到手的《雷丁监狱之歌》（The Ballad of Reading Gaol）里那些记得半真半假的零星残句上。有一句好像是“黑色被告席那可怕的围栏”。他的心神在那里盘桓了片刻，又仓皇逃开，一路穿过那些可怕的诗行——“日夜监视他的沉默的人们”，还有他跨过自己的棺材、走进那座阴森刑棚的句子。跟着，扭着身子挤进他脑海里的，是脸上蒙布、颈上套索的那几句。马布尔的喘息从他干裂张开的嘴唇里嘶哑地挤出。想象里，他脸上已经蒙了布。他觉得出它，闷着他，蒙得他眼前漆黑，差役们蹑手蹑脚地围着他忙活，把一切料理停当。马布尔在椅子里挣扎起来。

===Original===
Madame Collins’ voice came to him, apparently from an immense distance, asking him if he were ill. Even then he only came partially to himself. At her anxious questionings he only laughed. Annie Marble had heard laughter like that once in her life. It was a mirthless sound, a disgusting sound. Marguerite shrank back in horror, crossing herself. Marble’s chair scraped hideously as he dragged himself up from the table.
“Home,” he said, leaning first on the table, then on her shoulder, for support, “home, quickly.”
Downstairs they went together, he with dragging steps yet striving to hasten, she with horror and fright in her eyes. They went home as fast as a taxicab could take them. Mr. Marble’s fears had, of course, been baseless. Not a soul had been in the backyard. But he could not explain to Marguerite Collins about the silly fright he had given himself. Nor, on the other hand, could he convince himself that his fears were unjustified. The obsession was growing. Mr. Marble was experiencing more and more reluctance to spend his spare time anywhere whence he could not keep his eye on that backyard of his. And yet he lusted after Marguerite Collins of the warm hair as he lusted after little else. That was why he was so pleasantly excited to see his family drive off to Victoria on their way to the Grand Pavilion Hotel.
===Chinese===
科林斯夫人的声音传到他耳边，仿佛来自老远老远的地方，问他是不是不舒服。即便那时，他也只回过半截神来。她焦灼地连连追问，他只是笑。安妮·马布尔这辈子听见过一回那样的笑。那是一种全无欢乐的声音，一种令人作呕的声音。玛格丽特吓得缩起身子，在胸前画了个十字。马布尔从桌边拖起自己，椅子刮出一声刺耳的怪响。
“回家，”他说，先撑住桌子，又撑住她的肩膀借力，“回家，快。”
两人一同下了楼，他步子拖沓却拼命催快，她眼里满是惊骇惶恐。出租汽车能跑多快，他们便多快地到了家。马布尔先生的惊惧，当然，纯属无根无据：后院里一个人影也无。可他没法向玛格丽特·科林斯解释自己这场痴愚的虚惊；另一头，他也说服不了自己，这惊惧没有道理。心魔在一天天长大。马布尔先生越来越不愿意把闲暇的光阴花在任何照看不到自家后院的地方。然而他对那一头暖发的玛格丽特·科林斯，又是那样的欲火炽烈，胜过他念想的大多数东西。正因如此，眼见一家人登车往维多利亚车站（Victoria）去、奔大亭阁酒店（Grand Pavilion Hotel），他才那样喜滋滋地心花怒放。

===Original===
Marguerite was pleased, too. She was a woman of strong common sense, and rapidly recovered from the fright she had had.
For Marble began the happiest month he had known since James Medland’s visit. There was no bothering family to worry him at all. He cooked his own breakfast after a fashion, and his other meals he had out save for the occasions when he bought cooked food and consumed it at home. The evenings were very long and pleasant at first. He could sit and brood in the drawing-room with a book about crimes on his knee, drinking as he wished, without the worried eyes of his wife to cause him annoyance, and even though sometimes his thoughts began to stray towards detection and failure he was able at that period to change them, in consequence of the new interest he had. For sometimes as it grew dark, there would come a hurried little tap at the door, and he would go and open it to Marguerite Collins. She would come in superb, wonderful, ripe to rottenness, and then Marble would forget his troubles entirely for the time. He did not appreciate her taste in wine, but he saw that there was always wine for her. For himself he was content with whisky, and the time would fly by. At the end of the evening there would be a passing of a little bundle of money﻿—trust Marble to deal in cheques as little as possible under these circumstances﻿—and Marguerite would slip out as quietly as she came.
They were strange evenings, half dream, half nightmare. By a queer twist of thought, Marble found that the sharing of his house and of its view of the backyard was oddly comforting when Marguerite was with him. He could lose himself within the nest of her warm, white arms more completely than had ever been possible to him before. Her dark eyes were like velvet with passion, and her little gasps of love, only half simulated, led him on and on into all the mazy, misty byways of sodden animalism. From Marguerite at least he had value for his money.
===Chinese===
玛格丽特也正中下怀。她是个常识强健的女人，很快便从那场惊吓里回了神。
对马布尔来说，这是自詹姆斯·梅德兰（James Medland）登门以来他享过的最快活的一个月。再没有拖累人的家庭来烦他。早饭他马马虎虎自己对付，其余各餐都在外头吃，除非偶尔买了熟食带回家受用。起初，夜晚漫长而惬意。他可以坐在客厅里冥想，膝头摊着一本罪案的书，想喝多少喝多少，没有妻子那双含忧带虑的眼睛来聒噪他。即便有时心思开始飘向败露和完蛋，在那段日子他也拨得转来——全亏他有了这份新的乐趣。因为有时天色擦黑，门口会响起急急的轻轻一叩，他便去开门迎玛格丽特·科林斯。她进得门来，雍容华艳，妙不可言，熟艳得近乎糜烂，一时间马布尔便把烦恼忘了个干净。他不懂她那套对酒的讲究，却保证她回回有酒。他自己有威士忌便知足，于是光阴飞也似的过去。每到夜阑，便有一小卷钞票悄悄易手——这种情形下能不开支票，马布尔是绝不开支票的——玛格丽特便如来时一样，悄没声地溜出门去。
那是一个个奇特的夜晚，半是绮梦，半是魇寐。出于一种心思的怪弯，马布尔发觉，玛格丽特伴着他的时候，与人分享这所宅子、连同这扇望得见后院的窗，竟莫名地叫他心安。他可以在她暖白手臂围成的巢里沉溺，沉溺得比他此生任何时候都更彻底。她那双黑眼睛情欲熏蒸，天鹅绒似的，她一声声爱的轻喘——只做了一半的假——引着他步步向前，走进沉湎的肉欲那一切迷蒙错杂的幽径。至少在玛格丽特身上，他的钱没有白花。

===Original===
Even the awakening next morning, blear-eyed and foul-mouthed, was not as wretched as might have been thought. For at least he had grateful solitude, and to Mr. Marble solitude was very grateful, unless he had Marguerite with him. There was no wife with the anxious look in her eyes to worry him; he could roam round the house and satisfy himself for the thousandth time that the garden was undisturbed; he could dress himself slowly and leave the house without all the fuss of saying goodbye. He was usually half an hour late, of course, but that did not matter. He knew that dismissal from the office was near and inevitable, but he did not mind. Every day he could see in the eyes of the junior partner who had been so diffident about offering him five hundred a year a growing annoyance at his sodden appearance and unpunctual habits. Of course, he had done nothing to earn the salary he received. He had assisted the firm to no grand coup like the one he had brought off for himself. That﻿—he had known it all along﻿—was most unlikely now that he no longer had the spur of imminent necessity. But the new firm could dismiss him when it liked. He had twelve hundred a year of his own, and he did not want to be bothered with business. So to the office he went, red-eyed, unshaven, with shaking hands. His scanty red hair was fast turning grey.
The rest of the Marble family was endeavouring to enjoy itself. Some of its members were successful in varying degrees. The trio itself had been the object of amusement to the loungers in the palm-shaded entrance hall. Mrs. Marble was so badly dressed, despite Winnie’s misguided efforts, and she was so obviously frightened of porters and waiters; Winnie herself had roused some interest in a few breasts. She was young, anyone could see that, but not one of them guessed how young she was. Her clothes simply called for comment, and her actions likewise. Her face was over-powdered, and she was developing a habit of looking sidelong at the men in the lounge as she walked past them. That mixture of youth and innocence and yet apparent readiness awoke strange longings in the hearts of some of the old men who viewed her.
The cunning ones approached the mother first. There were chance conversations arising out of nothing in the hotel lounge, and Mrs. Marble was agreeably surprised to have men with grey hair and the most perfect manners treating her with as much respect as if she were a duchess. She was flushed and flustered, but it was very nice to have the society of these gentlemen. One or two of them gave her the pleasure of dining at her table with herself and her daughter, and sometimes one would accompany them on some excursion to places in the neighbourhood. Winnie thoroughly enjoyed herself.
===Chinese===
就连第二天清晨醒来——睡眼惺忪、满口宿臭——也不像想的那样难挨。因为至少他有惬意的清静，而对马布尔先生来说，除非玛格丽特在身边，清静最是受用。没有妻子那双焦灼的眼睛来烦他；他可以在宅子里巡行，第一千次地核实花园无人惊动；他可以从从容容穿衣，出门免掉那一套道别的啰嗦。当然，他上班总要迟到个半小时，不过这也无妨。他知道被辞退已经近在眼前、在所难免，但他不在乎。那位当初开出一年五百英镑还怕失礼的年轻合伙人，眼里对他这副委顿行状和屡屡迟到的做派一天天添着厌烦，他天天都看得见。当然，他本就没做什么对得起这份薪水的事。他也不曾替那家公司做成过什么惊天动地的大买卖，像他给自己做成的那件。那件——他心里始终有数——如今多半无望了，因为再没有燃眉之急逼他出手。可新公司要辞他，随时请便。他自己一年有一千二百英镑的进项，不愿再为买卖费神。于是他照旧上办公室去，红着眼睛，胡子拉碴，两手发颤。他那头稀薄的红发正在飞快转灰。
马布尔家其余的人正努力享乐，各人成效不一。他们一家三口，本是棕榈掩映的门厅里那班闲人打趣的对象。马布尔太太衣衫那样寒碜——温妮（Winnie）好心好意的张罗反倒添乱——而且明摆着怕搬运夫、怕侍者；温妮本人却在几颗人心里勾起了一点兴趣。她年轻，谁都看得出，可他们没一个猜到她究竟多年轻。她的穿戴简直招人议论，她的行止亦然。她脸上粉扑得太厚，又养成了一个习惯：从酒廊里那些男人身边经过时，眼睛斜斜地朝他们瞟。那种少女的年轻与天真、又掺着显而易见的有隙可乘，惹得某些打量她的老头儿心里生出奇怪的想望。
乖觉的先从母亲下手。酒店酒廊里凭空生出些不期而遇的攀谈，灰头发、举止无可挑剔的男人们对她敬重有加，仿佛她是一位公爵夫人，马布尔太太又惊又喜。她红着脸、心里发慌，可有这几位先生作伴实在叫人愉快。有一两位赏光同她母女同桌用膳，有时还有一位陪着她们到近处各处游览。温妮玩得畅快极了。

===Original===
There were one or two young men, too, who scraped acquaintance with the queer party. One of them dropped out when he discovered that Mrs. Marble did not possess much jewellery, and, in fact, did not care for jewellery, but the others stayed on. They danced with Winnie in the evenings, or took her “just for the rag” to the local theatre. They were nearly annoyed when they found that Mrs. Marble took it for granted that she was to accompany them; but there was no thought in her mind save just that. She could not picture a state of affairs in which anyone would sooner be alone with her daughter than have her present as well. But the men, young and old alike, found that there was one sure way of enjoying Winnie’s society unadulterated; that was to settle Mrs. Marble comfortably on the pier listening to the band, in a deckchair, and then take Winnie for a walk round. Mrs. Marble was quite pleased when she found how attentive the men all were to her, and to what pains they went to see that she was quite comfortable and had all she wanted. It was a pleasant change from seventeen years odd of married life with William Marble. And it was astonishing how often Winnie replied to Mrs. Marble’s question: “What would you like to do this morning?” (or this afternoon), with the ready response, “Oh, let’s go on to the pier and listen to the band, Mother.”
But amid all this enjoyment John was not enjoying himself. There was no place in the Grand Pavilion Hotel where he could sit and read in comfort, and the beach and the promenade were too crowded to permit such a thing either. He always had the Giant Twin, of course, but he did not always want to be out on it. Motorcycling, even on the finest example of the finest make of motorcycle in the world, begins to pall a little after three weeks’ enforced indulgence, and there came a time when John was frankly bored. He was bored with hotel meals, with hotel friends, and with hotel public rooms. Music at his meals ceased to have any attraction for him at all. The men who sought Winnie’s society looked upon him as an unmitigated nuisance, and were not too careful about concealing their opinion. And Winnie held the same opinion and did not try to conceal it at all. He could not even discuss motor-bicycles with anyone, in that he never met anyone who had ever owned such a thing.
John was bored, utterly and absolutely. After a fortnight’s stay he hinted as much to his mother, but hints were not very effective in his mother’s case. Three days later he tried again, with equal unsuccess. When he had endured three whole weeks he took the bit between his teeth and announced his intention of going home.
===Chinese===
也有一两个年轻人，同这群古怪的旅伴攀上了交情。其中一个发现马布尔太太并没有多少首饰——事实上压根儿不爱首饰——便打了退堂鼓，其余的却留了下来。晚上他们同温妮跳舞，或者带她去本地戏院，说是“不过是图个乐子”。待发现马布尔太太理所当然地认定自己也要同行，他们几乎要恼了；可她心里想的偏偏无非如此。她想象不出竟有这样的世道：有人宁愿单独同她女儿在一起，倒不愿她也在场。但这些人，不分老少，都寻出了一个稳当的路数，好不受掺和地独享温妮的陪伴——那就是先把马布尔太太舒舒服服安顿在栈桥上，往帆布躺椅里一坐听乐队演奏，再带温妮出去转上一圈。马布尔太太发觉这些男人个个对她殷勤备至，又那样费心叫她舒舒服服、样样遂意，心里好不欢喜。同比威廉·马布尔（William Marble）过了十七年有零的婚姻生活相比，这变化委实惬意。而温妮回答马布尔太太的问话——“今天上午想做点什么？”（或者今天下午）——那句脱口而出的“哦，妈妈，咱们上栈桥听乐队去吧”，居然那样频繁，也真够叫人吃惊的。
然而满堂欢娱之中，约翰（John）却乐不起来。大亭阁酒店里没有一个地方容他舒舒服服坐着读书，海滩和滨海步道又拥挤得容不下这等事。他当然还有那辆“巨人双生”（Giant Twin），可他并不总想骑出去兜风。骑摩托车——哪怕骑的是天下第一牌子里天下第一的那一辆——被迫纵情骑上三个星期之后，也开始叫人腻味，到后来约翰简直是明明白白地烦透了。酒店的饭食、酒店的朋友、酒店的公共厅室，一概叫他生厌。吃饭时的音乐于他再无半点吸引。那些追着温妮转的男人把他看作一个彻头彻尾的搅局货，而且不大费心掩饰这层看法。温妮看法相同，且一点不费心掩饰。他连摩托车都没人可谈——他从没碰上一个有过这种东西的人。
约翰烦透了，彻头彻尾、地地道道地烦透了。住满两个星期，他向母亲把话透了个影儿，可暗示落在他母亲身上素来不大灵验。三天后他又试了一回，同样无功。熬满整整三个星期，他索性咬紧牙关横下心，宣布自己要回家。

===Original===
“But, my dear, why?” asked Mrs. Marble.
John did his best to explain, but he felt from the start it was hopeless. The intuition proved correct, for Mrs. Marble had no sympathy at all with boredom, never being bored herself.
“I don’t think father will like it if you go home,” said Mrs. Marble. “He’s spent an awful lot on this holiday for you, and you ought to show that you appreciate it.”
===Chinese===
“可是，亲爱的，为什么呀？”马布尔太太问。
约翰竭力解释，可他打一开始便觉得无望。这直觉果然应验，因为马布尔太太对“烦闷”这回事毫无体恤——她自己从不烦闷。
“我想你回家去，爸爸会不乐意的，”马布尔太太说，“这个假期他为你花了好大一笔钱，你总该叫他看出你是领情的。”

===Original===
“But there’s nothing to *do*,” expostulated John.
“Why, there’s lots and lots to do, dear. You can listen to the band, or you can go out on the motorbike, or﻿—or﻿—oh, there’s lots to do. A great active boy like you ought to find things to do as easily as anything.”
“A great active boy can’t listen to a band all day and all night,” said John, “even if I was a boy, and even if I *liked* listening to bands, which I don’t, very much. Hang it, I can’t get hold of any decent books to read, and when I do I can’t find anywhere to read them.”
===Chinese===
“可这儿没一件事*可干*啊，”约翰分辩道。
“哎呀，可干的事多着呢，亲爱的。你可以去听乐队，可以骑摩托车出去，要么——要么——哦，多着呢。像你这样生龙活虎的大孩子，找点儿事做还不是易如反掌。”
“生龙活虎的大孩子也不能白天黑夜连轴听乐队啊，”约翰说，“先别说我算不算大孩子；就算我*爱*听乐队——其实我才不怎么爱。见鬼，我连一本像样的书都弄不到手，弄到了也找不着个读它的地方。”

===Original===
“Don’t argue with him, Mother,” cut in Winnie. “He’s only finding fault.”
“Finding fault” was in Mrs. Marble’s eyes a sort of vice to which the male sex was peculiarly prone at inconvenient moments. She suffered on account of it on occasions when Mr. Marble’s temper was not all that it should have been. Winnie seized the advantage conferred upon her by this dexterous tactical thrust.
“I don’t see why he shouldn’t go home, if you ask me,” she said. “He’d be company for father, and it’s only for a week, when all’s said and done.”
===Chinese===
“别跟他抬杠，妈妈，”温妮插嘴道，“他就是存心挑刺儿。”
在马布尔太太眼里，“挑刺儿”是男人特有的一种毛病，偏拣不凑巧的时刻发作。马布尔先生脾气欠佳的那些时候，她就为这毛病遭过殃。温妮抓住这一记灵巧战术突刺送上门的先手，乘胜进逼。
“要叫我说，我不明白他干嘛不能回家，”她说，“他去了还能给爸爸作个伴儿，归根结底也不过一个礼拜的事。”

===Original===
Her arguments were not particular happy, for Mrs. Marble remembered with a slight shudder the time when she had flung herself between her son and her husband. And she would be positively unhappy, whenever she remembered to be, at the thought of two helpless males alone in a house which cost her so much pains to run. But Winnie had her own reasons for wishing John away, reasons not unconnected with walks upon the pier and with visits to local cinematograph theatres.
“I should let him go,” said Winnie. “Then he can collect a few of the mouldy old books he wants to read. He’ll soon get fed up with being at home, and then he can come down here again. It’ll only be for a day or two. He won’t stand cooking his own breakfast longer than that. Then he’ll be able to tell you how father’s getting on.”
It was a wily move. Mrs. Marble, in the intervals between being intimidated by waiters and chambermaids and enjoying the luxury they represented, was occasionally conscience-stricken about her deserted husband. She heard from him very little﻿—only one or two straggling scrawls that told her nothing. More would have meant too much trouble to Mr. Marble. Winnie’s suggestion was therefore well timed.
===Chinese===
她的理由算不得怎么高明，因为马布尔太太一想起自己横身拦在儿子和丈夫中间的那一幕，便不由得轻轻打个寒噤。而且每逢想起，一想到两个没着没落的男人，孤零零守着一座她费尽心血操持的宅子，她心里也着实发苦。可温妮巴不得约翰走开，自有她的道理——那道理同栈桥上的散步、同本地电影院的几度出入，并非没有干系。
“要是我，就放他去，”温妮说，“正好叫他回去捡几本他要读的发霉老书。在家待不了几天他就腻了，到时候再下来就是。横竖不过一两天——自家弄早饭这份罪，他熬不了更久。回头他还能告诉你爸爸近来怎么样。”
这一手够狡猾。马布尔太太在受侍者和客房女仆的辖制、又享用着他们所代表的那份奢华的空当里，偶尔也会对撇下的丈夫心生愧疚。他的音信她得的极少——只有一两封七零八落的涂鸦，什么也没说透。再多写，对马布尔先生来说未免太费事。温妮这一提议，正赶在火候上。

===Original===
“Well, do that then, dear,” said Mrs. Marble. “Go home just for the night and get all the books and things you want. Of course, if father doesn’t mind, you can stay longer if you like. But you mustn’t do anything that might make him cross.”
It was hardly the freehanded congé that Winnie would have liked her to give, but, still, it was something.
When John announced his intention of starting off at once Mrs. Marble was shocked into protest. To her there was something unthinkable in changing one’s place of abode at ten minutes’ notice. She succeeded in persuading her son into postponing his departure until the next day﻿—Saturday.
===Chinese===
“那好吧，就这么办，亲爱的，”马布尔太太说，“回去住上一夜，把你要的书啊什么的一概取齐了。当然，要是爸爸不介意，你愿意多住几天也随你。可千万别做什么惹他动气的事。”
这离温妮巴望她大方放手的那种许可还差着一截，不过，总归是有所进展了。
约翰一宣布立刻就要动身，马布尔太太惊得连声反对。在她看来，只打十分钟的招呼便更换住处，这种事简直不堪设想。她好说歹说，总算劝得儿子把行期推到第二天——星期六。

===Original===
Even then she was full of instructions at the last moment.
“You know where the clean sheets are, don’t you, dear?” she said. “They’re in the lowest drawer in the big chest of drawers. Mind you air them before you put them on your bed. Oh, and when you come down again will you bring my white fur? It’s getting rather cold in the evenings now. You’re sure you know the way home all right? It’s rather a long way to go all by yourself.”
John had often before covered three times the distance in a single day, but he forbore saying so. He felt that it would be wiser to let his mother run on and have her full say out and then he would clear off without further argument. She continued, unmindful:
===Chinese===
即便到那时，临到末一刻她还是满腹的叮咛。
“干净床单搁在哪儿你知道吧，亲爱的？”她说，“就在大衣柜最底下一层抽屉里。记着先晾一晾再往床上铺。哦，回头再来的时候，把我的白皮草捎来好吗？如今晚上已经挺凉了。回家的路你真认得没有？那么远的路，你一个人走可不成。”
三倍远的路，约翰从前一天之内就跑过不止一回，可他忍住没说。他觉得，上策是让母亲絮絮叨叨把话倒尽，然后自己一走了之，省得再费唇舌。她浑然不觉，兀自说下去：

===Original===
“I shall be quite anxious to hear that you got home safely. Mind you write as soon as you’re there, and be sure you tell me how father is. And﻿—and﻿—don’t forget what I said about not doing anything to annoy father.”
That made John fidget uneasily in his chair. At long last Mrs. Marble ended with:
“Well, goodbye, dear. Have a good time. Have you got enough money? Then goodbye. Don’t forget what I said. We’re just going on the pier with Mr. Horne. Goodbye, dear.”
And Winnie and Mrs. Marble and Mr. Horne were gone.
===Chinese===
“你平平安安到了家，我才放得下心。记着一到就写信，千万告诉我爸爸怎么样。还有——还有——别忘了我的话，别做任何惹爸爸恼火的事。”
这话弄得约翰在椅子里不自在地扭动起来。好半天，马布尔太太才收了尾：
“好啦，再见，亲爱的。好好玩儿。钱带够了吗？那就好，再见吧。别忘了我的话。我们这就跟霍恩先生（Mr. Horne）上栈桥去。再见，亲爱的。”
温妮、马布尔太太和霍恩先生便去了。

===Original===
That was a most enjoyable day for John. For once he was neither the hotel prisoner nor yet was he at home with his father. It was the transition stage. He spent his time deliciously, luxuriously. He bathed by himself, at the far end of the town﻿—his last bathe before starting out for home. That took time, for he wanted to make the most of it. Then he came back to the Grand Pavilion Hotel, took the Giant Twin from the garage, where it stood impatient and intolerant of all these domesticated saloons and limousines. The kickstarter swung obediently, and the engine broke into its sweet thunderous roar. John swung himself into the saddle, and the Giant Twin sprang impatiently forward as he let in the clutch. They climbed the steep ascent of the side street without an effort, nosed their way through the squalor of the slums at the back of the town, and in fifteen minutes were out on the free open downs. But John was determined not to waste a minute of his day’s happiness. He curbed the ardour of the Giant Twin to a mere fourteen miles an hour﻿—a Rocinante speed worthy of his quixotic mood, as he told himself. They ambled along the great high road in wonderful spirits. The wind blew past him gently, and he filled his lungs with it, sighing with pleasure. It was twelve o’clock when he started; by one o’clock not thirty miles﻿—not half the way﻿—had been covered. John lunched by himself at a big yet homely hotel by the roadside. It was a decided change from lunch at the Grand Pavilion Hotel, with a band blaring only ten feet away, and mother talking platitudes﻿—she couldn’t help that, poor dear, but it grew wearisome after a week or two﻿—and Winnie looking round discreetly at the men, or, worse, chattering away to some greasy-haired fellow she had wangled mother into inviting. They were all greasy-haired, somehow, and not one of them knew how to speak to a fellow, not even the young ones. And as for the old ones! There was one doddering old idiot who had asked him if he kept white mice! John stretched his legs in comforting fashion under the table and lit a cigarette. Thank God, that was over, anyway. He couldn’t have borne that place another day. He hoped he would be all right with father. Father was such an uncertain sort of fellow nowadays. But apparently all he wanted was to be left alone, and that was all he wanted, as well. So they ought to get on all right. If they didn’t﻿—well, it couldn’t be as bad as the hotel, anyway, with mother fussing over him and Winnie scrapping all the time. There was a little bit of the boor and a little bit of the bear about John.
But he was lighthearted enough when he came down and started up the Giant Twin once more for the last run homeward. He still went slowly, partly of his own free will, partly because of the growing volume of Saturday traffic that he met. He turned away from Croydon, and the Giant Twin brought him triumphantly up the long hill to the Crystal Palace without an effort. Ten minutes later the motor-bicycle ran silently with the clutch out, down the slope of Malcolm Road, and pulled up gently outside Number 53. John dismounted slowly. It had been a glorious day. Even now it was not yet evening. There was nothing better than a late afternoon in August, at the end of a flaming day. The rather depressing little road looked positively heavenly to the exile after three weeks at the Grand Pavilion Hotel. There was just a touch of red in the sky, where the sun was beginning to sink. John was half smiling as he looked round him, while he fumbled in his pocket for his latchkey. He was even smiling as he put the key in the lock, and as he walked into the house.
Mr. Marble had lately been looking forward to his Saturday afternoons. Following a lazy morning at the office, and a lazy lunch in town, he could travel quietly homeward after the rush. And at home, for in this case it was worth daring the watchfulness of the neighbours, especially considering that arriving as she did before he came back they might well think that she had come on some neighbourly errand in bringing shopping or to see that all was well in the house, there would be waiting for him Madame Collins, Marguerite﻿—Rita, he called her nowadays. And they would have the whole afternoon and evening before them. She would not be leaving before dark. It would be a wonderful day. Eat, drink, and be merry, for tomorrow you die. Mr. Marble ate, and most decidedly he drank, and he was merry as well, if such a term can be applied to his nightmare feeling of wild abandon. And the abandon was only possible because he was at 53 Malcolm Road, and able to make sure that there was no chance of his dying suddenly yet awhile.
===Chinese===
对约翰来说，这是顶快活的一天。破天荒头一遭，他既不是酒店的囚徒，也不在家中陪着父亲。正是过渡的一段。他把这一天的光阴过得有滋有味、穷奢极欲。他独自在城子另一头下了海——动身回家前的最后一浴。这一浴费了些时候，因为他要受用个够。然后他回到大亭阁酒店，从车棚里推出“巨人双生”——它立在那里，对四周这些驯顺的家用轿车和豪华大轿车满心不耐、不屑与伍。启动杆应脚一荡，引擎便迸出那甜美而雷霆般的轰鸣。约翰翻身跨上鞍座，离合一松，“巨人双生”便急不可待地窜向前去。他们不费吹灰之力攀上侧街的陡坡，探着路钻出镇后贫民窟的龌龊，一刻钟后便驰上了空旷无羁的丘原。可约翰打定主意，这一天快活的光阴一分钟也不糟蹋。他把“巨人双生”的火性勒住，压到每小时区区十四英里——他对自己说，这是罗西南特（Rocinante）的速度，正配他那堂吉诃德式的心境。他们兴头十足地在大干道上信步溜达。风柔柔地拂过他，他把满肺的风吸饱，快活得直叹气。动身时十二点；到一点钟，还不足三十英里——一半路程还没走完。约翰在路边一家气派而家常的客店里独自用了午餐。比起大亭阁酒店的午餐，这分明换了个世界：那边乐队就在十英尺外大吹大擂，母亲净说些淡话——这她也没法子，可怜见儿的，可一两个星期听下来实在腻人——温妮则拿眼睛四下里有分寸地扫那些男人，更糟的是，还跟某个油头粉面的家伙聊个没完，那号人还是她连哄带骗撺掇母亲请来的。不知怎么，他们个个油头粉面，而且没一个懂得怎么跟小伙子说话，连年轻的也不然。至于那些老的！有个颤颤巍巍的老糊涂，居然问他还养不养小白鼠！约翰在桌子底下惬意地伸直双腿，点上一支烟。谢天谢地，那一切总算熬过去了。那地方他一天也再受不了。但愿他同父亲处得来。父亲如今是那样一个没准头的家伙。不过他显然只求个清净，而这恰恰也是约翰要的全部。所以他们应当合得来。就算合不来——嗯，横竖不能比酒店更糟，不会有人像母亲那样围着他啰嗦，也不会有温妮成天抬杠拌嘴。约翰这人，带着几分村气，也带着几分熊脾气。
可等他下楼来、再次发动“巨人双生”跑完最后一程回家路时，他心里还是轻快得很。他仍旧骑得慢，一半出于自愿，一半因为迎面而来的星期六车流越来越稠。他绕开克罗伊登（Croydon）一线，“巨人双生”毫不费力地、凯旋似的驮他爬上通往水晶宫（Crystal Palace）的长坡。十分钟后，摩托车捏下离合，悄没声地滑下马尔科姆路的坡道，轻轻停在53号门外。约翰慢慢跨下车。这是光辉灿烂的一天。此刻天还没擦黑。八月里一个炽日炎炎的白昼行将收梢，再没有比这傍晚将临的下午更美好的时光了。这条平日里叫人提不起劲的小街，在大亭阁酒店流放三周归来的游子眼里，简直俨然天堂。太阳开始西沉的地方，天边正洇着一抹红。约翰一面四下环顾，一面在口袋里摸门钥匙，嘴角噙着笑。他把钥匙插进锁孔时在笑，走进家门时也还在笑。
近来，马布尔先生总盼着他的星期六下午。在办公室里懒懒挨过一个上午，在城里慢悠悠用过午餐，他便可以避过人流，安安静静地回家。而家里——这一回，冒一冒邻居们警惕眼目的风险是值得的，何况她照例在他回宅之前先到，邻居们尽可以当作她是来走动走动：捎点采买的什物，或是来看看宅里是否安好——会有科林斯夫人、玛格丽特等着他；他如今唤她丽塔（Rita）。整个下午连整个晚上都在他们面前铺展着。天黑透之前她不会走。那将是美妙的一天。吃吧，喝吧，快活吧，因为明天你就得死。马布尔先生吃着，而且千真万确地喝着，也快活着——倘若“快活”二字也当得起他那种噩梦般狂乱放纵的心境的话。而这放纵之所以可能，只因他人在马尔科姆路53号，能够确证一时半刻还没有横死的危险。

===Original===
John came into the dining-room. There was no one there. But the sight of the room brought the first chill to his heart. The gilded furniture blazed tawdrily in the fading sunlight; the room was in an indescribable muddle: dirty dishes and empty bottles were littered about it, and cigarette ash and cigarette ends were strewn over the floor. And in the room there was a subtle and distasteful blend of scents. Overlying the stale tobacco smell and the fustiness of unopened windows was the smell of spilt drink, and permeating the whole there was yet another odour, slight yet penetrating﻿—a stale, unpleasant smell as of degraded hyacinths. John’s nose wrinkled in distaste as the vile reek assailed his nostrils. The drink, and the tobacco, and the fustiness, and the muddle he could account for, and he had been prepared for them on a smaller scale. But this other scent, irritating his clean boy’s palate, was different. It was more unclean even than the others.
He left the room hurriedly. He had already half decided that his father was not at present in the house. He set his foot on the stairs to go to his own room to open the windows, open them wide, so that the clean evening air would circulate there at any rate, but he withdrew it as a thought struck him. Most probably his father was in the back room﻿—it had been his habit for a long time now to sit there on most occasions. If he were there, and if, as John reluctantly admitted to himself was most likely, he were drinking, it would be well for him to go in and report his arrival as soon as might be. His father would be furious if he were in the house without his knowledge. John walked back to the drawing-room, turned the handle of the door, and entered.
But he went no farther than just beyond the threshold. There he stopped, for a sickening, hellish two seconds, while the hyacinth scent rushed in greater volume upon him, its presence now explained, and while the sight that met his eyes struck him dazed, as might a club. It was sickening, bestial, abominable. He fled in a staggering run, fumbling in dazed haste at the handle of the door. To blend with the tumult of horrible remembrance came the sight, just as he reached free air again, of his father lurching after him, mumbling some wild words which he could not catch, but whose import clearly was for him not to go away, but to stay while he could explain. But John fled.
===Chinese===
约翰走进饭厅。里头空无一人。可这屋子的景象却叫他心头第一次发冷。镀金家具在斜阳残照里烧出一片俗艳的光；屋里乱得难以言状：脏盘碟和空瓶子四处狼藉，烟灰烟头撒了一地。屋里还浮着一股微妙而讨厌的混合气味。压在隔宿烟味和不曾开窗的霉闷之上的，是泼洒出来的酒气；而渗透这一切的，还有另一种气味，淡淡的却钻鼻子——一股陈腐的、令人不快的气味，像是败坏了的风信子。这股恶臭冲进鼻孔，约翰嫌恶地皱起了鼻子。酒、烟、霉闷、凌乱，他都还解释得通，也都料到了——只是没料到这般规模。可这另一种气味，冲撞着他少年人干净的感官，却另是一路。它比其余几种更加不洁。
他急急离开了那屋子。他已半打定主意，父亲此刻多半不在宅里。他抬脚要上楼，去自己房间开窗，把窗子四敞大开，好歹让干净的晚风在那一间里流转流转；一个念头闪过，他又把脚收了回来。父亲极可能待在后屋——很久以来，多数时候他都惯于坐在那里。要是他在那儿，而且——约翰不情愿地向自己承认，多半如此——他在喝酒，那还是赶紧进去报个到为妙。若是他人在宅里父亲却毫不知情，父亲准会大发雷霆。约翰走回客厅，拧开门把，走了进去。
可他只跨过门槛一步便站住了。在那令人作呕、地狱般的两秒钟里，风信子的气味加倍汹涌地扑上身来——它的来历这下有了分晓——而眼前所见像一记闷棍，把他打懵了。那景象令人作呕、兽性、可憎。他跌跌撞撞夺路而逃，慌乱中胡乱抓挠门把。就在他重见天光的那一刻，又瞥见父亲踉跄着追来，嘴里咕哝着听不真切的疯话，那意思明明白白是叫他别走、留下听他解释。可约翰还是逃了。

===Original===
There was nothing else he could do. Every cell of his body called for air, air, air. Air to flood away that loathsome reek of hyacinths; air to flood his brain and blot away that memory of beastly, drunken nudity; air, air, air!
At the edge of the pavement stood his one trusty friend, the Giant Twin, who never would betray him. He leaned upon the friendly saddle for a second, while his whirling mind recovered itself to the slight degree possible. Air, air, air! He flung himself into the saddle, hands going automatically to ignition and throttle. The engine was still hot, and broke into its old friendly roar as he thrust at the kickstarter. Next second he was gone, wheeling wildly in the road, with the engine bellowing jubilantly as he forced the throttle wider.
The sunset was spreading over the sky, bloodred and tawny, as the sun vanished behind the houses, but it was still stiflingly hot. The air that raced past John’s cheeks might as well have come straight from a blast-furnace. It raced past his cheeks, tugged at his hair, filled his lungs to bursting, and yet it gave no relief. Wider and wider went the throttle, and now the Giant Twin was hurtling along the roads as though they were a racetrack. John did not know where he was going, nor did he care. Air was what he wanted, air, more air. He sat well back in the saddle, while the tornado of his own begetting wrenched at him with a myriad fingers. Yet as he did so he shuddered at the recollection of the reek of hyacinth scent. The throttle was wide open now, and they were swirling round corners at an acute angle amid a shower of flying grit. Air, more air! John’s hand moved to a forbidden lever, and the Giant Twin leaped forward even faster as the exhaust roared in thunder past the cutout.
It could not last. Not even the Giant Twin, ever loyal, could hold on those glassy roads at that speed. One last corner, and then the tyres lost their grip on the hardly noticeable camber. The Giant Twin leaped madly, plunging across the road, across the pavement. A cruel brick wall awaited them, in one rending crash of ruin.
===Chinese===
他别无他法。他身上的每一个细胞都在呼喊：空气，空气，空气。要空气灌进来，冲净那可憎的风信子恶臭；要空气灌满头脑，抹掉那野兽般醉态赤裸的记忆；空气，空气，空气！
人行道边上立着他唯一可靠的朋友——“巨人双生”，它永远不会出卖他。他在那亲切的鞍座上撑了一秒，让天旋地转的脑子多少定一定。空气，空气，空气！他翻身跨上车，两手不假思索地扑向点火和油门。引擎还热着，他一蹬启动杆，它便迸出那熟悉而亲切的咆哮。下一秒他已窜出去，在路上疯狂地甩了个弯，油门越拧越大，引擎欢腾地吼着。
太阳隐进屋舍后面，血红斑驳的暮色漫上天际，可天气依旧闷热得叫人窒息。从约翰脸颊边呼啸而过的风，简直像从鼓风炉里直喷出来的一般。它掠过他的脸颊，扯他的头发，把他的肺灌得快要胀裂，却带不来一丝宽解。油门越开越大，“巨人双生”此刻在路上一路狂飙，仿佛那条条道路都是赛道。约翰不知道自己往哪儿去，也不在乎。他要的是空气，空气，更多的空气。他稳稳坐进鞍座深处，任凭那场他自己掀起的旋风用千万根手指撕扯着他。可即便如此，一想起那风信子气味的恶臭，他仍不寒而栗。油门已开到顶，他们贴着锐角呼啸着掠过一个个弯道，碾起一阵飞沙走石。空气，更多的空气！约翰的手摸向那根不许碰的拉杆，废气绕开消音口如雷般轰出，“巨人双生”向前窜得愈发快了。
这势头撑不了多久。哪怕是永远忠诚的“巨人双生”，以那样的速度驶在镜面似的路面上，也抓附不住。最后一个弯道过后，轮胎便在那道几乎察觉不出的路拱上失了抓力。“巨人双生”疯狂地腾跃，横贯路面，冲过人行道。一堵无情的砖墙迎候着他们，等来的是天崩地裂的一声巨响。

## XI

===Original===
There was misery, misery undisguised and rampant, at 53 Malcolm Road. There were only two people living there now. Winnie had gone back to school, glad to escape from her mother’s helpless unhappiness and her father’s brooding gloom. But Mr. Marble and Mrs. Marble were there all the time. The blow of Mr. Marble’s dismissal from the new firm had fallen almost at once. He did not mind. He did not need the money, and it was such a terrible trouble to have to go up to the office every day. Unhappiness or no unhappiness, he preferred being at home, with his eye on the backyard and his pet books on crime and medical jurisprudence round him, to hanging about at the office all day, worrying about what was happening at home. And the sombre life of sitting solitary at home began to have a hideous fascination for him. There was no effort about it, no call for original thought or talent, and absence of effort was grateful to a man who habitually drank more than he should do, and whose mind was continually wandering down dark avenues, often traversed but always new. His wife was a nobody now, a phantom less real than the squint-eyed hangman that so often came tapping him on the shoulder; she was a soft-footed ghost that wandered about the house, coping ineffectually with the work that awaited her, and usually weeping, but so quietly that it did not disturb him. Sometimes Mrs. Marble wept at the memory of her dead son; sometimes she wept because her back hurt her. But she wept at other times, too, and she did not know why she did; actually she was weeping because her husband had ceased to love her. Very grey, very frail and very, very unhappy was Mrs. Marble at this time.
It was a strange, mad life that the two of them led nowadays. There was money in plenty, and very little use for it. The walls of the sitting-room were by now completely lined with Mr. Marble’s collection of books on crime; all the other rooms were crowded with massive and incredibly costly furniture, whose elaborate carvings were a source of woe to Mrs. Marble when she came to dust them. The tradespeople had left off calling, nearly all of them, and all the shopping was done by Mrs. Marble in hurried little excursions to the poverty-stricken shops near. There was money for servants, money for expensive and delightful foods, money for comfortable and manageable furniture. But no servant ever entered 53 Malcolm Road; the elaborate furniture was Mrs. Marble’s most weary burden, and for food they began to depend more and more upon ready cooked food bought as each meal came round in turn to augment the neglected store of the empty larder. Mrs. Marble had begun to neglect her duty of housekeeping long ago. But she had only begun to show incapacity of this order since John’s death.
In point of fact, Mrs. Marble had an idea, and was worrying over it. And when Mrs. Marble wanted to work anything out by sheer force of brainpower she was compelled to abandon all her other labours in order to devote all her time to the task. She was working something out slowly, but, as is always the case when mentalities of that sort are once bent upon completing a task, very, very surely. Yet even now she could not have expressed her suspicions in words, so obscure were they. But they were not connected with Madame Collins, strange to relate.
===Chinese===
