马尔科姆路53号上下愁云惨雾，那悲苦毫不掩饰，肆虐横行。屋里如今只住着两个人。温妮回学校去了，庆幸自己总算逃脱了母亲那无助的哀伤和父亲那郁结的阴沉。只有马布尔先生和马布尔太太终日守在屋里。被新东家辞退的打击，几乎立刻就落到了马布尔先生头上。他并不在意。那点薪水他本就用不着，再说天天上办公室去，实在是桩可怕的苦差。凄惶也罢，不凄惶也罢，他宁可待在家里，眼睛盯着后院，身边围着他心爱的犯罪学与法医学书籍，也不愿成天耗在办公室里，牵肠挂肚地惦记家里不知出了什么事。而这样独坐家中的阴郁生活，竟渐渐对他生出一股可怖的魅力。这样的日子毫不费力，无须独出心裁的思考，也谈不上才具；而省力的日子对于一个嗜酒过量的人正相宜——他的头脑不住地溜进一条条幽暗的曲径，那些曲径虽屡屡穿行，却回回都是新的。妻子如今在他眼里已算不得什么人，成了一个幻影，还不及那斜眼刽子手来得真切——那家伙时常来轻叩他的肩头；她是一缕脚步轻悄的幽魂，在屋子里游荡，徒劳地应付着等着她的活计，多半时候在垂泪，只是哭得极轻，搅扰不着他。有时马布尔太太想起死去的儿子便落泪，有时是背痛逼出了眼泪。可她别的时候也哭，还不知自己为什么哭；实际上，她哭是因为丈夫已经不爱她了。这时的马布尔太太，面色灰败，身形衰弱，非常、非常地不幸。
这两口子如今过的是一种古怪而疯癫的日子。钱有的是，却几乎派不上用场。起居室的四面墙壁，这时已被马布尔先生收藏的犯罪学书籍排得满满当当；其余各个房间都塞满了笨重而贵得骇人的家具，那上头繁复的雕花，每逢马布尔太太前来掸尘，便成了她的一场劫难。各家店铺的伙计差不多都不再登门了；一切采买都落到马布尔太太头上，得她匆匆跑到附近那些寒酸的铺子里去置办。雇仆人的钱有，买昂贵可口珍馐的钱有，添置舒适合用家具的钱也有。可是从来不曾有一个仆人跨进马尔科姆路53号的门；那一屋子雕琢繁复的家具成了马布尔太太最沉重疲惫的负担；至于吃食，他们越来越仰赖每到饭点便现买的熟食，以此添补那口久遭冷落、空空如也的食品柜。荒疏家务这件事，马布尔太太早就开了头；但露出这等不济，却是打约翰死后才有的事。
说实在的，马布尔太太心里起了一个念头，正为它犯愁。而马布尔太太但凡要单凭脑力把一件事琢磨个明白，就不得不撇下其余一切活计，把全部时间都投在这上头。她正在慢慢琢磨一件事，然而——凡是那种头脑一旦打定主意要办成一桩事，向来如此——琢磨得非常、非常扎实。只是直到此刻，她仍没法把心里的怀疑用言语说出来，那些怀疑实在太过混沌。但说来奇怪，这些怀疑竟与科林斯夫人毫不相干。

===Original===
Stranger still, it was this latter, dexterous though she was, who was the cause of the breakdown of the unstable state of affairs then prevailing. Those sums of money which she had received from Mr. Marble had only whetted her appetite for more. They had made a wonderful difference to the bank balance over which she gloated in private. Yet that bank balance was not nearly large enough yet for her purpose, increased though it was by sums wrung from the allowance made by her husband for housekeeping. Madame Collins took it very ill that Mrs. Marble should continue to stand in the way of her obtaining more.
For Mrs. Marble did. No more would Mr. Marble leave his house unguarded; no more could he be induced to relax his straining attention over that barren ten square yards of soil at the back of 53 Malcolm Road. For his obsession had grown upon him naturally as he gave it more play. In the days when it had been natural for him to go up to town every day it had been natural for his garden to take care of itself, but as soon as he had got into the comforting habit of watching over it continually he was unable to break himself of it. So he could not see her outside of his house, and in the house there was always his wife.
Madame Collins fretted and fumed. That bank balance of hers was mounting only by shillings a week when it ought to be mounting by pounds. She played desperately hard for fortune. She was as sweet as honey always to Mrs. Marble. Yet it was not easy to gain Mrs. Marble’s good graces. Anxiety about her husband occupied such of her attention as was not busy working out the problem she had set herself. And she resented bitterly any display of sympathy, for that might indicate that the sympathizer understood her trouble better than she did herself. Besides, Mrs. Marble had been “respectably brought up,” in a world where it was a serious social slur to have a drunken husband, and any reference to her husband’s failing had her up in arms at once.
===Chinese===
更奇怪的是，把当时那摇摇欲坠的局面搅塌的，恰恰是这后一位——别看她手腕那般灵巧。她从马布尔先生手里拿到的那些钱，只勾得她胃口更大。那些钱叫她的银行存款大大改观，她背着人对着那笔存款心花怒放，看了一遍又一遍。然而那笔存款离她的指望还差得远呢，虽说她已从丈夫给的家用津贴里克扣出一份份钱，源源添了进去。马布尔太太竟一而再、再而三地挡她的财路，科林斯夫人为此记恨极了。
因为马布尔太太确实挡着。马布尔先生再不肯让这所房子无人看守；也再没有人能劝得他松懈半分，松开他对马尔科姆路53号屋后那十平方码贫瘠泥土的死盯死守。因为他那份痴念，正随着一天天的纵容自然而然地疯长。从前他天天进城上班的日子，花园自生自灭原是顺理成章；可一旦养成了须臾不离、时时守望的安逸习惯，他便再也戒不掉。这样一来，在自家门外他见不着她，而在自家门内，又总有他的妻子在跟前。
科林斯夫人急得团团转，火冒三丈。她那笔存款一星期只涨几个先令，而照理该论英镑地往上涨。她孤注一掷地赌运气。她对马布尔太太始终甜得像蜜。然而要讨马布尔太太的好感谈何容易。马布尔太太的心思，除了耗在自己那道难题上的，剩下的全叫对丈夫的忧惧占了去。况且谁对她略表怜恤，她便记恨在心，因为那也许意味着，怜恤的人对她那桩苦处比她自己还看得明白。何况马布尔太太自幼受的是“体面人家的教养”，在她那个世道里，摊上一个酗酒的丈夫是天大的体面污点，谁敢提她丈夫这桩毛病，她立刻便竖起全身的刺。

===Original===
And Madame Collins had indiscreetly﻿—though she herself had thought at the time that it was a good stroke of tactics﻿—let it be known that she understood what was the matter with Mr. Marble, to find to her surprise that Mrs. Marble hotly resented her knowledge. To this cause for dissension must be added the fact that now that Mrs. Marble had admitted to herself her failure to live the life of a rich woman, and her inability to wear good clothes as if she had always been accustomed to them, she had a bitter envy of those who were more successful. She disliked Madame Collins for her opulence of figure and good looks, for the way in which she wore her clothes, for the very clothes she wore. But Mrs. Marble’s dislikes were as unremarkable as everything else about her, and she was unable to show them except by a faint and rather bewildered hostility, which Madame Collins was able to sweep aside. It was more than Mrs. Marble was capable of to be actively rude to anyone. So Madame Collins continued to call at rather frequent intervals, to talk in honey-sweet fashion to Mrs. Marble, and sometimes to penetrate into that well-remembered sitting-room, if Mr. Marble was not too fuddled, and to leave there a haunting scent of hyacinths and a clinging memory of rich flesh which sometimes penetrated into Mr. Marble’s drink-sodden mind. Generally, however, it roused little fresh longing in him; he was at the moment amply content with his books and his drink and the knowledge that he was guarding that garden.
Mr. Marble was rarely quite drunk. He never tried to be. All he ever attempted was to reach the happy stage﻿—after the grim preliminary period when his imagination had been stimulated﻿—when he was unable to think connectedly, so that he could not work out the long trains of thought that led inevitably to the picturing of detection and the scaffold. He could reach this stage comfortably quite early in the morning before the effects of the previous day’s drinking had worn off, and then he was able to keep like it for the rest of the day by the simple mechanical process of drinking every time he found his thoughts taking an unpleasant line. The system was not the result of careful planning; it was merely the natural consequence of the situation, and for a long time it worked well enough. Comfortably hazy in his thoughts, comfortably seated in his armchair by the sitting-room window, with a new book on his knee to glance at occasionally﻿—publishers’ announcements were almost all the post delivered nowadays, and Mr. Marble bought two books on crime a week on the average﻿—Mr. Marble almost enjoyed his life. His wife meant little to him, save that she was a convenient person to send out, green string bag in hand, to the grocer’s to buy more whisky when his reserve dropped below the amount he had fixed upon﻿—two untouched bottles. Mr. Marble ate little; his wife ate even less; there was little enough work to keep Mrs. Marble employed although she toiled inordinately hard in helpless fashion to keep the house in order. Mrs. Marble spent her days wandering round the house soft-footed, slipshod, touching and fumbling and replacing. Her mind was busy trying to work something out.
===Chinese===
科林斯夫人还干过一件欠考虑的事——虽说当时她还自以为是一步好棋——她放出口风，说自己明白马布尔先生究竟是怎么回事，结果却惊愕地发现，马布尔太太对她的知情怒不可遏。失和的缘由，除了这一条还得添上另一条：马布尔太太既已在心底承认，自己过不惯阔太太的日子，也穿不来好衣裳——穿不出那副仿佛自幼穿惯了的气派——她对那些比自己得法的人便怀着刻毒的妒羡。她不喜欢科林斯夫人那丰腴的身段、姣好的容貌，不喜欢她穿衣裳的那副做派，连她穿的衣裳本身也不喜欢。不过马布尔太太的嫌恶同她身上其余的一切一样不起眼，只能化作一丝隐约而近乎茫然的敌意流露出来，而这点敌意，科林斯夫人抬手便拂到一边去了。要马布尔太太冲谁恶言相向，那是她力所不及的事。于是科林斯夫人照旧隔三差五登门，用蜜一样的甜言蜜语陪着马布尔太太说话，有时还趁马布尔先生醉得不算太凶，钻进那间叫人记忆犹新的起居室，留下一缕缭绕不散的风信子香，和一段黏在空气里的丰腴肉体的暖腻余韵，这余韵偶尔会渗进马布尔先生被酒泡透的脑子。不过大体上说，这很少再激起他什么新的渴念；他眼下心满意足得很——有他的书，有他的酒，还有他知道自己在守着那座花园。
马布尔先生难得烂醉，他也从不追求烂醉。他求的只是抵达那个幸福的阶段——熬过想象力被撩起的阴森前奏之后——到了那时，他已无法连贯地思考，再不能顺着那些绵长的思路一路想下去，而那些思路，迟早总会通向对侦破和绞刑架的想象。赶在前一天酒劲还没散尽的清早，他便能舒舒服服进入这个阶段，随后靠一个简单机械的法子维持一整天：一旦发觉自己的念头往不痛快的地方滑，就喝上一口。这套章程并非苦心筹划出来的，不过是处境的自然结果，而且在很长一段时间里相当管用。脑子里一团舒适的迷雾，身子舒适地陷在起居室窗边的扶手椅里，膝上摊一本新书，偶尔瞟上一眼——如今送上门的邮件几乎清一色是出版商的新书预告，而马布尔先生平均每星期要买两本犯罪学的书——马布尔先生简直有些乐在其中了。妻子于他没多少分量，唯一的用处是家里有个顺手能使唤的人：每逢存酒落到他定死的底线——两瓶没开封的酒——以下，便打发她提着绿线网兜上杂货店，再买些威士忌回来。马布尔先生吃得少，妻子吃得更少；家里的活计本就少得可怜，可马布尔太太还是没着没落地拼命操劳，想把屋子拾掇齐整。马布尔太太成日里脚步轻悄、趿拉拖沓地满屋转悠，摸一摸，翻一翻，再放回原处。她的脑子正忙着琢磨一件事。

===Original===
It was through the agency of Madame Collins that she found the first clue to what she sought. Madame Collins had called in that evening as was her frequent habit and Mr. Marble had been just a little more sober than usual. In consequence the evening meal had been spent in the sitting-room, and supper﻿—a scratch meal, typical of Mrs. Marble﻿—had been served there. When the time came for Madame Collins to take her departure, Mr. Marble, surprisingly enough, had risen slowly to his feet with the intention of seeing her home. Mrs. Marble raised no objection; *that* was not what she was worrying about﻿—yet. There had been a brief delay while Mr. Marble was cramming his feet into his boots, soft feet, that for a week had known no greater restraint than that imposed by carpet slippers, and then they were gone. Mrs. Marble remained in the sitting-room. Once alone, her old restlessness reasserted itself. She began to wander round the room, touching, fumbling, replacing. She was seeking something, nothing in particular, just something. Really it was the solution of her problem that she was seeking.
Round the room went Mrs. Marble. She gazed for a space out of the window through which her husband stared so hard all day long, but it was quite dark outside and she could see nothing besides her own reflection. She picked up one or two of the ornaments on the mantelpiece, and put them back again. She ran her finger along the backs of the books that stood on the shelves. They did not interest her. Then she came to the book that lay on the arm of her husband’s chair, the book he had been reading in desultory fashion during the day. Mrs. Marble picked it up and ran the pages through her fingers. It was not an interesting book. She did not even know what the title﻿—somebody or other’s *Handbook of Medical Jurisprudence*﻿—meant. But at one point the book fell open of its own accord, and the open pages were well thumbed, proving that this portion had received more attention than the rest of the book. It was in the section on Poisons, and the paragraph was headed “Cyanides﻿—Potassium Cyanide, Sodium Cyanide.” A tiny wrinkle appeared between Mrs. Marble’s brows as she read this. She cast her mind back to that morning many months ago now, the morning after Medland’s dramatic arrival. Yes, that was the name on the label of the bottle she had found displayed on Will’s shelves. Potassium Cyanide. She went on to read what the book had to say on the subject.

> “Death is practically instantaneous. The patient utters a loud cry and falls heavily. There may be some foam at the lips, and after death the body often retains the appearance of life, the cheeks being red and the expression unaltered.”
===Chinese===
正是经由科林斯夫人之手，她才摸到了所寻之物的头一条线索。那天傍晚，科林斯夫人照她惯常的老样子来串门，而马布尔先生恰好比平日清醒那么一丁点。于是那顿晚饭便在起居室里用的，夜宵——一顿七拼八凑的便饭，十足是马布尔太太的手笔——也摆在了那里。到科林斯夫人该动身告辞的时分，出人意料，马布尔先生竟慢吞吞站起身来，打算送她回家。马布尔太太没有反对；*那*还不是她此刻发愁的事——暂时还不是。中间耽搁了一小会儿，马布尔先生正把两只脚往靴子里塞——一双软塌塌的脚，整整一个星期除了绒毯拖鞋，再没受过更大的拘束——随后两人便一道出去了。马布尔太太留在起居室里。一旦独处，她那老毛病似的坐立不安又冒了上来。她开始在屋里团团转，摸一摸，翻一翻，放回原处。她在找一样什么东西，说不上具体是什么，就是一样什么东西。其实她要找的，是她那道难题的答案。
马布尔太太绕着屋子打转。她对着窗外怔怔望了片刻——她丈夫成天死死盯着的正是这扇窗——可外头夜色已浓，除了自己的影子，什么也望不见。她拿起壁炉台上一两件摆设，又原样放了回去。她的手指顺着书架上一排书的书脊缓缓划过去。这些书提不起她的兴致。随后她走到丈夫椅子扶手上搁着的那本书跟前，那是他白天有一搭没一搭翻着的书。马布尔太太把它拿起来，让书页从指间哗哗掠过。这不是一本有趣的书。她连那书名——某位什么人写的*《法医学手册》*——是什么意思都看不懂。可是翻到一处，书页自行摊开了，摊开的那两页翻得起了毛边，可见比起书中其余部分，这一段下的功夫要多得多。那是“毒物”一章，段落的小标题是“氰化物——氰化钾、氰化钠”。读到这里，马布尔太太的眉心蹙起一道细纹。她的思绪回到好几个月前的那个早晨，梅德兰戏剧性登门之后的那个早晨。不错，正是她在威尔的架子上见到过的那个药瓶，标签上写的正是这个名字。氰化钾。她接着往下读，看这本书就这个问题还说些什么。

> “死亡几乎在一瞬间发生。患者发出一声大叫，沉重栽倒。唇间可能有些泡沫，死后尸体往往仍带着生时的模样，双颊发红，表情如故。”

===Original===
The wrinkle between Annie Marble’s brows was deeper now, and her breath was coming faster. She could remember what she had heard when she was half asleep the night of Medland’s coming. She had heard Will come up to the bathroom where his chemicals were kept, and she had heard him go down again. Then she had heard that loud cry.
In her next deduction her memory was at fault, but it was a fault that, curiously enough, confirmed her suspicions. Annie thought she remembered hearing a heavy fall at the same time as that loud cry. Of course she had not done so; young Medland had been sitting in a chair at the time when Marble said “Drink up,” but Annie was not to know that. Influenced by what she had read, Annie was quite certain that she had heard that heavy fall. Now she knew what it was she had heard dragged through the passage down the stairs to the kitchen. And she guessed whither it had been taken from the kitchen. She knew why Will spent all his time watching through the window to see that the garden was not interfered with. The problem with which she had been wrestling for weeks was solved. She felt suddenly weak, and she sank into the chair. All her other memories, crowding up now unsummoned, confirmed her solution. She could remember how there was suddenly more money at their disposal, and how Will had comported himself the next morning. She was sure of it all.
As she lay back in the chair, weak and wretched, she was startled to hear her husband’s key in the door. She made a spasmodic effort to hide what she was doing, but she was too weak to achieve anything. Her husband entered the sitting-room while she still held the book open in her hand. Her thumb was between the pages, at the point where there was that interesting description of the effects of potassium cyanide.
===Chinese===
此刻，安妮·马布尔眉心的那道纹更深了，呼吸也急促起来。她还记得梅德兰来的那天夜里，自己半睡半醒之间听见的动静。她听见威尔上楼到浴室去——他的那些化学药剂便收在那里——又听见他下楼。然后，她听见了那声大叫。
接下来的一步推断，她的记忆出了岔子，可说来也怪，这岔子偏偏坐实了她的怀疑。安妮自以为记得，就在那声大叫的同时，还听见了一记沉重的倒地声。她当然不曾听见；马布尔说“快把它喝了”的时候，小梅德兰还坐在椅子上呢，可安妮无从知道这一点。受了方才读到的文字的影响，安妮千真万确地认定自己听见过那记沉重的倒地声。如今她知道了，那天夜里被拖着走过走廊、拖下楼梯、拖进厨房的是什么。她也猜得到，那东西后来从厨房被弄到了哪里。她明白威尔为什么成天守在窗前，寸步不让地盯着那座花园。折磨了她好几个星期的难题，解开了。她只觉浑身骤然一软，跌坐进椅子里。其余的记忆此刻不召自来，纷纷涌上心头，桩桩件件都印证了她的答案。她记得家里怎么忽然多出了许多可供花销的钱，也记得威尔第二天早晨是怎样的言谈举止。这一切，她都确信不疑。
她虚弱而凄惶地瘫在椅子里，冷不丁听见丈夫的钥匙捅进门锁，吓了一跳。她手脚痉挛似的忙乱了一阵，想把手里的事遮掩过去，无奈浑身乏力，什么也没做成。丈夫走进起居室时，她手里还摊开着那本书。拇指夹在书页之间，正夹在那段关于氰化钾毒效的有趣描述上。

===Original===
When Mr. Marble crossed the threshold, he uttered an angry exclamation as he saw what Mrs. Marble was doing. He would tolerate no interference with his precious library. He strode forward to snatch the book out of her hand. Mrs. Marble sat helpless, and made no resistance. She even held out the book a little to him, offering it to his grip. But as she did so the book fell open at the place where she held it, fell open at the passage on the cyanides.
Mr. Marble saw this. He saw the look on her face, too. He stopped aghast. There was no need for words. In that brief space of time he realized that his wife knew. That she *knew*.
Neither of them said anything; neither of them was capable of saying anything in that tense moment. They eyed each other in poses curiously alike, she with her hand to her bosom as she peered at him, fluttering, tearful, and he with his hand, too, over his heart. Just lately he had not noticed so much the inconvenient tendency of that organ to beat with such violence, but now it was impressed upon him again. It thundered in his breast, depriving him of his strength, so that he had to hold on to a chair-back to support himself.
Annie gave a little, inarticulate cry. The book fell to the ground from her hand, and then sobbing, she fled from the room without again meeting his eye.
===Chinese===
马布尔先生一跨过门槛，看清马布尔太太在做什么，便怒喝了一声。谁也别想动他心爱的藏书，这他绝不容忍。他大步抢上前，要把书从她手里夺走。马布尔太太坐在那里全无办法，也不做丝毫抗拒。她甚至把书朝他微微递了过去，送到他手边任他来抓。可就在递出的一瞬，书在她捏着的地方摊开了，正摊在讲氰化物的那一段上。
马布尔先生看见了这一页。也看见了她脸上的神色。他惊骇地站住了。无需言语。就在那短短一瞬，他明白了：他的妻子知道了。她*知道*了。
两人谁也没做声；在那剑拔弩张的一刻，谁也说不出声来。两人对望着，姿势出奇地相像：她一手掩着胸口，心慌意乱、泪眼汪汪地窥觑着他；他也一手按在心口上。近来他倒不大留意那颗心爱这么狂跳乱撞的毛病，此刻它又结结实实给了他一番领教。那颗心在胸腔里擂鼓般轰响，抽走了他浑身的力气，逼得他不得不抓住一把椅背，才勉强撑得住身子。
安妮发出一声细微的、含混不清的喊叫。书从她手里跌落在地，随后她抽抽噎噎地逃出了房间，再没有迎上他的目光。

## XII

===Original===
There is no solitude like that which one can find in a London suburb. It is desolate, appalling. The weeks that passed now found the Marbles lost in this solitude, and over them, like a constant menace, hung the unvoiced menace of a secret shared. The days they spent together in the tawdry rooms downstairs; the nights they spent together in the vast gilt bed in the front bedroom, but for all that they were each of them lonely and frightened. The weight of their secret prevented all conversation save that about the necessary commonplaces of housekeeping, and even this they restricted self-consciously to the uttermost minimum. They did not exchange a dozen words a day; they said nothing, did nothing; they thought about nothing save the one dreadful thing about which they dared not speak. The solitude of the suburbs which they experienced was theirs from choice; they had cut themselves off voluntarily from their neighbours, and the neighbours in turn withdrew from them, sneering to each other about Mrs. Marble’s unhappy new clothes and the gorgeous furniture that they could see through the lower windows at Number 53. But this isolation was hardly new and was easily borne; it was far otherwise with the spiritual separation that encompassed them each individually.
They were living together alone in the tiny house; they were in each other’s society from choice﻿—they each soon found that they could not bear to have the other for long out of their sight﻿—but not once in weeks did their eyes meet. And never, never did they make any comment on their isolation.
Back into this nightmare world came Winnie, flushed with triumphs at school. She was undoubtedly beautiful now, and she was dressed to perfection, as soon as she had thrown off the shackles imposed by school rules. Her beauty had won over to her one party at school, and her almost unlimited pocket-money had won over another. Eleven months only younger than her dead brother, she was now just sixteen; a thoroughly good preliminary education at the secondary school﻿—to which she looked back with horror, and about which she had always been discreetly silent﻿—had saved her much trouble as regards actual school work and had placed her in the highest form after one term there. Miss Winifred Marble had the very highest opinion of herself.
===Chinese===
世上再没有一种孤独，比得上人在伦敦郊区所能寻见的那种。它荒凉，骇人。此后的几个星期，马布尔夫妇（the Marbles）便沉没在这样的孤独之中；一个两人共守的秘密虽不曾宣之于口，它的威胁却像一片常驻不散的阴云，悬在他们头顶。白天，他们一同厮守在楼下那几间俗艳的房间里；夜里，他们一同睡在前卧室那张阔大的镀金床上；尽管如此，两个人心里却各自孤独，各自惊惶。秘密的分量压住了一切交谈，只剩下持家过日子的必要俗务可谈；就连这些，他们也碍着心事，压缩到了最低限度。他们一天说不上十句话；什么也不说，什么也不做；心里所想的，只有那件可怕的事——那件他们不敢开口的事。他们所尝的这份郊区的孤独，是自己拣来的；他们主动同邻居断了往来，邻居们也就同他们疏远起来，只在彼此之间讪笑着议论马布尔太太（Mrs. Marble）那身不称心的新衣裳，还有从53号楼下窗子里望得见的那堂豪华家具。这种孤立本不算新鲜，也容易消受；而那种把他们各自裹在其中的心灵上的隔绝，就完全是另一回事了。
他们在这幢小房子里同住着，却又各自孤绝；彼此做伴是心甘情愿的——两人不久便都发现，对方离开自己的视线太久，自己就受不了——可一连几个星期，他们的目光不曾相接过一次。而对于这种彼此隔绝的光景，他们也从来不曾、从来不曾有过一句议论。
温妮（Winnie）就是回到这样一个梦魇世界里来的，脸上还带着学校里凯旋的红光。她如今确确实实出落得美了，一挣脱校规加身的枷锁，便把自己打扮得十全十美。她的美貌笼络住了学校里的一派人，她那份几乎无限的零用钱又笼络住了另一派。她只比死去的哥哥小十一个月，如今刚满十六岁；中学时代打下的扎实底子——那段岁月她回想起来不寒而栗，一向知趣地绝口不提——让她在正经功课上省了许多力气，入学一个学期，便升进了最高年级的班级。温妮弗雷德·马布尔（Winifred Marble）小姐对自己怀着顶高的评价。

===Original===
She came home in typical fashion. She had given her parents no exact information of the time of her return, and she was more or less unexpected when her taxicab drew up outside Number 53 Malcolm Road. She descended leisurely to the pavement. Malcolm Road might indeed be to her mind a horrible hole, but for all that she was not going to miss one tenth of the sensation she was aware she was making therein. She could see hurried heads appearing behind the curtains at all the houses around, and she allowed the neighbours ample time to view her mountainous luggage piled on the roof of the cab, and to admire enviously her smart blue costume. With a brief order to the driver to bring in her luggage she marched up the front path and knocked at the door with a resounding *rat-tat-tat*.
Indoors her father and mother were sitting together in the back room, he with a book on his knee as usual, she gazing into vacancy, following in her vague fashion all the unpleasant lines of thought that her husband had followed long ago. At the sound of Winnie’s knocks Marble rolled a frightened eye upon her. She rose in heart-fluttering panic.
“Will,” she said, “it’s not﻿—it’s not﻿—?”
===Chinese===
她回家，也回得一如既往地讲派头。她并没有把归期确切告知父母，因此当出租汽车停到马尔科姆路（Malcolm Road）53号门外时，她的到来多少有些出人意料。她从容不迫地下了车，踱到人行道上。在她心目中，马尔科姆路也许确是个糟透了的鬼地方，可尽管如此，她自知正在此地激起怎样的轰动，那份风光，十分之一也决不肯错过。她看得见四周各家的窗帘后面探出一颗颗仓皇的脑袋，于是她给足邻居们时间，让他们看够车顶上堆得小山也似的行李，再眼馋地欣赏够她那身俏丽的海蓝色套装。她简短地吩咐司机把行李搬进屋去，便昂然走上门前小径，*咚咚咚*地把门敲得震天响。
屋子里，她的父亲和母亲正一同坐在后屋：父亲照例膝上摊着一本书；母亲怔怔地望着虚空，懵懵懂懂地沿着丈夫很久以前走过的思路，把种种不祥的念头一一想过一遍。一听见温妮的敲门声，马布尔惊恐地朝妻子翻起一只眼睛。妻子心头突突乱跳，慌忙站起身来。
“威尔（Will），”她说，“该不会是——该不会是——？”

===Original===
Only police would knock like that at 53 Malcolm Road. Marble could make no answer for the moment. The knocking came again. Marble tried with shaking hands to light himself a cigarette. Come what may, he must try to look nonchalant, to bear himself calmly, as did all those men he had read about in his books when the fatal moment of arrest arrived. But his hands shook too much. His very lips were trembling, so that the cigarette quivered like a reed between them. The knocking was repeated. Then at last Mrs. Marble rallied.
“I’ll go,” she said, in a weak whisper.
Down the passage she went, soft-footed, ghostlike. Marble, still fumbling with his cigarette, heard the door open, after what seemed like ages of waiting. Then he heard Mrs. Marble say: “Oh, my dear, it’s you. Oh, dearie﻿—” and Winnie’s ladylike voice answering her. With the relief from tension the matches dropped from his fingers. The cigarette sagged from his mouth. He leant sideways against the arm of his chair, his eyes staring, too weak to move, while his heart thudded and fluttered back to its usual rhythm. That was how they found him, Winnie and her mother, when they came into the sitting-room to his expected welcome.
Such was the household to which Winnie returned, three days before Christmas. The girls at school, who envied Winnie her trunkfuls of clothes and her ample pocket-money, had been talking for weeks about what they intended to do this holiday. There had been talk of hunting, of dances, of theatres. There had been comparisons between the food they had at school and the food they would have at home during this period of delectable food. And in all this conversation Winnie had borne a part by no means equal to the part she usually bore in school conversations. Yet she had called up all the help of her imagination, and with its aid she had been able to produce some sort of picture of similar enjoyment in prospect for herself. That made the disappointment all the more bitter. The Marble family lunched that day, the first day of her arrival, on cold ham and stale bread and butter, and not enough of either. Her father’s clothes were baggy and spotted, and on his feet were wrecks of carpet slippers. He drank heavily of whisky during the meal, and he had obviously been drinking too much all the time she had been away. Her mother wore a shabby blouse and skirt, gaping wherever they could gape, and her stockings were in wrinkles up her thin calves. Winnie’s eyebrows puckered, and her lips curled a little as she observed these things.
===Chinese===
在马尔科姆路53号，只有警察才会那样敲门。马布尔一时竟答不上话来。敲门声又起了。马布尔用发抖的双手设法给自己点一支烟。无论来的是什么，他总得装出满不在乎的样子，摆出从容的架势，就像他在那些书里读到的家伙们，在被捕的大限时刻到来时那样。可他的手抖得太厉害，连嘴唇都在哆嗦，烟卷夹在唇间，像一茎芦苇似的颤动。敲门声再度响起。最后，还是马布尔太太强自振作了起来。
“我去开门，”她有气无力地低声说。
她顺着过道走去，脚步轻悄，像个幽灵。马布尔还在摆弄那支烟，仿佛等了一个世纪，才听见门开了。接着他听见马布尔太太说：“哎呀，亲爱的，是你呀。哎呀，宝贝儿——”还有温妮那副淑女腔在应着她。紧张一松，火柴从指间跌落，烟卷从嘴边耷拉下来。他歪身靠在椅子扶手上，双眼直瞪着，浑身绵软，动弹不得，心脏扑腾扑腾地跳了半天，才渐渐回到平常的节律。温妮和她母亲走进起居室、指望着他上前迎接时，看到的就是这么一副光景。
圣诞节前三天，温妮回来投身的就是这样一个家。学校里那些眼热她满箱满箱衣裳和大把零用钱的女孩们，几个星期来一直在谈论这个假期打算怎么过。谈打猎，谈跳舞，谈看戏；还把学校里的伙食，同这个美味时节里回家将吃到的伙食比较来比较去。而在这一类谈话里，温妮所扮演的角色，远不及她平日校园谈论中的分量。不过她也搬来了全部想象力救场，靠着它，总算给自己描出了一幅同样快活的前景。这就使得眼下的失望愈发苦涩。到家的头一天，马布尔一家的午饭是冷火腿配走了味的黄油面包，而且两样都还不够吃。父亲的衣裳松松垮垮、斑斑点点，脚上趿着的，是一双绒毯拖鞋的残骸。吃饭时他大口大口地灌威士忌，显然在她离家的整个日子里，他一直都喝得太凶。母亲穿着寒酸的短衫和裙子，能开绽的地方都开了绽，长袜皱皱巴巴地缩在瘦细的小腿上。温妮把这些看在眼里，眉头蹙了起来，嘴角也微微撇了撇。

===Original===
Then Mrs. Marble noticed that Winnie was dissatisfied, and inevitably she bridled. She knew her housekeeping was at fault, but she was not going to have her sixteen-year-old daughter running down her house.
“Isn’t there anything else to eat?” asked Winnie, when the last of the cold ham had disappeared, leaving her hungrier than when she started, accustomed as she was to the well-cooked and ample meals of the Berkshire school.
“No, there’s not,” snapped Mrs. Marble.
“But hang it all﻿—” protested Winnie.
===Chinese===
接着，马布尔太太察觉温妮心怀不满，便不由得动起气来。她自知操持有亏，可她决计不能容十六岁的女儿来数落自己的家。
“没有别的可吃的了吗？”冷火腿最后一口下了肚，温妮问道。伯克郡（Berkshire）那所学校的饭菜烧得又好、分量又足，她是吃惯了的；这一顿下来，反倒比动筷子之前更饿了。
“没有了。”马布尔太太厉声说。
“可这也太不像话了——”温妮抗议道。

===Original===
It was hardly the best of starts for a Christmas holiday. Winnie bore it for two days, and then, on Christmas eve, she began active operations. Her mother, whom she approached first, gave her no satisfaction.
“Oh, don’t worry me,” she said, with a heat unusual for her, “we’ve got enough to worry us as it is.”
“But what *have* you got to worry you?” said Winnie, genuinely bewildered. “Worry or no worry, we’ve got enough money and all that sort of thing, haven’t we?”
Mrs. Marble clutched at this straw for a moment, but she was not adept at deception, and her fainthearted statement that all was not well with them financially died away when she met Winnie’s incredulous gaze.
===Chinese===
这样的圣诞节假期，开头实在算不得好。温妮忍了两天，到圣诞前夜，便着手行动起来。她先去碰的是母亲，却没讨到半句满意的答复。
“哦，别来烦我，”她说，语气里带着一股她少有的火气，“我们要烦的事，眼下已经够多的了。”
“可我们*到底*有什么好烦的呀？”温妮说，当真纳闷得很。“烦也罢，不烦也罢，咱们的钱是够花的，别的一切也不缺，不是吗？”
马布尔太太抓住这根救命稻草撑了片刻，可她不善骗人，那句家里钱财上并不宽裕的心虚话，迎着温妮满脸不肯相信的目光，说到半截便没了声息。

===Original===
“Don’t be silly, mother,” said Winnie, and Mrs. Marble meekly bowed her head to the storm.
“No, it’s not money, dear. I’m sure your father gives me all I want in that way.”
“How much a week?” demanded Winnie, relentlessly.
Mrs. Marble made a last desperate stand against this implacable woman who had developed so surprisingly out of the little daughter she had once been.
===Chinese===
“别犯傻了，妈。”温妮说。马布尔太太温顺地向这场风暴低下了头。
“不是钱的事，亲爱的。你爸爸在钱那一头，向来是我要多少给多少，这我敢担保。”
“一星期给你多少？”温妮追问，毫不留情。
面对这个毫不容情的女人——当年那个小女儿竟出落成了这么一个人，着实出人意料——马布尔太太做了最后一次拼死的抵抗。

===Original===
“Never you mind,” she said. “It’s *my* business, and this is *my* house, and you’ve no right to interfere.”
Winnie sniffed.
“No right,” she said, “when you’ve given me cold ham three times and pressed beef once in two days? Do you know tomorrow’s Christmas, and I don’t believe you’ve done anything yet towards it? And look at your clothes! It’s worse than the last time I came home. I’m sure that before I went back to school I left you all nicely rigged out. You had such a nice costumes, and﻿—and﻿—” This was a false step, for neither Winnie nor her mother was yet able to bear a reference to poor dead John, for whom Mrs. Marble had bought mourning with Winnie’s help last holiday.
“Be quiet, do,” said Mrs. Marble, with tears in her eyes.
===Chinese===
“这你不用管，”她说。“这是*我*的事，这是*我*的家，你无权过问。”
温妮嗤了一声。
“无权过问，”她说，“两天里你就给我吃了三顿冷火腿、一顿压制牛肉？你知道不知道，明天就是圣诞节了，我看你到如今什么都还没操办呢？再瞧瞧你这身衣裳！比我上回回家时还要糟。我敢说，我回学校之前，把你们个个都打扮得漂漂亮亮的。你们那时有那么好的衣裳，还有——还有——”这话可说岔了：一提起可怜的、死去的约翰（John），温妮和母亲如今都还承受不住——上个假期，马布尔太太还在温妮的参谋下为他置办过丧服呢。
“别说了，好不好，”马布尔太太说，眼里含着泪水。

===Original===
They were not entirely tears of mourning, but they quelled Winnie effectively. Even she felt a little shy and embarrassed at sight of her mother crying. So she relaxed her inquiry, just when a little more pressing would have forced from her mother the astonishing facts that Mr. Marble was prepared for her to spend ten pounds a week on her housekeeping while she actually only spent two﻿—hardly as much as she had spent before they attained to riches.
But Winnie was at least persistent. After her mother she approached her father, daring even to break in upon his whisky-sodden reverie in the drawing-room.
“Father,” said Winnie, “are we dreadfully poor since you left off going to the City?”
Mr. Marble rolled a maudlin eye upon her. Then pride reasserted itself﻿—pride in his achievement all those months ago, which was still mentioned with bated breath by City clerks, but which had never received its meed of recognition in his own home.
===Chinese===
那泪水并不全是哀恸之泪，却着实镇住了温妮。眼见母亲落泪，连她也觉得有点讪讪的，怪不自在。于是她松了口，不再追问——偏偏就在这当口，只消再逼一句，便能从母亲嘴里逼出那桩惊人的实情：马布尔先生（Mr. Marble）原打算给她每星期十英镑的家用，而她实际只花两英镑——几乎还赶不上他们发迹以前的花销。
不过温妮至少有股缠劲。问过母亲，她又去寻父亲，竟敢闯进客厅，去搅扰他被威士忌泡得半沉的神游。
“爸爸，”温妮说，“打从您不去伦敦金融城（the City）上班，咱们是不是穷得厉害了？”
马布尔先生醉眼迷离地朝她转过眼去。接着，自尊心又抬起头来——那是他对好几个月前那桩功绩的自豪；那桩事至今还被伦敦金融城的职员们屏息谈论着，可在他自己家里，却从没得过一分应得的承认。

===Original===
“No,” he said. “We’ve got plenty.”
“That’s good. It’s Christmas tomorrow. I want some money. Lots of it. Mother hasn’t done anything about it yet.”
Far back in Mr. Marble’s dulled memory ghosts began to stir. He remembered those times﻿—they seemed ages ago now﻿—when he wanted his wife to spend money, and had had all the difficulty in the world to induce her to do so.
===Chinese===
“不是，”他说。“咱们的钱多着呢。”
“那就好。明天就是圣诞节了。我要点钱。要一大笔。妈到现在还什么都没操办呢。”
在马布尔先生迟钝的记忆深处，有几个幽灵开始骚动。他想起那些日子——如今恍如隔世——那时他还巴望着妻子花钱，费了九牛二虎之力才劝得动她花。

===Original===
He rolled obediently out of his chair and walked almost steadily across to the ridiculous gilt bureau in the corner of the room. He fumbled it open; fumbled out his chequebook; fumblingly signed a cheque.
“Banks shut at half-past three,” he said. “Better be quick.”
Winnie only had to give a fleeting glance at the cheque. It was for one hundred pounds.
“Thank you,” she said, and before she had left the room she was calling to her mother to put her hat on.
===Chinese===
他乖乖地从椅子里挪出身来，脚步近乎平稳地走到屋子角落那张可笑的镀金写字台前。他摸摸索索地打开写字台，摸摸索索地取出支票簿，又摸摸索索地签了一张支票。
“银行三点半关门，”他说。“最好快着点。”
温妮只朝那支票飞快地扫了一眼。票面是一百英镑。
“谢谢您，”她说。人还没走出房门，已经扬声喊母亲戴帽子了。

===Original===
Mrs. Marble had never been so hurried and flurried in her life before as she was that Christmas eve.
There was the rush to get the bus to Rye Lane. Then there was the rush to the bank to cash the cheque. Winnie put the money in her handbag as if she was accustomed to having a hundred pounds there every day of her life. Then there was a series of rushes up and down Rye Lane, crowded with Christmas shoppers, buying everything which Mrs. Marble had omitted to buy, which comprised a good many necessities as well as the inevitable luxuries of Christmas. She was almost dropping with unaccustomed fatigue when Winnie hailed a heaven-sent taxicab that appeared and piled her and the myriad parcels they had accumulated into it.
Yet even this did not satisfy Winnie. She was not even satisfied with bullying her mother next day into cooking turkey and reheating the ready-made Christmas puddings they had bought. She was not satisfied with insisting on having a clean cloth on the table and all the silver displayed thereon. She was not satisfied with giving presents﻿—bought with the money obtained yesterday﻿—to her father and mother, and with showing them what they had bought for her from the same source. She was not satisfied with hanging holly and mistletoe all over the house. Even when Christmas Day was over and her parents thought they had suffered all they could, she began to go systematically through the house “putting things straight.” That Berkshire school prided itself on the domestic training it gave its girls﻿—domestic training on a scale calculated for women who would not be likely ever to have dealings with the economics of a house of thirty pounds a year rateable value into which no servant or even charwoman was allowed ever to set foot. Winnie’s ideas were on the grand scale.
===Chinese===
马布尔太太有生以来，从没像那个圣诞前夜那样被支使得手忙脚乱、心慌意乱。
先是紧赶慢赶地搭公共汽车去赖巷（Rye Lane）。接着是赶去银行兑现支票。温妮把钱收进手提包里，那副神气，仿佛她生来天天包里都装着一百英镑。然后是在挤满圣诞采购人潮的赖巷里一趟趟地奔来奔去，把马布尔太太漏买的东西一一买齐——里头既有不少正经必需品，也有圣诞节必不可少的种种奢侈吃食。等温妮招呼来一辆仿佛天赐的出租汽车、把她和娘儿俩攒下的大包小包一并塞进车里时，她几乎要让这不习惯的劳累给累垮了。
然而即便这样，温妮还不满足。第二天，她连哄带逼，让母亲烤了火鸡、回锅热了买来的现成圣诞布丁，这还不算满足。她执意要在桌上铺干净的桌布、把全副银器都摆出来，这也不够。她用昨天到手的那笔钱给父母备下礼物，又让他们看同样从那笔钱里给她买来的东西，这也不够。她把冬青和槲寄生挂满整幢房子，这还是不够。等到圣诞节过完、父母自以为罪已受足，她又动手把整幢房子按部就班地“收拾归置”起来。那所伯克郡学校素来以它给姑娘们的家政训练自傲——那训练的规格，是照着这样一类女人设计的：她们这辈子多半不至于同哪一幢年课税估值三十英镑、连女仆乃至打杂女佣都从不许踏进门的人家，打经济上的交道。温妮的章法，素来是大手笔。

===Original===
She succeeded in upsetting Mrs. Marble, and by natural reaction she upset her father as well. Mr. Marble had been unhappy before, but it was a negative and inactive unhappiness. He had settled down into a groove, and a groove, with all its suggestion of permanency, was grateful to a man in the shadow of the gallows. Any disturbance of that groove was annoying. He had grown used to being badly fed, and he never noticed the other details of household management. He had even ceased long since from having any pride in his Empire furniture. But Winnie with her bustle and hustle disturbed him. Without realizing it, he had taken comfort from his wife’s inaction, knowing that that meant there was less chance of her giving away the secret he knew she held in her bosom. And now Winnie had descended upon them to alter all this. He did not like it. He liked it still less when he found that Winnie had her eye upon his drinking habits and was meditating interfering with them as well.
But happily Winnie was not of the stuff that carries things through to the bitter end in face of all opposition. She was like her father in that she was able to make one big effort which achieved much but left her incapable of more for a considerable time. Her activity died down, and in a brief time she found herself actually acquiescing in some of Mrs. Marble’s shiftless methods of housekeeping. And in a flash she found that she was incredibly bored.
She had once more got her mother’s wardrobe into some sort of order, and she had bullied and cajoled her into wearing the good clothes that were heaped untidily about her bedroom. By the time she had made her mother smart again, and had rearranged everything in accordance with the system that even only two terms at the school had ingrained into her, Winnie found that her interest in housekeeping was waning, and that life at 53 Malcolm Road was decidedly dull.
===Chinese===
她成功地搅得马布尔太太方寸大乱，一石激起，自然又搅乱了她的父亲。马布尔先生先前就不快活，但那是一种消极的、无所作为的不快活。他早已在一条车辙里安顿下来；而一条车辙，连同它那处处暗示的一成不变，对一个活在绞架阴影底下的男人，倒是一种安稳的慰藉。这条车辙但凡受一点搅动，都令他着恼。他已惯了吃得很坏，持家的其余种种细节，他从来不曾留心。就连那堂帝政式家具（Empire furniture），他也早就不再引以为豪。可温妮这么风风火火地一折腾，他就不得安生了。他不曾自觉地意识到，自己一直从妻子的无所作为里汲取着安慰——因为他知道，那就意味着她泄露怀中那个秘密的机会更小。如今温妮大驾压境，要把这一切统统翻改。他不喜欢。等他发觉温妮还盯上了他喝酒的毛病、正盘算着连这个也要插手时，他就更不喜欢了。
所幸温妮不是那种顶着一切反对、硬干到底的材料。在这一点上她像她父亲：能发一回大劲，成事不少，可此后好一阵子，再也使不出半分力气。她的干劲渐渐平息，不多时，她竟发现自己已在默许马布尔太太那几样没章法的持家手段了。而忽然之间，她发觉自己无聊得要命。
她又把母亲的衣柜理出了个眉目，又连唬带劝，让她把胡乱堆在卧室四下的好衣裳穿起来。待到她把母亲重新拾掇得齐整，又依照那学校仅仅两个学期便已刻进她骨子里的章法，把家中样样重新布置停当，温妮发觉自己对持家的兴味正日渐消退，而马尔科姆路53号的生活，实在是乏味得可以。

===Original===
So she wrote off one or two letters, to friends of hers at school. It hardly matters what she put into them, whether it was fact or fiction, whether she wrote about illness﻿—of course not infectious﻿—at home, or about domestic unhappiness. Whatever it was she wrote, it achieved its object. She received very shortly afterwards two invitations to spend the rest of her holidays with these friends of hers.
By that time neither Mr. nor Mrs. Marble was sorry to see her go. She had caused too much disturbance altogether. So they said goodbye to her in a philosophic manner. Mr. Marble, partly as a sheer thanksgiving offering, partly because Winnie took it as a matter of course, and partly even with a flicker of his old pride that a daughter of his should be going to stay as a guest at a house whose sole address was a name and a county, handed over another cheque. Life had grown so strange and unreal to them that they saw nothing out of the ordinary in letting a sixteen-year-old girl go away to stay with people they did not know, with something nearly approaching a hundred pounds in her handbag.
After all, Mr. Marble’s income was nearly twelve hundred a year; of that Winnie’s fees amounted to nearly three hundred, but of the remaining nine he hardly spent a quarter. A man who has five hundred a year for which he can find no use does not worry about odd hundreds, especially when he spends every waking minute in terror of being hanged.
===Chinese===
于是她提笔写了一两封信，寄给学校里的几位朋友。信里写了什么，其实无关紧要：是实情还是编造，写的是家里有人抱病——自然不是传染病——还是家中不睦，都不要紧。反正无论她写了什么，目的达到了。没隔多久，她便收到两份邀请，请她上这几位朋友家去，把余下的假期度完。
到这时，无论马布尔先生还是马布尔太太，都不为她的离去惋惜了。她惹出的动荡毕竟太多了。于是他们以一种达观的态度同她道了别。马布尔先生又交出了一张支票——几分是纯粹的谢恩献礼，几分是因为温妮把这事视作理所当然，甚至还有几分旧日自豪的微光在闪：他的女儿竟要去一户人家做客留宿，而那户人家的整个地址，不过是一个宅名加一个郡名。生活于他们早已变得如此离奇而不真实，以致放任一个十六岁的女孩、拎着装了将近一百英镑的手提包、去素不相识的人家小住，他们竟看不出半点不寻常。
说到底，马布尔先生的年收入将近一千二百英镑；其中温妮的学费一项就将近三百，剩下的九百，他连四分之一也花不掉。一个每年手握五百英镑却寻不出用途的男人，是不会为区区几百英镑操心的——尤其当他还把醒着的每一分钟，都浸泡在对绞刑的恐惧里的时候。

## XIII

===Original===The peace that had been so rudely disturbed was only with difficulty regained. The Marbles found it hard to settle down once more into the old groove. And no sooner had they begun to do so than another interruption to their peace﻿—if their panic-stricken existence can be so called﻿—occurred, this time at the hands of a more dangerous person, Madame Collins.

She had come to the limit of her patience. Nearly six months had elapsed since she had paid into her account a little roll of banknotes which might have been traced, had anyone taken the trouble, to Mr. Marble. After that last terrible occurrence, when that silly boy John had met with the motorcycling accident, she had indeed been content to wait a while, until matters should be quieter again, but this wait was too long altogether. Dressmaking in a back street in Dulwich with an automaton of a husband began to annoy her exceedingly. Anything was better than that, she decided. Even at Christmas time she had meditated action, but Winnie had been there when she called, and Winnie had looked her up and down with cool insolence and even Marguerite Collins had been abashed﻿—or, rather, had decided that it would be better if Winnie were not her enemy. She bided her time awaiting a more favourable moment.

One morning Mr. Marble was alone at home, for Annie had gone out on one of her all too infrequent shopping expeditions. She had been gone five minutes only, when to Marble’s ear came the familiar sound of her hurried, quiet knock. With an enormous effort﻿—everything was a great deal of trouble nowadays﻿—he heaved himself out of his chair and went to the door.

Marguerite Collins was determined to have no nonsense. As the door opened she stepped inside, and she was through into the drawing-room and sitting down before Marble had shut it again. Marble came and stood before her wearily and dull-wittedly. There was clearly going to be trouble, and Mr. Marble did not feel in the mood for trouble.

===Chinese===
那场被粗暴搅破的平静，好不容易才失而复得。马布尔一家（the Marbles）发觉很难再落回旧日的轨道里去。可他们刚要安顿下来，他们的平静——如果他们那种惶惶不可终日的日子也配叫平静的话——便又一次遭到打断，而这一回出手的，是个更加危险的人物：科林斯夫人（Madame Collins）。

她的耐心已经到了头。她把一小卷钞票存进自己账户，至今差不多有六个月了；那卷钞票，只要有人肯费心去查，是能查到马布尔先生（Mr. Marble）头上的。上次那桩可怕的变故——那个傻小子约翰（John）骑摩托车出了事——之后，她确实甘心又等了一阵，等风声重新平息，可这一等实在等得太久了。在达利奇（Dulwich）一条后街上做女装活计，守着一个木头人似的丈夫，这样的日子开始让她烦得要命。她拿定主意，随便什么都比这强。圣诞节的当口她就盘算过要动手，偏偏她上门那天温妮（Winnie）在家，温妮冷冰冰地、目中无人地把她从头到脚打量了一遍，连玛格丽特·科林斯（Marguerite Collins）都不由得讪讪然——或者说，她认定了还是别跟温妮结仇为好。她按住性子，等待更有利的时机。

一天上午，马布尔先生独自在家，安妮（Annie）出门购物去了——她难得出一趟门。她走了才五分钟，马布尔耳边便传来那熟悉的敲门声，又急促又压得低。他费了九牛二虎之力——如今干什么都是天大的难事——才把自己从椅子里撑起来，挪去开门。

玛格丽特·科林斯打定主意，容不得半点含糊。门一开她就跨了进来，不等马布尔把门重新带上，人已经穿进客厅，坐下了。马布尔走到她跟前站定，一脸疲惫，一脸木然。眼见得要出乱子了，而马布尔先生实在没有应付乱子的心气。

===Original===“Well, what is it?” asked Marble.

Marguerite did not answer at once. She threw back the fur from round her neck and drew off her gloves with slow, deliberate movements. With a gesture she made much of her round, white throat and her plump hands. Six months ago this alone would have stirred Marble into action, but now it left him unmoved. Those six months had been spent in sluggish over-drinking and in frothy anxiety. Besides, he had had his will of her, and Marguerite was not of the type to revive dead passion. All this Marguerite realized as she watched, keenly but covertly, his unshaved face and the expressionless blue eyes. It was as she feared. Well, it would have to be pure business, then, without the frail disguise of anything else.

“You are not pleased that I come to see you?” she asked, with the lisp and trace of accent that Marble had once thought so wonderful.

“No,” said Marble, in no mood for tact. As a matter of fact, he was rapidly growing incapable of thinking about anything save what lay outside under the soil of the dreary backyard.

===Chinese===
“呃，什么事？”马布尔问。

玛格丽特没有立刻回答。她把颈间的皮草往身后一撩，用缓慢而刻意的动作褪下手套，举手投足都在卖弄那圆润白皙的脖颈和丰腴的双手。换作六个月前，光这一手就足以让马布尔心猿意马，如今他却无动于衷。那六个月，他是在懒洋洋的酗酒和虚浮的焦灼中度过的。何况他早已把她弄到了手，而玛格丽特又不是能让死灰复燃的那类女人。这一切，玛格丽特都一边用犀利而隐秘的目光打量着他那没刮的脸和那双毫无表情的蓝眼睛，一边看明白了。果然不出她所料。好吧，那就只能是纯粹的生意了，再不必拿别的名目来虚掩。

“我来看你，你不高兴吗？”她问道，带着那口咬舌的腔调和隐隐的外国口音——从前马布尔还觉得那妙不可言。

“不高兴。”马布尔说，懒得讲什么客套。说实在的，除了外面那个阴沉沉的后院泥土底下埋着的东西，他很快就要想不动任何别的事情了。

===Original===But the monosyllable roused Madame Collins to fury. It hurt, the more so as she had realized it previously.

“You are not polite,” she said, a faint flush appearing on her soft﻿—over-soft﻿—cheeks.

“No,” said Marble.

“You admit it! Are you not ashamed? And do you not remember when you would not have said a thing like that to me﻿—no, not for worlds?”

“No,” said Marble.

===Chinese===
可这一个字便把科林斯夫人惹得勃然大怒。这话伤人，何况她事先心里早已明白。

“你可真没礼貌。”她说，那柔软的——未免过于柔软的——面颊上泛起淡淡的红晕。

“没错。”马布尔说。

“你倒承认了！你不害臊吗？再说，你记不记得从前——那时候你就是给我全世界，也决计不肯对我说这种话的，嗯？”

“不记得。”马布尔说。

===Original===“No, no, no! Have you nothing else to say to me except ‘no’?”

“No,” said Marble. He could hardly be said to be intentionally rude; but a man whose mind chooses that particular moment to run off on its favourite track towards arrest and execution is in no fit state to argue any point whatever with a hot-tempered woman﻿—especially when he is in the wrong.

Marguerite Collins bit her lips, and then restrained herself by a violent effort of will. After all, money﻿—so said her peasant soul to herself﻿—was sweeter than revenge at any time, sweet though they both were; if she could obtain no money, then perhaps she could have the other, but she would spare no effort to screw more money out of this weak-minded rentier first.

She spoke calmly, and she allowed just as much of the old sweetness to creep into her tone as she thought might soften Marble towards her.

“Listen, Will. I am in trouble. I am in great trouble. My husband﻿—you know what he is, I have told you, oh, so often﻿—he is unsupportable. I hate him. And now I think﻿—he hates me also. I must leave him. I must go away. I shall go back to Normandy, to Rouen. But I must have money. He has none. No more have I. Will, dear﻿—”

===Chinese===
“不，不，不！除了说‘不’，你就没有别的话要对我说了吗？”

“没有了。”马布尔说。这也算不上他存心无礼；只是一个人的心思偏偏挑上那个节骨眼，顺着它最熟的那条岔道溜下去——一直溜到被捕和处决——这样的人，实在没法同一位火性子的女人争辩任何问题，何况理亏的还是他。

玛格丽特·科林斯咬住嘴唇，随即凭一股狠劲把自己强压了下去。说到底，金钱——她那农妇的灵魂对自己说——无论什么时候都比复仇更甘美，尽管两样都甜；倘若到头来捞不着钱，那复仇或许还能捞着，可她还是要先使尽一切手段，从这个软骨头食利者身上多榨出些钱来。

她开口了，语调平静，又恰如其分地让往日的那份甜意悄悄渗进声音里去——她估量着，这么一点甜，正足以把马布尔对她的心肠泡软。

“你听着，威尔（Will）。我有难处了。天大的难处。我丈夫——你知道他那个人，我对你讲过的，啊，讲了不知多少回——他简直叫人忍无可忍。我恨他。如今我想——他也恨我了。我必须离开他。我必须走。我要回诺曼底（Normandy）去，回鲁昂（Rouen）去。可我必须有钱。他一个子儿也没有。我也没有。威尔，亲爱的——”

===Original===Marble made one of the greatest mistakes of his life when he said “no” for the fifth time that morning. The flush on Marguerite’s cheeks became deeper; she was scarlet with indignation. It is doubtful why Marble should have said it; a single one of those unspent hundreds from his annual income would have ended the matter for the time. But the refusal slipped out of his mouth before he was aware of it; he was only trying to temporize. City caution told him that this was blackmail, and that it is fatal to yield to a blackmailer; he also realized at the back of his mind that he most certainly had not in the house at that moment enough ready money to satisfy her, and he was not going to give her a cheque﻿—not he. So he said “no” really meaning “yes,” and had he not been so dull-witted that morning he would have bitten his tongue out rather than have said it.

Marguerite condescended to use a threat or two.

“That is a pity,” she said, “for I must have my freedom. If I were to tell my husband one or two little things﻿—ah, he would set me free, do you not think so? But it would cost you much money, more than what I have stooped to ask you. And your wife, she would not like that to happen, would she? She does not know at present, eh? If you would like her to﻿—?”

Marble’s face had turned from pale to flushed and back again to pallor.

===Chinese===
那天上午，马布尔第五次说出那个“不”字，铸成了他一生中最重大的错误之一。玛格丽特脸颊上的红晕加深了，她气得满脸通红。为什么马布尔偏要说这个“不”字，实在说不清；他年收入里还没动用的那几百英镑，只消随手拿出一张，就足以把眼下这桩事暂时了结。可是拒绝的话没等他省悟便溜出了口——他本只想拖延周旋。伦敦金融城（the City）式的谨慎告诫他：这是敲诈，而对敲诈者让步是致命的；他心底还明白，此刻家里断然凑不出够打发她的现钱，而支票他是决计不给的——他才不给呢。于是他嘴上说“不”，心里想的其实是“行”，倘若那天上午他不是那么昏聩，他宁可把舌头咬下来，也决不说出那个字。

玛格丽特屈尊施展出那么一两句威胁。

“那就可惜了，”她说，“因为我非拿到我的自由不可。要是我把一两件小事讲给我丈夫听——啊，他倒是会放我自由的，你不这样想吗？只是那要花掉你好大一笔钱，比我屈尊来向你开口讨的还要多。还有你太太，她可不乐意出这种事，对不对？她眼下还一点不知道呢，嗯？你要不要让她——？”

马布尔的脸由白转红，又由红转回惨白。

===Original===The stab had gone home. Anything rather than let Annie know. Annie held the key of his life in her hand; she had guessed his secret, he was sure of it. The knowledge had troubled him little up to the moment. She had been a cipher in his life for so long that he had hardly cared, save that it had made it uncomfortable to meet her eyes. But if Annie were to know of this! His drink-dazed mind realized for the first time how desperately necessary it was that Annie should be kept in a good humour. The terror in his breast made him lose control over himself.

“All right, I’ll pay you,” he said. “How much is it?”

He had cut the ground from under his feet. He had shown her which was the best course of action; he had shown her how much he feared Annie’s knowing; by his earlier refusal and later hurried agreement he had delivered himself over bound and naked to his enemy. Marguerite laughed a little, a malicious, throaty laugh. Then she spoke, mentioning the sum quite as if it were a matter of course.

“Three hundred pounds.”

“I﻿—I can’t afford all that!”

===Chinese===
这一刀正扎在心窝上。什么都行，千万别让安妮知道。安妮手里攥着他一生的钥匙；她已猜到了他的秘密，这一点他有把握。直到此刻为止，这层认知都不曾怎么困扰过他。这么多年来，她在他的生活里早就是一个可有可无的人，他也几乎不放在心上，只是对上她的目光时会浑身不自在而已。可万一安妮知道了这一桩！他那被酒泡昏的头脑头一回醒悟到：让安妮保持好心情，竟是这般性命攸关的大事。胸中的恐惧使他失掉了自持。

“好吧，我付给你。”他说，“要多少？”

他把自己的退路拆了个干净。他等于指给了她一条最好的进攻路线；等于亮明了他是多么怕安妮知道；先前的拒绝，加上这会儿慌里慌张的应允，他已经把自己五花大绑、剥得精光，交到了敌人手里。玛格丽特轻轻笑了，一声恶意的、压在喉咙里的笑。然后她开了口，报出那个数目，那口气俨然这事天经地义。

“三百英镑。”

“我——我可拿不出这么多！”

===Original===The surprise in Mr. Marble’s voice was obvious and genuine; but Marguerite was quick-witted enough to see that he really could afford the huge sum.

“Three hundred pounds,” she said again.

“But I haven’t got all that in the house, and a cheque﻿—”

“It is a cheque that I want,” said Madame Collins grimly, and seeing him still hesitate for a moment she added, “And your wife will be back soon, will she not?”

Marble went over to the gilt bureau and wrote out the cheque.

===Chinese===
马布尔先生声音里的惊愕又明显又真切；可玛格丽特脑子快得很，听得出这么大一笔钱，他其实拿得出。

“三百英镑。”她又说了一遍。

“可我家里没搁着这么多现钱，要是开支票的话——”

“我要的就是支票。”科林斯夫人冷冷地说。见他仍旧迟疑了一瞬，她又补上一句：“再说，你太太快回来了吧，嗯？”

马布尔走到那架镀金写字台跟前，把支票开了出来。

===Original===She was just re-clasping her handbag when they heard Annie Marble’s key in the door. When she entered the room Marble was the one that was obviously discomposed. She herself was honey-sweet as usual, calm and self-possessed.

“I have come to say goodbye,” she said. “Tomorrow I go to France.”

“To France?”

“Yes, I am going to have a holiday. I am sorry that you were out when I came, for I have so much to do that I fear I cannot stay. No, no, really I cannot. Goodbye, dear Mrs. Marble. I will send you a postcard from Rouen.”

With that she was gone. It was rather a pity that Mr. Marble should have been so obviously anxious to get rid of her. Why, she herself was most anxious to be out of the house too, so that she could hurry and cash that cheque before Marble could stop it, if by chance he recovered his spirits enough to do anything like that, but she showed no signs of it at all. It was perfectly true that at the time Mrs. Marble did not notice her husband’s nervousness, but little things like that, as time had already shown, had a way of staying in Mrs. Marble’s memory and re-emerging at inconvenient moments.

===Chinese===
她正重新扣拢手包，两人就听见安妮·马布尔的钥匙捅进了门锁。等她走进屋里，明显张皇失措的倒是马布尔。她本人却一如既往地甜得像蜜，又镇定，又从容。

“我是来道别的。”她说，“明天我就去法国。”

“去法国？”

“是啊，我去度个假。真遗憾，我来的时候你偏出去了，我有好多事要料理，恐怕不能多坐。不坐了，不坐了，真的不能坐了。再会，亲爱的马布尔太太。我从鲁昂给你寄明信片来。”

说完这话她就走了。只是马布尔先生那副巴不得她快走的焦急未免太露骨，不能不说是一桩憾事。可不是么，她自己才急着离开这所房子呢，好赶紧抢在马布尔止付之前把那张支票兑成现钱——倘若他碰巧缓过劲儿来、真会去止付的话——不过她自己的这份急切，倒是一点儿也没有外露。当时马布尔太太确实没有留意丈夫的紧张，可是这一类小事，正如时间已经证明的那样，自有法子留在马布尔太太的记忆里，专挑不凑巧的当口重新浮上来。

===Original===After Madame Collins’ departure Mr. Marble eyed his wife anxiously. He had realized now that she was a person of immense importance to his affairs, and, what was more, that she was, after all, someone who might, should it so happen, act independently. He had grown so used all his life to regarding her as the very reverse of a free agent, as obedient to himself almost as one of his own limbs, that the reflection that she might not be so startled him. There was only one thing, Marble knew, that would make her break out contrary to his wishes, but that was the least accountable of all factors. If Annie got to know that he had been unfaithful to her; if she had it forced home upon her that his love for her﻿—if ever it had existed; and it had in her imagination, which was all that mattered﻿—was dead, then she would be capable of doing the most unexpected things. She would not deliberately betray him﻿—not even terror-maddened Marble thought that﻿—but she might in her consternation allow something to escape her that would set in action that swarm of rumours and the resultant investigation that Mr. Marble so dreaded. It was of the first importance that she should continue to think he loved her. And the fact that he endowed this circumstance with its full importance was directly due to Madame Collins. At the moment he felt almost grateful to the latter for showing him this. But he eyed his wife anxiously, for all that. It was an added complication, and the burden of his troubles was already almost more than he could bear.

And yet perhaps, although Mr. Marble did not appreciate it, this new complication was for the time at least a blessing in disguise. It took Mr. Marble’s mind off the main point of his troubles, and that was more than anything else had done during the last year. The situation reacted upon him in such a way that for twenty-four hours Mr. Marble hardly drank any whisky at all.

But it was one thing to decide to make oneself agreeable to one’s wife, and quite another to carry it out. Mr. Marble felt positively embarrassed as he eyed his wife and tried to brace himself for action. He had lived with her in the closest proximity and yet in the harshest isolation for a year now; it would be a difficult matter to break the ice and start afresh. Besides, there lay between them the shadow of a terrible secret. That might serve to bind them closer together later on, but at present it was an obstacle almost insurmountable. Not all that day, not that night, not the next day did Mr. Marble make much progress.

He made no progress in his own estimation, that is to say. Thirty-six hours after deciding upon this course of action Mr. Marble still felt almost shy and almost embarrassed in his wife’s presence. But Mrs. Marble had noticed something. First and foremost, of course, she noticed that he was not drunk. That was obvious. This temperance was partly deliberate and partly reflex, dependent on Mr. Marble’s knowledge that it would be as well to keep his head clear, and to appear as attractive in his wife’s eyes as possible. But partly it was due to the fact that, with this new problem to think about, Mr. Marble had no thought to spare for his other troubles, and, consequently, no need to dull his mind to them.

===Chinese===
科林斯夫人走后，马布尔先生忧心忡忡地打量着妻子。他如今才明白，对他的种种利害而言，她是个至关重要的人物；更要紧的是，她归根到底是个万一出了岔子、可能自作主张行事的人。他这辈子惯于把她看成全然谈不上自由意志的人，看成几乎像自己的一条胳膊一样听自己使唤，以致一想到她未必如此，他竟为之心惊。马布尔知道，只有一件事能让她违逆他的意思闹起来，可那偏偏是所有因素里最没准数的一个。倘若安妮得知他对她不忠；倘若她被硬生生逼着认清：他对她的爱情——就算那爱情存在过；反正在她的想象里存在过，而唯有想象才算数——已经死了，那么她就干得出最出乎意料的事情来。她不会存心出卖他——这一点，连被恐惧逼疯了的马布尔都不这么想——可她惊慌之下，保不齐会漏出一句什么话，惹起那一大群流言，以及随之而来的、马布尔先生如此惧怕的调查。头一等要紧的，是让她继续相信他爱她。而他所以能掂出这件事的全部分量，恰恰是拜科林斯夫人所赐。此刻他几乎感激她点醒了这一点。尽管如此，他还是忧心忡忡地打量着妻子。这是新添的一重乱子，而他那副烦恼的重担，本来已经压得他几乎承受不住了。

然而，尽管马布尔先生自己并不领会，这个新添的乱子至少眼下说不定倒是塞翁失马。它把马布尔的心思从烦恼的要害上引开了，这一年来，还没有任何东西能做到这一点。这局面在他身上引起的反应是：整整二十四小时，马布尔先生几乎没怎么沾威士忌。

可是，打定主意讨妻子欢心是一回事，把这主意落到实处又是另一回事。马布尔先生望着妻子、暗暗给自己撑劲的时候，竟觉得说不出的窘迫。到如今，他与她同住一个屋檐下，贴得那么近，又隔得那么远，已经整整一年了；要打破这层坚冰、从头来过，谈何容易。何况横在他们两人中间的，还有一桩可怕秘密的阴影。那阴影往后或许能把两人拴得更紧，眼下却是一道几乎翻不过去的障碍。那一天，那一夜，再加上第二天，马布尔先生都没有多大的进展。

这是说，照他自己的估量，他没有进展。打定这个主意之后三十六个小时，马布尔先生在妻子面前仍旧感到近乎羞怯、近乎窘迫。可马布尔太太已经察觉出些什么了。首先，当然，她察觉出他没有喝醉。这是明摆着的。这份节制，一半出于有意，一半出于本能，全仗着马布尔先生心里明白：脑子最好保持清醒，在妻子眼里也要显得尽量有魅力。可还有一半是因为，心里挂着这个新难题，马布尔先生腾不出工夫去想别的烦恼，自然也就用不着拿酒把心思灌麻了。

===Original===But Mrs. Marble noticed more than his soberness. She caught him repeatedly looking at her with an anxious air﻿—the same manner as a courting lover might display. And he made one or two tentative attempts at conversation with her too. Seeing that he had said nothing to her for months save the one or two words that had to be said this was an enormous difference. He looked at her, he spoke to her, with a shyness that set her fluttering, and more than once he opened his mouth as though to say something to her and then held back at the last minute, obviously embarrassed. Mrs. Marble felt strangely pleased. After all, dear Will was her whole life, especially now that John was no longer with her, and, secret or no secret, this new wooing in this strange shy fashion was grateful to her and gave her a warm, comforted, feeling.

It was the evening after Madame Collins’ visit that it really began over again. They were sitting together in the back room, trying to talk, when Collins himself called. Mrs. Marble brought him in. He was a frail, pale, fair man, and he looked white and fagged. He sat down on the chair offered him with a sigh.

“I’ve come to see if you know anything about my wife,” he said wearily.

“What, Marguerite? Why, yes, she was here yesterday. She said she was just off for a holiday. Where was it she said she was going to, Will?”

===Chinese===
可马布尔太太察觉到的，还不止他的清醒。她一再撞见他带着一种惴惴不安的神情望她——那副情态，活像正在求婚的恋人。他还试着找话跟她搭讪了一两回。想想吧，好几个月来，他除了非说不可的一两个字，从没对她开过口，这一下真是天壤之别。他望她，跟她说话，那份羞怯搅得她心头乱颤；不止一回，他张了张嘴，像要对她说什么，临了又缩回去，分明是窘了。马布尔太太感到一种莫名的欢喜。说到底，亲爱的威尔就是她的整个生命，尤其在约翰已经不在身边的如今；而且，不管有什么秘密没秘密，这一场以这般古怪而羞怯的方式重新开始的求爱，让她受用，让她心里生出一种暖融融的、得了安慰的感觉。

两口子真正重新开起头来，是在科林斯夫人来访之后的那个晚上。他们正一起坐在后屋里试着说话，科林斯（Collins）本人竟登门来了。马布尔太太把他让了进来。他是个瘦弱、苍白、金头发的男人，面色灰败，一脸倦容。他在递给他的椅子上坐下，叹了口气。

“我来打听打听，你们知不知道我妻子的事。”他疲惫地说。

“怎么，玛格丽特？嗐，是啊，她昨天来过这儿。她说她正要去度假。她说她要去的是哪儿来着，威尔？”

===Original===“Normandy, of course,” said Marble. He wanted to appear to know as little as possible about the business.

“I thought as much,” sighed Collins.

Neither of the Marbles spoke, and after a moment Collins went on:

“She’s gone. I suppose she’s gone for good. I﻿—I don’t know whether she’s gone alone though.”

“But didn’t she say where she was going?” Mrs. Marble was quite irrepressible this evening, as a result of her husband’s flattering attentions.

===Chinese===
“诺曼底，这还用说。”马布尔说。他只想显得自己对这桩事情知道得越少越好。

“我猜也是。”科林斯叹道。

马布尔夫妇都没有作声，隔了片刻，科林斯又接着说下去：

“她走了。我看这一走就不回来了。只——只是我不知道，她是不是一个人走的。”

“可她没说她要上哪儿去吗？”这天晚上马布尔太太的话兴怎么也按不住，全是让丈夫的殷勤给抬起来的。

===Original===“No. I didn’t know that she was going. She took good care of that. She has taken everything with her.”

“Everything?” Mrs. Marble did not understand.

“Everything. All our savings she’s taken. All her own things, too. There’s even a bill of sale on the furniture, I found this morning.” Collins was resting his forehead on his hands. “She went yesterday,” he added inconsequently.

The Marbles felt the uselessness of trying to console him. No word was said for a space. Then Collins stood up and reached for his hat. He hesitated for a second.

“I’m sorry to have troubled you,” he said, weakly. “But I﻿—I just wanted to know.” Then, in a little spurt of feeling, he added, “It’s hateful to have to ask other people about my own wife. But﻿—I didn’t want her to go. I didn’t want her to go.”

===Chinese===
“没有。她要走，我事先一点都不知道。这一点她安排得可周到。她把什么都带走了。”

“什么都带走了？”马布尔太太没有听懂。

“什么都带走了。我们的积蓄，她全拿光了。她自己的东西，也一样拿光了。连家具都立了卖契抵押出去了，这是我今天早上才发现的。”科林斯的额头抵在两只手上。“她是昨天走的。”他又没头没脑地添了一句。

马布尔夫妇觉得，想安慰他也是白费。有好一阵子，谁都没有说话。后来科林斯站起来，伸手去够帽子，迟疑了一秒钟。

“对不起，打扰你们了。”他有气无力地说，“可是我——我就是想知道。”接着，心头一阵冲动，他又添上一句：“自己的妻子，倒要来问旁人，这滋味真不是人受的。可是——我本不想让她走的。我不想让她走啊。”

===Original===He almost broke down, but turned away and began to stumble to the door. Marble followed him. Halfheartedly, but in a man-of-the-world tone, he offered his help.

“If there’s anything I can do, Collins﻿—”

“I don’t think there is,” said Collins, feebly.

“Money?”

“No, I won’t want money. It was she who wanted money.”

===Chinese===
他几乎当场垮下来，但还是扭过脸去，深一脚浅一脚地往门口走。马布尔跟了上去，半心半意、却又拿出一副老于世故的腔调，表示愿意帮忙。

“科林斯，要是有什么用得着我的地方——”

“我看没有。”科林斯有气无力地说。

“缺钱用吗？”

“不，我用不着钱。要用钱的是她。”

===Original===Collins was feeling his way blindly, weakly, down the passage. His shoulders drooped. Clearly he was quite broken down by the desertion of his wife﻿—much more than Marble had expected. It became obvious that Marguerite’s tales of their unhappiness together had been one-sided.

“Well, if there *is* anything I can do﻿—” said Marble again.

It was the inevitable feeble proffer of help, and Collins declined it once more. Then he went out into the night, with dragging feet, hardly able to walk. There were tears in Mrs. Marble’s eyes when Marble came back to her in the sitting-room.

“Poor man,” said she.

Marble nodded.

===Chinese===
科林斯正摸索着往走廊那头去，脚步虚浮，浑身没劲，两个肩膀耷拉着。显然，妻子这一走，把他整个人都击垮了——垮得远不止马布尔预想的地步。事情再明白不过：玛格丽特从前念叨的两口子如何过不到一块儿，全是她的一面之词。

“那么，要是当真有什么我能效劳的——”马布尔又说了一遍。

这免不了又是一番软弱无力的帮忙表示，科林斯再一次谢绝了。随后他拖着脚步走进夜色里去，几乎连路都走不动了。马布尔回到客厅妻子身边时，马布尔太太眼里已经含着泪了。

“可怜的人。”她说。

马布尔点了点头。

===Original===“And what a hateful woman she must be!” she went on. “I thought the first time I saw her that she was﻿—well, you know, like that.”

Mrs. Marble had not thought anything of the kind; but she thought she did, after the event.

“Poor old Collins seems broken up about it,” was Marble’s comment.

“He must have loved her a lot. Poor chap! And she’s gone now, and left him alone. Hateful woman!”

===Chinese===
“那女人准是个蛇蝎心肠！”她接着说下去，“我头一回见她就在想，她这个人——唉，你知道的，就那种货色。”

其实马布尔太太当初压根儿没那么想过；只是事情过后，她觉得自己想过罢了。

“可怜的老科林斯，看样子叫这事给击垮了。”这是马布尔的评语。

“他从前一定爱她爱得很深。可怜的人！如今她走了，把他孤零零一个人撂下了。蛇蝎心肠的女人！”

===Original===The tears were even more imminent in her eyes now, as she stood by him, and there was a strange surge of emotion in her breast. Marble looked at her queerly. Both their hearts were throbbing tumultuously.

“You wouldn’t do a thing like that, would you?” said Marble, his hands playing with her sleeves.

Annie looked up at his face for a second﻿—only a second.

“Oh, how could I? Oh, Will, Will, dear.”

There was no more need for words. But as Marble kissed her﻿—her cheeks were wet, too, by now﻿—he felt a queer, guilty sensation internally. And yet he meant that kiss, he really did. Perhaps Judas felt the same once.

===Chinese===
这时她站在他身旁，眼里的泪水愈发一触即落，胸中涌起一阵奇异的情绪激流。马布尔神情古怪地望着她。两个人的心都在纷乱地狂跳。

“你决不会干出那种事来，对吧？”马布尔说，两只手抚弄着她的衣袖。

安妮抬眼望了望他的脸，只一秒钟——仅仅一秒钟。

“噢，我怎么会？噢，威尔，威尔，亲爱的。”

再不需要更多的话了。可是当马布尔吻她的时候——这时她的脸颊也已经被泪水打湿了——他心里却泛起一种古怪的、负罪般的滋味。然而这个吻是出自真心的，实实在在的真心。也许当年犹大（Judas）也曾有过同样的滋味。

## XIV

===Original===
And so, incredible as it might seem, sunshine had come again for a space to 53 Malcolm Road. The black pall of terror was lifted, and Annie Marble actually went singing in her high, cracked voice up and down the stairs as she did her housework. Not one word had passed between them concerning the shade of terrible danger that brooded over them, but now that each knew, and had shown that they could bear with the knowledge, the shade did not seem so black. It was a trouble shared, and a trouble shared *voluntarily* is a trouble robbed of half its weight.
Singing, Annie Marble went up and down the stairs. Marble, sitting downstairs, could hear her thin voice and her light step. For the present the voice did not call up a scowl to his brow; nor did the step seem too stealthy to be tolerated, as once it did. Whisky had lost its savour for the time being; there was no pressing need for it to dull his mind. Most of the time Marble’s mouth was twisted with a queer smile﻿—and he had not smiled for months﻿—as he thought of the difference his taking notice of his wife had made. He was glad of it, too, and now he could not think of his wife without that smile. He felt glad and comforted as he thought of her. Her present high spirits might be pathetic; they might even seem a trifle ridiculous, but they were, nevertheless, infectious. Within Mr. Marble’s bosom there was a fondness, almost a fatherly fondness, springing up for the woman who loved him so well.
And, estimating gains from the most sordid point of view it was a distinct advantage that he should have in the house a willing ally upon whom he could rely and who was possessed of sufficient knowledge of the facts of the case to help him should emergency arise.
Mr. Marble was even able occasionally to throw off his obsession completely and leave the house﻿—and the garden﻿—to his wife’s care, while he went forth through the dingy streets for exercise. The straggling rays of spring sunshine seemed to warm him, at a time when other people were hurrying along clasping their heavy coats to themselves in the bitter cold, and he blinked at the sunlight gratefully with house-blinded eyes.
As for Annie Marble, she was a changed woman. She could sing about the house; the housework seemed nothing to her nowadays, so comforting was the knowledge that darling Will was downstairs thinking about her; she routed out from some neglected kitchen shelf a stained copy of Mrs. Beeton﻿—a wedding gift, unlooked at since she first had two children on her hands, sixteen years ago﻿—and laboured joyously, though rarely successfully, compounding new delicacies for her beloved lord. For now there was no pinch for money, and Annie had found sixteen years ago that if one wanted to cook in the style of Mrs. Beeton there was a great need of money. And the will had to be there, too. So the evenings often found her sitting laboriously composing notes to the big stores ordering all sorts of strange things, things which she had never thought of ordering before, bottled oysters, asparagus, foie gras, queer lists for which Mr. Marble signed the cheques without a murmur. He felt that at last he was beginning to have some benefit from the money he had won at such risk during his slavery at the Bank. It was the first time.
And Mrs. Marble’s personal expenses, too, began to show a healthy increase. She did not venture up to Bond Street again, that was asking too much of her; she could not face the immense superiority of the young ladies in those shops; even High Street, Kensington, was a little above her head, but in Rye Lane she was exceedingly happy. The shops therein arrogantly called it “the Regent Street of South London” in their advertisements, and they did their level best to live up to the boast. Mrs. Marble’s frail little figure and her witless face, made almost pretty by reason of her happiness, became well known there. She would flit around the big shops, ordering here, trying on there, with in her manner a little trace of apology for troubling the assistants, for all her money. For her it was one of the very keenest of enjoyments, so keen that it almost hurt, to buy things, anything she liked, without having to think about the cost. But always she would stop in the middle of her shopping and hurry anxiously to catch a bus to get home in case her darling Will was growing anxious about her.
===Chinese===
于是，尽管说来叫人难以置信，马尔科姆路（Malcolm Road）53号又迎来了一段阳光灿烂的日子。恐怖的黑幕揭开了，安妮·马布尔（Annie Marble）做起家务来，居然一路哼着歌在楼梯上走上走下，那又高又哑的嗓音飘荡其间。关于笼罩在他们头顶的那团可怕的危险阴影，两人之间从未吐露过一个字；可如今既然彼此都心知肚明，也都表明了承受得住这份知情，那阴影便显得不那么漆黑了。这是一份分担了的烦恼，而一份被人*自愿*分担的烦恼，分量便被夺去了一半。
安妮·马布尔唱着歌，在楼梯上走上走下。马布尔坐在楼下，听得见她细细的嗓音和轻轻的脚步。眼下这嗓音已不会惹得他皱眉蹙额；这脚步也不像从前那样，轻得鬼鬼祟祟、叫人无法容忍。威士忌一时失了滋味；如今没有非它不可的迫切需要，不必靠它来麻钝自己的头脑。马布尔的嘴角多半时候都挂着一丝古怪的笑意——他已经好几个月没有笑过了——一想到自己关心妻子之后带来的变化，便不由得这样。他也为此欢喜，如今一想起妻子，脸上便会浮起那笑意。想起她来，他心里便觉得欢喜、觉得宽慰。她眼下这般兴高采烈也许有点可怜，甚至显得有点可笑，然而却自有感染力。在马布尔先生（Mr. Marble）的心头，对这个如此深爱着他的女人，正生出一种柔情，一种近乎父亲般的疼爱。
而且，就算从最势利龌龊的角度来掂量利害，家里有这么一个心甘情愿的同盟者也是明摆着的好处：他尽可倚仗她，而她对案情的底细又知道得足够多，一旦事急，足能帮他一把。
马布尔先生甚至偶尔能将心头的魔障整个抛开，把家宅——连同花园——交给妻子照管，自己出门穿过灰扑扑的街道去活动活动筋骨。在旁人都裹紧厚重的外衣、冒着凛冽严寒匆匆赶路的时节，春日里疏疏落落的阳光却似乎偏偏暖着他，他那一双久居室内的眼睛迎着日光直眨，心里满是感激。
至于安妮·马布尔，她简直像换了一个人。她能在屋里哼着歌；如今家务活在她算不得什么，一想到亲爱的威尔（Will）正在楼下惦记着她，心里便踏实无比。她从久已冷落的厨房搁板上翻出一本沾了污渍的比顿夫人（Mrs. Beeton）菜谱——那原是件结婚礼物，打十六年前头一回手边拖着两个孩子起，就再没翻开过——她兴致勃勃地埋头钻研，要给心爱的夫君调配些新鲜的美味，虽说十次里难得成一次。好在如今手头不再拮据；安妮早在十六年前就已明白，要想照比顿夫人的路子做菜，是万万缺不得钱的。而且还得有那份心气。于是晚间常见她坐在那里，一笔一画费劲地给各大商店写信，订购种种稀奇的东西，都是她从前想都没想过的：瓶装牡蛎、芦笋、鹅肝酱——面对这些古怪的货单，马布尔先生签起支票来毫无二话。他觉得，自己当年在银行苦役般的日子里冒着偌大风险赢来的那笔钱，如今总算开始尝到一点甜头了。这是头一回。
马布尔太太的私房开销也开始可观地见涨。她不敢再上邦德街（Bond Street），那对她要求太高了；她招架不住那些铺子里年轻女店员那股盛气凌人的优越感；就连肯辛顿高街（High Street, Kensington）也让她有点高攀不上，可在赖巷（Rye Lane）她却快活极了。那里的商家在广告里大言不惭地称它是“南伦敦的摄政街”，而且也当真竭尽全力要对得起这番夸口。马布尔太太那瘦弱娇小的身影，还有她那张憨傻的脸——因了心底的幸福，几乎称得上好看了——在那里渐渐出了名。她在各家大商店里飞来飞去，这儿订点货，那儿试穿一下，举手投足间总带着一丝歉意，仿佛为麻烦了店员而过意不去，尽管花的分明是她自己的钱。对她来说，买东西——买任何中意的东西而不必计较价钱——是世间最痛快的一种享受，痛快得几乎叫人发疼。只是她总会买到一半便停了下来，心急火燎地去赶公共汽车回家，生怕亲爱的威尔正在惦记她、为她着急。

===Original===
Yet this happiness, this moment of peace, was only a lull in the storm. They were both conscious of this, although they never admitted it even to themselves. And because they would not admit it they were still handicapped in their relations with each other. Annie found this one morning when she came home from Rye Lane and found Marble sitting spiritlessly in his chair in the sitting-room, in almost the same way as he had sat in the dark days gone by. There was a cloud on his brow; she could tell that at once. But she tried to act naturally. She came fluttering up to him with all her parcels, dropped the latter carelessly on the table, and bent over and kissed him lightly, spontaneously﻿—a trick she had never mastered before, not even in the honeymoon days.
“I’ve got back, you see,” said she. It was just the sort of thing one would expect her to say, and because of this it ought to have called up an instant smile; it would have done so yesterday.
But today there was no smile. Marble’s set, dull look frightened her, it was so like the look he had worn in the bad time. A little shudder ran through her, as she realized that it was calling up within her the same sensations, as though they were echoes, that she had known during the same period. A light had gone out in the world.
“What’s the matter, dear?” she said. “Aren’t you﻿—aren’t you well?” that was all she could say, because of the barrier still between them. She could not very well say: “Is your conscience troubling you?” or “Are you still afraid of detection?”
And Marble could only answer dully, “Oh, I’m all right,” and put her aside rather abashed and frightened. He could not tell her that what he had foreseen had happened; that by the second post, happily after she had gone out, there had arrived a letter from Rouen, a cruel, bitter letter, delicately phrased and worded, apparently telling him of the writer’s unbounded affection for him, but really only a sneering demand for money﻿—more money. The actual cash did not matter so much; Marble had enough and to spare to keep even Marguerite Collins quiet. No, it was not the money. It was﻿—although he did not allow himself to believe it﻿—the fact that the letter had brought back into his life what had been for a moment absent﻿—the sense of hideous insecurity, the knowledge that the future was laden with all sorts of ghastly possibilities, and had once more set his mind running on the things that might happen. Marble started drinking heavily again that day. He could hardly be blamed for so doing.
===Chinese===
然而这份幸福，这片刻的安宁，不过是暴风雨中一阵短暂的间歇。他们两个心里都有数，只是连对自己都不肯承认。而正因为不肯承认，他们彼此相处仍有一层隔碍。一天早晨，安妮从赖巷回家，发现马布尔无精打采地坐在客厅自己的椅子里，那副坐相几乎同过去那些黑暗的日子里的一模一样。他眉宇间笼着一层阴云；她一眼便看了出来。可她还是竭力装作若无其事。她抱着大包小包翩然扑到他跟前，随手把包裹往桌上一撂，俯下身去，轻快而自然地吻了他一下——这一手她从前可从来没学会过，连蜜月里也没有。
“你瞧，我回来啦。”她说。这话正是人家料定她会说的，也正因为如此，本该立刻换来一个微笑；换在昨天，必定会的。
可是今天没有微笑。马布尔那呆板阴沉的神情吓着了她，那太像他倒霉的日子里挂在脸上的模样了。她身上掠过一阵轻轻的寒噤，因为她意识到，这神情正在自己心里唤起当年同样种种的感受，恍如旧日的回声。世上有一盏灯熄灭了。
“怎么啦，亲爱的？”她说。“你是不是——是不是不舒服？”她只能说得出这一句，因为他们之间仍横着一道屏障。她总不便问：“是你的良心在折磨你吗？”或者“你还怕被人发觉吗？”
马布尔也只能木然答一句：“哦，我没事。”便把她轻轻推开，自己心里又窘又怕。他不能告诉她，他早就预感到的事到底发生了：第二班邮递送来了一封鲁昂（Rouen）的来信——幸而那时她已经出门——一封刻毒狠辣的信，措辞却婉转雅致，表面上倾诉着写信人对他的无限深情，骨子里不过是一句冷嘲热讽的讨钱——讨更多的钱。现钱本身倒在其次；马布尔的积蓄，就是拿去堵玛格丽特·科林斯（Marguerite Collins）的嘴，也绰绰有余。不，问题不在钱。而在于——尽管他不肯让自己相信——这封信把那片刻间曾从他生活里消失的东西又带了回来：那种骇人的不安全感，那种明知前途载满种种可怕可能的心境，于是他的心思又盘旋起种种可能临头的事。从那天起，马布尔又重新狂饮起来。这也实在怪不得他。

===Original===
Yet for all that he roused himself the next morning sufficiently to go into town; he cashed a cheque, and with the money he went into an exchange bureau, and bought many dirty hundred franc notes, which he put into a registered envelope and despatched it to Rouen.
It was in this fashion that the old atmosphere redeveloped at 53 Malcolm Road. It grew slowly, and the young friendship died hard, but the one grew and the other died, inevitably, infallibly. And the passionate love that Annie bore to Marble, the love that he had roused, was beaten down and trampled underfoot. Passion she had known before, in a vague sort of way, when they had first been married, and love she had always borne him, but the new love, this splendid brilliant thing that had just come into her life, borne of trouble shared with him, and which for this space had illuminated her whole being, was changed to poison and bitterness. It was bad for both of them.
The effect was not yet well marked by the time Easter came, bringing back Winnie from school. She had changed, just as she had changed during the other two terms. She was taller﻿—she nearly overtopped her father now﻿—and she was more beautiful than ever. Her manner had changed, too. She had gained in assurance﻿—it might be better put as insolence﻿—and her voice had gained in its marked throaty quality. Her complexion was wonderful and her figure was marvellous. Her upper lip was short and her eyelids drooping, and she carried herself with an easy erectness that accentuated the arrogance of her manner.
She was head girl of the school now, thanks to a capacity for producing good results in examinations without doing much work beforehand, and thanks also to an unexpected flair for lacrosse and for tennis; she was not the sort of girl to stand any nonsense from old-fashioned parents, not by a long chalk.
At first things did not go too badly. Matters had not declined very much from the old standard of perfection that they had reached during the good time that had just ended. Winnie’s drooping lids lifted a trifle in surprise the first lunchtime, when she saw the spotless white cloth and the bright silver, and was given a lunch not very inferior either in quality or quantity to that she was given at school.
===Chinese===
尽管如此，第二天早晨他还是强打起精神进了城；他兑现了一张支票，揣着现钱进了一家兑换所，买回许多油腻破旧的百法郎纸币，装进一只挂号信封，寄往鲁昂。
旧日的气氛就是这样在马尔科姆路53号重新滋生起来的。它长得慢，那份新生的情谊也不肯轻易咽气，可一个越长越旺，一个终究枯萎，不可避免，绝无例外。安妮对马布尔的那份炽烈的爱，那份被他唤醒的爱，就这样被击倒在地，遭人践踏。新婚之初她也曾朦朦胧胧地尝过激情，对他的情意更是从来就有，可是这份新生的爱，这件刚刚照进她生命里的辉煌灿烂的宝贝，这份由与他共患难而生的爱，这段日子里曾照亮她整个身心的爱，如今变了毒药和苦胆。这对他们两个都有害无益。
到复活节温妮（Winnie）从学校回来的时候，这影响还不算十分明显。她又变了，正如前两个学期里一直在变那样。她长高了——如今个子几乎要超过父亲——也出落得比以往任何时候都美。她的做派也变了。她添了几分自信——说成傲慢无礼也许更贴切——嗓音里那股特有的喉音也更重了。她肤色好得出奇，身段美妙无双。她上唇短短的，眼皮微微低垂，举手投足自有一种从容的挺拔，愈发衬出那一身倨傲的气派。
她如今当上了学校的学生长（head girl），一凭她不用怎么下功夫就能在考试中取得好成绩的本事，二凭她在长曲棍球和网球上出人意料的天分；她可不是那种肯受老派父母半句啰嗦的女孩，差得远呢。
起初事情过得还算不坏。比起刚刚结束的好时光里他们达到的那种尽善尽美的光景，也还没有败落多少。头一顿午饭时，温妮看见那雪白无瑕的桌布和亮闪闪的银餐具，吃到的午餐无论质与量都不比学校里的逊色多少，耷拉着的眼皮不由得微微一抬，露出几分惊讶。

===Original===
But the brief spell of intimacy her parents had enjoyed had left an unfortunate legacy behind it. They could quarrel now, which was more than they had been able to do before, and they rather took advantage of it. The disappointment of the decline of their happiness rasped their nerves, and they displayed a distressing tendency to snap at one another which Winnie deprecated. It was rank bad form for husband and wife to quarrel in public. Winnie considered that her presence was sufficient to make the quarrels take place “in public.”
Behind the wrinkle in Winnie’s brow many things were slowly developing. She liked to think of herself as cold-blooded and calculating. Calculating she may have been; cold-blooded she certainly was not. She could weigh up chances, and make a plan of campaign, but she never chose the cautious plan indicated by those chances. Winnie’s cold-bloodedness amounted to an ability to see the folly of recklessness combined with an inability to avoid being reckless.
First of all she was cautious. She refitted her wardrobe to the fullest extent she could possibly contrive; her father paid the bills without a murmur. He still could take delight in the fact that his daughter was at school with two Honourables﻿—daughters of a profiteering war peer﻿—and that during the last holidays she had met several other titled people to speak to. He did not object in the least to paying for her clothes under these circumstances.
Even while Winnie was studying spring fashions she found herself smiling wryly with relief that her parents had this queer fad for living in a poky house in a poky suburb. If they had launched out a bit when they had made their money, as once she had wanted them to, there would not be all this loose cash to throw about. Twelve hundred a year was not much; if they had a big house and a motorcar her father would certainly not be able to pay three hundred a year for her school expenses, nor these big sums for her clothes, and as for the cheque she had just wheedled out of him﻿—well, he would have thought more than twice about giving her that!
Winnie was acutely aware of the atmosphere of insecurity that hung like a fog about 53 Malcolm Road; of its true cause she was, of course, ignorant, but she appreciated it sufficiently to do her best to make hay while the sun still shone. She had plenty of clothes, and she had a monstrous sum in her handbag﻿—an amount undreamed of by her schoolfellows and most certainly unknown to her schoolmistresses. For all the fact that it was a school for profiteers’ daughters there would have been a huge commotion if it had got about that Winnie Marble habitually carried over a hundred pounds, a bulky roll of five and ten-pound notes with her. But Winnie was cautious so far; she took pains that it did *not* get about. Money was always useful; and at the back of Winnie’s mind there was a half-formed plan, in carrying out which she would find it more than useful, she expected.
Last holiday had been most successful. The girl she had stayed with had, of course, been only a girl, and the other guests who came at odd intervals had hardly noticed her. But they noticed Winnie all right. It would have been hard not to. Winnie, to the annoyance of her hostess and the chagrin of the daughter of the house, had climbed into the position of full guest; she attained brevet woman’s rank and clung to it like a leech. The other women had turned up their noses; the men had grinned and played up to her. And two of the men were likely to be useful to Winnie if ever she decided to act on that half-formed plan. They were powers in the world of musical comedy﻿—maybe because they, too, were war profiteers. But for all that it was a little inconvenient that she was not to be asked to that house again. She would be glad to have somewhere to go this holiday.
===Chinese===
可是她父母享受过的那段短暂恩爱，背后却留下一宗不幸的遗产。他们如今吵得起来架了——这可是从前办不到的——而且还真有些乘机发挥。幸福日渐败落带来的失望磨着他们的神经，两口子动辄恶语相向，那副模样实在不成体统，温妮很是不以为然。夫妻当众争吵，实在是失礼之尤。而温妮认定，只要有她在场，这些争吵便算是在“大庭广众”之下进行的了。
在温妮眉间那道皱褶背后，许多东西正在悄悄滋长。她喜欢把自己看作又冷酷又有心计的人。心计她或许真有；冷酷她却肯定谈不上。她会掂量成败的机会，也能定下方略，可她从不选取那些机会所指示的稳妥之路。温妮的所谓冷酷，不过是看得穿轻举妄动的愚蠢，却又偏偏免不了轻举妄动罢了。
首先，她行事还是有分寸的。她把自己的衣橱翻新到所能筹办的极致；父亲付起账来毫无二话。他至今仍能从中得意：他的女儿在学校里同两位“尊敬的”（Honourable）小姐同窗——那是某位发战争财的贵族的两个女儿——而且上个假期里还结识了好几位有头衔的人物，彼此交谈过。在这样的情形下，为女儿的衣裳付账，他毫无半点异议。
温妮一面研究春季时装，一面暗自苦笑，颇觉庆幸，亏得父母有这怪癖，偏要住在一处偏僻郊区一栋局促的小房子里。倘若他们当年发了财便阔起来撑起场面——她从前还真巴望过——如今哪还有这许多闲钱好随意撒开。一年一千二百英镑算不得多；倘若他们置了大宅、买了汽车，父亲决计付不出每年三百英镑的学费，付不出这些大笔大笔的衣装费，至于她刚刚连哄带骗从他手里磨来的那张支票——哼，他可就要一而再、再而三地掂量了！
温妮对笼罩在马尔科姆路53号四周那股雾一般的不安全气氛，感觉极其敏锐；至于它的真正缘由，她自然一无所知，可她心里十分有数，足以竭尽所能，趁着日头还在，先晒好自己的干草。她的衣裳已经够多，手提包里还揣着一笔吓人的巨款——那样的数目，她的同学做梦也想不到，女教师们更是万万不知情。虽说那本来就是一所专收发战争财者女儿的学校，可倘若传出去，说温妮·马布尔随身惯常带着一百多英镑——厚厚一卷五镑、十镑的钞票——准会闹起一场轩然大波。不过温妮到此为止还算谨慎；她处处留神，偏偏*不*让它传出去。钱总是有用的；温妮心底还搁着半盘没成形的主意，她料想，等到实行那主意的时候，这笔钱更会大派用场。
上一个假期过得大为成功。她借住的那户人家的姑娘，说到底不过是个小姑娘，那些隔三差五前来的宾客几乎不大留意她。可他们倒是都留意到了温妮。想不留意也难。温妮惹得女主人恼火、那家小姐气结，硬是爬上了正式宾客的位子；她挣得了一个成年女子的“荣誉军衔”，便像水蛭一般死死叮住不放。别的女客对她嗤之以鼻；男人们则咧嘴而笑，凑趣捧场。其中有两个男人，日后温妮若当真要实行那半盘主意，多半会大有用处。他们都是音乐喜剧界举足轻重的人物——兴许因为他们也同是发战争财的。不过尽管如此，那家人往后不会再请她登门，终究有些不便。这个假期若能有个去处，她自然求之不得。

===Original===
If she had, the storm might perhaps have been averted; perhaps everything might have been different. As it was, the eventual catastrophe was impossible to avoid.
It began in quite a small way, the way these things do.
“Oh, mother,” said Winnie, “you’re never going out in that hat?”
===Chinese===
她若真有个去处，这场风波或许便可以避免；或许一切都会是另一番模样。可事实是，那最终的灾祸终究无法躲过。
事情的起头极小，这类事情向来如此。
“哦，妈，”温妮说，“你该不会是要戴着那顶帽子出门吧？”

===Original===
“Why not?” asked Mrs. Marble. She had never liked the way Winnie had dismissed, with a bare word, all the fine clothes she had been buying.
“It’s too awful for words. That red and that blue﻿—”
It was unfortunate that she should have said that. The hat was one whose trimming Mrs. Marble had altered herself, and she was proud of the result.
“I think it’s very nice,” said Mrs. Marble.
“Oh, it’s not, mother. Those colours swear at each other most frightfully. Oh, dear, and your coat’s all wrinkled at the back. Why don’t you learn to put your clothes on properly?”
===Chinese===
“为什么不能？”马布尔太太反问。温妮只凭轻飘飘一个字眼，就把她置办的那些漂亮衣裳尽数贬倒，这种做派她一向看不惯。
“难看得没法形容。那种红配那种蓝——”
这话她实在不该说。这顶帽子的帽饰恰恰是马布尔太太自己动手改过的，她对改出来的效果颇为得意。
“我看它好看得很，”马布尔太太说。
“哦，才不好看呢，妈。这两种颜色搁在一块儿，冲撞得吓死人。哎呀，还有你这外套，后头全皱了。你怎么不学着点，把衣裳穿得像样些？”

===Original===
“I *do* put them on properly. I put them on better than you do. *I* don’t look fast.”
The last words slipped out almost without Mrs. Marble being aware of them. She felt sore and irritable, and it had been a tradition in her family when she was a little girl that everyone who had the self-assured manner and polished appearance that Winnie affected was “fast.”
Winnie did not mind being called fast by her mother. She only deigned to reply with a rather unladylike snort. But the word attracted her father’s attention, and he looked up sharply. He was irritable, too.
“Don’t talk to your mother like that, Winnie,” he said.
“Don’t interfere,” snapped Winnie.
===Chinese===
“我*就是*穿得像样。我穿得比你像样多了。*我*可不显得轻佻。”
末了那几个字，几乎是马布尔太太不知不觉间溜出口的。她心里正又气恼又烦躁；她做小姑娘的时候，娘家向来有个老例：凡是带着温妮那副刻意学来的自信举止和光鲜外表的人，一律是“轻佻”的。
母亲骂她轻佻，温妮并不放在心上。她只屈尊回敬了一声哼，那声哼颇不似淑女行径。可这个词惹起了她父亲的注意，他猛地抬起眼来。他心里也正烦躁。
“不许那样跟你妈说话，温妮，”他说。
“用不着你管，”温妮厉声道。

===Original===
She gave a last wrench at the wrinkled coat; but she was cross, and the coat was a hopeless misfit, anyway. Mrs. Marble staggered at the wrench. Winnie had meant nothing by it, but it brought Mr. Marble to his feet.
“Be careful, my girl,” he said.
It was that “my girl” which settled the matter. It was a horridly vulgar expression, and it took Winnie straight back to those dark days before she had ever gone to school in Berkshire. She turned and looked at her father, looked him up and down, and as she could find nothing to say she did something far more effective than any speech would have been. She turned away without a word spoken, her upper lip a little curled﻿—not much, that was the annoying part; it implied that her father was not worth being *too* contemptuous about﻿—and her best young ladyish expression on her face. It was more than flesh and blood could stand, especially flesh and blood that had been moistened by just not enough whisky for the last few days.
Marble caught her by the shoulder and swung her round again.
===Chinese===
她又朝那件皱巴巴的外衣狠扯了一把，算作收尾；可她正憋着气，下手没了轻重，再说那外衣横竖不合身得没救。马布尔太太被这一扯，身子一个趔趄。温妮原没什么用意，这一扯却叫马布尔先生霍地站起了身。
“放小心点，丫头，”他说。
